The parser writes `try { BODY } catch ($performed) { if ($performed) { const [$k, request] = $clause(Approval.approve); CLAUSE } }`, flagged `NodeFlagsVibeLang`, so TypeScript's flow analysis lets a clause start anywhere in the block; `$clause` types the bindings, and `$k` makes `resume` return `never`, so a clause that ends with a resume does not complete normally. The lowering runs BODY as a region under the runtime's `handle` and each clause as a generator that returns the statement's completion record, with the tail-resume request (D18) for a resume at a clause's end. `handle(...)` stays the expression form, which returns a value and takes `onReturn`. Not controversial.

**D133. `schema` declares a struct schema and its type** (extended by D280, the alias form `schema Name = <type>`; amended by D501). `schema User { id: string; nickname?: string; role: "admin" | "member"; home: Address }` is short for `const User = Schema.Struct({ id: Schema.String, nickname: Schema.optional(Schema.String), role: Schema.Literals(["admin", "member"]), home: Address })` and `interface User extends Schema.Schema.Type<typeof User> {}`. The parser writes each field's schema from its type: a primitive keyword, a literal (`Schema.Literal`), a union of literals (`Schema.Literals`) or of other forms (`Schema.Union`), `T[]`, `ReadonlyArray<T>`, and `Array<T>` (`Schema.Array`), a tuple, `Record<K, V>`, an object type (a nested `Schema.Struct`), or the name of another schema, which the struct refers to as a value, so any schema constant can be named. `name?: T` is `Schema.optional`, which accepts an absent key or `undefined` whatever `exactOptionalPropertyTypes` says, so decoding accepts every value the type allows; exact optional keys, checks, and transformations stay with `Schema.Struct`. Any other type, and a name whose value is not a schema, is EK2109. The interface is derived from the struct, not from the written field types, so the type and the schema cannot disagree; the written types are not checked as types. The interface goes to the parser's reparse list, which puts it before the constant in the same statement list. Not controversial.

**D134. Members are readonly unless written `mutable`**. In a `.vibe` file, every property signature and index signature of an interface or an object type is readonly unless it is written with `mutable`: `interface Cart { id: string; mutable items: Array<Item> }` means `interface Cart { readonly id: string; items: Array<Item> }`. The parser adds the `readonly` modifier, so declaration files write it and TypeScript code sees the same types. `mutable` is a modifier only when a member name follows it on the same line, so a member named `mutable` parses as before. Class members keep TypeScript's rule, and arrays and tuples are untouched, so `ReadonlyArray` is still written. A schema field written `mutable` gets `Schema.mutableKey`; an error's fields stay readonly whatever is written, because Effect's `TaggedError` types them `Readonly`. Readonly limits writes through the type, not the object: TypeScript lets a mutable object be assigned to a readonly type, so callers in either language pass the same values. Controversial because the same interface means different things in a `.ts` and a `.vibe` file, and code ported from TypeScript that writes through an interface gets TS2540 until its members say `mutable`; the payoff is that immutable data, the common case in Effect code, no longer repeats `readonly` on every field, which the docs and tests wrote 186 times.

**D135. An exported `main` runs when the host starts its module** (amended by D312 and D387). A module that exports a function declaration named `main` with no parameters ends with `if (import.meta.main) NodeRuntime.runMain(main())` in its JavaScript, so `node dist/main.js` runs the program, and importing the module, from a test or another module, runs nothing. `NodeRuntime` comes from `@effect/platform-node` when that package resolves from the module; the program resolves it as an implicit import of every module with such a `main` to find out. Without it, the module calls the runtime's `$runMain`, which `Runtime.makeRunMain` builds to behave as `NodeRuntime.runMain`: Effect's runner reports a failure nothing reported, SIGINT and SIGTERM interrupt the program where the host has a `process`, and the exit code follows `Runtime.defaultTeardown`. So a program needs no platform package to run, and Bun and Deno, which also set `import.meta.main`, run it the same way. A module that calls a runner at its top level starts its program itself and gets no entry statement, so a program never starts twice; that is how an application provides layers or passes runner options. A runner is the declaration the call resolves to: Effect's run functions (the prelude's `runPromise` among them), `ManagedRuntime`'s run methods, `NodeRuntime.runMain` and `BunRuntime.runMain`, the runtime's `runMain`, and a runner that `Runtime.makeRunMain` made. (Amended 2026-10-27: any call of a function or method spelled `runMain`, `runPromise`, `runFork`, `runSync` or `runCallback` counted, so `helper.runSync(1)` on an object of the program's own silently kept `main` from running. Evidence: case `entry-main-runner-names`, `TestEntryMainExplicitRunner`.) A `main` that compiles to plain JavaScript runs through `Effect.sync`. The examples' `run.vibe` files and the template's runner call are gone. `import.meta.main` needs Node.js 24.2 or later, and VibeLang already requires Node 26. Amended 2026-10-23 (release correction): bundles ran the wrong mains. esbuild, Rollup, Rolldown and Vite keep `import.meta` as it is in an ES module bundle, so every bundled module's `import.meta.main` was the bundle's, and an imported module's `main` ran before the entry module's; in a CommonJS bundle they empty `import.meta`, so no `main` ran and the program exited 0. A file emitted as CommonJS failed to load at all, since `import.meta` is an ES module's. Now the module ends with `runtime.$entry(import.meta.main, import.meta.url, main, () => NodeRuntime.runMain(main()))`, and `$entry` runs the arrow when the host started the module: never when `import.meta.main` is false; when it is true and `import.meta.url` names the file the host started (`process.argv[1]`, as given or with its links resolved), only if that file, which the host has already loaded and which `$entry` imports, exports this `main`, as an unbundled module and a bundle's entry module do; when it is true and names another file, as Bun, webpack and Rspack write each module's own URL beside a per-module `import.meta.main`, always. When `import.meta.main` is undefined, in a CommonJS bundle or in a file emitted as CommonJS, which passes `void 0` for both, `main` runs when the host's CommonJS main module (`process.mainModule`) exports it, once that module has run. A host that names no started file, such as Deno, trusts `import.meta.main`; a host that sets neither, such as a browser, runs nothing. Where `$entry` imports the started file or asks the CommonJS main module, `main` starts once that file has finished evaluating, not as its own module's last statement. Rejected: entry statements only for configured entries (a module that is both a library and a program, which this decision allows, stops running when started), and comparing `import.meta.url` with the started file alone (every module of an ES module bundle has the bundle's URL). Evidence: `packages/unplugin/test/entry.test.mjs` bundles compiled output with esbuild, Rolldown, Rollup, Vite, webpack, Rspack and Bun, as ES modules and as CommonJS; the plugin fixture's `script.vibe`; `packages/runtime/tests/entry.test.ts`; case `entry-main-commonjs`.

## Fewer tokens: calls (2026-10-16)

The token-reduction proposals were measured on the docs and examples (59,572 word and punctuation pieces in 632 code units). These decisions cover calls; the declaration keywords have their own section.

**D146. Every export of `effect`'s root is in scope** (extends D4 and D62; extended by D310 to the subpath packages' modules). A `.vibe` file sees every namespace and function that the `effect` package exports from its root, such as `Console`, `Stream`, `Option`, `Schedule`, `Duration`, `flow`, and `identity`, without an import, after the runtime's prelude names. A local declaration or import shadows one. A global value that JavaScript itself defines wins, so `Array`, `String`, `Number`, `Boolean`, `Symbol`, `BigInt`, `Function`, and `RegExp` keep their meaning and Effect's modules with those names need an import under another name; a host global such as the DOM's `Option` does not shadow Effect's. The emitted JavaScript imports each used module from `effect/<Module>`, never from the root, which would load every module. Subpath packages such as `effect/http` still need imports: their names (`HttpRouter`, `HttpServerRequest`) belong to one kind of program. Imports from `effect` were 5.9% of the corpus.

**D147. Calls are passed by name where a program is expected** (extends D7 and D49; superseded by D320 and D322). Where a parameter's declared type expects an `Effect`, including an array element or record field of `Effect.all`, an argument expression that calls a `.vibe` function, a function that returns an `Effect`, or a service member is passed by name: the callee receives a program that evaluates the expression each time it runs, as if the source had written `() =>` before it. `fork(fetchReport())`, `Effect.timeout(slowReport(), "20 millis")`, `Effect.race(cachedReport(), slowReport())`, `Effect.all([a(), b()])`, `HttpRouter.add("POST", "/users", createUser())`, and `NodeRuntime.runMain(main())` all mean what they mean in Effect. Elsewhere the call runs where it stands: `Console.log(fast())` logs fast's result, and `Effect.succeed(work())` succeeds with it. An expression whose value is already an `Effect` keeps its meaning. Each overload candidate decides by its own declared parameter, as for lambdas (D56). This applies to every Effect-typed parameter, including a plain callee such as `Stream.fromEffect`. Constructing that cold program does not lift an enclosing plain-annotated factory; its calls run when the Stream is consumed. EK1001 does not apply at the top level to a call passed by name. A hover on the callee says the call is passed as a program and runs later. Controversial because the call site no longer shows whether a call runs now or later; the parameter's type decides, and the hover says which. A program of several statements still needs `() => { … }`. Thunks around single calls were 2.6% of the corpus. 2026-10-24 (real-world migration): where a call runs, its contextual type is its success value's, which says nothing about the type arguments of the Effect its callee returns, so TypeScript infers none from it. Before, TypeScript inferred them as if the contextual type were that Effect, and an error instance, itself an Effect that fails with the error, gave E its own type: `function f(): Late!! { return late() }` was EK2001 for a `late` whose E no argument names. Where the contextual type is an Effect, the call stands where a program is expected, and TypeScript infers from it as usual. Case `running-call-contextual-type`. 2026-10-25 (real-world migration): a call that runs and never succeeds, stored in a variable, an object literal's field, an array literal's element or an assignment, is the warning EK1012. Running it where it stands is this decision's rule, and only a declared Effect type holds a program there (D262, D265, D269), so the binding or field never holds a value: an untyped `const missing = Effect.fail(new Missing())` failed the function at the const, and `{ follow: followUntil() }` ran an endless loop while the object was built, where Effect TypeScript stored both programs to run later. Nothing changes in what compiles; the note names the spellings that keep the program, a function (`() => followUntil()`) or the declared Effect type (`const missing: never!Missing! = …`, a declared object type), and the statement that runs it here. A stored call that succeeds with a value is not reported: nothing tells it from `const user = Users.find(id)`. EK1012 is adjustable, as EK4409 is. It fires in one of the 496 acceptance cases on `main`, `pipe-asserted-program`, whose `Effect.flip(erased())` the types say never succeeds because an assertion erased its failure channel (it succeeds when it runs), and none of the 747 documentation blocks. Cases `never-stored.fail`, `never-stored-spellings`.

**D148. Boundary hatches run plain TypeScript** (amended by D229-D232, D328 and D330; amended by D396). The prelude's `sync`, `try`, and `callback` are escape hatches whose first arguments are plain TypeScript, not VibeLang (D232). `sync(() => …)` is `Effect.sync`, so a throw is a defect; `sync(async () => …)` is `Effect.promise`. `try(() => …)` is `Effect.try`, so a throw is an expected `Cause.UnknownError`, or what an optional second argument such as `(error) => new BadJson({ error })` makes of it; `try(async (signal) => …)` is `Effect.tryPromise`. `callback((resume, signal) => …)` is `Effect.callback`: `resume(value)` succeeds, `resume.fail(error)` fails, `resume.exit(exit)` adopts an Exit, and a function the body returns runs on interruption, awaiting its PromiseLike result. A returned `Effect<void, never, R>` is the interrupt finalizer itself, and its requirements join the callback's R. Registration runs lazily on every execution; the first resume wins, normal completion skips the finalizer, and interruption aborts the signal before running and waiting for the finalizer with interruption disabled. Cleanup throws and promise rejections remain defects. This matches Effect 4.0.5 `src/internal/effect.ts:1161-1205,1210-1216` and its public finalizer type in `src/Effect.ts:1853-1859` (S19-30). A single expression is a hatch too, evaluated when the Effect runs: `sync(crypto.randomUUID())`. Inside a hatch's first argument there is no VibeLang lowering: calls do not run, so calling a `.vibe` function gives an inert Effect value for `runPromise` or `runFork`; `throw`, `async`, and `await` keep their JavaScript meaning; platform APIs and clock reads are allowed (no EK4401, EK4402, or EK4403); `return` returns from the hatch's function; and the function captures nothing of the `.vibe` file, whose values arrive as inputs (D229, D230). A hatch is recognized by name when no scope around the call declares it, so a parameter named `callback` stays a parameter. Controversial because VibeLang now has two meanings for the same function syntax, decided by the call it sits in; the hatch names keep that visible at the call site.

**D149. `try(` is a call in `.vibe` files.** A try statement starts with a block, so `try (` never starts one; the parser reads `try(…)` as a call whose callee is the identifier `try`, which only the prelude can bind. Effect's `{ try, catch }` options object passed to the hatch is EK4404, an error: the hatch would treat it as a value and succeed with the object itself.

**D150. Runners lead back in** (extends D38). `runPromise`, `runFork`, `runSync`, and `runCallback` are in the prelude, re-exported from `effect/Effect`. They are called from plain TypeScript: a `.ts` file, a hatch's body, or a module's top level. EK1005 still reports one in code a fiber runs, and does not apply inside a hatch.

**D151. The Effect module's most used functions need no prefix** (extends D146). `all`, `die`, `forEach`, `log`, `logDebug`, `logInfo`, `logWarning`, `logError`, `race`, `repeat`, `retry`, `sleep`, `timeout`, and `withSpan` are in scope as the Effect module's functions: `sleep("1 second")`, `retry(fetchUser(id), policy)`. They were 329 of the 538 `Effect.` member uses in the docs and examples. Operations that VibeLang spells another way stay qualified: `Effect.fail` is `throw`, `Effect.provide` is `with`, `Effect.scoped` and `acquireRelease` are `using`, and `Effect.sync`, `promise`, `try`, and `tryPromise` are the hatches (D148). Names that read poorly alone, such as `result`, `exit`, and `service`, stay qualified too. A local declaration shadows one; the emitted JavaScript imports each one used from `effect/Effect`.

**D152. Values of Effect's data types take their module's functions as methods** (extends D146; amended by D336). A value whose type one of `effect`'s data-type modules declares takes that module's data-first functions as methods: `readings().filter(hot).take(2)` is `Stream.take(Stream.filter(readings(), hot), 2)`, and `maybe.map(format).getOrElse(() => "none")` is `Option.getOrElse(Option.map(maybe, format), () => "none")`. The modules are `BigDecimal`, `Cause`, `Chunk`, `DateTime`, `Deferred`, `Duration`, `Effect`, `Exit`, `Fiber`, `HashMap`, `HashSet`, `Option`, `Queue`, `Ref`, `Result`, `Schedule`, `Sink`, and `Stream`; the module is the one whose declaration file declares the value's type, and every member of a union must come from the same module, as `None` and `Some` do. A function is a method when its first parameter is named `self`, effect's name for the data-first form of a dual, or ends in `OrOptions`, as in `Stream.flatten`. The receiver is that parameter, written as the call's `this`, so TypeScript checks it and infers type parameters from it, and a type guard on it narrows the receiver: after `if (maybe.isSome())`, `maybe.value` is defined. A type's own members win, so an `Effect`'s `pipe` stays its own, and arrays, strings, and objects keep JavaScript's methods. A method that returns an Effect is a call like any other, so it runs: `fiber.join()` waits for the fiber. The emitted JavaScript calls the module's function, so bundlers drop unused functions as they do for Effect code. Hover on a method names the function it calls, and completion after a dot lists the module's methods. The docs codemod rewrote 180 blocks and removed 682 of 62,163 proxy tokens (1.1%). Controversial because Effect gave up fluent methods on purpose, for tree shaking and one calling style: VibeLang keeps tree shaking, since the methods exist only at compile time, but the same dot now reaches a member on one type and a module function on another, and a member that a later Effect release adds to a type takes the call from the module function of the same name.

**D153. A method of a data type exists only in a call** (EK4501). `const read = maybe.getOrElse` has no function value to bind, because the lowering rewrites the call, not the property. EK4501 reports the access and suggests calling it or passing the module's function, `Option.getOrElse`, itself.

**D154. `.vibe` files have the Hack pipe operator** (supersedes D8; amended by D325). `x |> f(%, a)` evaluates the body `f(%, a)` with the topic `%` standing for `x`, as in TC39's Stage 2 proposal: `fetchUser(id) |> retry(%, policy) |> timeout(%, "1 second")`. The operator is `|` directly followed by `>`; it binds looser than every binary operator and the conditional operator, as an assignment does, and associates to the left. A body is a conditional expression, so an arrow function, an assignment, or a yield there needs parentheses. `%` where an expression starts is the topic of the innermost pipe whose body holds it; after an operand, `%` is still the remainder operator, so `n |> % % 2` is `n % 2`. The pipe has its body's type, and the body has the pipe's contextual type. Hover on `%` shows its type and whether it is a value or a program. The emitted JavaScript has no pipe: a topic used once, after only values that can neither run code nor change (literals, consts, functions, classes and namespace members), becomes the left side itself (`price |> double(%)` is `double(price)`); otherwise the left side runs once into a variable, and a function in the body that reads the topic keeps the value of the evaluation that created it. The formatter keeps `|>` together. VibeLang uses `%`, the token in the proposal's README, although the proposal's wiki disqualifies it for engine parsing complexity; VibeLang's parser decides by position, and VibeLang will follow the token TC39 chooses. The docs rewrite changed 21 blocks, 15 to `|>` and 6 data-type pipes to method chains (D152), and removed only 23 of 61,678 proxy tokens: `x |> f(%, a)` costs what `pipe(() => x, f(a))` costs. Its gain is reading order and any argument position, not tokens. Controversial because the proposal has been at Stage 2 since 2021, its topic token is undecided, and TypeScript will not parse `|>` until Stage 3, so `.vibe` code moves further from TypeScript.

**D155. A topic follows the by-name rule** (extends D147; superseded by D325). Where `%` stands for an argument whose parameter expects a program, and the left side runs an Effect, the left side is passed by name: that `%` is a program that evaluates the left side each time it runs, and the left side does not run before the body. `fetchUser(id) |> retry(%, policy)` means `retry(fetchUser(id), policy)`, and in a chain each `%` passes everything before it: `timeout` in `a() |> retry(%, p) |> timeout(%, t)` receives the retried program. Elsewhere the left side runs once, before the body, and `%` is its value. A pipe that passes its left side by name and also uses it as a value is EK1008, because the left side would run both before the body and inside the program. A pipe whose topic is passed by name runs nothing at a module's top level, so EK1001 does not apply to its left side. Controversial for the reason D147 is: the parameter's type, not the source, decides whether the left side runs now or later; the hover on `%` says which.

**D156. Pipe syntax errors**. A body that never uses `%` is EK1006: the left side's value would go nowhere, which the proposal makes an early error. A `%` outside every pipe's body is EK1007; it resolves to the error type, so TypeScript reports nothing more.

## Fibers (2026-10-17)

**D166. `fork` is a keyword** (amended by D322, D328 and D391; amended by D408). `fork getUser(id)` parses as `fork(getUser(id))` and `fork { ... }` as `fork(() => { ... })`, so the fiber belongs to the enclosing block (D95) and the operand is passed by name (D147). The operand binds as `await`'s does: `fork a().b` forks `a().b`. The keyword form needs its operand on the same line, starting with a name, `this`, `new`, `super`, `{`, or `await`; `fork(` stays a call, which means the same, and `fork [x]` stays an element access, so no code that parses today changes meaning.

**D167. `await` waits for fibers and Deferreds** (superseded by D225 and D226). Outside an `async` function, `await x` parses as the runtime call `$await(x)`, which runs where it stands: its value is what it waited for and its failures join E. It waits for a `Fiber`, a `Deferred`, or an array, tuple, or object of them, keeping the shape; when one fails, the fibers still running are interrupted and the failure propagates, as `Effect.all` fails fast. `await` before a call that already runs is EK4601, on a promise EK4602, on anything else EK4603, and inside a hatch's function that is not `async` EK4604. `async` stays EK1003, and inside an `async` hatch body `await` keeps its JavaScript meaning (D148).

**D168. `select` races its cases** (superseded by D197). `select { case const x = a(): ... case b(): ... default: ... }` starts each case expression, a program passed by name, in its own fiber in the order written; the first to finish wins, its statements run with its binding, and the others are interrupted; a failure of the first to finish propagates. Of cases ready at once, the first written wins, because a case that finishes while the cases start stops the rest from starting. A `default:` clause runs when no case finishes while they start (a poll). `break` leaves the select, as it leaves a `switch`; the last clause, or `default`, is the final branch, so a select whose clauses all return ends the function. `select {}` waits forever, as in Go. It parses as a labeled block around `$select(...)`, whose result is a union discriminated by the case's index.

**D169. `for concurrent` runs a loop body per item** (amended by D326). `for concurrent(n) (const x of xs) body` parses as `$forConcurrent(xs, n, (x) => { body })`, which is `Effect.forEach` with concurrency `n` (no limit without one) that discards results: every item runs in its own fiber, all join before the loop ends, and a failure interrupts the rest. `continue` ends one item (the parser writes it as a `return` from the body). A `break` that would leave the loop is EK4605 and a `return` is EK4606, because no loop runs on the body's fiber. Use `forEach` for results.

**D170. `detach` and `forkIn` are fork's variants** (amended by D322: they take a program written as a function). `detach(program)` is `Effect.forkDetach`: a fiber nothing owns. `forkIn(scope, program)` is `Effect.forkIn` with the scope first, so the program reads last. Both take the program by name and are prelude names.

**D171. `await`'s types reach one level**. The runtime waits through nested arrays and objects, but its types, and the EK4603 check, accept an array or object only of `Fiber`s and `Deferred`s themselves: recursive conditional types over arbitrary nesting exceed TypeScript's instantiation depth. Nest awaits instead.

## Names follow Effect (2026-10-17)

**D172. The provide block is spelled `provide`** (amends D130). `provide AppLive { ... }` replaces `with AppLive { ... }`, so the keyword is the name of the function it stands for, `Effect.provide`: VibeLang keywords reuse Effect's own names so a reader can map each form back to Effect. The parser reads `provide` as the block form only when a layer expression and an opening brace follow on the same line, so a local function or variable named `provide`, a call `provide(x)`, and an element access `provide[i]` keep their meaning. The old spelling still compiles the same way and draws EK1009, a warning, whose quick fix replaces `with` with `provide`; the language service matches EK quick fixes by the diagnostic source `EK`. The generated region function is `f$provide`. Not controversial.

**D173. `provide Key = value` provides one service** (extends D130). A provide block's providers are separated by commas, and a provider written `Key = value` provides that one service, as `Effect.provideService` does: `provide AppLive, Clock = testClock { ... }` compiles to `Effect.provide` with `[AppLive, Layer.succeed(Clock.Clock, testClock)]`, and the block's requirements drop every provided key. The parser rewrites the list into an array of layers and each `Key = value` into `$provideService(Key, "Key", value)`, a runtime function whose types check the value against the key's service shape. It has one signature, with the name before the value, so a method in the value is checked against the key's shape from the start (D92). `provide(A, Key = value) { ... }` means the same as `provide A, Key = value { ... }`, as `fork(x)` means `fork x`; the parser reads the parenthesized list as an expression, not as arrow-function parameters. 2026-10-24 (real-world migration): a requirement that is a type parameter `R` keeps `Exclude<R, I>` of the services `I` the block provides, as `Effect.provideService` leaves it; the block kept `R`. Case `provide-generic-requirement`.

**D174. An effect module stands for its key of the same name**. In a `Key = value` provider, a module whose export of the same name is a service key stands for that key: `Clock` for `Clock.Clock`, `Random` for `Random.Random`, `Scheduler` for `Scheduler.Scheduler`. Those three are references with defaults, so providing one swaps a runtime service for the block and the fibers it starts without changing any contract. A hover over the key names the service it provides. A key that is neither a service key nor such a module is TS2769, which names the key's expected type. Not controversial.

**D175. A provider list is read element by element** (amended by D362). The analyzer computes what an array-literal provider provides from each element's type. The array's own type merges the elements into one union, and because a layer is contravariant in what it provides, `Layer<Names> | Layer<never>` reduces to `Layer<never>`, which would hide `Names`. This also fixes `provide [NamesLive, RandomLive] { ... }` written as an array. Not controversial.

## Primitives (2026-10-17)

Every Effect primitive has a form in the language itself, in Effect's own words, believable as TypeScript. D176-D180 add the ones the primitives list kept.

**D176. A catch clause typed `Cause` catches every reason** (superseded by D217). `catch (cause: Cause)` (or `Cause<E>`, `Cause.Cause`) is `Effect.catchCause`: it sees expected failures, defects, and interrupts, binds the whole `Cause<E>` where E is what the try block can fail with, and may follow typed and untyped clauses, which still see expected failures only. Clauses after it never run (EK2106). It does not catch a control operation's abandonment, whose internal interrupt unwinds through. A try statement with one makes its function an Effect. A type annotation choosing semantics is new for `catch`; it reuses the typed clauses' shape (D131).

**D177. `throw cause` rethrows every reason** (amended by D348; superseded by D406). Throwing a value whose type is a `Cause` (or a union of them) is `Effect.failCause`: the failure keeps every reason, and the Cause's E joins the function's E. Throwing any other value keeps D13's meaning.

**D178. `finally (exit)` binds the Exit**. `finally (exit) { ... }` is `Effect.onExit`: the block runs on every exit without interruption, with `exit: Exit<void, E>`, E being what the try statement can fail with. A success carries no value, because the try block's statements have none; a `return` inside the try block still returns its value. `finally { ... }` keeps D15's meaning.

**D179. `exit`, `interrupt`, and `yieldNow` are bare functions** (extends D151; amended by D322: `exit` takes a program written as a function). `exit(program)` is `Effect.exit` with the program passed by name; `interrupt()` is `Effect.interrupt` and `yieldNow(priority?)` is `Effect.yieldNow` or `Effect.yieldNowWith`, functions in the runtime because Effect's own are values. A bare `yield` statement was rejected: it clashes with producers and is not Effect's word.

**D180. `uninterruptible`, `restore`, and `interruptible` blocks**. `uninterruptible { ... }` is `Effect.uninterruptibleMask`; `restore { ... }` inside it runs its statements with the interruptibility the region began with; `interruptible { ... }` is `Effect.interruptible`. Statements inside stay code of the enclosing function, so `return`, `break`, and `continue` keep their meaning. Each block calls the prelude function of its name, `uninterruptible(program)`, `restore(program)`, or `interruptible(program)`, which also take a program directly (by name, D147), so a block read as a block parameter means the same apart from jumps. `restore` finds the innermost region's restore through a fiber reference, so a restore outside any region runs its program unchanged; the block form there is EK2111. At a module's top level the blocks are EK1001. The words are contextual: a name followed by `{` on the same line is a TypeScript syntax error, so no code that parses today changes meaning.

## TC39 forms: blocks (2026-10-17)

**D186. `throw` is also an expression** (amended by D327) ([throw expressions](https://github.com/tc39/proposal-throw-expressions), stage 2). In a `.vibe` file, `throw` in expression position takes a unary expression, as the proposal's grammar has it: `users.get(id) ?? throw new NotFound({ id })`, `ok ? value : throw new Invalid()`, `(id) => find(id) ?? throw new NotFound({ id })`. It parses as `(() => { throw operand })()`, so it fails exactly as the throw statement does (an `error` value joins E, a Cause rethrows) and has type `never`; the right side of `??`, `||`, `&&`, and `?:` still runs only when chosen. A statement that starts with `throw` is the throw statement. Its operand is the whole expression, as in TypeScript. In expression position, an unparenthesized `throw` on the left of a binary operator (except comma), as a conditional's condition, or as the operand of `as` or `satisfies` is refused with EK1010: only its unary operand would be thrown. Write `throw (cond ? a : b)` in a catch-expression handler to throw the selected value (S10-12); right-hand throws such as `x ?? throw e` stay legal.

**D187. `do { }` is an expression** (amended by D322) ([do expressions](https://github.com/tc39/proposal-do-expressions), stage 1). `do { statements }` in expression position has the completion value of its last statement: an expression statement, an `if` with an `else` whose branches end in one, a `try`/`catch` whose blocks end in one, or a `throw`. A declaration, a loop, or an `if` without `else` at the end is TS1109, and a `return` inside is TS1108, as the proposal forbids it. It parses as `(() => { ...; return last })()`, so it runs where it stands, and a parameter that expects a program takes it by name (D147): `race(do { sleep("1 millis"); "cached" }, fetch())`. A statement that starts with `do` is the do-while statement.

**D188. A block after a call is its program** (its placement superseded by D376: a block is always the last argument) ([block params](https://github.com/samuelgoto/proposal-block-params), stage 1; Design tradeoff: where the block goes depends on the callee's type). In a `.vibe` file, a `{` on the same line after a name, a property access, or a call passes the block as a program: `f { block }` is `f(() => { block })`, and `f(args) { block }` is `f(args, () => { block })`, one call. Where `f` is an Effect dual, the block takes the place of its program: the checker also resolves the call against `f`'s data-first signature with its first parameter moved last and its data-last signature flattened to `(args, self)`, so `withSpan("load") { ... }`, `timeout("1 second") { ... }`, and `semaphore.withPermits(1) { ... }` infer every type parameter from the arguments and the block together. The lowering emits `f(block, args)` or `f(args)(block)` for those forms and `f(args, block)` otherwise, as in `it.effect("greets") { ... }`. When several forms fit, the data-first form wins, then the data-last, then the appended call. A block param body is a function body, so a value needs `return`. A heritage clause, `new`, and a provide block's providers keep their own `{`.

## TC39 forms: await operations (2026-10-17)

**D196. The await operations start programs concurrently** (amended by D258, D322 and D377; amended by D394). `await.all x`, `await.allSettled x`, `await.race x`, and `await.any x` come from the [await operations](https://github.com/tc39/proposal-await.ops) proposal (stage 1); an object operand comes from [Await Dictionary](https://github.com/tc39/proposal-await-dictionary) (stage 3, `Promise.allKeyed`). The parser writes them as calls of the runtime's `$awaitAll`, `$awaitAllSettled`, `$awaitRace`, and `$awaitAny`, with the operand bound as `await`'s is. Each element is a program passed by name (D147), started in its own fiber; an element that is a `Fiber` or a `Deferred` already running is waited for, not restarted, and a `do { }` element (D187) is a program with statements. Each operation behaves as the `Promise` method of the same name: `await.all` is `Effect.all` with unbounded concurrency (every result, the shape kept, an object's keys in the order written, fail fast with the rest interrupted, held fibers included); `await.allSettled` is `Effect.all` with `mode: "result"` and never fails with a typed error; defects and interruption propagate; `await.race` is `Effect.raceFirst` over any number of elements (the first to finish wins, success or failure; of elements ready at once the first written wins; an empty race never finishes); `await.any` is `Effect.raceAll` (the first success; failure only when every element fails). A race over an object is EK4608. Controversial: the docs rule is "`await.x` behaves like `Promise.x`; a named Effect function keeps Effect's meaning", so `await.race` takes the first to finish while `race(...)` (D151) takes the first success. The alternative, dropping `race` from the bare functions, loses Effect's own name for the common case.

**D197. `await.race` replaces `select`** (supersedes D168). The earlier `select { case ... }` form was replaced because `case` reads as comparing a value, and "select" is not Effect's word. `select { ... }` still parses so that the checker can report EK4607 at its keyword with the replacement: `await.race [a(), b()]`, one element per case, with `do { ... }` for a case's statements. A `default:` clause has no replacement form; poll with `Queue.poll` and an `if` (or `match`). The runtime keeps `$select`, which `$awaitRace` uses.

## TC39 forms: pattern matching (2026-10-17)

All from the TC39 [pattern matching](https://github.com/tc39/proposal-pattern-matching) proposal (stage 1), under the rule that new syntax comes from TypeScript or a TC39 proposal.

**D206. `match` is an expression of `when` arms**. `match (x) { when P: v; ... default: d; }` takes the proposal's syntax: arms are tried in order, the first match gives the value, and `default:` matches anything and must come last. An arm ends with `;`, and ASI applies. `match (x) {` followed by `when`, `default`, or `}` on the same line starts a match; any other `match(...)` is a call, so a function named `match` keeps working. As in the proposal, `and`, `or`, and `not` cannot share a level without parentheses (EK4705). An arm is one expression; `do { }` (D187) gives it statements.

**D207. The supported subset**. Literals; `let`, `const`, and `var` bindings; array patterns with a rest; object patterns with shorthand bindings, quoted and computed keys, and a rest; identifier and dotted matchers; extractors with an argument list (`Option.Some(let v)`); `and`, `or`, `not`; `if (expr)` guards; `default`; and `x is P`. Deferred: regular expression and relational patterns, `async match`, and iterables in array patterns (D212, D214).

**D208. Option, Exit, Result, and primitive classes are known matchers** (amended by D328). `Option.Some`/`None`, `Exit.Success`/`Failure`, `Result.Success`/`Failure` compile to the module's own guard and read `value`, `cause`, `success`, or `failure`; `String`, `Number`, `Boolean`, `BigInt`, and `Symbol` compile to `typeof`. The bindings get exact types and need no runtime protocol. Controversial: the proposal resolves every matcher through `Symbol.customMatcher` at runtime, so a local variable named `Option` that shadows the module still compiles to `Option.isSome`. The alternative, custom matchers on Effect's data types, needs Effect to ship them and types the bindings loosely.

**D209. Exhaustiveness is checked at compile time for finite unions** (amended by D294 and D346, which supersedes its run-time fallback for open subjects, and by D471: an arm that only binds). A match without `default` whose subject's declared type is a finite union (literals, `null`, `undefined`, `void`, and objects with a string literal `_tag`, which covers `error` classes, Option, Exit, and Result) and whose narrowed type after the last arm is not `never` is EK4701, as `Match.exhaustive` is. Over any other type, an unmatched value throws the proposal's `TypeError` at runtime (`$noMatch`). Controversial: the proposal has no static check, and a match over `number` without `default` compiles even when an arm is missing; reporting every non-`never` remainder would flag correct matches whose guards TypeScript cannot narrow. Amended 2026-10-21 (D294's note): a class pattern narrows as `instanceof` does, by derivation, so a class that only shares a handled class's fields is still left.

**D210. `or` and `not` cannot bind yet** (a restriction, D374). A binding inside `or` or `not` is EK4702. The proposal allows a binding in every alternative of an `or`; the lowering writes `or` and `not` as inline boolean expressions, which have nowhere to declare a name.

**D211. `is` binds only in an `if` or `while` condition** (a restriction, D374). `x is P` is a boolean at `instanceof`'s precedence. With bindings, it must sit in the `&&` chain of an `if` or `while` condition; the names are in scope in the rest of the chain and in the then branch or loop body. Elsewhere it is EK4703. Controversial: the proposal scopes `is` bindings to the enclosing block and the else branch too; this lowering nests the then branch inside the tests, so names cannot reach the else branch or the statements after.

**D212. Unsupported forms are reported, not misparsed** (a restriction, D374). A regular expression pattern, a relational pattern such as `< 10`, and a computed key other than a name or a literal are EK4704, whose fix is a guard: `and if (n < 10)`.

**D213. Matcher semantics** (amended by D515: a tagged class tests its tag). A matcher value with a `[customMatcher]` method decides. Otherwise a primitive class tests `typeof`; another class tests `instanceof`; another function is a predicate; any other value matches by SameValue, with `0` matching `-0`. A class is a function written with `class` or one whose prototype has methods, so built-ins such as `Date` and `Map` test `instanceof`. Deviation: the proposal gives built-in constructors brand checks (`Date` matches a cross-realm date), which a runtime helper cannot write.

**D214. Lowering and its limits** (amended by D368: reads are shared within a match; the limits are restrictions, D374; amended by D417 and D441). A match after `return` becomes a block whose arms return; a match statement becomes a labeled block whose arms break; any other match becomes an immediately invoked arrow function, which is a VibeLang function, so arms can call Effect functions in every position. The subject is held in a `const` that reads it once, before any arm, as `switch` reads its discriminant, and the checker narrows the subject through the `const` in each arm, as TypeScript narrows a switch's discriminant in its clauses. Limits: an arm of a match in expression position cannot `yield`; array patterns match arrays only; object patterns do not match primitives. Amended 2026-10-26: a subject that was a name, `this`, or a chain of property and literal element accesses had been used directly and read again by each test, so a getter there could select the wrong arm.

**D215. `customMatcher` and tagged errors** (superseded for class arms by D515: an `error` class matches by its tag, as `catchTag` does, and same-tag classes match alike). The runtime exports `customMatcher`, the engine's `Symbol.customMatcher` when it has one and `Symbol.for("Symbol.customMatcher")` otherwise. `TaggedError`, so every `error` class, has a static custom matcher: `instanceof` for `is` and `when NotFound:`, and the instance as the single value for `NotFound(let e)`. Two error classes with the same tag therefore stay distinct, unlike `catchTag`.

## Catching with match (2026-10-17)

**D216. A catch clause no longer tests a failure's type** (supersedes D131). Catch the failure and test it with pattern matching (D206): `catch (e) { return match (e) { when NotFound: "missing"; default: throw e; } }`, or `if (e is NotFound) { ... }` in statement form. No special catch syntax remains. A typed clause, `catch (e: NotFound)`, still compiles as D131 describes and draws the warning EK2112.

**D217. Recovering from a whole cause matches on its exit** (supersedes D176). `match (exit(program)) { when Exit.Success(let value): value; when Exit.Failure(let cause): fallback; }`, or `if (outcome is Exit.Failure(let cause))`, sees every reason: failures, defects, and interrupts. A catch clause typed `Cause` still compiles as D176 describes and draws the warning EK2113.

**D218. A match on a caught failure is checked like any match** (amended by D346 and D349). An untyped catch variable has the try block's expected failures as its type (D13), so `match (e)` without `default` must handle every one of them (EK4701, D209) and then needs no rethrow; `default: throw e` passes the others on with their types, through a throw expression (D186). The function's E is what the arms let escape.

**D219. The quick fix rewrites retired clauses when the result is exact**. EK2112's fix writes one `match` when every clause is a single `return` of a value and every clause names one variable, and an `if (e is T) { ... } else ...` chain otherwise, ending in `else { throw e }` when no untyped clause followed. A clause's type becomes a pattern only when each union member names a class, such as an `error` declaration or `Schema.SchemaError`; for a type alias or an object type the warning has no fix. EK2113's fix rewrites `try { return X } catch (cause: Cause) { return Y }` as `return match (exit(X)) { ... }`; other shapes keep the warning without a fix.

## Syntax decisions: services and layers (2026-10-17)

**D220. `service class` and `control class`** (amends D128; superseded by D223). A service is `service class Users { find(id: string): User!NotFound; region: string }`, and a control operation is `control class Approval { approve(request: string): boolean!! }`. `service` and `control` are modifiers of a class declaration, as `declare` is, and the members have no bodies, as a `declare class`'s members have none; the meaning is D128's. The spelling was provisional between this, a plain `abstract class`, and D128's `service Users { }`. `abstract class` needs no new syntax but repeats `abstract` on every member; the modifier reads as a TypeScript class and keeps Effect's word, `service`. The spelling without `class` still compiled, with the warning EK2120, whose quick fix inserted `class`. D223 reverses both.

**D221. A layer is a call** (supersedes D129). `const UsersLive = Layer.effect(Users) { const seen = new Set<string>(); return { find(id) { return getUser(id) }, region: "eu" } }` passes the block to Effect's own `Layer.effect` through block params (D188), and `Layer.succeed(Users, { ... })` builds a layer with no setup. It adds no syntax of its own: D129's layer declaration was provisional. The `layer UsersLive: Users { }` declaration still compiles, with the warning EK2121, whose quick fix rewrites it into the call; its EK2104 order rule stays for that spelling.

**D222. A layer block's members are typed by its service** (corrected by D344: a `using` in the setup is released when the setup block returns). The block is the effect `Layer.effect` expects, so TypeScript types the returned object against the service: `find(id)` needs no annotation, and a missing or mistyped member is TS2345 at the call, whose message chain names the member that does not fit. A member's own failures and call-time requirements must fit its contract, as in `Layer.succeed` (EK2001, EK3101). The setup's failures and requirements become the layer's, so `wire` places the layers it needs, and a `using` in the setup is released when the layer's scope closes, because Effect 4's `Layer.effect` supplies one. Not controversial.

**D223. Services and controls are written without `class`** (supersedes D220). `service Users { find(id: string): User!NotFound; region: string }` and `control Approval { approve(request: string): boolean!! }` are the spelling again, with D128's meaning. The class keyword is redundant in a service declaration. `service class` and `control class` still compile, with the warning EK2120, whose quick fix removes `class`. Layers stay calls (D221).

## Join (2026-10-17)

Using await for fibers can cause confusion with JavaScript Promises.

**D225. `join` is a keyword** (supersedes D167; amended by D328 and D391). `join fiber` parses as the runtime call `$join(fiber)`, which is `Fiber.join`: its value is the fiber's and its failures join E. `join [a, b]` and `join { a, b }` join every fiber of an array, tuple, or object and keep the shape; when one fails, the fibers still running are interrupted and the failure propagates, as `Effect.all` fails fast. The operand binds as `fork`'s does (D166), and the keyword form needs its operand on the same line, starting with a name, `this`, `{`, or `[` after a space. `join(` stayed a call until D391 made it the keyword with a parenthesized operand unless `join` resolves to a binding other than the prelude's; `join[i]` without a space stays an element access, so a local or imported function named `join`, `path.join`, and `words.join(",")` keep working. A `join` on anything but fibers is EK4611, and a `join` inside a hatch's function, which is plain TypeScript, is EK4612.

**D226. A plain `await` in VibeLang code is an error**. In JavaScript `await` waits for a promise, so VibeLang keeps that meaning and gives `await` no other: `await fiber` is EK4609, whose quick fix writes `join fiber`; `await d` on a Deferred is EK4610, whose quick fix writes `d.await()`; `await` before a call that already runs is EK4601; a promise is EK4602 and waits in a hatch, `try(async () => …)`; anything else is EK4603. The await operations, `await.all`, `await.allSettled`, `await.race`, and `await.any` (D196), stay, because they follow the `Promise` methods of the same names. Inside an `async` hatch body `await` keeps its JavaScript meaning, and in one that is not `async` it is EK4604.

**D227. A Deferred waits with `d.await()`**. Method syntax (D152) already reaches `Deferred.await`, so a Deferred needs no keyword: `ready.await()` returns its value and propagates its failure.

**D228. `join`'s types reach one level**. The runtime joins through nested arrays and objects, but `$join`'s types and the EK4611 check accept an array or object only of `Fiber`s themselves. Nest joins instead.

## Self-contained hatches (2026-10-17)

Hatch code is self-contained and accepts surrounding values through explicit inputs.

**D229. A hatch's TypeScript is self-contained** (amends D148). A hatch's first argument, the function or single expression of `sync`, `try`, or `callback`, may reference its own parameters and locals, globals, prelude names, imports, and the file's `error` declarations, but no other value binding of the `.vibe` file: not an enclosing local or parameter, and not a module-level declaration. A reference to one is EK4405, an error that names the binding, and its quick fix passes the binding as an input (D230). Types are exempt. Error declarations are exempt because an error class is a plain class, and failing with one, `new BadJson({ error })`, is what a hatch most often needs from its file. Controversial because a capture is the shortest way to hand a value to TypeScript. The rule makes a hatch's function read like a standalone TypeScript function that could move to a `.ts` file unchanged, and keeps a VibeLang value from entering TypeScript unannounced. This restriction may change.

**D230. Inputs fill the first parameters** (amended by D330). Values reach a hatch as inputs, the arguments after its function: `sync((a, b) => a + b, 2, 3)`, `try((text) => JSON.parse(text), text)`. They fill the function's first parameters, so TypeScript types each parameter from its argument. An async function receives its `AbortSignal` after them, `try(async (url, signal) => fetch(url, { signal }), url)`, and a callback's register function receives `resume` and the signal after them, `callback((ms, resume) => …, 2)`. The compiler lowers inputs with `bind`, `Effect.sync(((a, b) => a + b).bind(undefined, 2, 3))`, so Effect's own signal argument lands after them. A single expression takes no inputs. The alternative, Effect's arguments first, `callback((resume, signal, ms) => …, 2)`, keeps Effect's parameter positions but needs a wrapper function in the lowering and moves the inputs' parameters with each arity. Inputs keep `callback`'s type arguments from being written, because TypeScript infers no type argument once one is written; the prelude type `Resume<A, E>` annotates `resume` instead: `callback((source, resume: Resume<number, Error>) => …, source)`.

**D231. `try`'s error mapper stays second** (amended by D330). `try(code, mapper, ...inputs)`: the mapper keeps the place D148 gave it, and inputs follow it, `try((text) => JSON.parse(text), (error) => new BadJson({ error }), text)`. With inputs first, the mapper's place would move with the number of inputs; with the mapper last, `try(f, g)` would change meaning from D148's. The compiler tells a mapper from a function input by the overload TypeScript chose: a function in second place that accepts the `unknown` error is the mapper. The runtime's `try`, which TypeScript callers and a hatch passed as a value reach, sees only values, so there a function in second place is always the mapper.

**D232. Only the first argument is TypeScript** (amends D148; amended by D330). D148 made all of a hatch's arguments plain TypeScript; now only the first is. The inputs and the mapper are VibeLang code: the inputs run before the hatch starts, so a call among them runs, `sync((name) => name.toUpperCase(), readName())`, and a plain foreign call or a clock read among them is EK4401 or EK4403 as anywhere in VibeLang code. The mapper is an ordinary callback that may read the file's bindings, `(cause) => new ChargeFailed({ cartId, cause })`. The function decides between sync and async: an `async` function, or one whose return type has `then`, is `Effect.promise` for `sync` and `Effect.tryPromise` for `try`; an `any` or `unknown` return stays synchronous.

Amended 2026-10-23 (release correction): the runtime's `sync` and `try`, which TypeScript callers and a hatch passed as a value reach, see no types, so they decide by the function's declaration. An `async` function that declares a parameter after its inputs gets the AbortSignal there and runs as `Effect.promise` or `Effect.tryPromise`, so interruption aborts the signal; a rest or defaulted parameter gets none, as `Effect.promise` makes a signal only for a function that takes one. Any other function gets its inputs only, and a promise it returns is awaited without a signal. Before, no function got a signal there: `const run = sync; run(hasSignal)` read `undefined`, and interruption left a `fetch` running.

Amended 2026-10-23 (direct hatches): a direct `sync` or `try` gives an asynchronous function exactly its inputs, then the AbortSignal when the overload TypeScript chose passes one and the function declares a parameter for it, the rule the runtime's `sync` and `try` follow; both call it through the runtime's `$asyncHatch`. An arrow function without a rest parameter keeps D230's `bind`, under which `Effect.promise` applies the same rule. Before, `Effect.promise` passed `undefined` into a rest parameter, so `sync(async (...xs: number[]) => xs.length, 1, 2)` was 3, and gave the signal to a parameter typed for something else, so `sync(async (a: number, b?: number) => typeof b, 1)` was `"object"`. Called as a value, `sync` and `try` still give that parameter the signal: they see no types, and `length` counts `b?` as it counts `signal?: AbortSignal`.

## Catch expressions (2026-10-17)

Borrowed from Zig's inline catch (`parse(s) catch 0`), TypeScript has no such proposal, but `catch` after an expression is a syntax error today, so no code that parses changes meaning.

**D236. `catch` is also an expression** (amended by D305, D306 and D322). `x catch fallback` is `Effect.orElseSucceed`: the fallback is evaluated only after an expected failure, and because it is VibeLang code it may run a program itself, as `Effect.orElse`'s would. `x catch (e) => expression` is `Effect.catch`: the handler receives the expected failure, typically `getUser(id) catch (e) => match (e) { when NotFound: guest; when Timeout: cached(id); }`. The left side is passed by name (D147), so it runs inside the catch. Defects and interrupts pass through, and so does a cause that mixes an expected failure with either, as the `catch (e)` statement lets them (D13); Effect's own `Effect.catch` would recover from such a mixed cause. The parser writes `$catchExpression(() => x, () => fallback)` or `$catchExpression(() => x, (e) => expression)`, and the runtime runs it with the kernel's `catchExpected`, which the statement uses.

**D237. `catch` binds between `??` and `? :`**. It binds looser than every binary operator, `??` and `||` included, and tighter than `? :`, assignment, arrow functions, and `|>`. It associates to the left: `a() catch b() catch c` tries `b()` after `a()` fails and falls back to `c` after `b()` fails. `lookup(id) ?? "unknown" catch "failed"` applies the default inside the catch, `ok ? find(id) catch "none" : "skip"` catches in one branch, and `id |> find(%) catch "none"` catches inside the pipe's body. A handler is an arrow function, `(e) => …` or `e => …`, whose body extends as far as an arrow function's does, so a `catch` after it belongs to the handler; parenthesize the handler to chain after it. Any other right side, a parenthesized arrow function included, is a fallback. After a try block's `}`, `catch` is the statement's.

**D238. A catch expression is typed and checked as the statement is**. The value is `A | B`, A from the left side and B from the fallback or handler. E is what the fallback or handler lets escape: the handler's parameter has the left side's expected failures as its type, so a `match` without `default` must handle every one of them (EK4701, D209) and then removes them all from E, and `default: throw e` passes the rest on with their types (D186). R joins both sides'. A left side with no expected errors is EK2004; defects pass through. A try statement gets that warning only when its block runs Effects with E = `never` (D260). A hover on the keyword names the Effect function, the failures it catches, the value, and the failures that escape.

**D239. No catch expression in a hatch body**. A hatch's function is plain TypeScript (D148, D232), where `catch` is only a statement, so a catch expression there is EK2114; move it out of the hatch, or use a try statement inside. At a module's top level it runs a program, so EK1001 applies.

## Effect's language service (2026-10-17)

Every Effect language-service rule is covered by language syntax or an editor diagnostic; syntax is preferred where it directly expresses the rule.

**D240. Every rule of Effect's language service has an answer**. The inventory is `@effect/language-service` 0.87.5's README and source: 77 diagnostics, 4 hover features, 7 completions, 15 refactors, 3 codegens, and 2 other features, 108 in all. The user guide's Devtools page answers each, in this order of preference. VibeLang's syntax or semantics rule out 36, such as `missingStarInYieldEffectGen`, since a call already runs, and `lazyPromiseInEffectSync`, since `sync(() => promise)` already compiles to `Effect.promise`. Existing EK diagnostics cover 23, such as `catchUnfailableEffect` as EK2004 and every `global*` rule as EK4402 or EK4403. Five are Effect 3 only. The rest are new diagnostics, EK4801 onward, quick fixes, refactors, hovers, and completions, except two: `outdatedEffectCodegen`, since VibeLang has no codegen comments, and `RpcClient`'s go to definition, which needs a definition hook the language server does not have. No rule needed a language change: the one candidate, nested `provide` blocks, already has D173's `provide A, B { … }`, which EK4808's quick fix writes.

**D241. EK4801-EK4825 are lint**. Unlike VibeLang's other diagnostics, none marks code without a meaning, so each has a default category and a project can change it: every one except EK4813 is in `compilerOptions.vibelang.diagnostics`, and nine of them are off until a project turns them on. An `@vibelang-diagnostics` comment changes a category from its line to the end of the file, and `@vibelang-diagnostics-next-line` for the next line, as Effect's `@effect-diagnostics` comments do; `*` names every adjustable code. Neither applies to the other EK codes, so `@ts-expect-error`'s rule (D53) stands. 2026-10-24 (real-world migration): EK4802 asks about each part of a returned expression that can be the returned value, when the whole is not an Effect: a conditional's arms, the operands of `??` and `||`, and the right side of `&&`. `emit: (n) => n === 0 ? Effect.interrupt : Console.log(n)` under a `void!never` contract compiled without a word: the call ran, and `emit(0)` succeeded with the interruption as its value. The quick fix calls the arm, `Effect.interrupt()`, which runs it. Cases `returned-effect-arm.fail`, `returned-effect-arm-called`. 2026-10-24 (real-world migration): EK4802 also reports a returned Effect value, Effect subtype or program literal that is data because the function's success type names no value: `void`, `unknown`, `any`, or an unconstrained type parameter its call infers, written or contextual. Any value is a success value there, so D24 and D269 return it as data where Effect code runs it: `Sink.forEach((n) => announce)` never ran `announce`, `withSpan("refresh") { return refresh }` succeeded with `refresh`, a callback with an `unknown` success returned a by-name program unrun, a catalog handler returned `Clock.currentTimeMillis` unrun, and a function written `unknown!!` returned an Effect subtype (an Exit, a class that extends Effect) as its value. Its note names the two spellings that say which was meant: call it to run it, or write its type as the success type, `(void!!)!E!R`, to return it. Running these instead would change programs that compile today, so it waits for The design. Of the 468 acceptance cases on `main` it fires in two, at four returns that pin the data reading: `factory-value-kept`'s two thunks D269 keeps as values (`Effect.suspend(() => () => 1)`) and `effect-subtype-success-data`'s two Exits under a written `unknown!!` (S22-3); it fires in none of the 747 documentation blocks. Cases `effect-value-as-data.fail`, `effect-value-as-data-spellings`. 2026-10-25 (real-world migration): EK4802 also reports a program named by reference, a function with no parameters that returns an Effect, where the success type names no value, as it reports a program literal there: a command handler `() => listFlows` under a `void!!` contract, with `const listFlows = () => { ... }` at the top level, succeeded with the function and ran none of it, and so did a parameter or field returned by name from `Effect.suspend`'s thunk. Its note names the same two spellings: call it, `listFlows()`, to run it, or write its type as the success type, `(() => void!!)!E!R`, to return it. It fires in none of the 496 acceptance cases on `main` and none of the 747 documentation blocks. Case `program-name-as-data.fail`. 2026-10-25 (real-world migration): the same holds for a returned value whose type is a union with an Effect value or a program among its members, such as a local that a conditional between an Effect value and a running call initialized: in `Effect.suspend(() => { const r = code === undefined ? Effect.void : set(code); return r })` the call runs where it stands, and with no code the thunk succeeded with `Effect.void`. EK4802 reports such a return where the success type names no value; its note says to return the program where it is chosen, as an `if` statement that returns its call does, or to write its type as the success type, and its call-it quick fix is not offered where a member of the union is no program. It fires in none of the other 497 acceptance cases and none of the 747 documentation blocks. Case `returned-union-as-data.fail`.

**D242. Three rules also apply to `.ts` files** (superseded by D266). When VibeLang serves a project's `.ts` files, it replaces the TypeScript server that would run `@effect/tsgo`, so its three correctness rules about how Effect code runs report there too: a discarded Effect (EK4801), `yield` without `*` in an Effect generator (EK4819), and `yield*` of an Effect that never succeeds without `return` (EK4820), each with a quick fix, plus the Effect 4 rename on TS2339. The other rules are about VibeLang's forms or are style; for them, serve `.ts` files with `@effect/tsgo`. Files under `node_modules` are not checked. This is the first EK code in a `.ts` file, which until now kept TypeScript's meaning exactly.

**D243. EK4813 stands in for TS2339**. An Effect 3 name of the Effect module, such as `Effect.catchAll`, is already TypeScript's TS2339. EK4813 replaces it with the Effect 4 name, from the migration table of Effect's language service, and its quick fix writes that name; a `.ts` file keeps TS2339 and gets the same quick fix. As a replaced type error, it is an error that cannot be lowered.

**D244. Defaults stricter than Effect's**. Effect's language service turns off `extendsNativeError`, `newPromise`, and `unsafeEffectTypeAssertion`; VibeLang warns (EK4805, EK4812, EK4810), because a failure in VibeLang is an `error` declaration, VibeLang code cannot wait for a promise, and a contract is only as good as the types it checks. Effect's suggestion level has no VibeLang counterpart, so its suggestions that mark likely bugs (`returnEffectInGen`, `leakingRequirements`) are warnings, and style rules are off.

**D245. Refactors are code actions of kind `refactor.rewrite`**. The language server gains a refactor seam, `ls.RegisterRefactorProvider`, beside its quick fixes, and advertises `refactor.rewrite`. Effect's 15 refactors become seven in `.vibe` files, because VibeLang has one function form and no `Effect.gen`: "Write the inferred contract" and "Remove the contract" (Effect's "Toggle return type signature" and its `annotate` codegen), "Convert to a schema declaration" (an interface or object type alias, Effect's "Structural Type to Schema" and `typeToSchema`), "Convert to a VibeLang function", with or without an `error` declaration per `await` (Effect's four async refactors; each `await p` becomes a `try` hatch that passes what `p` reads as inputs, D229), "Convert to a pipe" and "Convert the pipe to calls" (Effect's pipe and data-first refactors, and `missedPipeableOpportunity`), and the two pipe styles.

**D246. Effect's hovers and completions, VibeLang's way**. A layer's hover lists what it provides, needs, and fails with, and `wire`'s hover lists the order it builds its layers in and which layers each uses: Effect's layer hover and layer graph, as text and without its external graph link. The language server gains a completion seam, `ls.RegisterCompletionHook`, that answers before TypeScript: a string where a `Duration.Input` goes completes `"2 seconds"` and the other units, `Schema.brand("…")` completes the declared name, and an `@vibelang-diagnostics` comment completes its marker and the adjustable codes. Effect's `Self`, `Effect.gen`, `Effect.fn` span, and namespace import completions have nothing to complete in VibeLang.

**D247. Platform package scoping**. Superseded on 2026-10-18: publish VibeLang 0.0.1 with unscoped `vibelang-<os>-<cpu>` packages.

### Known gaps

These record the compiler's state when each was written, as of 2026-10-18 unless marked. Where a later decision changes a rule, the gap stands against that decision's rule, and the specification, not the compiler, defines the language (D360).

- **Integration (content mapper):** vue-tsc 3.3.13 and svelte-check 4.7.7 run on TypeScript 6.0.3, not 7.0.2. TypeScript 6 cannot resolve `.vibe` imports, so both report TS2307 on them in `examples/vue-vite` and `examples/svelte-vite`. `vibelang -p` checks those apps' `.vibe` and `.ts` files. The gap closes when the TypeScript 7 content mapper (`packages/ts-mapper`) ships and the framework checkers run on TypeScript 7.
- **Catch expressions (D236-D239):** no quick fix rewrites `try { return x } catch (e) { return y }` as `return x catch y`. The editor tests cover hover and coloring of the keyword, not completion inside a handler.
- **Catching with match (D216-D219):** EK2112 has no quick fix for a clause type that is a type alias, an object type, or a generic reference; EK2113's quick fix covers only `try { return X } catch (cause: Cause) { return Y }` without `finally`. EK2105 and EK2106 still report on the retired forms.
- **Compile and editor performance:** a file that uses any effect root name (D146) imports `effect` itself, whose index loads about 515 more declaration files; small programs spend most of their compile time parsing them (0.10 s of 0.14 s for an example, 220 MiB peak), and the language server's first diagnostics take about 2x upstream tsgo's on Effect TypeScript. Importing each used module instead would remove it; see the Performance section of `typescript/VIBELANG.md`.
- **Pattern matching (D206-D215):** no regular expression or relational patterns, `async match`, or bindings in `or` and `not`; `is` bindings stop at the then branch or loop body; array patterns match arrays only and object patterns skip primitives; an extractor with a custom matcher types its bindings loosely; an arm in expression position cannot `yield`. Hover on bindings is not covered by the editor tests.
- **Await operations (D196):** no concurrency limit (use `forEach` with `concurrency` or `for concurrent(n)`); `fork` inside an element starts a fiber and yields it, unjoined (write the call alone).
- **TC39 forms:** A throw expression in a parameter default of an Effect function does not run (TypeScript rejects `yield` in parameters); D350 makes it EK1001, which the compiler does not report yet. Hovering `throw`, `do`, or a block param's `{` shows nothing of its own. Inside a layer body, `name(args) { ... }` is a layer method, so a block param statement there must start with `const`. Under D376 a block is always the last argument; the compiler still places it by D188's type-directed rule. `.ts` files have no block params.

- **Runtime:** At extraction, Stream adaptation retained a one-element buffer instead of D26's zero-capacity handoff; `$programs` handled iterables but not records; `$trusted` matched exception names or tags. Later decisions and the runtime README take precedence over this extraction snapshot.
- **Setup and examples:** The initial end-to-end build stopped on compiler features; A later check reported `scripts/e2e.sh` passing. The prototype checks validated seven projects with the prototype. A later native-compiler check reported the native examples passing except file-tree and pipeline; after the compiler fixes, all seven native examples ran.
- **Build integration (V):** Plugin tests passed 4/4 at landing; template and example runs initially stopped on compiler errors. Later checks confirmed the template compiling, running, and passing two Vitest tests. Sources outside Vite's root remain unsupported (D107).
- **Compiler foundation (F1):** Only ES module output is supported. Incremental build metadata ignores `compilerOptions.vibelang`. Dynamic `import()` specifiers are not rewritten. F4 subsequently supplied prelude completions (D78).
- **Programs and callbacks (F2):** Declarations carry no `$plain` signature yet, which D384 requires, so a declaration consumer passes an Effect function where a source consumer passes its plain form. Context-sensitive lambda parameters use the first candidate that types them. The `Money` protocol example needs an explicit boolean return annotation.
- **Contracts and recursion (F3):** Cached types in recursive components can require an extra written contract (EK2102). Cycles through lambdas use the checker's single call type rather than the fixpoint. Region closures capture their own `arguments`. Generic-call type argument inference does not fill a lambda's `infer` channels.
- **Services and wiring (F5):** EK3201 is checked at runners, not where a contract omits a service. EK3202 omits provider file positions. Later checks supplied service-member completion (D78); D103 later covered the top-level service-call gap reported here. Expected Effect method types are read once rather than per overload candidate; lowered methods do not preserve `super` or `arguments`. The file-tree example has duplicate `effect/Effect` imports.
- **Resources and concurrency (F6):** C-style `for` heads retain TypeScript `using` behavior, without Effect failure or interruption cleanup; Effect acquisitions there report TS2850. Region closures capture their own `arguments`. Returning a block-owned fork returns an interrupted fiber with only a warning; D386 makes it the error EK4838. Plain-call Disposables register release one step late. DOG fixed duplicate prelude imports in declaration emit (D123). User Disposables need `ESNext.Disposable` in `lib`; runtime and program must load one copy of Effect.
- **Boundaries and tracing (F8):** Platform APIs reached through local aliases, parameters, or objects returned by platform calls are not followed by EK4402. Anonymous lambdas have no automatic tracing span.
- **Editor (F4):** Hover omits control classes. ES quick fixes, refactors, and catch-specific narrowing completions remain unimplemented (D33). Zed settings are untested. Headless editor checks passed on Linux with VS Code 1.98.2 and 1.105.1, not on this Mac. Serving `.ts` files requires disabling the built-in TypeScript extension or Cursor's `useTsgo` setting (D76).
- **Handlers, producers, and streams (F7, recorded from compiler notes):** A clause's return computed from a non-tail resume's result stays out of that resume's type, and EK2119 only warns when it does not fit (D97; D351 makes it an error); continuation escape is not checked. EK4201 recognizes named combinators and options, but misses a control-requiring producer stored in a variable before it is passed to a Stream function. A TypeScript fork wrapper is EK4202 only when its declared requirements name the control. Producer bodies capture their own generator's `arguments`; `for (using x of producer)` takes JavaScript's loop and cannot iterate a producer. These are compiler-note limitations, not a filed F7 gap report.
- **Dogfood (DOG):** `Array.from(producer())` gives a confusing error; use a Stream consumer. `~vibelang/service?` appears in completions. `dist/` contains compiled test files. The docs include the missing user pages for handlers, producers, and HTTP. DOG fixed duplicate Effect installations, assertion trust, dropped errors and requirements from layers that provide no services, and duplicate prelude imports in declarations.
- **Calls (D146, D147; the compiler still implements D147, D155, D262-D269 and D110, which D320-D325 and D383 replace):** At a module's top level, a service member call and a call of an Effect value, such as `Sensor.read()` or `Clock.currentTimeMillis()`, still need `() =>` where a program is expected, because the checker types them only inside function bodies; pass an Effect value itself, `Clock.currentTimeMillis`, where one exists.
- **Pipes (D154, D155):** `|>` parses only in `.vibe` files. The topic has no narrowing: `x |> (% !== undefined ? %.length : 0)` does not narrow `%`. `yield` in a body needs parentheses.
- **Methods (D152):** Method syntax applies in `.vibe` files only; a `.ts` file calls the module's function. An optional chain, `maybe?.map(f)`, has no methods, so TypeScript reports TS2339: the lowering would have to stop the whole chain at a nullish receiver, not only the call. Write `Option.map(maybe, f)` there.
- **Primitives (D176-D180):** `finally (exit)`'s success Exit carries no value. A catch clause typed `Cause` ignores its type argument: the binding is always `Cause<E>` of the try block. EK2106 after a Cause clause says the clause before it is untyped. `throw cause` in a function that compiles to plain JavaScript (D10) is not special-cased. A restore block inside a nested function counts the enclosing uninterruptible block as its region.
- **Fibers (D166-D171):** A local declaration named `fork` shadows the keyword's function, so `fork x` calls it, a defect against D328. A `select` case binds with `const` only, without a type annotation. Hover on `await`, `select`, and `for concurrent` is implemented but not exercised by the editor tests. `default:` counts a case as ready only if it finishes while the cases start; one that needs a scheduler turn is not ready.
- **Services and layers (D221-D223):** A mistyped member of a `Layer.effect` block is reported as TS2769 at the call, not at the member; the message chain names the member. EK2104's order rule applies only to the old `layer` spelling; in the block, setup and members are ordinary statements and an object literal.
- **Effect's language service (D240-D244):** `strictEffectProvide` (EK4816) exempts any function named `main`, not only an entry module's. EK4808 sees nested `provide` blocks, not chained `Effect.provide` calls in a pipe. EK4811 checks throws and running calls, not every Effect type. `RpcClient` go to definition is not implemented: the language server has no definition hook. Refactors, hovers, and completions apply in `.vibe` files only. The async refactor is not offered for a function with `for await` or an `await` inside an awaited expression.
- **Join (D225-D228):** `join [a, b]` needs a space before `[`, since `join[i]` stays an element access. `join` is the keyword in `join x` even where a local named `join` exists; call such a function with `join(x)`. As with `fork`, the keyword form is not recognized directly after a unary operator such as `!`; parenthesize: `!(join x)`. Hover on `join` is implemented but not exercised by the editor tests.
- **Self-contained hatches (D229-D232):** `callback`'s type arguments cannot be written alongside inputs; annotate `resume` with `Resume<A, E>`. The runtime's `try` reads any function in second place as the mapper (D231). Called as a value or from TypeScript, `sync` and `try` pass the AbortSignal only to an `async` function that declares a parameter for it (D232); seeing no types, they also pass it to an optional parameter typed for something else, `b?: number`, which a direct call leaves `undefined`. A hatch's function may still read an enclosing function's `this` or `arguments`. D330 and D327 make each of these a compile error; the compiler does not report them yet.

## The (B) projector keeps D24 (2026-10-18)

**D248. Where L3 and D24 disagree, the switch keeps D24 or asks** (D250 withdrawn by D251; D366 states the rule every renderer meets; amended by D416). The text-only projector decides at run time whether a call's result runs (L3, `$isRun`), and to it an `Exit` is a program. D24 decided from types that a call returning an `Exit`, a `Config`, or an error instance returns data, and D43 relies on it: `resume(Exit.succeed(value))` passes the `Exit` as data. Wherever the fork's types say data and L3 would run, the switch keeps D24's meaning or reports EK9100, which asks for an annotation. It never changes what code does without a diagnostic. So `sync(() => Exit.succeed(42))` written without a return type reports EK9100 under the switch, and `sync((): Exit.Exit<number, never> => Exit.succeed(42))` is plain (L2) and returns the `Exit` as data under both compilers. Evidence: the case `vibelang-effect-value-calls`, migrated with that annotation, and a regression case that keeps the unannotated form and expects EK9100.

**D249. A trusted contract becomes a value, `trusted(impl, [Errors])`** (amends D23; completed by D389; amended by D400 and D514). The (B) projector reads one file at a time, so it cannot see a JSDoc tag on another file's declaration, and D23's `@vibelang trusted` and `@throws` cannot reach its emit. The TypeScript file states the contract as a value instead: `export const parsePortTrusted = trusted(parsePort, [PortError])`, a public `vibelang-runtime` export that takes the implementation's parameters and returns `Effect<A, E>`, where a listed class is an expected failure and any other exception is a defect, as `$trusted` does today.
