The contract stays beside the function it describes, which is why D23 put it there, and TypeScript callers keep calling `parsePort`. Under D248, a dropped tag must never change behavior silently. Until the switch, the fork's checker warns at every `.vibe` call of a D23-tagged declaration whose lowering uses the tag (in an Effect function, outside boundary callbacks), with a quick fix that adds `<name>Trusted` beside the declaration and rewrites the call. A call at top level or in a plain or boundary callback is plain under both compilers, so it gets no warning. From the switch on, such a call is an error, because a `PortError` would otherwise become a defect without a diagnostic. Rejected: passing tag contracts into Project's FileContext (cross-file input to the per-file mapper, which breaks stock TypeScript tooling, the reason for (B)), and `Effect.try` at each call site (moves the contract away from the function). The cases `trust-levels` and `ch10-typescript` migrate to `trusted(...)` values, so they keep proving typed failures from TypeScript.

**D250. L3 treats an `Exit` as data** (withdrawn by D251) (amends D248). The run check `$isRun` excludes an `Exit` by its type ID, as it already excludes a `Config`, a Context key, and an error, and `$run`'s types keep `Exit<A, E>` as a value. So D24 holds in both renderings by construction: a call that returns an `Exit` returns it as data, D43's `resume(Exit.succeed(value))` passes it as data, and calling an `Exit` value still runs it. This also covers an `Exit` that only the checker knows, such as one returned by a helper imported from a `.ts` file, which a text-level diagnostic could not see. It needs no annotation and no EK9100 for `Exit`, so D248's example `sync(() => Exit.succeed(42))` is correct code under both compilers, and `vibelang-effect-value-calls` passes unmigrated. D248's rule stands for every other place where L3 and the fork could disagree. Rejected: EK9100 where the text proves an `Exit` (it misses checker-only `Exit`s, so it would violate D248), and a fork-only checker diagnostic (it does not cover stock TypeScript tooling).

**D251. At run time an `Exit` runs; the checking rendering rejects running a D24 data type** (narrowed by D254). D250 was unsound. In Effect 4.0.5, `Effect.succeed` is `exitSucceed` (`effect/src/internal/effect.ts:957`), so an ordinary succeed or fail program is an `Exit` object at run time, and excluding `Exit` from `$isRun` turned those programs into data: `lambda-forms` and `ref1-existing-service` regressed, and two helpers that return the same `Exit`, one declared `Exit<number>` and one `Effect<number>`, could not be told apart. Only the static type separates data from a program, and the executable rendering has no types. So L3 runs an `Exit` at run time, as before. The checking rendering, which TypeScript type-checks for the fork and for stock tooling alike, rejects a run position whose static type is a D24 data type, such as `Exit`, with a type error that names the annotation. `$run`'s first overload takes `T & DataEffect`, which keeps `generic<T>(x: T)` inferred as `Effect<T>`. A helper declared `Effect` runs the same way in both renderings, and a value declared `Exit` can never run silently, which is D248's rule. Rejected: keeping D250 (silent wrong results for every opaque helper that returns `succeed` or `fail`), and carrying static types into the emit (not available to a projector that reads text only).

**D252. The (B) projector keeps D152's data methods by dispatching unproven receivers at run time** (amended by D253) (implements D152 under (B)). A projector that reads one file at a time cannot always prove from text which of D152's modules declares a receiver's type: the census of the docs found 131 such method calls. They type-check through the prelude's module augmentation in the checking rendering, as the fork's D152 does. The executable rendering emits the module's function where the text proves the module, and `$method(x, "isSome", args)` elsewhere. `$method` dispatches on the receiver's TypeId to exactly one of D152's modules. As D152 requires, a value's own member of that name wins, and arrays, strings, plain objects and any value without one of those TypeIds keep JavaScript's method call, so `$method` never calls a module function on a value D152 does not cover. Unproven calls lose tree-shaking for their module function; a proven call keeps it, and the docs and examples prefer forms the text proves. Rejected: errors at unproven receivers, and passing receiver modules from the fork into Project (it breaks the content mapper's per-file model for stock tooling).

**D253. A data method whose module the run time cannot tell apart asks for an annotation** (amends D252). In Effect 4.0.5 an `Exit` carries both the Exit and the Effect TypeIds: `makeExit` builds its prototype from `ExitTypeId` and the primitive prototype, which spreads `EffectProto` (`effect/src/internal/core.ts:100-101, 531-548`). `Effect.succeed` and its siblings are Exits, and Cause errors such as `NoSuchElementError` carry both the Effect and Cause TypeIds. So `$method` dispatches an unproven receiver only when exactly one module among the call's candidates matches a TypeId the value carries. For a method name that two such modules both define (Effect and Exit: `asVoid`, `isFailure`, `isSuccess`, `map`, `mapBoth`, `mapError`, `match`; Effect and the Cause errors), an unproven receiver is a projector diagnostic that asks for an annotation or for `Module.fn(x)`, never a run-time guess (D248). Likewise an optional or uncalled access to a data method on an unproven receiver is a diagnostic, matching the fork's TS2339 and EK4501. A value's own member wins only when it is callable, and candidates are only exported module functions.

**D254. The checking rendering rejects running only an `Exit`**. The run-time check `$isRun` already tells a `Config` (its `~effect/Config` key), a Context key (`~effect/Context/Service`), and an error (an `Error` instance, or a YieldableError by its Effect identifier across realms) from a program, and keeps each as data (`effect/src/Config.ts`, `Context.ts`, `internal/core.ts`; verified in the fast-call review). So `$run`'s types keep those values as data too, matching what runs, and D251's type error stays only for `Exit`, the one D24 type the run time cannot separate from a program, because an `Exit` carries Effect's TypeId. Types and run time agree on every other D24 type, so nothing changes silently (D248), and code such as `function withFallback(name: string): Config.Config<string> { return Config.orElse(…) }` followed by `withFallback("MISSING")()` needs no annotation or wrapper. Rejected: keeping the guard on Config, keys, and errors, which makes users wrap every such value reached through a call although the run time already handles it.

## The name VibeLang (2026-10-18)

**D255. The language is VibeLang** (supersedes D125). The language was renamed before the 0.0.1 launch. The packages are `vibelang` (the compiler and its `vibelang` binary), `vibelang-runtime`, `vibelang-vite`, `vibelang-unplugin`, `create-vibelang`, and one `vibelang-<os>-<cpu>` package per platform binary. Source files end in `.vibe`, the editor language id and the Neovim filetype are `vibe`, the configuration key is `compilerOptions.vibelang`, the trust tag is `@vibelang trusted`, and the editor extension is `vibelang.vibelang`. Diagnostic codes keep the prefix `EK`. The issue tracker `effeck/effeck` on smithers.sh keeps its name until it moves. The single documentation site is vibelang.sh; retired domains redirect there. `node scripts/rename-to-vibelang.mjs` applies the rename to any tree, and `scripts/brand-gate.mjs` fails on a retired spelling.

## Launch audit corrections (2026-10-18)

**D256. D15 binds VibeLang's own cleanup; Effect operators keep Effect's semantics** (D22 governs; amended by D355). VibeLang never drops a failure from cleanup it runs itself: `using`, `finally`, scope closing, and `abandon` under interruption. This does not extend to failures an Effect operator discards by design, including `timeout`/`race` dropping a loser's cleanup failure. In Effect 4.0.5, `timeout` calls `timeoutOrElse`, which calls `timeoutOption`, which races the source against the timer with `raceFirst` (`effect/src/internal/effect.ts:3870, 3836, 3895`). `raceAllFirst` resolves on the first fiber's exit and interrupts losers on the parent's exit through `onExitUnsafe` and `fiberInterruptAll`; their exits do not join the parent's cause (`effect/src/internal/effect.ts:1668-1700`). Keep this limitation visible and pin the observed behavior with a runtime case so an Effect upgrade that changes it shows up. Report the lost finalizer failure upstream; do not fork `timeout`.

**D257. Overload ranking applies D147 per candidate and prefers passing a program by name** (superseded by D322). Among applicable overloads, a candidate whose parameter expects an `Effect` and passes a program argument by name outranks one that would run that same argument. A running call whose result type is `never` makes no parameter applicable during ranking: it means the call fails, not that every parameter accepts it by vacuity. Thus `Effect.race(Effect.fail(a), Effect.fail(b))` selects the two-program form, passing both programs by name, instead of running the second argument as the curried form's `options`. The same rule covers `raceFirst`, `zip`, and `orElse`; a genuinely data-last call such as `pipe(x, Effect.race(y))` keeps the curried form.

**D258. `await.allSettled` never fails with a typed error; defects and interruption propagate** (amends D196). It captures every typed failure, E, as a `Result.fail`, and propagates defects and interruption exactly as `Effect.all(…, { mode: "result" })` does. A defect is a bug, and interruption is the caller's cancellation, rather than an outcome to settle. In Effect 4.0.5, `Effect.result` calls `matchEager` with `onFailure: Result.fail`; `findError(cause)` leaves a cause with no typed failure, including defect-only and interruption-only causes, unchanged (`effect/src/internal/effect.ts:3534, 3718-3745`). `all` applies `result(effect)` in mode `"result"` (`effect/src/internal/effect.ts:4697`). Keep the per-element `Result` type; an `Exit` per element would expose Cause to every caller. Pin defect and interruption propagation with runtime cases.

**D259. The language server treats `null` as absent for optional client fields**. In client-to-server messages, the VibeLang language server reads `null` as absent wherever LSP 3.17 permits the client to omit the field. It never sends `null` for an optional field itself, and required fields stay strict. This tolerates Zed 0.195.3's actual payload so VibeLang works by default in that editor. Implement the tolerance in the fork's LSP decoder, retain the payload as a fixture, and report the spec violation upstream to Zed so the tolerance can eventually be retired.

**D260. Plain contracts keep JavaScript try/catch** (amends D37 and D54; superseded by D282 and, for `finally`, by D340 and D333). A function with a plain contract keeps JavaScript's `try`, `catch`, `finally`, and `throw`: its catch receives every exception its try block throws, as `unknown`, exactly as TypeScript does. The lowering inserts no `$caught` rethrow and no expected-failure wrapper there. Defects are an Effect-level notion: an exception becomes a defect only where it escapes a plain function into an effectful caller, unchanged. An Effect region still catches expected failures only and lets defects and interruption pass through (D13). EK2004 applies to a try statement only when its block runs Effects and E is `never`; its message says defects pass through the catch, rather than claiming the catch can never run. compiler regressions pin coercion through a throwing `Symbol.toPrimitive`, a throwing getter, a revoked Proxy, and a throwing prototype lookup; an Effect try with E = `never` still warns and propagates a defect at runtime. Amended 2026-10-23 (release correction): D282 supersedes this decision's JavaScript `throw` and `catch`. In a plain function `throw` is a typed failure, one that can escape it is EK2003, and its catch receives typed failures only; EK2004's text stands.

## Type checking and lowering corrections (2026-10-18)

**D261. Projects may trust TypeScript packages by name** (extends D22). `compilerOptions.vibelang.trustedPackages` lists package-name globs, for example `["@smthrs/*"]`, matched against the nearest `package.json` name, including workspace packages outside `node_modules`. Globs match the whole name: `*`, `?`, and character classes match within a slash-delimited segment. A matching package receives D22's terms: an export that does not return an Effect is a synchronous call whose exceptions are defects, its Effects run where called, and calls into it do not report EK4401. The option does not list expected failures: D249's `trusted(impl, [Errors])` remains their per-function contract. Without a matching option, foreign calls keep EK4401.

**D262. An explicitly Effect-typed top-level const holds a program** (extends D147 and D103; superseded by D322 and D323). At a module's top level, a `const` whose declared type is an Effect is a program position: `export const migrate: Effect.Effect<void, E, R> = Effect.suspend(() => …)` builds the cold value and runs nothing when imported. Running `migrate` later executes its body. Calls retain their Effect value at the top level; operands still follow D147's parameter positions. The annotation must be on the const declaration, rather than inferred or asserted on its initializer. Without it, the same top-level Effect call still reports EK1001 (D103); mutable bindings and non-Effect annotations do not opt in.

**D263. Plain contracts keep JavaScript throws and calls** (completes and amends D260). A function with an explicit non-Effect return annotation, or a plain contract inferred by the checker, is plain JavaScript. Every `throw` in that function, inside or outside `try`, is JavaScript's throw: it does not run an Effect, infer an error channel, or cause EK2003. Its declaration keeps its plain return type, and its emitted function returns a value or throws, never an Effect object. Calls whose types return non-Effect values, including plain-function parameters and trusted or untrusted TypeScript imports, are JavaScript calls there: no EK4401 and no Effect inference. Exceptions propagate unchanged and become defects only where they escape into an effectful caller. Running an Effect under a plain contract remains EK2003. compiler regressions A-1, B-4, and E-1 pin synchronous validation and callback APIs.

**D264. Shared web-standard globals keep their names** (amends D146). Web-standard globals defined by every runtime VibeLang targets win over effect-root names, as JavaScript's built-ins do: `Request`, `Response`, `Headers`, `URL`, `URLSearchParams`, `TextEncoder`, `TextDecoder`, `AbortController`, `AbortSignal`, `Blob`, `FormData`, `structuredClone`, and `fetch`. Of effect@4.0.5's root names, only `Request` collides today: `new Request("https://x.test")` constructs the web Request, and Effect's module requires `import { Request } from "effect"`. DOM-only globals such as `Option` still lose to Effect's modules. Explicit imports and local declarations keep precedence.

**D265. Contextually Effect-typed object fields hold programs** (implements D147, no new rule; superseded by D322 and D323). D147 already names the fields of a record among the places where an expected `Effect` takes a program by name (spec 213), as D262 is D147's top-level case. The compiler now applies it to every object-literal field whose contextual type is an Effect: a service implementation's member, a parameter's record field, or a field of a declared type. `Layer.sync(Journal, () => ({ read: Effect.sync(() => …) }))`, `make({ read: work() })`, and `const journal: { read: Effect.Effect<number, never, never> } = { read: work() }` build values and run none of the stored programs. Running the field later executes the program, on every invocation. A field whose contextual type is not an Effect keeps the ordinary rule: a program call runs where it stands. Operands retain their ordinary program positions, as in D262.

**D266. EK diagnostics belong to VibeLang files** (supersedes D242). A `.ts`, `.tsx`, `.mts`, or `.cts` file keeps TypeScript's verdict: no VibeLang-specific EK diagnostic attaches there, regardless of project diagnostic options or inline directives. The shared EK diagnostic constructor enforces the source-file boundary for every registered code. This removes EK4801, EK4819, and EK4820 from ordinary TypeScript; other EK rules already enter through VibeLang-only checks, including EK4813's replacement of TS2339. Ordinary TS errors remain. Editor hints may be offered separately. The Ownership.ts terminal `yield* logWarning.pipe(annotateLogs, andThen(onLapse), andThen(Effect.interrupt))` is valid TypeScript; the identical generator in `.vibe` still receives EK1002 because `function*` there declares a producer.

**D267. A tagged template runs like a call of its tag** (extends D24 and D147; amended by D322). A tagged-template expression whose return type is Effect itself runs where a call would run, in a statement or an expression, and evaluates to its success value. Its failures and requirements join the enclosing program's channels. A program position passes it by name under D147; hatches keep TypeScript, and positions that cannot run follow the call diagnostics, including EK1001 at module top level and EK4301 in a synchronous callback. Plain tags and D24 data results keep their JavaScript meaning. A discarded structured Effect result (such as SQL Statement) reports EK4301 and asks for an explicit call of the result or an Effect return annotation; an Effectful template in an unlowerable position also reports EK4301. The projector also runs an explicit `Effect.service(Key)` lookup: Effect returns the key itself at run time, which this call runs rather than keeping it as D24 data. Both compiler renderings preserve native tag invocation, its receiver, template object, and substitution order. Never emit an Effect-valued template cold in a position where a call would run. Evidence: `tagged-template-runs`, including the fake SqlClient migration shape that left Smithers' tables uncreated.

**D268. Purity crosses files with the function** (the one plain-call ABI, D366; amended by D384: the declaration carries the `$plain` signature; amended by D411 and D421; amended by D442, then D446; amended by D483; amended by D500). A provably pure export carries a runtime marker, `f[$direct] = f$direct`, in both the native compiler and (B), including local named exports and aliases. Re-exports and import-then-export preserve the same function object. The checking projection merges a phantom namespace containing `$plain: $Plain<typeof f>` into the exported function; its plain call signature survives aliases and barrels. Parity strips only this generated namespace, retaining the public Effect signature. In plain callback positions an importer passes `$adapt(f)`: its branded overload selects the brand's plain signature, and its unbranded overload is `<F>(f: F) => F`, preserving generics without a conditional inference boundary. Known unbranded Effect imports stay in their original contextual positions. At runtime it is `f[$direct] ?? f`; correctness never depends on types. Without a marker the function remains an Effect function. Evidence cases: `plain-export-lists` maps to `[2, 4]`, `plain-imports` maps to `[3, 6]`, and an impure import running `Effect.log` retains its Effect behavior.

**D269. A function declared to return an Effect may return a program; a typed `const` holds one at any scope** (extends D7, D262, and D265; superseded by D323). In a function whose declared return type is `Effect<A, E, R>` (its annotation, its binding's annotation, or for a lambda the declared member or parameter type it is checked against, D44), a `return` whose expression is a program that TypeScript would reject as an `A` returns that program unrun. A program is a function literal with no parameters, converted as D7 converts it, or a non-call expression whose type is `Effect` itself (D24). Such a function is a factory: its body runs when it is called, as plain JavaScript under D263, and running its result runs the program, every time. Every path of a factory returns a program (EK2116); its body runs no Effect and looks up no service (EK2115); it has no plain variant, so D9 rejects it where a plain function is expected (EK4301). A `const` whose declared type is `Effect` holds its initializer in any scope, as D262 holds it at the top level and D265 in a declared field: `const built: Made = make(config)` calls `make` now and runs nothing. VibeLang callers do not change: a call to a factory runs it (D24), is passed by name where a program is expected (D147), and is held where a declared Effect type holds it. Only TypeScript callers and held programs see the two phases, and the phases are TypeScript's. Code that compiles today keeps its meaning: each new form is TS2322 today, and where `A` is a type parameter that the call infers, as in `Effect.suspend(() => () => 1)`, the returned function stays the value. Rejected: inferring the phases from a plain prefix (it silently moves work in existing exports to call time and recursion onto the JavaScript stack), a `hold(…)` marker (the quotation operator Chapter 5 rules out), and a recipe alone (it unblocks 1 of 29 files). Evidence: `smithers-lanes/a4-probes-opus/`. 2026-10-23 (real-world migration): the initializer is held through parentheses, a type assertion, a `satisfies` clause and a non-null assertion, which leave its value as it is, so `const p: number!! = make() as any` calls `make` and runs nothing; before, such a wrapper ran the call. Evidence: `held-assertion-initializer`. 2026-10-23 (real-world migration): a conditional initializer is held too, `const p: number!! = ready ? make() : Effect.succeed(0)`: its value is one of its branches, so neither branch runs at the const, only the condition is evaluated there, and the chosen branch runs each time the const is run. Before, the branch ran at the const, and its value did not fit the declared type (TS2322). Evidence: `held-conditional-initializer`. 2026-10-23 (real-world migration): a pipe initializer is held too, `const p: number!! = make() |> Effect.map(%, f)`: the pipe's value is its body's, so the body builds the program and the left side keeps its parameter position (D154); at a module's top level a left side whose topic lands where a program is expected is not EK1001, as a call argument there is not. Before, the body ran at the const, and at the top level both calls drew EK1001. Evidence: `held-pipe-initializer`, `held-pipe-initializer-returned`, `held-pipe-initializer.fail`. 2026-10-24 (real-world migration): an unannotated factory lambda whose returns TypeScript types as an Effect, as `() => Effect.void` is, keeps TypeScript's type where the declared program type names the candidate's type parameters, as `R2` in `(a: A) => void!!R2`: it took the declared type, so inference read `R2` from `R2` and the call's type and declaration named an unbound `R2`. The same holds for such a lambda in a branch of a conditional argument, `run(watching ? observe : () => Effect.void)`, which takes the argument's contextual type (2026-10-24, real-world migration). Case `factory-lambda-infers-channels`.

**D270. A union consisting entirely of Effects runs where it stands** (clarifies D24 and D147; kept by D321). A call whose type is a union in which every member is Effect<A, E, R> itself counts as an Effect for D24 and D147. It runs where it stands; its success is the union of the members' successes, its error channel the union of their errors, its requirements the union of theirs. A union that mixes an Effect with a non-Effect, or with a structured Effect subtype, is not an Effect and stays data. The checker's call types, the execution plan, and the lowering share one predicate. Evidence: `union-effect-calls` and `union-effect-channels.fail`.

**D271. An explicit JavaScript iterator contract keeps a JavaScript generator** (extends D263 to generators and amends D26; superseded by D370: every `function*` is a producer). A generator function (`function*`, `async function*`) whose explicit return annotation is a JavaScript iterator type (`Generator`, `Iterator`, `IterableIterator`, `Iterable`, `AsyncGenerator`, `AsyncIterator`, `AsyncIterableIterator`, `AsyncIterable`) is plain JavaScript: it compiles to the same generator TypeScript would, and its `yield` is JavaScript's. A generator function with no annotation, or one annotated as a `Stream`, keeps VibeLang's stream-producer form. JavaScript iterator contracts preserve `.next()`, spread, `yield*` delegation and its return value, throws and catches, and async iteration with `for await`. EK1002 still rejects an unannotated producer passed to a JavaScript generator API; an explicitly iterator-annotated generator may be passed to that API. Evidence: Smithers mcp McpClient cooperative coroutines silently returned Streams despite their `Generator` types, so `.next()` died at runtime; cases `plain-generator`, `plain-async-generator`, `generator-stream`, and `plain-generator-yield.fail` pin the ruling and TypeScript's invalid-yield diagnostic.

**D272. A curried topic-only pipe stage keeps method-pipe inference** (extends D154; amended by D325; amended by D418). A stage `g(%)` whose callee `g` is itself a call expression, as in `x |> f(args)(%)`, types and lowers exactly as `x.pipe(f(args))`. The stage receives the method pipe's contextual type, preserving data-last Schema combinators whose outer call infers the input schema from that context. The parser normalizes this form before checking and lowering; both compiler renderings emit the method call. Other topic forms retain D154/D155, including extra arguments, member reads on the topic, and additional topic references inside the stage factory. The left side must call nothing (no call, tagged template, or `new` outside nested functions), and every earlier stage of the chain must be such a stage: a call there may run a program (D24), and `.pipe` would then reach its result instead of the program, so `load() |> Effect.map(f)(%)` keeps D154 and passes `load()` by name. A function literal on the left, `(() => { ... }) |> f(args)(%)`, is a program passed by name as well, since a function has no pipe method (real-world migration, 2026-10-24). Bind a schema built by a call first: `const s = Schema.NullOr(Schema.String)`, then `s |> f(args)(%)`.

**D273. Block-param calls accept ordinary postfix operations** (clarifies D188). A block-param call is a primary expression like any call: member access, element access, and another call may follow its closing brace. Thus `Layer.unwrap { return Layer.empty }.pipe(Layer.fresh)` parses like the corresponding parenthesized program argument. Parsing resumes the ordinary call/member loop after the block; D188's existing argument placement and block boundaries stay in force. Invalid members, indices, and calls on the result keep TypeScript's diagnostics.

**D274. A single-result Effect body returns its Effect without a generator** (amends D36 and D104; amended by D326, D327, D341, and D372). When an Effect function's lowered body is empty or one return, and every call in it that runs is in a result position (through conditionals, commas, and parentheses), the function returns that Effect: `Effect.suspend(thunk)` when a result runs a program, `Effect.sync(thunk)` when every result is a value, or `Effect.void` for an empty body. A value result beside a program result becomes `Effect.succeed(value)`, as for by-name results (D147). The body still runs each time the Effect runs, a throw in it is still a defect, and nothing in the thunk can capture or park, so the frame invariant holds. The thunk is an arrow, so `this`, `arguments`, and `super` are the function's, and it is the property of an object literal named after the source, so a stack frame reads `PrimitiveImpl.<name>` at the `.vibe` line (Effect calls the thunk as a method). Functions with a default or destructured parameter, async and generator functions, traced functions, and handler clauses keep the generator. Evidence: `Effect.fnUntraced` costs every call two run-loop operations more than the TypeScript a twin replaces (suspend, iterator, final success), and Effect yields every 2048 operations, so in the agent/harness twin the supervisor fiber ran inside CellTurn's last frame and judged it (4 failures, A-12's extra evaluator request). With D274 the twin fails exactly the tests TypeScript fails at budgets 1024 (11), 1792 (4), and 2048 (none). Cases `debug-stack` (the thunk's frame), `vibelang-lower`, `vibelang-infer`, and `vibelang-concise-comments` pin the emitted forms.

**D275. VibeLang settings may sit at the tsconfig's top level** (amends D34). A `vibelang` object at the top level of `tsconfig.json` holds the same settings as `compilerOptions.vibelang`, with the same validation, and a config that `extends` another inherits them as it inherits compiler options. TypeScript 5.x, typescript-eslint, and editors' TypeScript servers reject `compilerOptions.vibelang` as an unknown compiler option (TS5023), so a VibeLang project's tsconfig broke every stock tool that reads it; they ignore an unknown top-level key. The docs show the top-level form; `compilerOptions.vibelang` stays accepted for existing projects. Evidence: `@smthrs/fs`'s ESLint target failed with TS5023 until its settings moved to the top level.

**D276. dprint formats .vibe files through a process plugin that writes them as TypeScript** (amended by D372: the formatter never reorders import declarations). The `vibelang` package ships a dprint process plugin, `npm:vibelang/dprint-plugin.json`, which is the native compiler started with `--parent-pid`. For each `.vibe` file it parses the file, writes each VibeLang form as TypeScript of the same shape, asks dprint to format that with the configuration's own TypeScript plugin and `typescript` settings (a host format request), and writes the result back: `A!E!R`, `mutable x`, patterns, and headers such as `provide X` become identifiers of the same width; a block param `f(args) { }` becomes `f(args)(($v) => { })`, which dprint lays out as Effect's `Effect.fn(name)(body)`; `match` becomes an object literal whose properties are the arms, so `catch (e) => match (e) {` stays on one line; `x catch y` becomes `x.$v(y)`, so a line break before `catch` stays; `|>` becomes `||`; `fork`, `join`, `throw`, and await operations become `void`; `is` becomes `in`; `provide` and mask blocks become labeled blocks; `error` and `schema` become type aliases and `service` and `control` classes; extra try clauses become labeled blocks glued back after formatting. A module declaration (D283) keeps its head, which TypeScript reads as a namespace, and its body first formats as a `.ts` file of its own, since dprint does not sort a namespace's imports, before the file's pass indents it inside the braces; an identifier specifier, `from text`, becomes the string `"./$vtext"`, which sorts with relative imports. The parser records each form's tokens for this (`parser.ParseVibeLangSyntax`), since the tree holds only the TypeScript a form abbreviates. A file whose result would not parse to the same tree, each module body's tree included and parentheses, the braces around an if or loop body of one statement, and the order of imports and re-exports aside, fails instead of changing; so does a file with a syntax error or a comment inside a form's own tokens. The superseded `layer` declaration and the retired `select` stay as written. Rejected: a wasm plugin (dprint's wasm ABI and the size of the Go compiler), dprint-plugin-exec with a nested dprint run per file (a second configuration and a process per file), the language server's formatter (TypeScript's formatter, not dprint's style), and a new formatter (it would not follow the repository's dprint settings). Not controversial.

2026-10-21 crash fix: language-server document formatting reuses D276's reversible TypeScript projection, with the editor's TypeScript formatting settings. It no longer walks the synthesized checker tree, whose match-arm token ranges overlap. dprint remains the formatter that follows the project's dprint configuration.

**D277. ESLint lints `.vibe` files through the compiler's ESTree** (amends D35). `vibelang/eslint`, an ESLint parser in the `vibelang` package, runs `vibelang --estree`, which converts the VibeLang parser's tree to ESTree (`internal/vibelangestree`). TypeScript forms convert as `@typescript-eslint/typescript-estree` 8 converts them, so typescript-eslint's rules and the plugins that read TypeScript apply unchanged; the test holds the two to the same output node for node on every `.ts` file in this repository, and a run over Smithers' 8,524 `.ts` files found one difference, now fixed. The forms whose TypeScript long form would mislead a rule take node types of their own: `VibeEffectType` for `A!E!R`, `VibeInferType` for D57's `infer` outside a conditional type, `VibePipeExpression` and `TopicReference` for `\|>` and `%`, `VibeCatchExpression`, `VibeProvideStatement` and `VibeServiceProvider`, and `VibeMaskStatement`. A pipe is not a `BinaryExpression`, as Babel has it, because a pipe statement runs its body and `no-unused-expressions` would report it. Every other form keeps the TypeScript the checker reads, so a block param starts its own code path and an `error` is a class. A module declaration (D283) is `VibeModuleDeclaration`, its `id` and a `TSModuleBlock` whose statements convert from the body's own source file as a `.ts` file's do (the differential test holds a body to typescript-estree too), and scope analysis gives the body a module scope of its own beside the file's, so a name both declare is no redeclaration and a file name the body reads is undefined. An identifier specifier, `from text`, is an `Identifier` whose `value` is its name, never the `./main.vibe.text.js` the compiler resolves. Names the parser writes for the checker are marked `synthesized` and kept out of scope analysis; a typed catch clause's `$caught`, a handle clause's `$performed` chain, a match statement's label, and a schema's interface are left out. Ranges are UTF-16 offsets into the `.vibe` text, and the tokens and comments are one scan of the source, so reports, fixes, and `eslint-disable` comments land on what the author wrote. Rules that need type information do not run on `.vibe` files, as D35 has it: no TypeScript program loads one. Rejected: linting the TypeScript 7 projection (`packages/ts-mapper`), whose lowering (`$gen`, `$run`, `$use`) changes the shapes rules match and has no source position for most of what it writes; rebuilding TypeScript 5 nodes for typescript-estree's own converter, which walks token children that synthesized nodes do not have; and switching Smithers to Oxlint, whose built-in TypeScript parser rejects VibeLang syntax and which accepts no custom parser (D35 assumed VibeLang added no syntax). ESLint's custom parser interface is the one vue-eslint-parser and svelte-eslint-parser use. Evidence: `@smthrs/fs`'s lint target checks its `.vibe` sources with the repository's rules and fails on a seeded violation; `packages/compiler/test/eslint`.

**D278. Declarations that stand for classes are classes** (amends D126 and D128; amended by D345; amended by D407). `error`, `service` and `control` declare named TypeScript classes. The keyword supplies only the base class and what VibeLang derives from the name; everything else a class declaration accepts keeps its TypeScript meaning, and the transform lowers the declaration to that class. For `error` this includes `abstract`, `extends` another error, `implements`, constructors with `super`, methods, accessors, `override`, static members and private members, so `SmithersError` with its validating constructor and `toJSON` is an `error` declaration. Two rules are VibeLang's own. Every `error` class, a subclass included, is tagged with its own name: `catchTag("IntegrationError")` matches the subclass, and `is SmithersError` and `match` match the whole family through `instanceof`. A tag is the bare class name, never qualified by package and module as a service key is (D93), because `catchTag("NotFound")` is how Effect code names a failure; two packages that declare `error NotFound` share the tag and are told apart by class. Behaviour that is not idiomatic Effect is dropped rather than given syntax: a base error renamed at construction (`new SmithersError(…, { name: "AdapterError" })`) becomes a subclass. Services and controls keep D128's EK2103 for a member with a body, because an Effect service implementation is a plain object that would not inherit it. Class-based declarations preserve ordinary TypeScript class behavior through their generated classes. The goal is to retain idiomatic Effect behavior. Evidence: today's parser rejects `extends`, `implements`, constructors, methods and static members in an `error` (TS1128, TS1005, TS1434), which kept `@smthrs/errors`' `SmithersError` a 219-line `Data.Error` class.

**D279. `schema error` declares an error that serializes** (extends D126, D133 and D278). `schema error FsError { code: Code; method: string; description: string; path?: string }` is short for `class FsError extends Schema.TaggedError<FsError>()("FsError", { code: Code, method: Schema.String, description: Schema.String, path: Schema.optional(Schema.String) })`. Fields map to schemas as a `schema` declaration's do (D133), and D278's class rules apply: `extends`, constructors, methods, and a tag of its own per class. A field whose type has no schema, such as a function, is a compile error that names the field. The tag is the bare class name (D278). Where code has already persisted an error under a qualified tag such as `"flows/fs/FsError"`, the package that reads those records keeps them readable by also accepting the old tag when decoding; that is data compatibility in the package, not syntax. Evidence: Smithers declares `Schema.TaggedError` in 327 files and `Data.TaggedError` in 147, and 228 of the Schema tags are qualified, so without this form most of its errors would stay Effect's TypeScript class form in `.vibe`.

**D280. `schema Name = <type>` declares a schema of any mapped type** (extends D133). The alias form writes the schema of a type with D133's mapping, unchanged: `schema Code = "root_missing" | "read_failed"` is short for `const Code = Schema.Literals(["root_missing", "read_failed"])` and `type Code = typeof Code.Type`, and the same holds for a union of other forms (`Schema.Union`), arrays, tuples, `Record<K, V>`, object types and named schemas. One name is both the schema and its type, as with the struct form. Refinements and transformations stay `Schema` constants, as D133 has it, and a type outside the mapping is EK2109. Evidence: Smithers writes `Schema.Literal` or `Schema.Literals` 1,666 times, most beside a hand-written type of the same union.

**D281. `service Name: Type` declares a service of any shape** (extends D128, D223 and D278; amended by D415; amended by D501). `service Search: SearchApi` is short for `class Search extends Context.Service<Search, SearchApi>()(key) {}`, with the key derived as for every service (D93). `Type` is any type: an interface declared elsewhere, a primitive (`service Prefix: string`), a function type. The body is optional and holds only static members, such as a layer; an instance member there is EK2103, as D278 keeps for services. Reading is unchanged: `Prefix()` returns the implementation (D90) and `Search.find(q)` calls a member (D41, D90). Evidence: Smithers declares 103 services as `Context.Service<X, Service>` with the interface beside them, 8 with a plain `string` shape, and about 60 with other named shapes; the fs rewrite had to wrap a string service in an object member, which changed every caller.

2026-10-21 crash fix: a shape cannot refer to its declared service key, directly or through a named shape (EK2103). This includes `service X: X`, `typeof X`, `ReturnType<typeof X>`, and `keyof typeof X`; name the implementation's type instead. The check resolves names before constructing the base type, so locally shadowed names and property names remain valid.

**D282. `throw` always fails; plain functions never fail and need nothing** (supersedes D263, amends D260 and D67; amended by D328 and D329; amended by D399; amended by D455). In a `.vibe` file, `throw` is a typed failure everywhere outside a hatch, and `try`/`catch` keep VibeLang's meaning. Only a hatch (`sync`, `try`, `callback`), whose function is plain TypeScript, has JavaScript's `throw` and `catch`. A function with a plain return annotation (D67) is plain JavaScript because it never fails and never needs a service: a `throw` in it is a compile error, as running an Effect in it already is (EK2003), and the fix is an Effect contract (`A!E`) or a hatch. Exceptions thrown by TypeScript code it calls still propagate unchanged and become defects where they reach an effectful caller. To catch such an exception, use the `try` hatch. Throw keeps one meaning throughout VibeLang; plain functions never fail or need services. Reading any `.vibe` code as Effect code is never wrong. Amended 2026-10-23 (release correction): a constructor, an accessor and a class static block run as plain JavaScript wherever they stand, as a module's top level does, so they never fail either. A throw that can escape one is EK2003, and EK1001 at a module's top level; a throw that their own catch handles stays inside. Code that must throw a JavaScript exception, such as a test's throwing getter, belongs in a `module` declaration (D283). Evidence: `plain-code-throw.fail`, `plain-code-throw-caught`. 2026-10-25 (real-world migration): EK2004 also warns where a plain function's try block can fail, so its catch runs, and the block calls TypeScript code whose exception passes through that catch: an untrusted function, a tagged declaration without `@throws`, or a trusted package's export whose name says it throws in Effect's API, one that ends in `Sync` or has `OrThrow` in it. Ported from TypeScript, whose catch received every exception, `try { return Schema.decodeUnknownSync(Schema.String)(JSON.parse(text)) } catch { return undefined }` caught `JSON.parse`'s SyntaxError (D101) and threw the decoder's SchemaError, and `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(identity(schema)))` threw the `.ts` helper's exception, both with no diagnostic; before, EK2004 warned only when the try block could not fail at all. It reports the first such call of each try block, and its note names `Result.try` and a `module` block, whose catch is TypeScript's. Calls of built-ins are not reported, though their exceptions pass through too (D101): a block of them would warn at every array method. The catch itself is unchanged, as this decision rules. It fires in one of the 496 acceptance cases on `main`, `plain-throw-caught`, at the call whose exception that case shows passing through (its expected diagnostics now list it), and none of the 747 documentation blocks. Cases `plain-catch-passing.fail`, `plain-catch-result-try`.

**D283. A `module` declaration escapes to TypeScript** (amended by D364) (2026-10-21; with D148 and D229 the escapes from VibeLang; syntax from TC39's [module declarations](https://github.com/tc39/proposal-module-declarations)). `module text { export function clean(value: string): string { … } }` declares an inline module whose body is TypeScript, checked and compiled as a `.ts` file would be: JavaScript's `throw` and `catch`, `async`/`await` and platform APIs mean there what they mean in TypeScript. Its exports are reached only through an import, `import { clean } from text`, so a hatch may use them as it uses any import (D229). The module is self-contained: it imports what it needs itself, its relative specifiers resolve from the `.vibe` file's directory, and it cannot read the `.vibe` file's own bindings. In a `.vibe` file `module` never means a TypeScript namespace. The module form provides a TypeScript escape after D282 limited JavaScript's `throw` to code that has escaped to TypeScript.

## TypeScript-style Effect forms (2026-10-21)

TypeScript-style constructs that have a VibeLang form should draw a warning and an automatic fix. D284-D290 make each such construct a warning with a quick fix, and D291 applies the fixes to a project. They write the forms the parser accepts today; D278-D281, decided the same day, add forms (classes in `error` declarations, `schema error`, `schema Name = type`, `service Name: Type`) that widen these rules once the parser accepts them, as each entry says. All eight codes, EK4830-EK4837, are lint in D241's sense: warnings by default, adjustable in the `vibelang` setting `diagnostics` and by `@vibelang-diagnostics` comments, and never errors that change emit or the exit status. A fix is offered only where the VibeLang form keeps the program's meaning; where it would not, the warning says what would change and offers none; where VibeLang has no form, nothing is reported. Every fix comes from one plan that also words the warning (`tsforms*.go`), so the editor, the CLI note, and `--fix` cannot disagree. Measured on the migrated Smithers packages at the T2 base (`@smthrs/errors` and `@smthrs/fs`, 27 `.vibe` files): EK4830 0, EK4831 1, EK4832 2, EK4833 9, EK4834 11, EK4835 4, EK4836 0, EK4837 1. `vibelang --fix` applied 26 fixes in 9 files and left the two without one; both packages still type-check, `@smthrs/errors`' suite passes (65 tests), and `@smthrs/fs`'s passes (197 tests) when its `.vibe` suites load one copy of vitest, as before the fixes. The three `throw new Error(…)` it wrote from `Effect.fail(new Error(…))` draw EK4804, which `Effect.fail` hid.

**D284. An error class that an error declaration writes draws EK4830, and a Schema error class EK4837**. A class that extends `Data.TaggedError(tag)`, the prelude's `TaggedError(tag)`, `Data.Error`, or `Schema.TaggedError<Self>()(tag, fields)` with an empty body, no `implements` clause, and no modifier other than `export`, draws the warning. The fix writes `error Name<T> { fields }` in place of `class … {}`, keeping `export`, the type parameters, and the JSDoc above, and dropping the fields' `readonly`, which every error field already is (D134). It applies only when the base is a TaggedError, the tag is a string literal equal to the class name, and the fields are an object type written in place: then the declaration is the same class, `TaggedError` being `Data.TaggedError` with a custom matcher (D3, D215). It removes the import of `Data` or `effect/Data` when the class was its last use. Without a fix, the note says what a declaration would change: a tag other than the name would become the name, `Data.Error` would gain a `_tag`, and fields from a named type must be written out. A Schema error class draws EK4837 instead, also without a fix: a declaration would stop it being a Schema (decoding, encoding, and the checks at construction) and, with a tag other than its name, change its tag. D279's `schema error` is the declaration that writes a Schema error; until the parser accepts it, EK4837 has no fix, and with it the fix applies when the tag is the class name and every field maps as D286 maps a struct's, while a qualified tag such as `"flows/fs/FsError"` still changes, the data-compatibility step D279 leaves to the package. It has its own code so that a project whose errors are Schemas by design can turn it off meanwhile and keep EK4830. A class with members, a constructor, decorators, or another base draws nothing for now: D278 gives an `error` declaration everything a class declaration accepts, but the parser does not accept it yet, and the rule widens to those classes when it does. Evidence: cases `tsforms-errors` and `tsforms-errors-fixed` return the same result; `@smthrs/fs`'s `FsError` (a Schema error tagged "flows/fs/FsError") draws the warning without a fix, and `@smthrs/errors`' `SmithersError` (a constructor and members) draws nothing.

**D285. A Context.Service class draws EK4831** (D308 made the fix write a member's Effect type as `Effect<…>`, and D309 restores the `!` form; amended by D501). `class X extends Context.Service<X, Shape>()(key) {}` draws the warning when Shape is an object type. The fix writes `service X { members }` when Shape is an object type written in place, the class has no type parameters, members, `implements` clause, or `make` option, and every member is a property, which becomes a value member, or a method whose return type is an Effect type, which becomes an operation with the `!` form of that type (D127), as a member without a body must write its contract (EK2101). A comma between members becomes a line or a semicolon. The declaration's key is derived from the package, the module, and the name (D93), so the written key string goes away: lookups through the class are unchanged, because providing and requiring both use the class, but the key appears in "Service not found" messages, and a `Context.Service` declared elsewhere with the same string would no longer share its slot. The note says what the key becomes. Effect's own `deterministicKeys` rule rewrites keys in the same spirit. A shape that is a named type draws the warning without a fix, since the declaration needs its members written out; static members also refuse the fix; a shape that is not an object type, such as `Context.Service<Port, number>`, draws nothing. D281's `service Name: Type` writes both of those, and the fix extends to them, keeping the class's static members as the declaration's body, once the parser accepts it. Evidence: `tsforms-services` and `tsforms-services-fixed`; `@smthrs/fs`'s `FlowInvoker`, whose shape is the exported interface `Service`, draws the warning without a fix.

**D286. A constant that holds a Schema.Struct draws EK4832** (amended by D474). `const X = Schema.Struct({ … })`, a single `const` binding without a type annotation, exported or not, at any scope, draws the warning when a `schema` declaration (D133) writes the same struct. The parser writes a declaration's fields back into Schema calls, so the fix writes each field's type from its schema and applies only when every field maps back exactly: the keyword schemas (`Schema.String` is `string`, `Schema.Null` is `null`, and so on), `Schema.Literal` of a literal, `Schema.Literals` of two or more, `Schema.Union` of two or more members that are not all literals and not unions, `Schema.Array`, `Schema.Tuple`, `Schema.Record`, a nested `Schema.Struct`, `Schema.optional` and `Schema.mutableKey` around a field in the order the parser writes them (`name?:` and `mutable name:`), and a name or a dotted name of any other schema, which a field names as a value, as in `count: Schema.Finite`. The declaration also declares the interface X, the struct's Type, so the fix applies only when no other type of that name is in scope, or when the only one is the struct's own type written beside it with the same export: `type X = typeof X.Type`, `type X = Schema.Schema.Type<typeof X>`, or an empty interface extending the latter, which it removes. A type alias becomes an interface, which has no implicit index signature; `vibelang --fix` refuses the rewrite where that adds an error. A check, an annotation, a pipe, `Schema.optionalKey`, `Schema.NullOr`, a spread, a computed key, or a comment keeps the constant and draws nothing: no declaration says the same. D280's `schema Name = <type>` extends the rule to a constant that holds any schema the mapping writes, such as `const Code = Schema.Literals([…])` beside `type Code = typeof Code.Type`, once the parser accepts it. Evidence: `tsforms-schemas` and `tsforms-schemas-fixed` decode the same values and refuse the same input.

**D287. An Effect type written with its type arguments draws EK4833** (amended by D308, which narrows it to `Effect.Effect<…>`, and by D309, which keeps that scope and restores the `!` form in the fix). `Effect.Effect<A, E, R>`, the prelude's `Effect<A, E, R>`, or an imported `Effect`, any type that names effect's Effect interface written with one to three arguments, draws the warning, and the fix writes the `!` form, which the parser expands back to `Effect.Effect<A, E, R>` (D127). An empty slot is `never`. In the return annotation of a function with a body, where a `!` form without dependencies infers them, `Effect<A, E, never>` becomes `A!E!` and `Effect<A, E, infer R>` becomes `A!E` (`A!never` when E is `never`); elsewhere a missing slot is `never`, so `Effect<A, E>` becomes `A!E` and `Effect<A>` becomes `A!!`. A slot holding a function, conditional, or `!` type, or an object type after a `!`, is parenthesized, and so is the whole form where it would join its surroundings: as a union or intersection member, an array element, an indexed access, an operand of `keyof` or `readonly`, a conditional type's part, or a slot of another `!` form. Refused: a return annotation with a body that writes fewer than three arguments (EK2003 already asks which channels), `infer` in another slot or outside such an annotation, and references the parser wrote or JSDoc holds. Evidence: `tsforms-types` and `tsforms-types-fixed`, including a `@vibelang-diagnostics-next-line EK4833:off` that keeps one spelling.

**D288. A running Effect.succeed or Effect.fail draws EK4834**. In a VibeLang function a call runs where it stands (D147), so a running `Effect.succeed(x)` is `x`, and a running `Effect.fail(e)` fails as `throw e` does (D46), a throw being a typed failure everywhere outside a hatch (D282). Only a call that runs counts, as the lowering decides it: one passed by name, held by a declared Effect type, or in a hatch builds a program and draws nothing. The fix of `Effect.succeed(x)` writes `x` where a value stands (a return, an initializer, an argument, an element or field, an arrow function's body, a branch of `?:`, the right side of `??`, `||`, or `&&`), parenthesized where `{` or a comma would misparse; it refuses an `x` that is itself an Effect, and refuses a call whose removal could change the function's form: it applies only when the function keeps an Effect contract, is a declaration that D10 compiles with its Effect form beside it, is a lambda or method whose expected type asks for an Effect function or a program, or performs another Effect operation the fixes keep. Without that rule, `{ exists: () => Effect.succeed(true) } as unknown as FileSystem` would become a plain function returning `true`, which its caller would see (a regression the gate below did not catch, since it type-checks; found by `@smthrs/fs`'s FileRouterFailures test). The fix of `Effect.fail(e)` writes a throw statement for a statement or a return, and a throw expression (D186) for an arrow function's body, a branch of `?:`, and the right side of `??`, `||`, or `&&`; it refuses an `e` whose type is a Cause, which `throw` rethrows reason by reason (D177). Calls with type arguments are refused. The value can have a narrower type than the success type, a literal staying a literal, so `--fix` refuses a rewrite whose program would then report an error. Evidence: `tsforms-direct` and `tsforms-direct-fixed`.

**D289. Effect's catch combinators draw EK4835**. `Effect.catchTag(self, "Tag", handler)`, `Effect.catchTags(self, { Tag: handler, … })`, and `Effect.catch(self, handler)` in a VibeLang function body draw the warning, and the fix writes a catch expression (D236): one `match` arm per tag and `default: throw e;` (D216, D218), or, for `Effect.catch`, the handler itself, or a fallback when the handler takes no parameter. An arm tests the failure's `_tag` with an object pattern, `when { _tag: "Tag" }:`, which is what catchTag tests; it keeps that structural test even when the static type names an error class, because a structurally typed value need not have the class's prototype. Every failure in the program's E must have a literal `_tag` for catchTag and catchTags, so that the arms remove exactly the failures catchTag would. The left side runs inside the catch as the program argument did; an Effect value is called to run it, and so is a handler value that is an Effect value, since catchTag runs what its handler returns. A handler must be an arrow function with at most one plain parameter and an expression body, or a block that only returns one; the catch variable takes its parameter's name. A catch expression is parenthesized where it does not stand alone, such as the left side of another catch, since its handler extends as far as an arrow function's body. One difference is VibeLang's by design (D13, D236): a cause that mixes an expected failure with a defect or an interruption passes through a catch expression, while Effect's combinators recover the failure and drop the rest. Refused: the data-last forms inside `pipe`, type arguments, a tag that is not a string literal, and any other handler. Evidence: `tsforms-catch` and `tsforms-catch-fixed`; `@smthrs/errors`' tag-routing tests, whose `SmithersError<Tag>` carries a tag from its options, keep passing with object patterns.

**D290. Effect.withSpan, Effect.provide, and Effect.provideService draw EK4836** (amended by D376: the fix for `withSpan` writes `withSpan(() => self, name)`). In a VibeLang function body, `Effect.withSpan(self, name)` becomes the block param call `withSpan(name) { return self }` (D188, D151) wherever it stands, unless a local `withSpan` shadows the prelude's; the block's return gives the program's value, so the span covers the same program. `Effect.provide(self, layers)` and `Effect.provideService(self, key, value)` become a provide block (D130, D172, D173) where a block can stand: as a return, whose `return` inside the block returns from the function, and as an expression statement. A layer array becomes the block's comma list. The program runs inside the block as the call ran it: a call runs where it stands, and an Effect value is called. Refused: an options argument, the data-last forms inside `pipe`, a provide anywhere a block cannot stand (such as an initializer), and a key that is not a name or a dotted name. Evidence: `tsforms-blocks` and `tsforms-blocks-fixed`.

**D291. vibelang --fix applies every safe fix to a project** (amended by D372: a fix keeps the program's meaning; no new error is a second check; and by D470: EK1002's direct style). `vibelang -p tsconfig.json --fix [other flags]` applies the quick fixes of EK4830-EK4836 and of the retired spellings (EK1009, EK2112, EK2113, EK2120, EK2121, whose fixes D172, D219, D221, and D223 make exact) to the project's `.vibe` files outside `node_modules`, writes the files it changes, prints one line per file with its fixes by code, and then compiles as the rest of the command line says, so `--noEmit` checks the result and the remaining diagnostics print as usual. Only diagnostics the project reports are fixed, so the `diagnostics` setting and `@vibelang-diagnostics` comments choose what it touches. Fixes come from the language server's own code actions, through its public request, so the editor and the CLI write the same text. They apply in rounds: each takes, per file, the fixes whose edits do not overlap, and the next round rechecks the edited program for the rest, so nested constructs converge. A round's fixes are kept only when the edited program reports no error that the original did not, compared by file, code, and message: when the round as a whole would add one, each file's fixes are tried together, then one at a time, and each fix that adds an error is refused, named with its line and the first error it adds, and not tried again. After the last round, an import of `effect` or one of its modules that the fixes left unused is removed, again only if that adds no error. Nothing is written until the rounds end, and a second run finds nothing to fix. `--fix` with `--build` or `--watch` is an error. Not a subcommand, because `vibelang` adds no commands (D30); not a separate binary, because the fixes need the checker and the language service in-process. Not controversial.

Gaps remaining after D284-D291, with the syntax each would need. No new syntax was added. D278-D281 settle what were the largest ones (members and constructors on errors, a Schema error declaration, schema aliases, and services of any shape); the rules gain those fixes when the parser implements them.

- **Exact optional fields in a schema.** `Schema.optionalKey` keeps `Schema.Struct` (no EK4832). Proposed: `exact name?: T`, beside `mutable name: T` (D134).
- **A provide block as a value.** `const cli = Effect.provide(app(), AppLive)` has no block form, since `provide` is a statement (no EK4836). Proposed: `provide` in expression position with the completion value of its last statement, as `do { }` has (D187).
- **The ! form that infers errors.** `Effect<A, infer E, R>` has no `!` spelling (no EK4833). Proposed: `A!infer!R`.
- Not done, not syntax: `x.pipe(f(a), g(b))` to `|>` (D245 converts nested calls, not method pipes, and D272's exact `x |> f(a)(%)` is longer than the pipe); a quick fix for EK1002's `Effect.gen(function* () { … })` (D470 adds it); an Effect-typed lambda whose form follows its body, `{ run: () => Effect.succeed(1) } as unknown as T`, which would need the fix to write a contract, `(): number!! => 1`.

## A skill for agents (2026-10-21)

**D292. The VibeLang skill ships in the `vibelang` package**. `packages/compiler/skills/vibelang/SKILL.md` is an agent skill in the standard format, a `name` and a `description` in YAML frontmatter followed by Markdown: VibeLang's model, every form beside the Effect code it replaces, the rules with their diagnostic codes, and the idiomatic choice for each job with the docs page that owns it, in about 4,000 tokens. The `vibelang` package lists it in `files`, so every project that installs the compiler has the skill for that version at `node_modules/vibelang/skills/vibelang/SKILL.md`, and an agent's instructions can point there. The Token counts page publishes its size beside the docs' (o200k_base, js-tiktoken 1.0.21), and `bun docs/tools/token-counts.ts --check` fails when a published number drifts. A change that adds or changes a form or a rule updates the skill. Rejected: a root `skills/vibelang/` directory, as the prototype had, which ships nowhere and can disagree with the installed compiler; a separate npm package, another version to keep in step; and the docs site alone, which an offline agent or one on an older compiler cannot rely on.

**D293. Both sides of a catch expression take its contextual type** (amends D238). A catch expression's contextual type types its left side and its fallback, or what its handler returns, as TypeScript's contextual type types both branches of a conditional expression. A literal on either side stays literal where that type expects one: in a function annotated `Outcome!!`, `{ _tag: "Selection", selection: hydrate(tokens) } catch (error) => ({ _tag: "Error", error })` checks as `Outcome`. Before, both tags widened to `string` (TS2322), because the parser passes each side to `$catchExpression` as a function whose generic Effect type gave it no context, and Smithers' `Incur.select` kept a try statement. Where the expression runs, its contextual type is its value (D24); where it is held as a program (D147, D269), the sides take that program's success type. Without a contextual type nothing changes: the value is `A | B`, each side typed on its own. A side outside the contextual type is still TypeScript's error, now reported on the literal that does not fit. For a side whose value is one literal, as in `load() catch "slow"`, TypeScript widens the inferred return type against the contextual signature's return type, which skipped the extensions; that widening now sees the contextual type the extensions give the function's return statements, so `"slow"` stays `"slow"` in a `Mode` position. Rejected: `as const` or a type argument at each use (the long form the rewrite removes), and inferring `$catchExpression`'s type parameters from the contextual type (TypeScript matches a contextual type against the call's declared return type, an Effect, while a running catch expression's contextual type is its value). Evidence: `catch-expression-context` (a handler, a fallback, a declared const, an argument, a block-bodied handler, chained catches, and a literal fallback) and `catch-expression-context.fail`.

**D294. A match D209 proves exhaustive runs its last arm without testing it** (amends D209 and D214; amended by D326, D341, D346, and D368: only an inert test is omitted, its reads are kept, and coverage is no reason; superseded by D405). When a match without `default` has a subject whose declared type is a finite union and whose narrowed type after the last arm is `never`, D209's proof, the native compiler emits the last arm without its tests and drops the `$noMatch` call: after `return`, in a match statement, and in the immediately invoked function, in plain and Effect functions alike. The arm's declarations still run, so its bindings and extracted values are bound as before, and a module whose every `$noMatch` is dropped no longer imports it. A guard proves nothing (D209): a guarded last arm takes part in the proof only when earlier arms already cover every case, and then no value of the type reaches it. Over any other type the tests and `$noMatch` stay, and an unmatched value still throws the proposal's `TypeError`. The (B) projector reads one file without types (D252) and keeps the tests; the two renderings differ only for a value outside the declared type. Trade-off: such a value, which only an unsound cast, `any`, or a second copy of an `error` class can produce, now takes the last arm instead of throwing. TypeScript's exhaustive `switch` and Rust's `match` make the same choice, and an author who wants the run-time check writes `default: throw …`. Rejected: keeping the final test, which leaves a branch no well-typed program reaches, so 100% branch coverage was impossible and authors wrote a `default` that hides a missing case from EK4701; and extending the proof to every subject whose remainder is `never`, such as a `string` matched by `String(let s)`, which D209 deliberately leaves to the run-time check. Evidence: the idiomatic `@smthrs/fs` rewrite needed `default` arms to keep its branch coverage; case `match-exhaustive-last-arm` pins the emitted `arms.js`; under V8 coverage with source maps, a three-arm match over `"draft" | "review" | "published"` goes from 87.5% to 100% branch coverage. Amended 2026-10-21 (release correction): the proof did not hold for class patterns, and the claim that only a cast, `any` or a second copy of a class reaches the last arm was false. `$is` typed a class pattern as a type predicate, which TypeScript narrows by shape, while the run time tests `instanceof` or the class's custom matcher (D213, D215). Two packages' `error NotFound`, a family's base beside an unrelated error with its fields, a structural object of a class type, and an instance a custom matcher refuses, all well typed and without a cast, took a wrong arm or returned `undefined`. Now a class pattern narrows as `instanceof` does, by derivation, so EK4701 reports the look-alike class no arm names and `is` leaves it in the remainder. A value's type still cannot prove what `instanceof` or a custom matcher returns, so a match whose arms call `$is` or `$extract` (a class, custom matcher, predicate or named value) keeps its last arm's tests, written as `runtime.$assertMatch(test, subject)`: a call, not a branch, that throws the proposal's `TypeError` when the test fails. Tests that reach every arm still cover every branch, so the coverage goal holds for every match; only a match whose arms test literals, shapes, primitive classes, `Option`, `Exit` or `Result` runs its last arm untested, and the trade-off above applies to it alone. Evidence: cases `match-class-patterns`, `match-class-patterns.fail`, `match-exhaustive-coverage` (V8 block coverage reports no missed range) and `match-exhaustive-last-arm`.

**D295. Data-first functions whose receiver is not named self are methods too** (amends D152; amended by D336). D152 makes a function a method when an overload's first parameter is named `self` or ends in `OrOptions`. effect 4.0.5 gives 41 data-first functions another name for the receiver, so D152 missed them: `cause.findErrorOption()` was TS2339, and the idiomatic rewrite of `@smthrs/fs` had to write `Cause.findErrorOption(cause)`. The compiler's `dataFirstParameters` table lists each such function with its receiver's name: `n` for BigDecimal's `abs`, `format`, `isInteger`, `isNegative`, `isPositive`, `isZero`, `negate`, `round`, `sign`, `toExponential`, and `toNumberUnsafe`; `input` for `Cause.findErrorOption`, Duration's `toHrTime` and `toNanosUnsafe`, and Exit's `findError` and `findDefect`; `cause` for `Cause.pretty`; `effect` for Effect's `annotateLogs`, `annotateSpans`, `tx`, `withExecutionPlan`, `withLogger`, `withLogSpan`, `withTracer`, `withTracerEnabled`, and `withTracerTiming`; `schedule` for Schedule's `toStep`, `toStepWithMetadata`, and `toStepWithSleep`; `stream` for `Stream.toChannel`; and `left` for Stream's `cross`, `crossWith`, `mergeLeft`, `mergeRight`, `race`, `zipLatest`, `zipLatestWith`, `zipLeft`, `zipRight`, `zipWith`, and `zipWithArray`. Only the overloads whose first parameter has the listed name are methods, so a curried data-last overload, such as `Stream.zipLeft(right)` or `Effect.withLogSpan(label)`, never is. Functions that take one of their module's types first but construct or run it are not methods: the runners `Effect.runFork`, `runPromise`, `runPromiseExit`, `runSync`, and `runSyncExit`, the constructors `Effect.acquireRelease`, `acquireDisposable`, and `acquireUseRelease`, `Duration.fromInput` and `fromInputUnsafe`, and `Stream.fromEventListener`. A Go test, `TestDataFirstParametersMatchEffect`, walks effect's declarations and fails when an export takes one of its module's own types first under another name and is neither listed nor excluded, so an effect upgrade that renames a receiver or adds such a function must be decided here. The (B) projector follows the same table: `cmd/vibelang-data-methods/generate.ts` reads it for the prelude's D252 methods and dispatch candidates, `cmd/vibelang-forms` marks method signatures with the checker's own test (`vibelang.TakesSelfFirst`), and the projector's `dataMethods` and `dataLastMethods` list the new names. Rejected: every function whose first parameter has one of its module's own types (it makes the runners and constructors methods, and `program.runPromise()` reads as an operation on the program while runners keep their own rules, D38), and a list of receiver names (`n`, `input`, and `left` also name ordinary parameters, as `BigDecimal.fromNumberUnsafe(n: number)` shows). Evidence: `data-methods-first-parameter`, `data-methods-first-parameter.fail`, and the idiomatic `@smthrs/fs`.

**D296. A JavaScript generator never fails** (amends D271; its iterator-annotated `function*` is superseded by D370, and its rule holds for generator methods). A `function*` whose return annotation is a JavaScript iterator type (D271) follows D282 like any plain function. Its `throw` is a typed failure, so one that can escape it is EK2003: "Generator<string> is a JavaScript iterator annotation, but steps can fail with Invalid, and a plain generator never fails." Its `catch` receives typed failures only: a throw it handles stays inside, marked as D54's plain lowering marks it, and any other exception passes through, including one a caller passes in with `generator.throw()`. Its calls stay TypeScript's and run nothing, so only its throws can fail it. Code that must speak a JavaScript protocol by throwing, such as a coroutine driven by `generator.throw()`, belongs in a `module` block (D283) or a `.ts` file; Smithers' McpClient is `.ts` today and is unaffected until it migrates. JavaScript exceptions belong in an explicit TypeScript escape, so reading VibeLang as Effect is always valid. Rejected: treating the annotation as an escape to TypeScript, which would give `throw` a second meaning inside `.vibe` code. Evidence: `plain-generator-throw.fail` and `plain-generator`, whose catch now lets an injected exception through.

## Smithers tooling (2026-10-21)

**D297. A .vibe file compiles with its own package's VibeLang settings** (amends D34 and D261). A program reads another package's `.vibe` source when a project imports a workspace package that exports it, and the checker lowers that file to type its functions. The settings that decide what the file means, its trusted packages (D261), diagnostic categories (D34, D241), and tracing (D34), are those of the package that owns it. A `.vibe` file of the compiled project's own package (the nearest `package.json` with a name) takes the compiled tsconfig's settings, so a test tsconfig that extends the package's keeps its changes. A `.vibe` file of another package takes the settings of the nearest `tsconfig.json` above it, with what that config extends, or the compiled project's when there is none. A program without a tsconfig (`vibelang --ignoreConfig`, or files named on the command line) has no VibeLang settings and no package of its own, so there every package's `.vibe` file takes its own package's settings (real-world migration, 2026-10-23: a docs check that typechecks Markdown code blocks with `--ignoreConfig` reported EK4401 for calls that other packages trust). TypeScript's own options still apply to every file of the program. `vibelang/project`, the bundler plugins, and the Node loader already compile such a file with its own project, so the checker now agrees with the build that emits the file: a consumer sees the Effect types the package's own build infers, and a package states what it trusts once. Evidence: with `@smthrs/errors` and `@smthrs/fs` migrated, `apps/mvp` reported EK4401 for `isSmithersErrorCode` in `packages/errors/src/SmithersError.vibe`, and `.smithers/flows` 25 EK4401s in `@smthrs/fs`, for calls their own packages trust. Go tests `TestForeignVibeFileUsesItsOwnProjectSettings`, `TestForeignVibeFileMatchesItsOwnBuild`, `TestSamePackageKeepsTheCompiledProjectSettings`, `TestLinkedWorkspaceVibeFileUsesItsOwnProjectSettings` (a package linked into `node_modules`, with its settings at the top level or in `compilerOptions`), and `TestCommandLineProgramUsesEachPackageProjectSettings`, and the runnable case `foreign-project-settings`, which fails with EK4401 without this rule. Rejected: applying the rule only to files found by searching `node_modules` (a relative import or a `paths` mapping into another package would keep the importer's settings, while the bundler plugins compile that file with its own project), copying every dependency's `trustedPackages` into each dependent's tsconfig (one copy per edge, and a dependent's settings would then change a dependency's verdicts), and dropping EK diagnostics in such files (a dependent's check would then miss real errors in sources it compiles).

**D298. Declarations import a VibeLang module through its .js output** (extends D31; consolidated in D365). A declaration file names a relative `.vibe` module as `./user.js`, whether a `.ts` or a `.vibe` file wrote the import and whether or not the project sets `rewriteRelativeImportExtensions`: `export { find } from "./User.vibe"` in `index.ts` declares `export { find } from "./User.js"`, and an inferred type prints `import("./User.js").NotFound`. TypeScript keeps a `.ts` specifier in a declaration because it resolves `./x.ts` to `x.d.ts`; no TypeScript version resolves `./user.vibe` to `user.d.ts`, so `@smthrs/errors`' published `index.d.ts` failed with TS2307 for every consumer. Evidence: the cases `declaration-ts-imports-vibe`, with `rewriteRelativeImportExtensions` (re-exports, a type re-export, an inferred import type, and `typeof import()`), and `declaration-ts-imports-vibe-no-rewrite`, without it, as a build that emits only declarations sets it; the Go test `TestModuleSpecifierRewriteVibeExtension`. Rejected: rewriting only under `rewriteRelativeImportExtensions` (the declaration is unusable either way), and emitting `user.vibe.d.ts` beside `user.d.ts` (a second declaration of one module). Not controversial.

**D299. Whole-project compiles are cached on disk, keyed by what they read** (extends D105). `vibelang/project` stores each successful whole-project compile, and `vibelang/register`, `vibelang-vite`, and `vibelang-unplugin` reuse it in later processes while its fingerprint holds: the compiler file and the native binary its launcher runs; the tsconfig, every config it extends, and the configs its references name; for each `.vibe` file of another package that the program reads, the `tsconfig.json` whose settings the checker gives it (D297) and what that extends; the listing of every directory the include patterns scan; lockfiles and package-manager state in the project's directory and above; every file the compile read, a project file by content and a dependency file by size, modification time, and inode; and the `package.json` nearest each. Configs and directories are read before the compiler starts, and a compile whose sources carry a timestamp at or after its start (less 50 ms, or 2 s on a filesystem that keeps whole seconds) is not stored, so an edit during a compile cannot be cached against the old output. A failed compile is never stored. An entry is written to a temporary file and renamed, so concurrent processes read whole entries. The default directory is `vibelang-cache-<uid>` under the system temporary directory, created mode 0700 and ignored when another user owns it or others can write it; `VIBELANG_CACHE_DIR` moves it, `VIBELANG_DISABLE_CACHE=1` turns it off, and the API takes `cache: false | { directory }`. The alternatives were `vibelang -b` with a persistent output directory (a process per check, 20-40 ms each, and outputs rewritten in place under concurrent readers) and `vibelang --listFilesOnly` as the check (382 ms for `@smthrs/fs`, far above a 50 ms warm start). Not covered: a file outside `include` and outside `node_modules` that newly wins module resolution, and a `node_modules` link changed by hand. Evidence: `@smthrs/errors`, which the Smithers CLI loads on startup, compiles in 281 ms and loads from the cache in 3 ms, and `@smthrs/fs` in 1336 ms and 11 ms; `packages/compiler/test/cache.test.mjs` pins each invalidation.

**D303. A module declaration lowers inline and declares itself in a file of its own** (implements D283; superseded in part by D364: exports are live bindings, evaluation follows import order, and names are case-sensitive; its inline lowering superseded by D403). The parser gives each top-level `module text { … }` of `main.vibe` a TypeScript source file, `main.vibe.text.ts`, a supplemental file of `main.vibe` (TypeScript 7's content-mapper seam) whose text is `main.vibe`'s with every character outside the body blanked. Its positions are therefore `main.vibe`'s, so diagnostics, hovers, definitions, and source maps land in the body as written; its relative imports resolve from `main.vibe`'s directory; and it cannot see `main.vibe`'s bindings, as D283 requires. `from text` parses as a string literal that names that file, `./main.vibe.text.js`, which the program resolves to it without asking the file system. The JavaScript holds the module inline.
