A module that the file imports becomes an arrow function that returns a frozen object of the module's exports, called once before the file's own code and awaited when the body awaits at its top level. The body's imports move to the file's top level as namespace imports, one per specifier, and the body's references to them become members of those namespaces, so they stay live bindings, need no renaming, and keep TypeScript's import elision. An import from the module becomes constants destructured from the object; in CommonJS output it becomes the generated name whose members TypeScript's module transform reads. A module that the file never imports, or imports only types from, emits nothing, as TypeScript elides such an import of a `.ts` file. Inline, because one `.vibe` file stays one JavaScript module: `vibelang/register` and the bundler plugins map a `.vibe` file to its output and load it unchanged, `import.meta` inside the body is the file's, as TC39's proposal specifies, and relative specifiers resolve from the file's directory with no loader rule. A sibling `main.vibe.text.js` would need every loader to resolve a file that is not on disk. Declarations go the other way: a declaration file can hold a namespace but not a module, and a namespace cannot import, so each module's declarations are TypeScript's own declaration emit of its body, `main.vibe.text.d.ts` beside `main.d.ts`, which imports what it uses from `./main.vibe.text.js`. Rejected: one declaration file with each body as a `declare namespace` and hoisted import aliases; checking the body inside the `.vibe` file's tree as a namespace with VibeLang's rules switched off (every rule keyed on the file would need an exemption, and name resolution would see the file's bindings); and `data:` URL modules (relative imports fail inside them). Differences from TC39's proposal: a module evaluates after every module the file imports, not in import order; an import from it reads its exports once, when it has run, so a later assignment to an exported `let` is not seen; and the module's name is not a value, so `import(text)`, `export module`, `export default module { }`, module declarations below a file's top level, and module expressions are not supported (EK4408 reports `export module` and a nested declaration; the other two are syntax errors). Module names that differ only in letter case are one name, because a body's file name is lower case. 2026-10-24 (real-world migration): every name the lowering adds to a file's top level differs from the generated names of a module body's hoisted imports. Both were `Effect_1` for effect/Effect in a file that uses `Effect`, and the file declared the name twice and did not load. Cases `module-declaration-generated-names`, `module-import-generated-names`, `module-declaration-import-names`.

**D303 implementation correction (2026-10-21).** Module holders and the runtime namespace used to freeze their exports have unique emitter-owned names. Freezing uses the runtime's captured intrinsic, so a body-local, imported, or file-level binding named `Object` cannot replace it. An exported `__proto__` is a computed own data property on the frozen null-prototype object. EK4408 warns when a module name also names a value binding declared or imported by the file; globals and type-only bindings do not collide. This is a warning because an unused module with a colliding value already compiles and runs correctly; rejecting it would be a breaking change. Strict collision rejection requires a separate language decision.

**D303 CommonJS correction (2026-10-21).** Re-exports from module declarations assign their value snapshots to CommonJS `exports` explicitly: named and default aliases, `export *`, and `export * as ns`. A `__proto__` re-export uses the runtime's captured `Object.defineProperty` to create an own property without changing the exports object's prototype. The ES module output and declaration shapes are unchanged.

## Error classes (2026-10-21)

**D304. A `_tag` field declares an error family's tag type** (2026-10-21; extends D278 and D279; amended by D345 and D347). D278 tags every error class, a subclass included, with its own name, and TypeScript lets a subclass narrow a property's type, never change it, so a value of the base's type may carry any subclass's tag. A base whose subclasses carry their own tags therefore declares its tag type with a field named `_tag`: `error SmithersError { _tag: string; code: SmithersErrorCode }` is `class SmithersError extends TaggedError("SmithersError" as string)<{ readonly _tag: string; readonly code: SmithersErrorCode }> {}`, and `schema error` passes its tag the same way, `Schema.TaggedError<FsError>()("FsError" as string, { … })`. The constructor never takes `_tag`. An `abstract error`, which has no instances of its own, has the tag type `string` without the field. Every other error keeps the literal tag D126 gives it, so `catchTag("NotFound")` still removes `NotFound` from E and D209's exhaustiveness still holds; on a family's base, `catchTag("SmithersError")` matches the base's own instances and keeps `SmithersError` in E, which is sound. A subclass lowers to `class IntegrationError extends $errorSubclass(SmithersError, "IntegrationError") { declare readonly _tag: "IntegrationError" }`: the runtime's `$errorSubclass` returns a subclass that sets the tag after the base's constructor runs and reports it as the error's `name`, typed as the base itself, so the subclass keeps the base's constructor, statics, private members and nominal type. Its fields are `declare readonly` members, and a subclass that declares fields and no constructor gets `constructor(args: $ErrorArgs<typeof Base, Pick<Sub, "field">>) { super(args) }`, the base's fields and its own as one object. A schema error subclass extends with `Base.extend<Sub>("Sub")({ _tag: Schema.tag("Sub"), … })`, so its schema holds both sets of fields and its own tag. EK2117 reports a tag that does not fit (a subclass's name and its base's tag type, or a concrete error's name and its own `_tag` field), an error that extends a class that is not an error, a `schema error` that extends an `error`, and an `error` that extends a `schema error`; EK2118 reports a subclass that declares fields and no constructor when its base's constructor does not take one object of fields. Rejected: inferring a base's tag type from its subclasses, widening every error's tag to `string` (catchTag would never remove a failure from E), keeping the base's literal tag under a relaxed relation (`catchTag("SmithersError")` would remove failures that are `IntegrationError`s at run time), and a tag type parameter, `SmithersError<Tag>`, as the TypeScript class had (every family member's type would need an argument). Evidence: cases `decl-error-family`, `decl-error-positional`, `decl-schema-error` and `decl-error-family-tag.fail`; `@smthrs/errors`' `SmithersError` rewritten as an `error` declaration. Amended 2026-10-23 (release correction): two parts of a family did not hold. EK2117 compared a subclass's `_tag` field only with its base's tag type, so `error Sub extends Base { _tag: "Other" }` compiled with the tag type `"Other"` while the run time tags it `"Sub"`, and `catchTag("Other")` removed from E a failure it never matches; EK2117 now reports a concrete subclass whose `_tag` field's type leaves out its name, as it reports a root. And a schema error base's schema tested its own literal tag, so encoding a subclass's instance through the base, which type-checks, threw, as did decoding a subclass's record. Now the base's schema accepts its family: the parser writes `static { $schemaErrorFamily(this) }` in the base of a family (a `schema error` with a `_tag` field, or `abstract`) and `static { $schemaErrorMember(this) }` in every other `schema error` subclass, and the base's schema is the union of its own and its subclasses' own schemas, so it encodes an instance with the schema of the instance's class and decodes by tag. Each read of the base's schema is a new union, which lists the subclasses defined before that schema first decodes or encodes: a struct built from the base at a module's top level also lists the subclasses defined after it and before its first use, and a subclass whose module has not run cannot be decoded. A base instance encodes and decodes as before, and each subclass's own schema is unchanged. Rejected: rejecting the encode or decode at compile time (a value of the base's type may be a base instance, which encodes correctly today) and a `Schema.String` tag on the base (it drops a subclass's fields and decodes a subclass's record as a base instance carrying the subclass's tag). Evidence: cases `decl-schema-error-family` and `decl-error-family-own-tag.fail`.

## Effect 4.0.6 (2026-10-25)

**D305. VibeLang follows Effect 4.0.6's defect precedence** (2026-10-25; amends D13 and D236). VibeLang pins `effect@4.0.6`. Where Effect's runtime combines an expected failure with a cleanup's or a concurrent sibling's defect (`ensuring`, `onExit`, `acquireRelease`, scope close, `callback` cleanup, concurrent `forEach` and `all`, and `raceAll` when every racer fails), each `Fail` becomes a `Die` of the same error, in order, original first (Effect-TS/effect#9433). Every typed recovery (`catch`, `catchTag`, `result`, `option`, `match`, `ignore`, `retry`, Stream) skips a cause with a defect and re-fails with its failures made defects; an interruption beside a failure doesn't block recovery; `mapError` maps every failure in place (#9516). VibeLang's forms that lower to those operators follow unchanged: `finally`, `finally (exit)`, `using`, `await.all`, `await.any`, `await.allSettled`, `for concurrent`, `retry` and `exit`. Where VibeLang handles causes itself it applies the same rule. When the kernel abandons a continuation, it combines the clause's or fiber's exit with the cleanup failures defects first (K16a, K16b). Source `catch` and catch expressions re-raise a cause with a defect with its failures as defects (K16d). A retired typed clause before a Cause clause never matches a failure beside a defect. Consequences for existing code: a failure whose cleanup crashed is now observed as a defect (`cause.hasFails()` is false, and `Effect.catchDefect` receives the original error); Effect's recovery operators no longer swallow the defect; `await.allSettled` propagates it instead of settling `Result.fail`. Nothing changes in types or syntax: `Effect.catchDefect(p, f)` and `match (exit(p))` (D217) already recover defects in the fewest tokens, and a `catch` form for defects would make catching a bug look as ordinary as catching a domain error. Evidence: F11a-F11e (Effect alone, `experiments/tests/defect-precedence.test.ts`), K16a-K16d, and the case `failures-mixed-causes`. D256's `timeout` limitation stands on 4.0.6 (F11e).


**D306. `catch` recovers a failure beside an interruption, as `Effect.catch` does** (2026-10-25; amends D13 and D236; separable from D305). When a cause holds no defect, `catch` and the catch expressions receive its first expected failure and drop any interruption or further failure beside it, exactly as `Effect.catch`, `catchTag` and `result` treat that cause on Effect 4.0.5 and 4.0.6; `retry` follows the same rule on 4.0.6 (F11c). Under D13, VibeLang's `catch` was the only recovery form that refused such a cause. A cause made only of interruptions still passes, so `catch` never stops cancellation. A fiber that is itself interrupted skips every `catch` while it unwinds interruptibly, as Effect skips every recovery frame. One rule stays VibeLang's own: a failure raised while a handler abandons a continuation carries the abandonment's internal interrupt and passes every `catch` inside that continuation (K14d), because recovering it would resume code the handler abandoned. Programs that observe the change: `try { join f } catch (e) { … }`, where `f` was interrupted while its cleanup threw `e`, and `try { interrupt() } finally { throw e }` inside a `catch`; both now reach the handler.


## Data types (2026-10-25)

**D307. `data` declares a tagged union with Effect's own `Data.TaggedEnum`** (extends D126, D133 and D206-D209; its four type parameters are a restriction, D374). `data Delivery { Waiting; Sent { tracking: string }; Rejected { reason: string } }` declares what Effect's documentation writes for a tagged union: the type `type Delivery = Data.TaggedEnum<{ Waiting: {}; Sent: { tracking: string }; Rejected: { reason: string } }>` and the constructors `const Delivery: Data.TaggedEnum.Constructor<Delivery> = Data.taggedEnum<Delivery>()`, under one name. A variant without fields is a bare name, and `Delivery.Waiting()` constructs it; a variant's fields are written as an `error`'s are, `name: Type` or `name?: Type`, and are readonly, as `Data.TaggedEnum` makes them. A line break or `;` separates fields and variants, and a comma is EK1013, as in an `error`'s body, so the two change together if the field separator rule changes. A generic data type, `data LoadState<A, E> { Idle; Loaded { value: A }; Failed { error: E } }`, passes its type parameters through the interface `Data.TaggedEnum.WithGenerics` asks for, named `LoadStateDefinition`, `interface LoadStateDefinition extends Data.TaggedEnum.WithGenerics<2> { readonly taggedEnum: LoadState<this["A"], this["B"]> }`, and its constructors are `Data.taggedEnum<LoadStateDefinition>()`. `export data` exports the type, the constructors and, for a generic type, the definition, so a declaration file that infers a type from the constructors can name it. The constant's type is written out, `Data.TaggedEnum.Constructor<Delivery>`, or, for a generic type, the object `Data.taggedEnum` returns for its definition without the `Types.Simplify` around it: a declaration file then names Effect's types instead of expanding them, which TypeScript cannot do for a recursive data type such as `data Tree<A> { Leaf { value: A }; Node { left: Tree<A>; right: Tree<A> } }` (TS5088). Construction, `$is`, `$match`, `Equal` and `Hash` are Effect's: a value is a plain record with a `_tag`, and two values with equal fields are `Equal.equals`.

A variant is a pattern. `when Delivery.Sent:` and `x is Delivery.Sent` test the tag, as `when { _tag: "Sent" }` does, and `when Delivery.Sent { let tracking }` also reads the fields its braces list, binding each from the matched value: the variant fixes its fields, so a field needs no presence test, and an optional field the value leaves out binds `undefined` where `{ _tag: "Sent", let note }` would not match. What makes `Delivery` a data type is its value's type, constructors whose `$is` Effect's `Data` module declares, so a tagged union a `.ts` file declares with `Data.taggedEnum` matches the same way. The checker narrows a variant's test by its tag, keeping the members of a union whose tag may be the variant's, or, for a subject of no tagged type, narrowing it to the variant's values, as `Delivery.$is("Sent")` does; the lowering runs the test as that guard. A match without `default` over a data type must handle every variant (D209): EK4701 names what is left by variant, `This match does not handle every case of Delivery: Delivery.Rejected is left.`, and its last arm runs untested (D294), since a tag is a literal. Hover shows the declaration as written, and go to definition goes from `Delivery.Sent` to the variant. EK2122 reports a data type without variants, EK2123 a variant declared twice, EK2124 a name `Data.taggedEnum` reserves (a variant named `$is` or `$match`, a field named `_tag`, or another declaration of `NameDefinition`), EK2125 a variant member that is not a readonly typed field, EK2126 more than four type parameters or a constrained one, which `WithGenerics` cannot pass, EK2127 `Name.Variant` naming no variant, in place of TypeScript's TS2339, EK4706 a field list after a matcher that is not a variant or an argument list after one that is, and EK4707 a variant pattern whose subject's type has no such case.

Rejected: a variant with an unnamed payload, `Sent(string)` (Effect's `TaggedEnum` has named fields only; unnamed payloads are not supported); a class per variant with `Data.TaggedClass` (a union of classes is not what Effect writes for a tagged union, and `$match` would be missing); recognizing `when Name.Variant` by its syntax, as D208 recognizes `Option.Some` (the parser cannot see an imported data type, nor one a `.ts` file declares); leaving the test to `$is` at run time (`Data.taggedEnum`'s object makes a new constructor each time a variant is read, so nothing tells `$is` a variant from a predicate, and `when Delivery.Sent:` used to match every value); narrowing by the constructor's result type (the run-time test reads only the tag, so a different data type's variant with the same tag would reach the arm unnarrowed); field patterns after any class matcher, `when NotFound { let id }`. Evidence: cases `data-declaration`, `data-import` (a recursive generic type across files), `data-ts-consumer` (TypeScript constructs and matches through the declaration file), `data-match-emit`, `data-declaration.fail` and `data-patterns.fail`.

## Contracts (2026-10-25)

**D308. A contract is written `Effect<A, E, R>`; the `!` form is retired** (superseded by D309, which restores the `!` form and keeps this decision's meaning of `Effect<…>`; supersedes D127; amends D57, D93, D285 and D287). Effect uses ordinary type arguments; a concise alias remains a possible proposal. His rule for every detail: "Least surprising; follow idiomatic Effect patterns." A contract in a `.vibe` file means exactly what the same `Effect<…>` type means in TypeScript.

- A type argument left out means what it means in TypeScript: `Effect<A>` is `Effect<A, never, never>` and `Effect<A, E>` is `Effect<A, E, never>`, in the return annotation of a function with a body too. EK2003's rule that such an annotation write all three channels (D57) is retired; it existed so that `Effect<A, E>` would not be read as the `!` form's `A!E`, whose dependencies were inferred.
- Partial inference is unchanged: in the return annotation of a function with a body, a channel written `infer Name` comes from the body, as in `Effect<User, NotFound, infer R>` (D57). An unannotated function is still fully inferred.
- A plain return annotation keeps its meaning (D67, D282): `: number` is a plain function that never fails and needs nothing. The retired `number!!` was an Effect function that cannot fail and needs nothing; it is `Effect<number>`.
- Service and control members, `declare function` and other declarations without bodies write an Effect type, and a bare return type there is EK2101, reworded: "has no body to infer from, so its contract must be written as an Effect type." D93's rule that those types write all three arguments is retired with EK2003's, so `find(id: string): Effect<User, NotFound>` fails with `NotFound` and needs nothing, as the retired `User!NotFound` there did.
- The `!` form is EK1014, an error. The parser still reads it as the type it stood for, so a file that has one checks, and EK1014's quick fix writes that type: `A!E!R` becomes `Effect<A, E, R>`, `A!E!` becomes `Effect<A, E>`, `A!!R` becomes `Effect<A, never, R>`, `A!!` becomes `Effect<A>`, and `A!E` becomes `Effect<A, E, infer R>` in the return annotation of a function with a body and `Effect<A, E>` anywhere else. The parentheses the form needed around a slot or around itself go away, since a type argument and a type reference need none. `vibelang -p tsconfig.json --fix` applies the fix (D291), so a project migrates in one command. The fix writes the prelude's `Effect`, which a file that imports the `Effect` namespace from `effect` still has as a type.
- Hovers, messages, quick fixes and refactors write Effect types as `Effect<…>`, in TypeScript's short form where the defaults allow: `Effect<string>`, not `Effect<string, never, never>`. Declaration files write a contract as it is written, `Effect<A>` included, through the runtime's `Effect` type, an alias of effect's `Effect.Effect` with the same defaults, so TypeScript callers see Effect's own type.
- EK4833 (D287) now reports only an Effect type written through the Effect module, `Effect.Effect<A, E, R>`, where the prelude's `Effect` names the same type, and its fix drops the module and keeps the type arguments as written. The prelude's `Effect<…>` and an `Effect` imported as a type draw nothing. EK4831's fix (D285) writes a service member's Effect type the same way.
- The ESTree converter keeps `VibeEffectType` (D277) for the retired form, which still parses. The experimental content mapper (`internal/vibelangmapper`) keeps projecting the retired form and does not report EK1014; it now reads a written `Effect<A>` or `Effect<A, E>` as the compiler does.

The `!` form moves to the [proposals page](https://vibelang.sh/proposals#effect-type-shorthand) as a potential proposal, because Effect is used enough to warrant its own syntax that reduces tokens. Measured with o200k_base (js-tiktoken 1.0.21), the README's program is 117 tokens against 202 for Effect TypeScript, 42.1% fewer (116 tokens and 42.6% with the shorthand), and the shorthand saves 1 to 5 tokens per contract: `User!!` is 2 tokens and `Effect<User>` 3; `User!!Db` is 3 and `Effect<User, never, Db>` 7. Breaking: every `.vibe` file that writes the `!` form reports EK1014 until `--fix` or a hand edit rewrites it. Evidence: cases `retired-effect-types.fail` (each form's EK1014 and its message) and `retired-effect-types-fixed` (what `--fix` writes, which compiles and runs), `effect-type-defaults` and `effect-type-defaults.fail` (`Effect<A>` and `Effect<A, E>` check the channels they leave out as `never`; `infer` still infers; a service's members written `Effect<…>`).

**D309. The `!` form is restored as an alias of `Effect<A, E, R>`, and is the documented form** (supersedes D308; restores D127; keeps D308's meaning of `Effect<…>`). The shorter contract spelling reduces tokens while preserving Effect's type semantics. Both spellings are valid and interchangeable in every position a type can take. The guides, the skill, the README, the examples and the templates write the `!` form, as they did before D308, and say once, where they introduce contracts, that `Effect<A, E, R>` is accepted too.

- The `!` form means what D127 defined: `A!E!R` is `Effect<A, E, R>`; an empty slot is `never`, so `A!E!` needs nothing, `A!!R` cannot fail, and `A!!` neither fails nor needs anything; dependencies left out entirely, `A!E`, are `infer R` in the return annotation of a function with a body and `never` everywhere else. Service and control members and other declarations without bodies write a contract in either spelling; `A!E` there needs nothing.
- `Effect<A, E, R>` keeps D308's meaning, TypeScript's: a type argument left out is `never`, so `Effect<A>` is `A!!` and `Effect<A, E>` is `A!E!`, in the return annotation of a function with a body too; `infer Name` infers a channel (D57). EK2003's three-channel rule and D93's three-argument rule for EK2101 stay retired, and EK2101 keeps D308's wording. The two spellings differ only where a channel is left out: `A!E` on a function with a body infers R, while `Effect<A, E>` checks R as `never`.
- EK1014 is retired. No diagnostic reports the `!` form, its quick fix and its entry in `vibelang --fix` (D291) are gone, and the number is not reused. A file that `--fix` rewrote under 0.0.8 or 0.0.9 keeps its `Effect<…>` types, which still mean the same.
- EK4833 keeps D308's scope: it reports only `Effect.Effect<A, E, R>`, and the prelude's `Effect<…>` draws nothing. Its fix writes the `!` form again, as D287 described; since a type argument left out is now `never` in a body's return annotation too, `Effect.Effect<A>` there becomes `A!!` and `Effect.Effect<A, E>` becomes `A!E!`, where D287 refused them. EK4831's fix writes a member's Effect type in the `!` form (D285).
- Diagnostics, quick fixes, refactors, hovers, inlay hints and signature help print Effect types in the `!` form, as before D308. Declaration files write the types the parser expands: `Effect.Effect<A, E, R>` for the `!` form, and the written `Effect<…>` through the runtime's alias.
- The grammars (tree-sitter, TextMate), the formatter, the ESTree converter (`VibeEffectType`, D277) and the content mapper read both spellings, and the formatter keeps the spelling the source used.
- The shorthand leaves the proposals page, since it is implemented again.

Measured with o200k_base (js-tiktoken 1.0.21) after the docs went back to the `!` form: the README's program is 116 tokens against 202 for Effect TypeScript, 42.6% fewer (117 and 42.1% under D308); the 46 cheat-sheet pairs save 11.4% (10.9%), and their Effect types entry 28.4% (19.8%); the skill is 4,772 tokens (4,787). Not breaking: everything that compiles on 0.0.9 compiles the same, and code written for 0.0.6 with the shorthand compiles again. Evidence: cases `terse-contracts` (each `!` form that 0.0.8 reported as EK1014, compiled and run), `effect-type-spellings` (the `Effect<…>` spellings `--fix` wrote, compiled and run), `effect-type-defaults` and `effect-type-defaults.fail` (`Effect<A>` and `Effect<A, E>` check left-out channels as `never`; `infer`; service members), `contract-spellings-mixed` (both spellings in one file, a service implemented across them), and `tsforms-types-fixed` (EK4833's fix).

## The platform standard library (2026-10-26)

**D312. The entry file names its platform; the entry provides its layer** (amends D135 and D122; amended by D387; decided by D375: the platform import is the final form, the explicit trigger chosen over an implicit default). The file that exports `main` names its platform once, with a platform import at its top: `import "vibelang/platform/node"`, `"vibelang/platform/bun"` or `"vibelang/platform/browser"`. When `main` needs services that the named platform's bundle provides, its entry statement provides the bundle around it: `runtime.$entry(import.meta.main, import.meta.url, main, () => NodeRuntime.runMain(Effect.provide(main(), NodeServices.layer)))`. `node` names `@effect/platform-node`'s `NodeServices`, `bun` `@effect/platform-bun`'s `BunServices`, and `browser` `@effect/platform-browser`'s `BrowserServices`, which Effect 4.0 does not declare; the program resolves the named platform's modules as implicit imports of the entry module, as D135 resolves `@effect/platform-node`. A bundle's services are the `Out` of its `layer`'s declared type, read from the installed declarations, not from a list in the compiler. When `main` needs a service that only the platform's experimental bundle provides (D313: `ExperimentalNodeServices` from `vibelang-runtime/experimental/platform-node`, `ExperimentalBunServices` from `vibelang-runtime/experimental/platform-bun`, `ExperimentalBrowserServices` from `vibelang-runtime/experimental/platform-browser`), the entry provides that bundle instead of Effect's, since its layer holds Effect's bundle and declares its services; Effect's comes outside it only when `main` needs a service the experimental bundle does not declare or its layer needs services itself. (Amended 2026-10-27: the entry provided both, `Effect.provide(Effect.provide(main(), ExperimentalNodeServices.layer), NodeServices.layer)`, which built the platform's services twice. Evidence: case `entry-platform-experimental-only`'s `node-mixed`.) A `provide` inside `main` wins for the code inside it, as `Effect.provide` does. The import loads nothing: the compiler removes it from the JavaScript and the declarations, and TypeScript's TS2882 for the unresolved specifier is not reported. The runner is D135's: `NodeRuntime` when `@effect/platform-node` resolves, else the runtime's copy. (Amended 2026-10-27: a file that names the browser runs `main` with `@effect/platform-browser`'s `BrowserRuntime.runMain` when that resolves, else the runtime's copy, and resolves and imports nothing of Node's; before, it imported `NodeRuntime` whenever `@effect/platform-node` resolved, and Vite warned about externalized Node modules. A page never says which module it started, so `$entry` runs nothing in a browser; the page's script starts `main` with a top-level `BrowserRuntime.runMain(Effect.provide(main(), layer))`, and a host that says, such as Bun, runs it with the browser's runner. Evidence: case `entry-platform-browser`, which runs the page in Chromium.) Without a platform import nothing changes: the entry provides no layer, and EK3205 names the services `main` needs and, for those `@effect/platform-node`'s bundle provides, the import that would provide them; with a platform import whose package does not resolve, it says the package is not installed. EK3205 names only the services the bundles do not provide, and for each of Effect's and the experimental services the layer that EK4402 names for it, and without a platform import its hint counts the experimental services the Node bundle provides (amended 2026-10-27; it said only `provide layer { … }`, and the hint left out `Experimental.Process`; evidence: case `ek3205-layers.fail`); when `main` needs both kinds, the entry still provides the bundle, so a program run despite the warning fails at start naming a service EK3205 named. Tests never run the entry statement (`import.meta.main` is false in a module a test imports): `it.effect` provides its own layers, and EK3201 names what it leaves out. Effect's canonical entry is `NodeRuntime.runMain(main.pipe(Effect.provide(NodeServices.layer)))`; the compiler now writes it. A file names one platform: a platform import that names a second, different platform is error EK3206 (added 2026-10-27; before, the first import silently won). The trigger is one function, `namedPlatform` in `entry.go`, so the alternatives still in the owner's queue, a library call such as `export default Node.run(main)` or a `"vibelang": { "platform": "node" }` setting, replace it without touching how the layer is provided. Rejected for now: an implicit default that picked the platform by which package resolves (the owner prefers the platform named in the program). The ts-mapper projection, which cannot resolve packages, still runs `main` without a layer and keeps the import. Evidence: cases `entry-platform-layer` (the import with no provide, with an inner provide, and with a `main` that needs nothing, whose JavaScript and declarations carry no trace of the import), `entry-platform-layer-bun` (the import picks Bun with both packages installed), `entry-platform-layer-experimental` and `entry-platform-layer-services.fail` (EK3205 beside the import, without it, and with an uninstalled platform; EK3201 in a test); `examples/file-tree` and the platform guide's snippets, run with the installed `@effect/platform-node` and `@effect/platform-bun`.

**D310. Every Effect namespace is global** (extends D146; amends D264; decided by D375). Every module of `effect`'s subpath packages is in scope in a `.vibe` file without an import, under its module name, as the root's modules are (D146): `HttpRouter`, `HttpClient`, `ChildProcess`, `ChildProcessSpawner`, `TestClock`, `Hex`, `SqlClient`, and the rest of the 232 modules that the 21 subpaths of effect@4.0.6's `exports` map list (`effect/ai`, `cli`, `cluster`, `devtools`, `encoding`, `eventlog`, `graphql`, `http`, `http-api`, `net`, `observability`, `persistence`, `process`, `reactivity`, `rpc`, `schema`, `socket`, `sql`, `testing`, `workers`, `workflow`). The owner asked to "make Effect namespace global and every namespace below Effect global"; D146's reason that subpath names "belong to one kind of program" lost to the token cost of the imports. When two globals share a name, in order:

1. A JavaScript or shared web global wins, as D264 rules: `Headers` is the web's, not `effect/http`'s, and `Response` the web's, not `effect/ai`'s. `Event` joins D264's shared web globals, because every runtime VibeLang targets defines it, so `effect/eventlog`'s `Event` needs an import.
2. A root module wins over a subpath module: `Utils` is `effect/Utils`, not `effect/rpc`'s.
3. When two subpath packages each have a module of the name, neither is in scope: `Model` (`effect/ai`, `effect/schema`) and `Prompt` (`effect/ai`, `effect/cli`). TS2304 on one names both imports.
4. A local declaration or an explicit import shadows any of them.

DOM-only globals lose, as in D146: `Worker` is `effect/workers`' module, and the DOM's is `globalThis.Worker`. The DOM declares `Transferable` only as a type, which keeps its meaning in a type. The guide's page on Effect's modules lists these collisions; `TestEffectNameCollisionsDocumented` computes them from the installed package and TypeScript's lib files, and fails when either changes.

A file that uses a subpath module's name resolves its package, such as `effect/http`, as an implicit import, and the checker resolves the name to the package's export; a member name, such as the `Message` of `x.Message`, loads nothing. The emitted JavaScript imports each module from its own file (`import * as HttpRouter from "effect/http/HttpRouter"`), and a declaration file imports a module a type names from its package (`import type { HttpRouter } from "effect/http"`). Files below a module's own directory, such as `effect/schema/SchemaJITCompiler/enable`, are parts of that module, which no index lists, and are not in scope. Platform packages (`@effect/platform-node`, `-bun`, `-browser`) stay imports: they are not below Effect, and platform code belongs behind injection. Completions offer a subpath module once a file of the program loads its package, since the program has no declarations of a package no file loads; until then the editor's auto-import offers it with an import, which compiles and which `--fix` removes. `effectSubpathNames` is generated from the installed package's `exports` map and indexes, and `TestEffectSubpathNamesMatchEffect` fails when Effect's exports change.

Cost, on a generated 300-module project that uses only root names (`vibelang -p` with emit, median of 9 runs on the shared build server): 1,123 ms on 0.0.10 and 1,086 ms with D310, with the same 572 files; a program that uses a subpath module loads its package, 7 more declaration files for `effect/http` than for `effect/http/HttpRouter` alone and 52 more for `effect/cluster`. Breaking only where a `.vibe` file in a project whose `lib` includes the DOM used the DOM's `Worker` by its bare name. Evidence: cases `vibelang-subpath-names` (`Hex`, `ChildProcess`, `TestClock` without imports, run; a web global and a local declaration win; the declaration file's import) and `diag-shared-subpath-name.fail` (`Model`'s TS2304 and its note); Go tests `TestEffectSubpathNamesMatchEffect`, `TestEffectNameCollisionsDocumented`, `TestDeclarationImportsSubpathModule` (stock TypeScript accepts the declaration file) and `TestEditorSubpathModules` (completions, hover, definition).

The owner's wording, recorded 2026-10-26: each Effect module's namespace is global under its module name, the root's modules as before and the modules inside Effect's folders, such as `effect/process/ChildProcess`, `effect/http/HttpClient`, and `effect/testing/TestClock`; a module's members are never flattened into the global scope: `HttpClient` is global, `HttpClient.get` is not. The three real clashes in effect@4.0.5 keep the tiebreak: `Utils` is the root's, and `Model` and `Prompt` stay imports.

**Platform code leaves `.vibe` scope** (part of D310, the owner's ruling of 2026-10-26; amends D108; decided by D375). Node.js is available in a `.vibe` file only through dependency injection, Effect's services, or by escaping to TypeScript: a hatch, a `module` block, or a `.ts` file. So:

- Node's globals (`process`, `Buffer`, `require`, `module`, `exports`, `__dirname`, `__filename`, `global`) and the web globals that do I/O or read the clock or randomness (`fetch`, `XMLHttpRequest`, `EventSource`, `WebSocket`, `setTimeout`, `setInterval`, `setImmediate` and their clears, `queueMicrotask`, `console`, `crypto`, `performance`, `navigator`, `localStorage`, `sessionStorage`, `indexedDB`, `caches`, `BroadcastChannel`, `SharedWorker`, `importScripts`, the DOM's `document`, `window`, `self`, `location`, `history`, `alert`, `confirm`, `prompt`, animation and idle callbacks, the global event listener functions, `Bun`, and `Deno`) are not in scope. EK4402 now stands at every use, a value held included, where D108 reported only a call, a `new`, a tagged template, or a property read. `Worker` is Effect's `effect/workers` module (D310).
- A value import of a Node.js or Bun built-in module, `node:*` or a bare name such as `fs`, is EK4402 at each value it binds, and an import for its effects at the module; `import type` stays allowed, and so does an import in a `module` block, whose body is TypeScript. `NodeHttpServer.layer(createServer, { port })` takes a `createServer` that a `module` block re-exports.
- ECMAScript's built-ins stay (`Array`, `Map`, `JSON`, `Math`, `Promise`, `Symbol`, `Date`, `Intl`), with EK4403 for `Date.now()`, `new Date()`, and `Math.random()`, and so do the web's pure helpers, which touch nothing outside the program: `URL`, `URLSearchParams`, `TextEncoder`, `TextDecoder`, `structuredClone`, `AbortController`, `AbortSignal`, `Blob`, `FormData`, `Headers`, `Request`, `Response`, `Event`, `EventTarget`, `atob`, `btoa`, and the web streams. `AbortSignal` stays because `try(async (input, signal) => …)` hands one to TypeScript. `.ts` files keep every global.
- One EK4402 per site says it is platform code and names what replaces it, from one table (`platform.go`): an Effect API first, which needs no import (D310, D311), then VibeLang's experimental service (D313: `Experimental.Process`, `.FileSystem`, `.Crypto`, `.Locks`), then the escape to TypeScript with the reason. The guide's page on platform globals (`docs/user/src/platform/platform-globals.md`) lists the same answers, row by row, and `TestPlatformGlobalsDocumented` fails when a global or a member the table answers has no row, or a row says something else, or a global has no answer; `docs/tools/platform-globals.test.ts` fails when a row's example is not in a block that the snippet checker compiles.
- Because Node's names are gone from `.vibe` scope, Effect's module names never collide with them: `Crypto` is Effect's, and D311's `Crypto.randomUUIDv4()` applies.

Breaking: `.vibe` code that imports Node.js at its top level, holds a platform global as a value, or reads `process.env` and the like stops compiling. In the Smithers migration snapshot (1,623 `.vibe` files), 131 top-level value imports of `node:*` modules in 90 files, 71 of them tests (63 `node:path`, 27 `node:fs/promises`, 15 `node:os`, 7 `node:crypto`, 6 `node:child_process`, 6 `node:fs`, and 7 others), and 15 direct uses of removed globals outside hatches and module blocks, most of them `globalThis.crypto`. The release notes say how to fix each kind. Evidence: cases `platform-scope.fail` (each kind of use and import, and the escapes that stay allowed), `platform-values` (a `module` block hands Node values to `.vibe` code, run), `ek4402-platform.fail`, `ek4402-services.fail`, `globalthis-platform.fail`; Go tests `TestPlatformReplacementMessages`, `TestPlatformServicesTable`, `TestPlatformDocsTable`, and `TestPlatformGlobalsDocumented`.

**D311. A module's name also names its export of the same name** (extends D41, D90 and D310; decided by D375; amended by D369: the aliases and the exports-win pairs come from a pinned table). The owner's ruling: for every global module `M` with an export also named `M`, the bare name aliases that export, a deliberate change from TypeScript. Effect names many modules after their main export: the module `Option` exports the type `Option.Option`, `Stream` the type `Stream.Stream`, and `FileSystem` the service key `FileSystem.FileSystem`. Where the module has no meaning of its own, its name stands for that export:

- In a type, `M` is `M.M`: `Option<number>` is `Option.Option<number>`, `Stream<A>` is `Stream.Stream<A>`, `(fs: FileSystem) => …` takes the service. A type that an ECMAScript lib file or a shared web standard declares globally under the name keeps it, as D264 rules for values; DOM-only types lose their bare names, as DOM-only values do (D146): `Cache`, `Console`, `Crypto`, `FileSystem`, `Scheduler`, `Transferable` and `Worker` in effect@4.0.6.
- `M.member`, where the module exports no `member`, resolves through `M.M`. In a `.vibe` function body, when `M.M` is a service key whose service declares the member, it is the service member call `M.M.member(...)` (D41, D90), as before: `FileSystem.readFileString(path)`, `Path.join(dir, name)`, `Crypto.randomUUIDv4()`, `ChildProcessSpawner.string(ChildProcess.make("git", ["status"]))`, `Stdio.args()`. Otherwise it reads the export's own member: `FileSystem.of(implementation)` is the key's `of`. A value member of the service read without a call is EK4503, as `Users.region` is on a key: `Path.sep` says to read `Path().sep`, and `Terminal.columns`, a program as Effect declares it, adds that `Terminal.columns()` runs it (revised 2026-10-27; it was TS2339 on the module's type).
- `M()` is `M.M()`, the implementation, when `M.M` is a service key, in a function body, as D41's `Users()` is.
- The module's exports win: `Option.some`, `FileSystem.layerNoop`, `Path.layer`, `Console.log` and `Clock.currentTimeMillis` keep their meaning, and code that writes `Option.Option<A>` compiles as before. A key as a value stays explicit: `Layer.succeed(FileSystem.FileSystem, implementation)`.
- The rule holds for every Effect module the file reaches by its own name, and reads the declarations, not a list: a module the prelude supplies, a module of a namespace the prelude supplies (D313's `Experimental`: `Experimental.Process.hostname()` calls the `Process` service, `Experimental.Process()` is its implementation, and the type `Experimental.Process` is the service), and a module imported from `effect` under its name: after `import { Prompt } from "effect/ai"`, which D310 requires because two subpath packages have a `Prompt`, the type `Prompt` is `Prompt.Prompt`. The checker gains one hook for the qualified form, `RegisterQualifiedNameResolutionHook`, which resolves `X.M` in a type where X exports a module named M and no type.
- The emitted JavaScript writes the export: `(yield* FileSystem.FileSystem).readFileString(path)`, `FileSystem.FileSystem.of(implementation)`. A declaration file writes the type `Option` as `Option.Option` and imports the module (`import type { Option } from "effect"`, `import type { Prompt } from "effect/ai"`), which stock TypeScript accepts; an inferred type prints as `import("effect/Option").Option<number>`.
- Completions after `FileSystem.` list the module's exports, the export's members, and in a function body the service's members; hover and go to definition on `FileSystem.readFileString` reach the service member's declaration.
- `vibelang --fix` (D291) writes what D310 and D311 make shorter, after the quick fixes and before it removes unused imports: it removes an import of an Effect module under its own name, from the module, effect's root, or its subpath package, and an import of a module's export of the module's own name from the module, unless a JavaScript or shared web global takes the name; a use the module's name cannot stand for becomes `M.M`, and an export of the binding keeps the import. It writes `M.M` as `M` where D311 gives `M` that meaning, except `Effect.Effect<…>`, which EK4833 writes in the `!` form. A file's rewrites are kept only when the program reports no new error. On ten Smithers migration files with platform services, it made 59 rewrites and cut 189 of 10,409 tokens (1.8%); a second run finds nothing. Go test `TestFixPreludeRewrites`.

Tokens (o200k_base, js-tiktoken 1.0.21), with D310: reading a config file through `FileSystem` and `Path` is 25 tokens, against 46 with named service imports and 28 with `FileSystem.FileSystem.readFileString(Path.Path.join(...))`; running `git status` is 29 against 57; a UUID is 12 against 22. Breaking: in a type in a `.vibe` file, the DOM types listed above lose their bare names to Effect's; to name one, alias it in a `.ts` file. A value gains meaning only where it was TS2339 (a member the module lacks) or TS2349 (calling a namespace). The experimental content mapper (`internal/vibelangmapper`, `packages/ts-mapper`) implements neither D310 nor D311 yet. Evidence: cases `experimental-module-services` (`Experimental.Process.pid()`, `Experimental.Crypto.hmac`, `Experimental.Process()` and the type `Experimental.Process` with no import, run; `Experimental.Process.layerTest`), `module-services` (each call above, `Terminal.readLine()`, `Crypto.randomBytes`, `(fs: FileSystem)`, `FileSystem()`, `FileSystem.layerNoop` and `Path.layer`, run under test layers; the declaration file) and `module-aliases` (`Option<number>`, `Chunk<number>`, `Option.Option<number>`, an imported `Prompt` as a type and its `ast` through the export, `FileSystem.of`, run; the declaration file); Go tests `TestModuleServiceDeclarations` (stock TypeScript accepts `FileSystem.FileSystem`, `Control.FileSystem.FileSystem`, `Option.Option<number>` and the imported `Prompt.Prompt`; a type parameter named `FileSystem` shadows), `TestModuleServiceThroughPreludeNamespace` (a module inside a prelude namespace: member call, `M()`, exports), `TestEditorModuleServices` and `TestModuleServicesDocumented` (the guide's lists of service modules and of DOM types that lose their names, computed from the declarations).


## Experimental platform APIs (2026-10-26)

**D313. `Experimental` holds what Effect lacks** (decided by D375; part 1: the namespace, `Experimental.FileSystem` and `Experimental.Crypto`; part 2, continued: `Experimental.Process` and `Experimental.Locks`). The owner's rules: platform APIs are wrapped, effectful and injected; VibeLang implements nothing that Effect has; what Effect lacks goes under `Experimental`. Each addition lives in `vibelang-runtime/experimental/<Effect module name>`. Where Effect has the module, the addition extends it: an implementation interface, `Service`, a superset of Effect's, under its own key (`vibelang/experimental/FileSystem`), `PlatformError` failures, Effect's member names, and a test layer. The key is a class service, as Effect declares one (`class FileSystem extends Context.Service<FileSystem, Service>()("vibelang/experimental/FileSystem")`), so the type `FileSystem`, the requirement, is not Effect's. The first 0.0.11 candidate used the implementation interface as the key's type too; since it extends Effect's, Effect's types (whose `Exclude` is assignability) let a layer of Effect's `FileSystem` satisfy the experimental requirement, and the entry (D312) chose Effect's bundle for it, while the keys differ at run time: a program compiled and then died with `Service not found: vibelang/experimental/FileSystem`, under VibeLang and stock TypeScript alike. The entry now also matches a needed service to a bundle's by identity, assignable both ways, so a service whose type only extends a bundle's is not that bundle's. Evidence: cases `entry-platform-experimental-only` (Crypto-only and FileSystem-only mains under the node and bun imports, run on Node and Bun), `entry-platform-experimental-standard.fail` (Effect's bundle leaves the experimental requirement: EK2002, EK3205) and `entry-platform-layer-experimental` (a service whose type extends Effect's `FileSystem` gets the experimental bundle); the runtime's `experimental-identity.test.ts` (stock `tsc` rejects the `R = never` claim) and `experimental-consumer.ts`. Where Effect has none, as for `Process` and `Locks`, the addition is a new service in the same style, as `Stdio` is: readonly members, a `TypeId`, a `Context.Service` key (`vibelang/experimental/Process`, `vibelang/experimental/Locks`), a `make` constructor, `PlatformError` failures, and a `layerTest(partial)` with fixed defaults. The aggregate `vibelang-runtime/experimental` exports each module as a namespace, and the runtime's root exports the aggregate as `Experimental`, a prelude name (D4), so a `.vibe` file reaches `Experimental.FileSystem` without an import, a local declaration or import of the name shadows it, and the emitted JavaScript imports it from the runtime like the other prelude names. A file may instead import a service by name from its module's subpath, which shadows the prelude's Effect module of that name (D146); Effect's members, `lstat` among them, keep working on the experimental service because its interface is a superset. Per-platform layers live in `vibelang-runtime/experimental/platform-node`, `-bun` and `-browser`. A layer of a service that extends Effect's provides the experimental service and Effect's service of the same name from one implementation, the Effect platform package's own with the additions, so Effect's users of a service, such as `Logger.toFile`, and the experimental one share it. Platform-neutral modules import only `effect`; `node:` modules appear only under the Node and Bun layers; `@effect/platform-node`, `-bun` and `-browser` 4.0.6 are optional peer dependencies of the runtime. A program that does not use `Experimental` carries none of it: the service keys are marked pure, so a browser bundle of the runtime drops them.

- `Experimental.FileSystem` adds `access(path, { executable })` (`X_OK`; on Windows existence only, as Node's), `open(path, { nonBlocking })` (`O_NONBLOCK`; `BadArgument` where the platform lacks it, as `noFollow` fails) and `File.chmod(mode)` (`fchmod`). The proposal also listed `lstat`, but Effect 4.0.6, which VibeLang now follows, has `FileSystem.lstat`, so the experimental service has Effect's. Every other member, and every open without `nonBlocking`, is Effect's own; `File.chmod` on Effect's file reads the descriptor that Effect's Node file keeps. Effect does not export the helpers a nonblocking open needs, so the Node and Bun implementation copies `@effect/platform-node-shared` 4.0.6's file class, its `makeFileInfo`, the open flags and the errno mapping. Browsers have no layer, as in Effect. `layerNoop` provides both keys from Effect's `makeNoop`, with `open` failing with `NotFound`.
- `Experimental.Crypto` adds `hmac(algorithm, key: Redacted<Uint8Array>, data)` (raw key; Effect's digest algorithms), `sign("ECDSA-P256-SHA256" | "Ed25519", privateKey: Redacted<Uint8Array>, data)` (PKCS#8 DER) and `verify(algorithm, publicKey, signature, data)` (SPKI DER; `false` for a signature that does not match). One WebCrypto implementation serves every platform: `globalThis.crypto.subtle`, read when the service is made. ECDSA signatures are WebCrypto's r‖s, 64 bytes. A key of the wrong kind fails with `BadArgument`, whose message names no key bytes; without WebCrypto the members fail with `Unknown`, as Effect's browser `digest` does. `layerTest` provides both keys with zero random bytes and real digests and signatures.
- `Experimental.Process` is one service, since Effect 4.0.6 has none: `ChildProcessSpawner` signals only the children it spawned, through their handles; `Stdio` reads the arguments and nothing reads the process id, platform or host name; `Clock` has no time origin. `pid`, `platform`, `arch`, `execPath`, `versions`, `hostname`, `availableParallelism`, `timeOrigin` and `memoryUsage` are Effects that cannot fail, as `Stdio.args` is, so a test fixes them; `kill(pid, { killSignal, group })` sends Effect's `ChildProcess.Signal`, `"SIGTERM"` by default as `KillOptions` does; `isRunning(pid, { group })` sends signal 0. `kill` fails with `NotFound` (ESRCH) and `PermissionDenied` (EPERM); both fail with `BadArgument` for a pid that is not a positive integer (0 and negative ids name this process's group or every process, so a group is `{ group: true }`, never a negative pid), for a signal the platform lacks, and for `group` on Windows. `isRunning` answers true for EPERM, since the process exists, and for a child not yet reaped. `ExperimentalNodeProcess.layer` and `ExperimentalBunProcess.layer` are one implementation over the host's `process` object and `node:os`, read through `process.getBuiltinModule`, so the service needs no `@types/node`. Browsers and Workers get no layer: the facts are Effects that cannot fail, so a browser layer would have to die for `pid` or `execPath`, and a layer with half its members failing is a surprise, not a service. `layerTest` defaults to pid 1 on `"linux"`, a host with no other process (`isRunning` false, `kill` NotFound). The proposal's `homeDirectory` is left out: Effect 4.0.6 added `UserDirectories.home` (`effect/persistence/UserDirectories`), which reads `HOME`, or `USERPROFILE` on Windows, through the `ConfigProvider` and fails with `NotFound` when it is unset, where `os.homedir()` falls back to the user database.
- `Experimental.Locks` ports Smithers' `WebLock`, its proven code kept, since nothing in Effect locks across tabs or threads: `hold(name, { steal })` and `tryHold(name)` hold an exclusive Web Lock in the caller's scope, an interrupted wait is withdrawn, a lock granted in the moment of the interruption is given back, and `Held.lost` completes when another context steals the lock. The changes are the D313 signatures: a refused request fails with `PlatformError` instead of dying (`NotSupportedError` is `BadArgument`, `SecurityError` `PermissionDenied`, `InvalidStateError` `BadResource`, others `Unknown`); `query` is new and reads `LockSnapshot { held, pending }`, filling what the lock manager leaves out of a `LockInfo`; `make` takes the lock manager. `ExperimentalBrowserLocks.layer` is `navigator.locks`, across one origin's tabs and workers. Node.js has its own `navigator.locks`, which coordinates the threads of one process and never other processes, so `ExperimentalNodeLocks.layer` exists for code that locks within a process and is not in `ExperimentalNodeServices.layer`, where a program would take it for a cross-process lock. Bun has no Web Locks and no layer. A layer built where `navigator.locks` is missing (Bun, an insecure page) is a defect that says so. `layerTest` grants every request at once and never loses a lock.
- EK4402 names them, with their layers, where each was a gap shown in a hatch: `fs.constants.X_OK` and `O_NONBLOCK` name `Experimental.FileSystem`; `crypto.createHmac`, `createSign`, `createVerify`, `sign`, `verify` and `crypto.subtle` name `Experimental.Crypto`; `os.hostname`, `os.availableParallelism`, `os.platform`, `os.arch`, `process.pid`, `platform`, `arch`, `execPath`, `versions`, `memoryUsage` and `kill`, and `performance.timeOrigin` name `Experimental.Process`; and `navigator.locks` names `Experimental.Locks`. `lstat` names Effect 4.0.6's own `FileSystem.lstat`, and `os.homedir` its `UserDirectories.home()`. A gap with no service, such as `os.uptime()`, still shows the hatch. The messages write the short form with no import, `Experimental.Process.hostname()`: D311 makes the namespace's module `Process` name its key, `Experimental.Process.Process`.
- Layers: `experimental/platform-node` holds `ExperimentalNodeFileSystem`, `ExperimentalNodeCrypto`, `ExperimentalNodeProcess`, `ExperimentalNodeLocks` and `ExperimentalNodeServices`, `-bun` the same without `ExperimentalBunLocks`, and `-browser` `ExperimentalBrowserCrypto`, `ExperimentalBrowserLocks` and `ExperimentalBrowserServices`. `ExperimentalNodeServices.layer` and `ExperimentalBunServices.layer` are the Effect bundles plus `FileSystem`, `Crypto` and `Process`, so one layer in `main` provides Effect's platform services and the experimental ones; `ExperimentalBrowserServices.layer` is `ExperimentalBrowserCrypto.layer` plus `ExperimentalBrowserLocks.layer`, since `@effect/platform-browser` has no bundle. (Amended 2026-10-27: every export of the experimental platform modules starts with `Experimental`. `NodeServices`, `NodeCrypto`, `NodeFileSystem`, `BunServices`, `BunCrypto`, `BunFileSystem` and `BrowserCrypto` had the names of exports of Effect's platform packages, so a file that imported both needed an alias, and EK4402 named both kinds of layer under one name.)
- Lifetime: each addition has an Effect patch prepared (not opened). When Effect releases a member, the experimental module re-exports Effect's, then a later VibeLang release deletes it.

Rejected: separate `vibelang-platform-*` packages (more installs for the same code); module-level accessor functions, Effect 3's style; a fork of Effect's open for every open (only `nonBlocking` needs the copy); importing each experimental module explicitly with no prelude name (the owner put additions under a global `Experimental`); an experimental fork of Effect's child process spawner for a child's exit signal, which goes to Effect as a patch only; a separate `Os` service for the host facts (two layers and two test fakes for one platform's facts); `hold(name, effect)`, which runs an effect while holding the lock (a scoped `hold` composes with `using` and is the proven `WebLock` shape; `Effect.scoped` gives the other); an in-process lock manager written for Bun (new code that would reimplement Web Locks' steal, `ifAvailable` and query semantics); a negative pid for a process group, as `process.kill` takes (one sign error signals a group); and bundles of the experimental services alone, beside Effect's (a program would provide two bundles, and import one under another name). Evidence: the runtime's tests run `FileSystem` and `Crypto` through every layer on Node and Bun against the real file system (execute bits, a FIFO opened without blocking, a refused final symbolic link, `fchmod` after a rename, unsupported platforms) and against known vectors (RFC 4231 HMAC-SHA-256 and HMAC-SHA-512, RFC 8032 test 1 for Ed25519, RFC 6979 A.2.5 for ECDSA P-256), bundle the browser layer, and compile a stock TypeScript consumer of the declarations; `experimental-process.test.ts` spawns children with Effect's `ChildProcessSpawner` under Bun and under Node.js and checks every fact, `kill`, `killSignal`, groups and each failure; `experimental-locks.test.ts` runs every case of Smithers' `WebLock` suite in Chromium (Playwright) and under Node.js, contends across Chromium tabs (wait, inherit when the holder's tab closes, steal), shows Node.js's locks exclude a worker thread but not another process, and keeps fake lock managers only for refusals and the interrupted-grant race; case `experimental-platform` runs `FileSystem` and `Crypto` from a `.vibe` program through the prelude's `Experimental` and an explicit import, with its declarations, and cases `experimental-process` and `experimental-locks` use the other two.

## Calls work as a TypeScript developer expects (2026-10-27)

A review of the specification on 2026-10-27 found that a call's meaning depended on its parameter's type, the overload TypeScript tried, a binding's annotation, assignability at a return, and in places a name's spelling, and that the specification had no general rule for evaluation order, generated names, or lexical context. These decisions replace those rules with one principle and three laws. Each records what it supersedes.

**D320. Calls work as a TypeScript developer expects** (supersedes D147, D155, D257, D262, D265 and D269; amends D7, D24, D166, D187, D196, D236 and D267). The owner's words, 2026-10-27: "calls should work exactly how a typescript dev intuitively expect". Chapter 13 states it as the governing rule for calls. A call does its work where it stands. Its callee and arguments are evaluated once, left to right, before the call, as in JavaScript. Its value is what the callee returns; for an effectful callee that is the success value, read as if the call had an implicit `await`. Delaying work means writing a function, `() => f(x)`, as in TypeScript. No type, annotation, union, overload or name spelling changes when, whether or how often code runs: a type may reject a program, never change what it does. Every rule of Part II is held to one test: if a TypeScript developer reading a call would be surprised by when or how often something runs, the rule is wrong. This is the design the review called option B, after Kotlin's suspending functions: the one place a type decides is whether a call runs, read from the callee's declared return type (D321), and every program is built by syntax (D322). To TypeScript, every VibeLang function is still a function that returns a cold `Effect` (D332). Reason: D147 passed an argument by name when its parameter's declared type was an `Effect`, so the parameter's type, not the call site, decided whether `fetchUser(id)` ran once at the call or once per retry. Its operands were evaluated after later arguments (`Effect.retry(eff(mark("a")), Schedule.recurs(mark("b")))` traced `b,a,run`, where Effect TypeScript gives `a,b,run`) and on every attempt (`retry(failUntil(i++), Schedule.recurs(5))` left `i` at 3, where Effect TypeScript evaluates `i++` once), while Chapter 13 promised left-to-right, once-only arguments; and overload resolution had to rank candidates by evaluation strategy (D257). Cost: arrows around single calls were 2.6% of the docs' tokens in D147's census; the README program is unchanged at 116 tokens. Rejected: keeping by-name passing but evaluating its operands once (the parameter's type would still decide whether a call runs); every call returning an unrun program with an explicit `run` (it reverses settled decision 1, results with no marker at each call, and adds a token to every running call); option B with explicit service lookup, `run p`, and adapters for Effect-returning TypeScript (each costs tokens, and adapters add friction to the TypeScript interop settled decision 7 requires). Prior art: Kotlin's suspending functions, JavaScript's `await`, and Koka's implicit effectful application.

**D321. A call runs when its callee's declared return type is definitely an Effect** (amends D24 and D68; replaces D24's "instantiated type" and its S22-3 and S5-1 amendments; keeps D270; confirmed by D378 and D379; amended by D393, D436, D437, D438, D445, D449 and D454). The signature TypeScript resolves for a call decides, read before its type parameters are instantiated. The call runs when that signature's return type is definitely an Effect: the `Effect` interface with any type arguments, written directly, as `A!E!R`, or through a type alias; a conditional type each of whose branches is definitely an Effect or `never`, as `Effect.all` (`All.Return`) and `Effect.retry` with options (`Retry.Return`) declare; or a union each of whose members is definitely an Effect (D270). A type parameter, `any`, `unknown`, an intersection, a union with another member, and an interface that extends `Effect` with structure of its own (`Config`, `Exit`, a service key, an error instance) make the call return data, as D24 had it. Calling a program value, `p()`, runs it. No expected type, annotation on a receiving binding, or instantiation enters the rule: `identity(program)` returns the program whatever `T` becomes. An optional call `f?.(x)` runs when `f` is present and its declared return type is definitely an Effect (D68), so D68 and D270 follow one rule: a function declared to return `Effect<A> | undefined` returns data, and a callee of type `(() => Effect<A>) | undefined` runs. Returning an Effect value never runs it: `return e` returns `e`'s value. Where the success type names no value (`void`, `unknown`, `any`, an unconstrained type parameter), returning an Effect value or a program is EK4802, now an error, and a call of `any` whose value an Effect function or a program returns is EK4409, now an error by default. Reason: the instantiated type made a generic helper behave differently at `T = Effect` (parametricity), and S22-3 made a `return` depend on assignability to the contract's A, so `return Exit.succeed(n)` returned an Exit as data under a written `unknown!!` and ran it under a contextual `Effect<unknown>`. A release action written `() => cleanupProgram` returned the program unrun with a warning, so the release never ran; it is now EK4802. Option B's statement named "a union" among what never decides; this decision reads it as a union that may hold a non-Effect, since every value of a union of Effects is an Effect, a TypeScript reader expects that call to run, and Effect's conditional return types need the same "every branch" reading. Rejected: the `Effect` interface alone, without conditional types (`Effect.all([…])` would return an unrun program).

**D322. Programs are built only by syntax** (supersedes D147, D155, D257, D262 and D265; amends D7, D49, D166, D187, D196, D236 and D267; amended by D377, D381 and D391). These forms build a program, and no others: a function literal; a block after a call (D188); `fork e`; an element of an array or object literal after `await.all`, `await.allSettled`, `await.race` or `await.any`; the left side of a catch expression; the blocks of `provide`, `uninterruptible`, `restore` and `interruptible`; a `for concurrent` body; and a hatch's first argument. Nothing else turns a call into a program: not a parameter's type, an element's or field's type, an overload, a declared `const`, or a pipe. A call that runs where the declared type at its destination, or an element or field type of it, is the `Effect` interface or a parameterless function returning one is EK1018, an error whose quick fix writes `() =>`, unless its success value is itself an Effect. EK1018 includes a call whose success type is `never`, so `race(Effect.fail(a), Effect.fail(b))`, D257's case, is an error instead of failing before `race` starts. D7's conversion stays, as a change of representation only: where the declared type at the destination is the `Effect` interface, or a type parameter whose constraint includes it and no function type, a parameterless function, written there or passed as a value, is passed as an `Effect` that calls it on each run, and collections convert element by element (D49, D69, D111). Building that `Effect` runs nothing. Consequences for earlier decisions: `fork(x())` with parentheses is the keyword with a parenthesized operand, a program by syntax, unless `fork` resolves to a binding the program declares (D166, D391); a `do` expression runs where it stands and builds a program only as an await element (D187, D196); a catch expression's left side is its protected region by syntax (D236); a tagged template follows D321 alone (D267); EK1001's exception for a typed top-level `const` (D262) is gone, and a module holds a program as a function, `export const migrate: void!E!R = () => { … }`, which the declared type converts. Language change: every by-name site is EK1018 with a quick fix, `retry(f(x), p)` to `retry(() => f(x), p)`, `Effect.all([a(), b()])` to `await.all [a(), b()]` or `all([() => a(), () => b()])`; a site changes silently only where the call's success value is itself an Effect, which is now passed after the call runs once. Evidence for the census and the cost: D147.

**D323. Annotations never hold a program or run a return** (supersedes D262, D265 and D269, and D24's S22-3 and S5-1 amendments; retires EK2115 and EK2116; confirmed by D379; amended by D390). A declared Effect type on a `const`, a field, or a function's return no longer holds a call or turns a returned function into the function's program. `const p: number!! = make()` runs `make()`; its success value does not fit, so it is EK1018, fixed by `const p = () => make()`. A function declared to return `number!!` returns a number: `(input): number!! => { const s = input.value; return () => s }` is TS2322, since a function is not a number. Work done when a function is called, with a program for later, is a plain function that returns a function, `function capture(input): () => number!! { … }`, which a VibeLang caller runs with `capture(input)()` and TypeScript sees as `(input) => () => Effect<number>`. A TypeScript API whose callers need the `Effect` itself, built when they call, is written in TypeScript, in a `.ts` file or a `module` declaration (D283). Reason: a factory's two phases depended on its annotation and on TypeScript rejecting the returned function as A, so deleting the annotation from a program that still type-checked changed what ran and when; an annotated `const b: number!! = make("b")` did not run where the unannotated `const a = make("a")` did; and D269's amendments had to rule separately on parentheses, assertions, `satisfies`, non-null assertions, conditionals and pipes. Factories also broke "every VibeLang function returns a cold `Effect` to TypeScript". Language change: each held initializer is EK1018 with the fix `() =>`; each factory is TS2322, fixed by one of the two rewrites above. D269's migration evidence counted 29 files that needed a factory; each keeps its TypeScript API by moving the factory into a `module` declaration, or changes its API to return a function.

**D324. A function literal is always a function; its form follows its destination's declared type** (supersedes D39; amends D9, D44, D56, D66, D112 and D113; amended by D380 and D381; amended by D392). A function literal, or a named function used as a value, is never turned into a call. What it compiles to depends only on the declared type at its destination: the parameter type of the signature TypeScript selects, a declared variable, field or return type, or a contextually typed object literal's field. A destination that returns a definitely-Effect type, or a contract-less callback parameter of a VibeLang function, takes the Effect form. Any other function type, `void`, `unknown`, `any` and type parameters included, takes the plain form, and a body that can pause, or whose `throw` can escape, is EK4301 (D101's defects unchanged). A return type that mixes an Effect with other types takes the plain form when the body performs no Effect operation and cannot fail, else the Effect form (D112); the destination declares both. The `Effect` interface, or a type parameter constrained to a type that includes it and no function type, converts a parameterless function to a program (D322). A type that includes both a function type and an Effect, as `HttpRouter.add`'s handler does, takes a function, never a program, whatever the body does. No destination: plain when Chapter 15 allows, else the Effect form. A type parameter that only the function itself fixes is no destination (D44's 2026-10-24 note). Overload resolution stays TypeScript's (D56): a function literal's type includes its contract, as an `async` function's type includes its `Promise`, so a candidate that asks for a plain function rejects one that can pause or fail. Resolution picks a signature and so a form, and never changes when a non-literal argument is evaluated. Reason: D39 converted a parameterless lambda to a program when its body could pause, so adding a `log` call to a health handler changed the value `HttpRouter.add` received from a function to an Effect, which the router tells apart at run time. Language change: a parameterless lambda that can pause, passed where a function or an Effect is accepted, is now passed as a function: `HttpRouter.add("GET", "/health", () => checkHealth())` gives the router a handler function, which it calls for each request with the same result. `Effect.withErrorReporting(() => serve())` converts its lambda as before, but its declared return type is a conditional with a function branch, so under D321 the call returns the wrapped program instead of running it: write `Effect.withErrorReporting(() => serve())()`. A discarded or wrongly returned program there is EK4801 or EK4802, so no such site changes silently.

**D325. A pipe's topic is its left side's value** (supersedes D155; amends D154 and D272; retires EK1008; amended by D383). `x |> body` evaluates `x` once, before anything in the body, and `%` is its value, wherever `%` stands. A topic that fills a parameter expecting a program is a value there, so `fetchUser(id) |> retry(%, policy)` is EK1018; the program is written `retry(() => fetchUser(id), policy)` or `retry(policy) { return fetchUser(id) }`, and several combinators around one program nest blocks (D188). D154's emitted shortcut, writing the left side in the topic's place, applies only where nothing in the body before the topic can observe evaluating the left side, so a body that reads `fn` after `source()` reassigns it calls the new function (D326). D272's curried stage keeps its typing and lowering, with the left side's value as the receiver. Reason: D155 let the parameter's type decide whether the left side ran once or per attempt, the context dependence D320 removes; a TypeScript reader reads `a |> f(%)` as `f(a)`. Language change: each by-name pipe is EK1018; `pipe(() => f(), stage, …)` (D110) still builds a program from Effect's data-last forms, recognized by symbol (D328). In two large codebases 1.1% and 2.6% of Effect pipes kept two or more stages (`docs/research/pipe-census.md`).

**D326. Evaluation order and binding time follow one law** (amends D10, D27, D154, D169, D196, D225, D228, D231, D232, D274 and D294). Chapter 13's rule E: every expression is evaluated in JavaScript's order, each operand once, and a call that runs runs at its place in that order. Its parts: operands left to right, the callee reference before the arguments, the receiver kept, a getter read once per written access (E1); a running call after its operands and before anything to its right, with no operand evaluated again after a pause (E2); `S.m(a)` looks the service up, reads the member, then evaluates the arguments, as `S().m(a)` (E3); short circuits test the success value and skip the unchosen side (E4); an optional chain stops before any argument (E5); a pipe's left side before its body (E6); a keyword form's own operands in the order written, before any program starts, so `for concurrent(n) (const x of xs)` evaluates `n` then `xs`, `wire(a(), b())` evaluates `a()` then `b()` and then builds in dependency order, `join` and a non-literal await operand read each entry once and use that one reading to wait and to interrupt, an await operation iterates its iterable when it starts on each run, and a hatch evaluates its function, mapper and inputs in that order (E7); a program evaluates nothing when built and its whole body on each run, with fresh locals and captured bindings read live (E8); the receiver and argument values bound per call, the parameters, defaults, destructuring and `arguments` per run (E9); building a program runs none of its work, which promises no sameness between runs (E10); and every compiled form, optimized or not, and every meaning-keeping automatic fix, produces the frame lowering's observations, or is not applied (E11). Chapter 13 tabulates when each form is evaluated. Reason: a compiler audit on 2026-10-27 found the shortcuts that broke these one at a time: a pipe substitution read the callee before the left side; trusted-call hoisting evaluated a later argument before a getter, and an optional call's argument whose callee was absent; a hatch's options object evaluated inputs before the mapper; `wire` permuted its argument expressions; the `for concurrent` head read the iterable before the limit; `join` read each getter twice and joined a different child than it interrupted; by-name `||` and `??` tested the program object instead of its success; an await operation iterated its operand once at construction and reused it on every run; and `using` read the disposal method again at release. One law with an equivalence clause replaces their patches. Service lookup before the arguments matches the emitted code and ECMAScript's member reference; looking up after them was considered and rejected, since `S.m(a)` is `S().m(a)`. Default parameters per run were measured on the current build. Prior art: ECMA-262 EvaluateCall (13.3.6.1) and Kotlin's suspending calls.

**D327. Lowering keeps JavaScript's lexical context** (amends D92, D104, D186, D187, D229 and D274; EK1019, EK1020). Chapter 13's rule L: `this`, `arguments`, `new.target`, `super`, private names, and a named function expression's own name keep their ECMAScript meaning through every generated function: a generator frame, a region closure, a thunk, a `do` or `throw` expression's arrow, a handler clause, a producer body, a pipe temporary, a plain variant. `super` keeps its home object and receiver in reads, writes, compound assignments and updates, with a computed key evaluated once; the bindings keep their identity through pauses and on every run. A `break` or `continue` that would leave a `do` expression, and a `var` inside one, are EK1019. A function declared in a `.vibe` file is not a constructor: `new` on one is EK1020. An enclosing function's `this` and `arguments` read in a hatch's function are EK4405, as any other enclosing binding is (D229). A form whose lowering cannot keep one of these meanings is a compile error that names it, never a different meaning; a throw expression in a parameter default throws when the default is evaluated, or is rejected, never skipped (D186). Reason: D92's amendment fixed `super` and `arguments` in methods one case at a time, and the known gaps listed region closures and producers that captured their own `arguments` and hatches that read an enclosing `this`; the audit found a named function expression whose name bound the generated generator, an arrow inside an Effect function that read a different `arguments`, `this` in a default and in a class's computed key, `new.target` dropped when a constructor's arrow moved into a generator, and `super` updates left in generated generators. Prior art: ECMA-262 10.2 and arrow functions' lexical bindings; TypeScript's downlevel `_this`, `_arguments`, `_newTarget` and `_super`. Language change: only `var` or a jump that leaves a `do` expression, and `new` on a VibeLang function, stop compiling.

**D328. Generated names are fresh, and features are recognized by resolved symbol** (amends D104, D110, D123, D148, D151, D152, D166, D208, D225 and D282; amended by D391). Chapter 13's rule H: every name the compiler introduces is distinct from every source binding where it is visible; a source identifier keeps its binding wherever lowering moves it; a reference to a runtime or Effect function goes through an import binding the compiler owns; no TypeScript identifier is reserved, so locals named `runtime`, `Effect_1`, `answer$direct`, `$match1`, `SyntaxError` or `Users_base` mean what they say; a rule that gives a name meaning applies only when the name resolves, through aliases and re-exports, to the declaration the rule names; keyword forms never consult scope; declaration files follow the same rules; and renaming a binding consistently never changes a program. So a hatch is recognized by symbol, not by "a name no scope declares" (D148); `pipe`'s program argument by symbol, through an alias (D110); a local `fork` or `join` no longer changes `fork x` or `join x` (the known gaps of D166 and D225), while `fork(x)` is the keyword unless `fork` resolves to a binding the program declares, which it then calls (D391); a local `Option` or `Number` is an ordinary matcher, not the built-in one (D208, for Chapter 3); and EK2004's test for a trusted export "named `...Sync` or `...OrThrow`" becomes a list of the resolved Effect exports whose contracts say they throw (D282). Reason: the audit found user bindings captured by generated `$direct` names, match temporaries, match intrinsics, concurrency helpers, a service's generated base class, and a builtin failure check, and a user `SyntaxError` parameter that captured the generated test; D303 recorded two `Effect_1` declarations in one file; D135's runner list matched spellings until its 2026-10-27 amendment.
