Prior art: Kohlbecker and others' hygienic expansion (1986), R7RS 4.3, Racket's sets of scopes, ECMAScript's ResolveBinding, and TypeScript's `createUniqueName`. Language change: only programs that depended on a capture or on a spelling change, and a local `fork` stops capturing the keyword.

**D329. EK4401 applies in every function a fiber can call** (amends D100 and D282; completes D5; amended by D399 and D400). A call of a plain TypeScript function that nothing trusts is EK4401 in every function of a `.vibe` file, a function with a plain return annotation included. Code no fiber can run stays exempt: a module's top-level statements, and a function literal written there as an argument to a TypeScript function, whose exceptions go to that TypeScript code (D100). A named function is never exempt, since a fiber may call it. A function value the program received, such as a parameter whose type TypeScript declares plain, is called under its type, and its exceptions are defects. D101's classification stays: a trusted call's listed failures are defects in plain code, since a plain function can carry no typed failure, and rejecting them would forbid `lines.map((l) => JSON.parse(l))`. Reason: D5 requires a declared trust level for calls to non-Effect code ("we don't know if it can throw"), and `function viaPlain(n: number): number { return risky(n) }` compiled while the same call in an Effect function was EK4401, so a plain annotation bypassed the boundary. Language change: plain functions that call untrusted TypeScript get EK4401; the fixes are a hatch, a `trusted(impl, [Errors])` value exported beside the TypeScript function (D249, D389), or a `module` declaration.

**D330. Hatches have one calling convention, decided by syntax** (amends D148, D230, D231 and D232; EK4410; amended by D396, D432 and D439). A call is a hatch when its callee resolves to the prelude's `sync`, `try` or `callback`, through any alias (D328); a hatch used other than as a callee is EK4410. `try`'s second argument is always the mapper, as the runtime's `try` already read it; to pass a function as the first input without a mapper, write `try(code, undefined, input)`. A hatch evaluates its first argument, then the mapper, then the inputs, once each (D326); a single-expression first argument is evaluated when the hatch runs, inside its boundary. A function written `async` is asynchronous: it is called with its inputs and then an `AbortSignal`, and its parameters must accept that call, which TypeScript checks. Any other function is synchronous and receives its inputs only; a result with a callable `then` is awaited without a signal. The function's declared return type plays no part. The runtime's `sync`, `try` and `callback`, which TypeScript code calls, follow the same convention, telling an `async` function by its kind. Reason: `const h = sync; h(f, 1)` passed an `AbortSignal` into `b?: number` where the direct call left it `undefined`; a declared return type with a `then` member chose the promise path for data such as `{ then: 7 }` and the synchronous path for `number | Promise<number>`, which then returned a Promise typed `number`; and the direct `try` built Effect's options object so that the inputs were evaluated before the mapper. Language change: `const run = sync` is EK4410; a function input in `try`'s second place is now its mapper, a type error when it does not fit; an `async` function whose parameters cannot take the signal is a type error; a function not written `async` gets no signal, and one that declares a parameter for it is a type error.

**D331. Callback polymorphism follows the language a function type is written in** (amends D12 and D68; amended by D398; superseded by D500). A contract-less function type written in a `.vibe` file accepts any contract wherever it types a callback, written in place or through a type alias or interface that a `.vibe` file declares: an alias means what it names. A function type that TypeScript declares, in a `.ts` file, a library, or a `module` declaration, keeps TypeScript's meaning, a plain function: `Predicate.Predicate<T>`, `LazyArg<A>`, or a type of the project's own declared in TypeScript is how a `.vibe` function asks for a plain callback. Reason: `f: (a: number) => number` accepted an effectful lambda while `f: Fn`, with `type Fn = (a: number) => number`, reported EK4301 on it, so extracting an alias, a refactor TypeScript treats as meaning nothing, changed which programs compiled; the review also proposed a plain marker in the type and explicit effect rows (Koka), both new syntax. Language change: a callback typed through a `.vibe` alias gains channels; a function that called such a callback synchronously, as a `sort` comparator, gets EK4301 and the fix of a TypeScript-declared type.

**D332. The TypeScript boundary has one table, and its trust is stated** (amends D67, D92, D121 and D268; amended by D384 and D389). Chapter 10 lists what each `.vibe` declaration is to TypeScript and when its work happens: an unannotated or Effect-contract function returns a cold `Effect`, keeping the receiver and argument values at the call and binding parameters afresh on each run; a plain-annotated function runs at once (D67); a plain function that returns a program runs at once and returns a function; a producer returns a `Stream`; a method has its annotation's type, or without one follows D92, so that protocols JavaScript reaches structurally (`toJSON`, `toString`) keep plain methods. A VibeLang consumer of declaration files behaves as a consumer of the source; a fact that would relax a foreign-code rule comes only from compiler metadata the consumer checks, and without it, as today, the function is foreign code; metadata that selects a faster form (D268) changes cost only. The E guarantee bounds `Fail` reasons of checked code, on assumptions Chapter 10 lists in full: true TypeScript declarations, complete error lists in trusted contracts, honest `any` and assertions, libraries calling callbacks as typed, and a right fiber table. Trust attaches to the declaration a call resolves to; the values Effect's exports return are trusted because Effect's version is pinned (D121), and values other trusted functions return are not. Reason: the review asked for the boundary's phases in one place, for declaration consumers to behave as source consumers, and for the trusted computing base to be named. Language change: none beyond D323's factories.

**D333. The plain-JavaScript form is an unobservable optimization** (amends D10, D54 and D260; amended by D412). A function compiles to plain JavaScript only where that form produces every observation of its frame form (rule E11). Its `try`, `catch` and `finally` mean what they mean in frame code (Chapter 17): a `finally` that fails while a failure is in flight adds its failure, through the runtime's combining helper, where JavaScript's `finally` would replace the first. A plain call cycle recurses as frames would: it starts on the JavaScript stack and continues on heap frames past a depth budget, and the budget's mechanics are the implementation's (`typescript/VIBELANG.md`); a stack overflow that still happens is a `RangeError` defect. A function with a plain return annotation is an ordinary JavaScript function with the host's stack limit. This states D10 once. Reason: Chapter 15 claimed JavaScript's statements matched the machine's because nothing inside could pause, and a probe of `try { o.a.b } finally { o.c.d }` reported one defect on the plain path and two on the frame path; D10, Chapter 15 and Chapter 29 described recursion three ways. Language change: a plain function whose `finally` fails during a failure reports both failures, as frame code does.

**D334. A block param is a program wherever it goes** (clarifies D188; superseded by D376). A block after a call is always the program `() => { … }`, and the call's written arguments are evaluated left to right before the call in every placement, so placement never changes when anything runs. Placement reads the callee's resolved declaration: a signature that takes a program first and the written arguments after it gives `f(block, args)`; else a signature that takes the written arguments and returns a function whose one parameter is a program gives `f(args)(block)`; else the block is the last argument. Blocks nest to wrap one program in several combinators. Reason: the review read D188's dual placement as type-dispatched evaluation; it dispatches only which parameter receives a program. Language change: none.

**D335. D309 stands; a written channel is what callers see** (amends D73). The `!` form's inference of R when R is left out, on a function with a body, is the owner's ruling (D309) and stays. Chapter 14 no longer calls the two spellings interchangeable: they name the same type wherever every channel is written, and differ by a visible token where R is left out, `User!NotFound` inferring it and `User!NotFound!` or `Effect<User, NotFound>` checking it as `never`. A call contributes its callee's contract as declared, channel by channel: a channel the contract writes is what callers see, even when the body needs less, and a channel it leaves to inference is the body's. Before, a contract with any inferred channel was not complete under D73, so callers of `number!Base` saw the body's `SubA | SubB` while callers of `number!Base!` saw `Base`. A recursive call runs, as every call does, so it has type A while the fixpoint runs, and only a generic recursive function needs every channel written (EK2102). Reason: settled decision 2 asks that inference never be silently confused with `never`; the difference here is a visible `!`. Language change: callers of a contract that writes E and leaves R see the written E, which may be wider.

**D336. Data-type methods come from a table pinned with Effect** (amends D152 and D295). Which functions are methods of a data type is a table that ships with the compiler: for each of D152's modules, the functions whose data-first overload takes the module's own type as its receiver, with D295's exclusions. It is generated from the declarations of the Effect version the runtime pins and changes only in a VibeLang release that changes that pin, checked by `TestDataFirstParametersMatchEffect`. A parameter's name plays no part in the rule; D152's `self` and `OrOptions` names and D295's receiver names are how the table was found, not what it means. A type's own member wins, and an Effect release that adds a member that shadows an entry is decided in the release that adopts it. Reason: a parameter's name is not part of a function's type, so renaming `self` in Effect would have removed a method, and D152 let a member that a later Effect release added take a call silently. Language change: none for the pinned Effect version.

## Failures, resources, and handlers (2026-10-27)

The spec review of 2026-10-27 found that Part II's failure, resource, and handler rules disagreed with the machine of Chapter 20 and with Effect, in places by design. These decisions make Part II follow the machine. Each names what it supersedes; Chapters 3, 6, 7, 9, 17, and 20 to 25 state the rules.

**D340. Cleanup combines failures the same way on every path** (2026-10-27; amends D15 and D46; supersedes D54's and D260's JavaScript `finally`). When cleanup fails with `f` while its region fails with `c`, the region fails with `c ⊕ f`: `c`'s reasons, then `f`'s, an equal reason listed once, and every `Fail` made a `Die` when a `Die` is present (D305). After a success or a jump, a failing cleanup's cause is the region's. This is the machine's `Final` rule and what `Effect.onExit` and `Effect.ensuring` do on `effect@4.0.6` (`combineFinalizerCause`, `internal/effect.ts:4012`). It binds `finally`, `finally (exit)`, `using` releases, Scope close, abandonment, fiber exit, and iterator close (D356), in every function, a function that compiles to plain JavaScript included. A plain function never fails, so only defects leave it as JavaScript exceptions; when cleanup adds a defect to a pending one, the exception that leaves is a `SuppressedError` (`error` the cleanup's, `suppressed` the pending one), as ECMA-262's `DisposeResources` reports two failures, and the runtime turns one it made back into the combined cause where it reaches an Effect frame. Reason: D15 said a cleanup failure is never dropped, while D54 compiled a plain function's `finally` to JavaScript's, which replaces the pending exception; the 2026-10-27 review and compiler audit reproduced the difference (`plain=Cleanup framed=First`). Prior art: Java 7's suppressed exceptions, Python 3's exception chaining, ECMAScript's `SuppressedError`. Language change, silent: a plain function's caller now sees the body's failure beside the cleanup's, and an outer `catch` receives the body's first failure.

**D341. Every lowering produces the frame lowering's Exit** (2026-10-27; amends D10, D274, D294; turns G10's criterion into a rule). A plain JavaScript function or region, a single-result body, a tail resume, an exhaustive match's untested last arm, a trusted-call wrapper, and any later optimization must end with the Exit the frame lowering gives: the same value, or the same reasons in the same order. Chapter 26's observation model adds side-effect order and count and resource and fiber lifetimes. Where JavaScript's own statement differs, the lowering uses the runtime's combining form or does not apply. Reason: an optimization that changes a failure is a different language, and settled decision 4 forbids it. Not a language change: it restores the rule the optimizations were meant to keep.

**D342. `using` releases exactly once, through one owner** (2026-10-27; supersedes D94 and D25's 2026-10-23 amendment; amends D25; amended by D401 and D481). A scoped acquisition, an initializer whose requirements include `Scope`, owns its release: `using` supplies the block's Scope and adds no release, no disposal call, and no mask, even when the value is a `Disposable`. Any other initializer's value must be a `Disposable`, an `AsyncDisposable`, `null`, or `undefined`, and `using` owns its one disposal; when evaluating the initializer can pause, `using` holds interruption off from its start until the disposal is registered, as `Effect.acquireDisposable` does, so a pending interruption cannot separate a value from its release. The holder pattern of D94's 2026-10-26 note is no longer needed. Reason: D94 disposed a value twice when its acquisition's release also disposed it, and D25 left a Disposable made inside a masked acquisition unreleased; ECMAScript's `using`, C#'s `using`, Python's `with`, and Effect's `acquireRelease` each release once, and Chapter 21 already said a value that needs release has one owner at every instant. Rejected: deciding at run time from whether the acquisition registered any finalizer, which the runtime did (`$using`), because a finalizer that exists does not show which value it releases; and requiring an explicit adapter around every Effect that returns a Disposable, which costs tokens where `using`'s ECMAScript meaning already says what to do. Language changes, silent: `using` over `Effect.acquireRelease` of a Disposable whose release disposes it closes once instead of twice; a scoped acquisition that returns a Disposable without registering its disposal is no longer disposed by `using` (register it in the acquisition, as `Effect.acquireDisposable` does); and an Effect initializer without `Scope` that returns a Disposable now runs with interruption held off (to let cancellation reach it, define it with `acquireRelease(…, { interruptible: true })`).

**D343. `using` reads its disposal method once, `Symbol.asyncDispose` first** (2026-10-27; amends D94's 2026-10-23 note). For a value whose disposal it owns, `using` reads the method when it registers it: `[Symbol.asyncDispose]` if present, else `[Symbol.dispose]`, as ECMAScript's `await using` (`GetDisposeMethod`) and `Effect.acquireDisposable` choose. At block exit it calls that method with the value as `this` and waits for a returned promise; a throw or a rejection is a defect, combined by D340. Reason: the runtime read the property again at release, so replacing the method or a getter changed which cleanup ran (the compiler audit's disposer probes), and preferring `Symbol.dispose` disagreed with `await using` and Effect. Language changes, silent: a replaced disposal method no longer runs, and a value with both methods is disposed asynchronously.

**D344. A `using` in a layer's setup belongs to the setup's block** (2026-10-27; corrects D222's last clause). A layer block's setup is a function body, so its `using` resources are released when the setup returns, before any member runs; the compiler already does this. A resource that must live as long as the layer is acquired without `using` in the layer's Scope, as Chapter 4 says. Reason: D222 said such a `using` lived until the layer's scope closed, contradicting D14, D47, and Chapter 4. Not a language change.

**D345. Error classes keep full class semantics; families stay open** (2026-10-27; keeps D278 and D304). An error may extend an error declared in any file, as any class may. A family whose base has a `_tag` typed `string`, or is an `abstract error`, is therefore open: no file lists all its members. Rejected: sealing a family to its base's file (Scala 3's `sealed`, Kotlin's `sealed` classes), which would let the checker enumerate a family, because it takes away part of D278's rule that error declarations have every class semantics; D346 gets exhaustive matching from the subject's declared type instead.

**D346. A `match` without `default` is exhaustive only over a finite union of concrete cases** (2026-10-27; supersedes D209's run-time fallback; amends D209, D218, D294; amended by D407, D428 and D515). A `match`, or a `catch` that matches, may leave out `default` only when its subject's declared type is a finite union of literals, `boolean`, `null`, `undefined`, `void`, `data` types and variants, `Option`, `Exit`, `Result` and their cases, and concrete classes with a literal tag. The compiler removes what each arm matches, as TypeScript narrows: a literal arm its literal, a variant or case that case, a class arm its class and every class below it; a guarded arm removes nothing. Whatever is left after the last arm is EK4701. Every other subject is open and needs `default`, or an arm that matches every value of its type: an open family's base (an `error` whose `_tag` is `string`, or an `abstract error`), any class code may extend, `unknown`, `any`, `object`, a type parameter, and a non-literal `string` or `number`, so tag literals against a `_tag` typed `string` need `default`. EK4701 names the open type and offers `default` or narrowing the subject's declared type to the union of concrete classes the match handles; a function whose callers should handle its failures exhaustively declares that union as its E, `number!Denied | Busy`, as Effect users write `Effect<number, Denied | Busy>`. Class patterns still test `instanceof` or the custom matcher at run time, so a value outside its type fails with the proposal's `TypeError`. D294's untested last arm still makes every read and call its test would make, a matcher expression's getter or a shape pattern's property read included; it skips only the branch. Reason: D209 checked only finite unions and let every other `match` fail at run time, and D304 made every family base's tag `string`, so a `match` without `default` on a caught family failure compiled and turned a typed failure into a defect (the 2026-10-27 review reproduced it with a missing subclass and with a concrete base's own instances). Prior art: Rust's `#[non_exhaustive]`, Kotlin's `when`, which needs `else` over a type that is not sealed. Language change, compile error: a `match` over an open subject that relied on having no `default` now needs one, or a narrower subject type; in a `catch`, the quick fix writes `default: throw e`.

**D347. An error subclass sets its own fields after `super(…)`** (2026-10-27; amends D304's generated constructor). A subclass that declares fields and no constructor takes one object of its base's fields and its own; its generated constructor's parameter has the base constructor's parameter type, instantiated with the type arguments the subclass gives its base, plus its own fields; the constructor passes the object to the base's constructor, then sets each of its own declared fields that the object has, in declaration order, reading each once, as TypeScript initializes a subclass's fields after `super()` returns. EK2118 still reports a base whose constructor takes anything but one object of fields. Reason: the generated constructor relied on the base passing the whole object through to `TaggedError`, so a base constructor that rebuilt its argument left the subclass's fields `undefined` (the compiler audit's subclass-field probes, one of which also lost a generic base's type arguments). Language change, silent: those fields now hold their values.

**D348. A Cause `throw` is decided by the operand's declared type** (2026-10-27; amends D177; superseded by D406). `throw e` rethrows every reason when `e`'s declared type is a Cause type, resolved by symbol to `Cause.Cause<E>`, or a union whose every member is one; `unknown` and `any` are not. A union that mixes a Cause type with another type is EK2140. A Cause `throw` is Effect work: a function containing one compiles to frames, and under a plain contract it is EK2003. Reason: the plain path threw the Cause object as a typed failure, so a `catch` typed `string` received a Cause, and a `Cause.die` throw was caught as if expected (the compiler audit's Cause-throw probes); D177 left unions and `unknown` undefined. Language change: a mixed union and a Cause throw under a plain contract are compile errors; the plain path's results now match the frame path's.

**D349. A catch binding is checked as code after the try block** (2026-10-27; amends D13 and D218). The catch binding's type is exactly the try block's expected failures. A binding pattern's defaults and computed keys run after the catch recovers and before its block; their failures and requirements join the function's E and R, not the try block's, so the same catch never handles them. Reason: a default in a catch binding ran a failing call that neither the contract check nor the declaration saw (the compiler audit's catch-default probes). Language change, compile error where a written contract leaves out such a failure.

**D350. A throw expression in a parameter default is EK1001** (2026-10-27; amends D186). A parameter default cannot fail or run a program (EK1001), and a throw expression there is EK1001 too. Reason: D186 let such a throw silently do nothing, because the lowering cannot run it. Language change, compile error: compute the value in the body.

**D351. A handler has one answer type, and EK2119 is an error** (2026-10-27; supersedes D97's warning; restores D50; amended by D410 and D467). Every `resume` of a handler has the handler's answer type H, the type of every value the handled code can end with. For a handle statement the compiler computes H in one step, leaving out each clause return computed from a non-tail `resume`'s result, then checks each of those against H; one outside H is EK2119, an error, whose fix is to write the enclosing function's success type; H is then the written type, plus `undefined` when the try block, or a clause that has not resumed, ends without a value, the same condition under which the computed H gains it. For the `handle` function, H is the program's result type, or `never` with `onReturn`, and EK2110 stays an error. Reason: D97 knowingly typed a waiting `resume` more narrowly than its run-time value and only warned, and a review probe crashed with `n.toFixed is not a function`. Prior art: Eff's and OCaml 5's handler typing, where a continuation's codomain is the handler's answer type. Rejected: D50's full fixpoint, which need not terminate on a growing answer type. Language change, compile error: programs that drew EK2119 must write their success type.

**D352. A function known from a declaration is TypeScript to the handler check** (2026-10-27; corrects Chapter 9). EK4202 applies to every function whose `.vibe` source is not part of the compilation, VibeLang's own `.d.ts` output included, because a declaration states a contract, not whether the body forks. The compiler already does this (`reach.go`, `checkTypeScriptCallee`). Reason: Chapter 9 claimed declarations carried metadata that Chapter 27 says the compiler neither emits nor reads. Not a language change.

**D353. Every block owns its forks, an arrow's expression body included, and an escaping block-owned fiber is an error** (2026-10-27; supersedes D96; amended by D386: the code is EK4838; amended by D408 and D409). `() => fork p()` means what `() => { return fork p() }` means. A fiber a block owns is interrupted when the block exits, so returning it, or a value holding it, from the block, assigning it outside the block, or capturing it in a function that outlives the block is an error, EK4838 (D386); `Effect.forkScoped`, `forkIn`, and `detach` give longer lifetimes. Reason: D96 made a function's lifetime depend on whether its body had braces, so EK4823's quick fix changed ownership, and a returned block-owned fiber arrived already interrupted with no diagnostic. Prior art: Trio's nurseries, Kotlin's `coroutineScope`, Swift's `TaskGroup`. Language change, compile error: `() => fork p()` and other escaping forks; the quick fix writes `Effect.forkScoped(() => p())`.

**D354. Interrupting several fibers signals all before waiting, and fiber forms read their operands once, in source order** (2026-10-27). `join` of several fibers, the `await` operations, and `for concurrent` request interruption of every fiber they stop before they wait for any, as `Fiber.interruptAll`, concurrent `Effect.forEach`, and `Effect.raceAll` do. `join` reads its operand and each element once and joins the fibers it read; its operand's type is checked through every property and index signature, so a dictionary of fibers is accepted and one whose index signature allows a non-fiber is EK4611. An `await` operation whose operand is not a literal evaluates and enumerates it once each time the operation runs. `for concurrent(n) (x of items)` evaluates `n`, then `items`, once each, and keeps the head binding's `const`. A block's exit keeps Effect's Scope order instead: it closes its region one owned thing at a time, newest first, so a later fork is gone before an earlier resource is released. Reason: the runtime interrupted siblings one at a time and deadlocked when one's cleanup waited for another's; it read getters twice, enumerated an iterable when the program was built, and evaluated the loop's iterable before its bound (the compiler audit's concurrency probes). Language change, silent: such programs now terminate, read each operand once, and see their own head order; assigning to a `const` loop item is TS2588.

**D355. The E guarantee is stated with its assumptions, and D256 stands beside D15** (2026-10-27; amends D15, D46, D256). In an accepted program every `Fail` reason that leaves a function has a type in that function's E; E bounds expected failures, not defects or interruptions. The guarantee assumes TypeScript's typing holds, foreign contracts are truthful, and hatches' mappers return their types; Chapter 10 lists the trusted boundaries. D15's guarantee covers the cleanup VibeLang runs; `timeout`, `race`, `raceFirst`, and `await.race` keep Effect's rule and drop a loser's exit, cleanup failures included, which Chapters 7 and 17 now say beside the rule. Reason: Chapter 17 stated D15 without D256's exception, and no chapter said what E promises. Not a language change.

**D356. A construct closes the iterators it opens on every exit** (2026-10-27; amended by D404). `for...of` over an iterable that is not a producer or `Stream`, and array destructuring, close the iterator, by calling `return()` once, on every exit before it is done: a jump, a failure, a defect, an interruption, or an abandonment, whether the exit starts in the head, a binding pattern's default, or the body. The iterable's static type exempts nothing, arrays included. A `return()` that throws while a failure is pending is combined by D340, where ECMAScript's `IteratorClose` drops it. Reason: the lowering closed iterators only when the loop body could pause, skipped arrays by type, and never protected destructuring (the compiler audit's iterator-close probes). Language change, silent: iterators are now closed on those exits, and a failing `return()` is reported.

## The specification revision: types, modules, and tooling (2026-10-27)

These decisions come from a review of the whole specification against its own rules, the compiler's behavior on small probes, and ECMAScript's, TypeScript's, and Effect's semantics. Each says what it supersedes; Chapter 29 marks those decisions superseded.

**D360. The specification is the one normative text** (supersedes this log's header of 2026-10-16, under which the specification followed the log). Part II (Chapters 13-17), the runtime model of Chapter 20, and every statement marked [Rule] define what a program means. Part I teaches by example and follows the rules; an example that disagrees with a rule is wrong. [Effect], [Kernel], and [Experiment] mark evidence. [Restriction] marks a limit of the implementation, not a rule (D374). This log records reasons, evidence, and history: where it disagrees with the specification, the specification wins and the log is corrected. A decision changes the language only in a revision that updates every rule, example, diagnostic, and declaration listing it affects, and a decision that a later one replaces is marked superseded here and in Chapter 29. The user guide and the agent skill follow the specification. Reason: the specification called every rule decided while this log held an open amendment (D25), four provisional decisions (D310-D313), and amendments that contradicted the chapters (D222 against Chapter 4 on `using` in a layer's setup, D257 against Chapter 13's overload rule, D303 against the live bindings that Chapter 10 implies), and Chapter 29 listed D13 seven times and left out D130-D135 and D247-D313, so two readers could derive two languages. Prior art: ECMA-262 keeps normative algorithms apart from notes, and a revision changes the algorithm, not a note beside it.

**D361. A service's identifier is nominal: its brand is required** (supersedes D45's optional brand; amends D1's emit clause, D93, and D281; amended by D466; superseded by D501). A service has two types, as Effect's `Context.Key<Identifier, Shape>` has. The identifier, which the service's name denotes as a type, is the shape's members, when the shape is an object type, plus a required phantom brand whose type is the service's key; R, a layer's outputs, and what a provider removes from R hold identifiers only, so a type is assignable to the identifier only if it carries that key. The shape, `S.Shape` or Effect's `Context.Service.Shape<typeof S>`, is the members alone: implementations are checked against it, in `Layer.succeed`, `Layer.effect`, `Effect.provideService`, `provide S = value`, and a default, and `implements S` in a `.vibe` file checks a class against it. `S()` returns the implementation typed as the identifier, so a parameter typed `db: S` still takes it and calls its members. The brand's property is declared as a type only: no object has it and no program can name it. Declaration files write `interface S extends S.Shape, ServiceBrand<"<key>">`, the key class with the base `ServiceKey<S, S.Shape>`, and the namespace `S` holding `Shape`. Reason: an optional property does not stop a structural type from being assigned to it, so `Effect<number, never, { get(): number!! }>` could be assigned to a requirement of either of two services with that member; a program that required `Alpha`, relabeled the requirement as `Beta` through such a type with no cast, and provided `Beta`, compiled and died with `Service not found: …#Alpha`. Effect separates the identifier from the shape for exactly this reason. Evidence: a stock TypeScript check of the emitted shapes against `effect@4.0.6` rejects a structural type and another service as `Alpha`, refuses a `Beta` layer as a provider of `Alpha`, and accepts object literals in `Layer.succeed` and `Effect.provideService` and classes that implement `Alpha.Shape` or `Context.Service.Shape<typeof Alpha>`. Language changes: a TypeScript class that wrote `implements Users` writes `implements Users.Shape`; a plain object passed to a parameter typed with a service's name is TS2741, and the parameter is typed with the shape instead; a program that relabeled one requirement as another stops compiling, where it used to die at run time.

**D362. A provider provides only what it provides on every path** (amends D40, D130, D173, and D175). A provider removes from R only the services it provides whatever value it has at run time. A `Layer<Out, E, In>` provides `Out`; a `Context<S>` provides `S`; `Key = value` provides `Key`'s identifier, with `value` checked against `Key`'s shape; a list or a non-empty tuple provides what each of its required elements provides, read element by element; a value of a union type provides only what every member provides, and adds every member's errors and requirements. Anything else, such as a value of an array type that may be empty, a value that is neither a layer nor a `Context`, or `any`, is EK3207. A service's `static readonly default` is checked against the service's shape as `Layer.succeed`'s argument is, member contracts included (TS2322, EK2001, EK3101). Provider expressions are evaluated once, in the order written, before any layer is built. A `using` in a layer's setup block is released when that block returns, as D344 corrects D222. Reason: `Effect.provide` accepts a layer, a non-empty tuple of layers, or a `Context`, and stock TypeScript rejects the rest (TS2769); the compiler had credited a union of two layers with both services, an empty array with its element type's services, an invalid value with nothing at all, and a default of the wrong type with the service, and each program compiled with a closed R and died with `Service not found` or a `TypeError`. Language changes: each such program is now a compile error.

**D363. `wire` is recognized by symbol, keeps evaluation order, and never lets a layer provide its own input** (amends D27 and D93). A call is `wire` when its callee resolves to the runtime's `wire`, under an import alias or as a namespace member too; any other use of `wire`, such as storing it, is EK3208. Its arguments are evaluated once, left to right, and the resulting layers are composed in dependency order; a spread argument of a tuple type contributes its elements, and a spread of an array type is EK3204. The result needs each argument's inputs minus the outputs of the other arguments, so a layer that needs `S` and provides `S` still needs `S`; `wire(L)` is `L`. Reason: the planner recognized only the bare name, so an aliased or namespace call, or a spread, skipped the ordering and kept the planned type, and died at run time; the lowering permuted the argument expressions themselves, so `wire(a(), b())` ran `b` first; and the result type subtracted each layer's outputs from its own inputs, typing a decorator's layer as needing nothing. Language changes: an aliased, namespace, or spread call that failed at run time now composes in order; arguments with side effects run in the order written, where they ran in dependency order; a self-dependent layer keeps its input, which is EK3201 where R must be empty; a stored `wire` is EK3208.

**D364. A `module` declaration is an ECMAScript module** (supersedes D303's snapshot exports, its evaluation after every other import, and its case-insensitive names; amends D283; amended by D403). An import, a re-export, or a namespace import of a module declaration's export is a live binding. A file's dependencies, its module declarations among them, are evaluated in the order of its import declarations, then its own body; a module declaration the file never imports, or imports only types from, is not evaluated. Temporal dead zones, cycles, and a body's top-level `await` follow ECMAScript. Module names are case-sensitive. A body's declarations keep their source names, so a direct `eval` in the body sees them; an implementation that cannot keep one of these properties for some construct reports EK4408 instead of changing its meaning. The rest of D303 stands: the body is checked as a `.ts` file of its own, the JavaScript holds it inline, and its declarations go to a declaration file beside the file's. Reason: TC39's module declarations are module records, and an import denotes a binding, not a sampled value; D303's snapshot came from the convenience of an inline lowering, which can keep bindings live with namespace accessors that read the body's own bindings. A probe exported `count` and `bump()` and printed 0 after `bump()` where a `.ts` module prints 1. Language changes: an importer now sees an exported `let` change; a module declaration is evaluated at its first import's position among the file's imports, where it ran after all of them; two module names that differ only in case are two modules.

**D365. Source specifiers are rewritten for the loader in every import form** (consolidates D31, D63, and D298; closes the dynamic `import()` gap listed under F1). A relative specifier naming a `.vibe` or `.ts` source is written with the output's `.js` name in JavaScript and declarations: in a static import or export, `import type`, an import type, and a dynamic `import()` whose specifier is a string literal. Only the extension changes, and the path is spelled as the emitted loader reads it: a file path for `require`, an escaped URL for an ES module `import`. A computed dynamic specifier is not rewritten. Reason: a literal `import("./side.vibe")` stayed a source name and failed to load unless `rewriteRelativeImportExtensions` was set, and CommonJS output URL-encoded a path with a space, so `require` could not find it. Language changes: those imports now load.

**D366. A declaration file is a semantic ABI** (amends D65, D123, and D268; places D248-D254 under it; amended by D384; amended by D411, D416 and D426). For every export, a consumer compiled against the emitted `.d.ts` gets the same runtime operation, contract, service and control identities, and legal callback uses as a consumer of the source. A declaration cannot prove what a body does, so a consumer of declarations alone treats a plain function or a function whose R names a control operation as foreign code (EK4401, EK4202): it may reject more, never run or type a call differently. A pure function's direct variant travels only as the property `f[$direct]` of the exported function (D268); D65's named `f$direct` exports, `default$direct` included, are not public, and no generated function is. Declaration files are standard TypeScript, without the `!` form or VibeLang keywords; every declaration form emits the same at every nesting level, namespaces included; and every name declaration emit adds is fresh against the file's names. The type-directed compiler defines the language: the text projector and any other renderer either produce the same observations on every accepted program or report an error there (EK9100). Reason: the compiler emitted a service inside a namespace without its key type and a service's `<Name>_base` constant under a name the file already declared, so stock TypeScript rejected declarations of programs that ran; Chapter 9 promised metadata that Chapter 27 said is neither emitted nor read; and two plain-call ABIs (D65, D268) coexisted. A round-trip suite, a stock TypeScript consumer and a VibeLang consumer of the declarations alone, holds the compiler to the rule. Language changes: JavaScript code that imported a generated `f$direct` export no longer finds it.

**D367. A pattern's built-in tests apply only to the declarations they name** (applies D328's recognition by symbol to patterns; with D328, supersedes D208's clause that a shadowing local is not consulted). A pattern's names resolve by scope, as every reference does (D328, rule H4). `Option`, `Exit`, and `Result`'s matchers, the primitive classes, and a data type's variants have their built-in tests only when the name resolves to that declaration, under any import alias, and a data type's variant counts as one only when its data type resolves to a `data` declaration or a `Data.taggedEnum` constant. A local declaration of the same name shadows them, and the pattern then uses the local value as an ordinary matcher (D213). Reason: renaming a local predicate must not change a program, but `x is Number` with a local `Number` ignored the local, while the same predicate named `StringMatcher` was used; and a local `Option` was rewritten to `Option.isSome` and rejected. Prior art: Scheme's hygienic macros and ECMAScript's ResolveBinding give a name its meaning through its binding. Language changes: a pattern that names a local shadowing one of those names now uses the local, silently where it compiled before.

**D368. A pattern reads each value once and keeps every test it cannot prove inert** (amends D214 and D294; amended by D405). Within one `match` or `is`, each property of each value is read at most once and an iterator is advanced at most once per element, when a test first needs it, so a subpattern's test and binding see one value; matcher expressions are evaluated when their arm is reached, in arm order. An exhaustive match runs its last arm untested only when every operand of the test is a literal or a data type's variant named through its declaration and every read is a data value's tag or a literal-typed field; any other test evaluates its matcher and reads, and a test that then fails is a defect. Branch coverage is not a reason to omit a test, so D294's rationale is withdrawn, and its class-pattern correction stands. Reason: `when { value: 1 and let n }` tested one read of a getter and bound a second, so `n` was 2 in an arm that required 1; and the untested last arm dropped `registry.State.Waiting`'s getter call, so the program read the registry fewer times with the optimization than without it. Prior art: the TC39 proposal caches property reads and iterator results for one match. Language changes: a subject with getters observes fewer reads, and an exhaustive match's last arm evaluates the matcher operands it skipped.

**D369. Module aliases come from a table pinned to the Effect version, as data-type methods do** (amends D311; D336 makes the same rule for D152's methods). Which Effect modules stand for an export of their own name (D311), and which pairs of a module's export and its same-named service's member share a name, are entries of a table generated from the pinned `effect` declarations and shipped with the compiler, beside D336's table of methods. A test regenerates both tables from the installed declarations and fails when they would change an entry, so an upgrade of the pinned Effect changes them only in a VibeLang release that lists each changed entry as a language change. A module name has these meanings only where it resolves to an Effect module, through the prelude, an import under any alias, or `Experimental`; a local of the same name shadows it (D328). Where a module's export and its service's member share a name, `M.x` is the export and `M.M.x` the member, and the guide lists every such pair. Rejected: reporting a shared name as ambiguous, which would reject `Console.log`, the module's function that returns an `Effect`, beside the service member that returns `void`. Reason: D311's "the module's exports win" depended on what each Effect release happened to export; pinning makes the alias part of the language's versioned API. Language changes: none for `effect@4.0.6`, whose table reproduces today's resolutions.

**D370. Every `function*` is a producer** (supersedes D271 and the iterator-annotation part of D296; keeps D98's generator methods). A `function*` declaration or expression is a producer whatever its return annotation says; a JavaScript iterator type in that annotation is EK1002. A generator method in a class or an object literal is a JavaScript generator, whose body is plain code (D282). A JavaScript generator function belongs in a `module` declaration or a `.ts` file. Reason: an annotation that still type-checks must not change what code means (Chapter 13's rule for annotations), and D271 made removing `: Generator<T>` turn a JavaScript generator into a Stream; syntax, method or function, already tells the two apart. Language changes: a `function*` with an iterator annotation is a compile error, fixed by making it a generator method or moving it to a `module` declaration.

**D371. A `for...of` loop means what its value is** (amends D26 and D98; amended by D402 and D404). Over a producer's Stream, the loop runs the producer on its fiber under a `Yield` handler; over another Stream, it pulls the stream and closes it on exit; over anything else, it follows the iterator protocol and calls `return()` on every early exit, failures and interruption included. The compiler emits JavaScript's own loop only where it behaves the same: a static type with no `Stream` member and a body that cannot pause. A union, `any`, or `unknown` therefore never selects a meaning. Reason: Chapter 8 said only that the static type decides, and a union of a Stream and an array, or `any`, had no stated meaning. Language changes: none for a loop whose type named one kind.

**D372. One observation model judges every optimization and every rewrite** (amends D10's "unobservable", D274, D276, D291, and D294; makes G10 a gate). Observations: the exit and its Cause, reason by reason in order; effects outside the program, in order and number, including getters, proxy traps, iterator steps, and custom matchers; the binding each reference reads, with its `this`, `arguments`, `new.target`, `super`, and self-binding; the owner and order of each resource's and fiber's lifetime; and the suspension points the language defines. The exit part is D341's rule; this decision adds the rest and applies all of it to rewrites as well. Run-loop operation counts, Effect's fairness yields, generated names, time, and memory are not observations. An optimization is valid only if equivalent to the frame lowering under these observations, with every suspension point kept. A quick fix, `--fix`, an automatic refactor, and the formatter produce an equivalent program with the same types, are idempotent, and keep comments, or are not applied; reporting no new error is an additional check, not evidence. The formatter changes layout only and never reorders import or export declarations, statements, or members, since import order is evaluation order; it may sort the names inside one import's braces. Reason: each of these type-checked while changing a program: a catch-tag rewrite captured another handler's variable, a provide rewrite evaluated the layer before the program, import cleanup deleted another module's initialization, the formatter reordered side-effecting imports, `--fix` stopped after 16 overlapping fixes, rewrites dropped comments, and the last-arm optimization dropped a getter call. Language changes: none to programs; the formatter no longer sorts import declarations, and fixes that would change meaning are refused.

**D373. The static guarantees state what they assume** (complements D355, which states the E guarantee). E bounds the Fail reasons that leave a function (D355), not defects or interruption; R bounds the services a program looks up; a plain function neither fails nor needs a service; `Scope` in R records an obligation to release, not the absence of use after release; a control operation runs on its handler's fiber or the runtime fails. They hold relative to a trusted base: the compiler, the runtime, and `effect@4.0.6`; TypeScript's types where VibeLang uses them unchanged, `any` and assertions included; foreign declarations' contracts; trust declarations (D22, D23, D121, D249, D261); escapes (hatches, `module` bodies, `.ts` files); the fiber-behavior table, backed by the runtime's check; and, for a package consumed through declarations, the conservative foreign-code checks (D366). Reason: a claim of effect safety means nothing without its assumptions, and Chapter 9 implied that declarations certify a checked body, which no metadata supports. Language changes: none.

**D374. Implementation limits are restrictions, not rules** (amends G19's failure condition, D107, D171, D210, D211, D212, D214's limits, D228, D303's unsupported forms, and D307's limit of four type parameters). Each limit that comes from the current implementation is marked [Restriction] and listed in Chapter 30, with what it rejects, why, and its diagnostic. A restriction rejects a program; it never gives one another meaning. A construct that one checker pass cannot decide gets a restriction or asks for an annotation, so G19's condition "its rule changes so that one pass decides it" is withdrawn. Reason: when a lowering, a pass structure, or a coverage tool decides what a program means, every repair of the implementation becomes a new language exception. Language changes: none.

**D375. The platform decisions are decided** (D310, D311, D312, and D313 lose "provisional"; D312's platform import is the final form). The review of 2026-10-27 kept each as written. For D312, the platform import names the platform explicitly, once, in the entry file, which is the explicit trigger chosen; the alternatives considered, a library call such as `Node.run(main)`, an explicit `provide` in every `main`, a tsconfig setting, and an implicit default, are rejected. Language changes: none.

## The consistency pass: the approved call rules (2026-10-27)

The three revisions of 2026-10-27 were merged and read end to end against the decision list approved the same day. These decisions record where the approved list settled what the revisions left open or decided differently, and what the end-to-end read fixed. Each records what it supersedes or amends.

**D376. A block param is always the last argument** (supersedes D188's placement by the callee's type and D334; keeps D188's syntax and D273; EK1022). `f(args) { block }` is `f(args, () => { block })`, and `f { block }` is `f(() => { block })`, whatever `f` is. A callee that takes its program first, as an Effect dual's data-first signature does, or returns a function that takes it, as a dual's data-last signature and a curried combinator such as `semaphore.withPermits(1)` do, does not accept the block last; that call fails with TypeScript's own type error, and a note names the parameter that takes the program and gives the spelling with an arrow in its place, `withSpan(() => { … }, "load")`; no new code is needed. A type decides only whether such a call is rejected, never where the block goes. A block statement whose `{` begins the line after a statement that ends, with no `;`, in an identifier, a member access, or a call is EK1022, a syntax error: `return once` followed by `{ … }` on the next line returned the function and left the block unreachable; join the lines to pass the block, or end the first line with `;`. Reason: D188 chose `f(block, args)`, `f(args)(block)` or `f(args, block)` from the callee's declared signature, so a type chose where a block went; all four reviewers recommended one placement, and the approved list reversed its proposal to keep D188 as an exception (its decision 3), because placement by type breaks the rule that call-site syntax decides. Prior art: Kotlin's and Swift's trailing lambdas, always the last argument. Language change, compile error: `withSpan("load") { … }`, `timeout("1 second") { … }`, `retry(policy) { … }` and `semaphore.withPermits(1) { … }` are type errors whose note gives the arrow form, and `--fix` writes it (EK1023 where the appended form would still type-check, D382); `it.effect("name") { … }` and `Layer.effect(S) { … }` keep their meaning; a block statement placed after a call or a `return` on the line before needs a `;`.

**D377. An await operation's literal elements are deferred by syntax** (amends D196 and D322; the approved list's decision 4 and its point (c); amended by D394). Every element of an array or object literal after `await.all`, `await.allSettled`, `await.race` or `await.any` is a program, whatever its type: nothing in it is evaluated until the operation starts it on its own fiber, and each run of the enclosing program starts it afresh. The literal's own parts, its computed keys and spread elements, are evaluated once, in source order, when the operation runs, and a non-literal operand is evaluated and enumerated once then, as before (D354). The value each element gives is treated as `Promise.all` treats its values: an `Effect` value runs on its own fiber, a fiber or a `Deferred` is waited for, and any other value, such as `1`, is the element's result. The value decides, at run time; no static type enters the rule. Reason: D196 waited for an element "that is a `Fiber` or a `Deferred`", which the compiler read from the element's type; the approved list keeps keyword operands deferred because the keyword says so, on the condition that the operation consults no types. Language change: none for elements that are calls; an element typed as a fiber or a Deferred is now recognized by its value, with the same result.

**D378. Decision 5's static rule is D321's: the callee's declared return type** (confirms D321; the approved list's decision 5, as ruled 2026-10-27; amended by D393, D458 and D486). The approved list keeps a static rule for whether a call's result runs, its decision 5, and this decision states that rule in D321's words: a call runs when the declared return type of the signature TypeScript resolves for it, read before its type parameters are instantiated, is definitely an Effect. D24's data types (`Config`, `Exit`, a service key, an error instance), `any` and `unknown` stay data. A call of a generic callee whose declared return type is `T`, such as `identity(program)`, returns data whatever `T` becomes, even where the call's instantiated type is an Effect. Reason: a TypeScript developer expects `identity(x)` to hand back `x`, and reading the instantiated type would let inference decide when code runs (D320). A run-time check, `yield* $run(f(a))`, is prototyped and measured before any change (G21); it would run the program `identity(program)` returns, so adopting it needs its own decision. Language change: against D24's instantiated reading, a generic helper whose result is an Effect returns it as data; write `identity(program)()` to run it.

**D379. The return boundary's S22-3 and S5-1 are removed** (confirms D321 and D323; the approved list's point (d), as ruled 2026-10-27). `return e` returns `e`'s value, and returning an Effect value never runs it, whatever the contract: an `Exit` returned under a written `unknown!!` and under a contextual `Effect<unknown>` is data in both. Where the success type names no value, written or contextual, returning an Effect value or a program is EK4802, an error (D321). Reason: S22-3 and S5-1 let the contract's A decide whether a returned Effect ran, so a type decided whether an expression that is not a call ran. A draft of this pass kept them open; the ruling removes them. Language change: a returned Effect value that ran under S22-3 or S5-1 is data, a type error where it does not fit A and EK4802 where A names no value; write `return p()`.

**D380. A function literal prefers a candidate that takes it as a function; opposed preferences are EK1021** (amends D56 and D324; the approved list's point (b); amended by D392; amended by D500). Among the overload candidates that accept a call, where one takes a function literal argument as a function and another as an `Effect`, which would convert it to a program (D7), the first wins. Where two such arguments of one call prefer different candidates, the call is EK1021, whose fix writes the signature: an explicit type argument, or an annotation on a literal. Otherwise TypeScript's order decides, as D56 says. Reason: the approved list passes a lambda as a function where a function is expected and as a suspended Effect where an Effect is expected (its decision 2(a)), and when one overload set offers both, the function form wins, as a TypeScript reader expects a lambda to stay a lambda. Language change: a call whose overloads list the Effect form first now takes the function form; an API that tells the two apart at run time, with `Effect.isEffect`, receives the function the reader saw.

**D381. A program is a function with no required parameters** (amends D7, D322 and D324; the approved list's point (a)). Where the declared type at a destination is the `Effect` interface, or a type parameter constrained to include it and no function type, a function value converts to a program when it has no required parameters: every parameter is optional, has a default, or is a rest parameter. EK1018's destinations read the same way. Reason: the approved list keeps the zero-required-input rule the compiler applies. Language change: none against the compiler; "parameterless" in D322 and D324 means this.

**D382. A migration analyzer reports every site whose meaning changed silently, as EK1023** (amends D320, D322 and D323; the approved list's point (e)). A migration-only analyzer that knows the earlier rules, D110, D147, D155, D188's placement, D262, D265, D269, and D24's S22-3 and S5-1, runs with `--fix` and in the release that adopts this revision. Most earlier sites now fail to type-check: a running call at an Effect destination draws EK1018, whose quick fix writes `() =>`, a block after a dual draws TypeScript's error with a note, and a factory draws TS2322 at its return. A site whose new reading type-checks, such as a call passed by name whose success value is itself an Effect, a held `const` or field of such a type, a function value whose form changed, or a block after a dual whose appended form type-checks, and a site the analyzer cannot classify completely, is EK1023, an error through 0.x that no setting acknowledges in bulk. `--fix` reads the analyzer's sites, rewrites each from the inside out into the syntax that keeps its old meaning, rechecks, and refuses an edit that adds an error; a second run edits nothing. It rewrites a factory that is not exported into a plain function that returns the program, and refuses an exported one with EK1023, since its TypeScript API would change; the author chooses a `module` declaration or the new API (D390). Reason: the approved list's decision 1 names factories as the one place behavior could change silently. The approved list and the implementation design name this code EK1017, which the registry already gives to `provide` used as an expression; the specification gives it the next free number, EK1023, after EK1021 (D380) and EK1022 (D376). Language change: none beyond the decisions it migrates.

**D383. `pipe` passes its first argument as the value it is** (supersedes D110; amends D8's built-in and D325). `pipe(x, f, g)` is `g(f(x))`, every argument evaluated once, as any call's are; a function literal first is a function, never a program by look-ahead, so `pipe(() => fetchUser(id), retry(p))` does not type-check, since data-last `retry` takes an `Effect`. Over programs, nest the calls: `retry(() => fetchUser(id), p)`. Reason: D110 read the first stage's signature to decide whether `pipe`'s first argument was a program or a value, a type deciding representation; the approved list's decision 1 removes it with the other by-name rules. Language change, compile error: each `pipe(() => …, stage)` over programs; `--fix` writes nested calls.

**D384. A declaration carries the plain call signature** (amends D268, D332 and D366; the approved list's decision 7; amended by D411 and D421; amended by D483; amended by D500). A function that compiles to plain JavaScript declares its plain call signature in its `.d.ts`, as D268's phantom `$plain` member of a namespace merged with the function, so a VibeLang consumer of the declaration passes the plain form where a plain function is expected, as a consumer of the source does: `[1, 2].map(double)` is `[2, 4]` either way. A function whose declaration has no `$plain` member is an Effect function to its consumers, and passing it where a plain function is expected is EK4301, never an array of unrun Effects. The run-time marker `f[$direct]` is unchanged, and the `$plain` signature joins the trusted base (D373). Reason: compiled from source, `[1, 2].map(double)` gave `[2, 4]`; compiled against a declaration with no plain signature, it gave two unrun Effects, so a declaration consumer behaved differently from a source consumer, against D366's first rule. Language change: none for source consumers; a declaration consumer gets the source's result, and a package built before this revision draws EK4301 at such a site until it is rebuilt.

**D385. "Every function is an Effect", stated once** (states settled decision 1 for the reader; no rule changes; amended by D397; amended by D500). Seen from TypeScript, every VibeLang function is a function that returns a cold `Effect`: calling it runs nothing, and each run binds the parameters afresh and runs the body (E9, D332). The two exceptions are a function with a plain return annotation, an ordinary JavaScript function (D67), and a producer, which returns a `Stream` (D370). Inside VibeLang, a call of a function whose declared return type is an Effect runs it where the call stands (D321), and an arrow, `() => f(x)`, delays it (D322). The introduction, Chapter 2, Chapter 10's table, and Chapter 13 state it in these words. Reason: a reviewer read the bare sentence as false for plain-annotated functions and producers, and the first revision qualified it for TypeScript callers only. Language change: none.

**D386. An escaping block-owned fiber is EK4838, an error** (amends D353; the approved list's decision 8; amended by D409). The rule D353 states, that a fiber a block owns may not escape the block by being returned, stored outside it, or captured by a function that outlives it, reports EK4838, the code the registry holds for a returned block-owned fork, promoted from a warning to an error no setting lowers and widened to every escape; D353's EK4620 is not used. The quick fix offers `Effect.forkScoped(() => p())` or `detach(() => p())`. Reason: the approved list promotes the existing EK4838, and one rule keeps one code. Language change, compile error: a returned block-owned fork that drew the warning.

**D387. A file's entry statement and EK3205 depend on that file alone** (amends D135 and D312; the approved list's decision 11; amended by D420). Whether a module gets an entry statement depends on whether it exports a parameterless `main` and calls a runner at its top level, the runner recognized by its resolved symbol (rule H4); what the entry provides depends on its own platform import; and whether EK3205 reports a service `main` needs depends on the entry file alone. No other module's imports enter any of the three. Reason: D135's spelling test let `helper.runSync(1)` suppress `main` (fixed by D135's amendment of 2026-10-27), and EK3205 was not reported when another module imported `main`, so an unrelated importer silenced a warning about the entry. Language change: EK3205 is reported for an entry file that another module also imports.

**D388. A value member named like a property of the key's class is EK4503 too** (amends D41; the approved list's decision 12). Reading a service's value member without a call, as `Person.name`, is EK4503 whatever the member's name: a member named like a property of the key's class, such as `name` or `length`, is no exception, since that read would return the class's own property, `"Person"`. Reason: such a read type-checked and silently returned the class's name. Language change, compile error: write `Person().name`.

**D389. The trusted contract is `trusted(impl, [Errors])`** (completes D249; amends D332's wording; amended by D400). The specification teaches one form for a trusted TypeScript contract: a `trusted(impl, [Errors])` value exported beside the implementation, which returns an `Effect`, so a call of it runs as any Effect-returning call does, and an exception of a listed class is an expected failure. The `@vibelang trusted` and `@throws` tags (D23) are deprecated: the native compiler still reads them, with warning EK4406, and a renderer that reads one file at a time rejects a call that depends on them (EK9100, D366). Every chapter, upgrade step, and diagnostic repair names `trusted(impl, [Errors])`. Reason: the revisions of 2026-10-27 still recommended the tag in several places, including the upgrade step for an untrusted call in a plain function, and a recommendation of a deprecated form would become an error under such a renderer. Language change: none beyond D249.

**D390. A factory has no VibeLang form** (amends D323; settles the consistency pass's factory question). A function that does work when it is called and returns an unrun Effect for later is written in TypeScript, in a `.ts` file or a `module` declaration, or in VibeLang as a plain function that returns a function, `function capture(input): () => number!! { … }`, which TypeScript sees as `(input) => () => Effect<number>`. Rejected: an Effect contract whose body returns an arrow, `(input): number!! => { …; return () => snapshot }`, since its body would run at the call only because the arrow does not fit A, and removing the annotation from a program that still type-checks would move work from the call to the run (D320, D323); a `const` bound to an arrow, since the arrow is a VibeLang function, cold to TypeScript like any other, and a declared Effect type on the `const` changes only how the arrow is passed (D324); and a factory keyword, like D269's rejected `hold(…)` marker, since it would give syntax to behavior idiomatic Effect does not use: `Effect.fn` and `Effect.gen` bodies are cold, and Effect's own factories are TypeScript. The 29 migration files D269 counted keep their TypeScript API through a `module` declaration, or change it to return a function; a factory left in the old form is TS2322, and `--fix` rewrites it or, for an exported one, refuses it with EK1023 (D382). Language change: none beyond D323.

**D391. `fork` and `join` are contextual keywords, and every keyword operator takes a parenthesized operand** (amends D166, D225, D322 and D328; ruled 2026-10-27; amended by D432, D443, D452 and D462). `fork(e)` and `join(e)` are the keywords with a parenthesized operand, a program by syntax as `fork e` is, as `typeof(x)` is `typeof x`, unless the name resolves to a binding the program declares or imports, in which case the call is an ordinary call of that binding. The prelude's `fork` is the keyword's own binding and never makes `fork(e)` a call. Which one it is follows the binding, never the spelling (rule H). `fork e` and `join e` without parentheses are always the keywords. The await operations take a parenthesized operand the same way, `await.all([a(), b()])`, and `await` cannot be a binding. Reason: D322 made `fork(fetchReport())` an ordinary call, so the parentheses D166 said "mean the same" turned a deferred operand into a running call and EK1018; a TypeScript reader reads `fork(f())` as the keyword applied to `f()`. Language change: none against D166; against D322's text, `fork(f())` keeps its deferred meaning, and a program that declares its own `fork` calls it.

## The second review: rules a type, an overload, or a body still decided (2026-10-27)

A second review of the revised specification on 2026-10-27, by four reviewers working apart, found places where the revision still let an overload's order, an annotation, a contract's `Scope`, an expected type, a run-time `isEffect` test, braces, or a body decide what a program does, rules stated as proofs that do not hold, and terms left undefined. These decisions repair them. Each says what it supersedes or amends; Chapter 29 marks those decisions. Where the reviewers proposed different repairs, the decision names the one it rejected and why.

**D392. A function argument takes its own form, and the order of overloads never chooses it** (2026-10-27; amends D56, D112, D324 and D380; amended by D421, D422, D450, D457 and D482; amended by D500). Every function has an own form, fixed before any destination is read: the form its return annotation names, plain for a plain annotation and Effect for an Effect contract, and without one, plain when it is eligible for the plain form (Chapter 15) and Effect otherwise, as an `async` function's type includes its `Promise`. A destination receives the own form where it accepts it; otherwise a plain own form lifts to the Effect form, a function with no required parameters converts to a program, and an Effect own form never becomes plain (EK4301). Overload resolution tries every candidate first with each function argument in its own form, so it picks the candidate TypeScript itself would pick; only when none accepts are the candidates tried with lifting and conversion, where D380's preference of a function over a program applies. Where the candidates one step accepts give a function argument different forms, the call is EK1021, which asks for a return annotation on the literal or an explicit type argument. A call of the Effect form runs none of the body: the body runs when the program it returns runs, so Chapter 13 no longer says both forms "run the body when the function is called". Reason: with `consume(f: (n: number) => number): string` declared before `consume(f: (n: number) => Effect<number>): string`, `consume((n) => events.push(n))` passed a plain function and pushed once; with the order swapped it passed a function returning a cold program, which an API that discards the result never runs, and pushed nothing, while TypeScript picks the plain overload in both orders because `number` is not assignable to `Effect<number>`. Prior art: C#'s overload resolution prefers the delegate whose return type matches a lambda's inferred return type (better conversion from expression). (Corrected by D444: this entry also cited Kotlin, whose lambdas take their `suspend` kind from the expected type, not from their own.) Rejected: rejecting every function literal that two candidates could accept, as one reviewer proposed, because TypeScript's own choice is unambiguous there and is what a TypeScript reader expects; the rejection, EK1021, remains for the calls that choice leaves open. Language change: a function eligible for the plain form, passed to an overload set that lists an Effect callback before a plain one, now takes the plain overload; EK1021 also reports plain-versus-Effect disagreement.

**D393. Rule R reads every type parameter in scope uninstantiated, and no subtype of `Effect` is definitely an Effect** (2026-10-27; amends D321 and D378; amended by D426, D449 and D458; its `flow` example is EK4301 under rule F, D435). A call's declared return type is the return type written in the declaration its resolved signature comes from, with every type parameter in scope there left uninstantiated: the signature's own and those of every enclosing class, interface, function, method, and type alias. A type parameter is never definitely an Effect, whatever instantiates it. Any interface or class type that extends `Effect`, with members of its own or none, returns data. So `new Box(program).get()`, where `get(): T`, and `flow(parse, fetchUser)(raw)`, whose callee's declared return type is `flow`'s type parameter, return the program as data, and `interface Job extends Effect<number> {}` is data until called. Reason: D378 said "before its type parameters are instantiated" without saying which, so one reading made `new Box(program).get()` run while `get(new Box(program))` returned data, and moving a function into a method changed whether its calls ran, the parametricity D321 exists to keep; `Job` was in neither of rule R's lists. Language change: against an implementation that instantiated a class's or an enclosing function's type parameters, such calls return data; a statement that drops the program is EK4801.

**D394. An await operation adopts nothing at run time** (2026-10-27; amends D196 and D377; amended by D433). A literal element of an await operation is a program by syntax, and its result is its program's success value, never run or waited for again: `await.all [exit(() => a()), exit(() => b())]` gives two `Exit` values. A literal element whose type is a program, definitely an Effect or a function with no required parameters, is EK4613, since its result would be the program unrun; an element of `Fiber` or `Deferred` type is EK4609 or EK4610, whose repairs write `join f` or `d.await()` as the element. A non-literal operand's element type decides how each element runs: definitely an Effect, it runs on its own fiber; a function with no required parameters converts to a program (D49) and runs; anything else is EK4603. Reason: D377 treated each element's value as `Promise.all` treats a thenable, by a run-time test, and on `effect@4.0.6` `Effect.isEffect` is true for an `Exit`, a `Config`, a tagged error, and a service key, so `await.all [exit(…), exit(…)]` would have run each `Exit` and failed with the first failure where the compiler returned both, against D377's own "no change for calls"; and a function element, a program by Chapter 13's definition, came back unrun. `Promise.all` can adopt every thenable because JavaScript has one asynchronous value type; Effect has data types that are also Effects, which is why rule R reads declared types. Language change: an element that is a fiber or a Deferred, which D377 waited for, is EK4609 or EK4610; a non-literal operand whose element type is not a program is EK4603.

**D395. A destination's `null` and `undefined` are ignored, "an Effect" is rule R's, and a call whose value is a program meets EK1018 no more** (2026-10-27; amends D322 and D324). Rule F's table reads a destination type with its `null` and `undefined` members removed, as TypeScript does when it finds a contextual signature, so the optional callbacks Effect declares, `while?: ((e) => boolean | Effect<boolean>) | undefined`, accept what their non-optional types accept. "Definitely an Effect" in that table is rule R's predicate, and the conversion to a program applies to a destination's element and field types, as EK1018 does. EK1018 exempts a call whose success value is itself a program, an Effect or a function with no required parameters, so `retry(makeSync(store), policy)` passes the program `makeSync` returns. Reason: the function-values table had no row for a union with `undefined`, its "an Effect" did not reuse rule R's definition, and EK1018 rejected a call that returns a program-valued function while it accepted the same call of a plain-annotated one. Language change: `retry(makeSync(store), policy)` and its kind compile.

**D396. A hatch's first argument is a function literal or a single expression, by syntax** (2026-10-27; amends D148 and D330; EK4411; amended by D432; amended by D503). A function literal in first place, an arrow function or a `function` expression, is the hatch's function; any other first argument is a single expression, evaluated when the hatch runs, whose value is the result. A single expression whose type is a function, such as `sync(Math.random)`, and inputs after a single expression, are EK4411. Reason: "a function" was not defined, so `sync(Math.random)` could return the function, as the single-expression reading has it, or call it, as a TypeScript reader of `Effect.sync(Math.random)` and the runtime's `sync` do. Language change: such calls are compile errors; write `sync(() => Math.random())`.

**D397. An unannotated method's form follows its declaration, never its body** (2026-10-27; supersedes D92's body rule; amends D74 and D385; amended by D430, D448 and D460). An unannotated method that implements a plain member of an interface or base class its class declares, or that is a protocol method, is checked as a plain function: a body that can pause, or let a `throw` or a listed failure escape, is EK4301. A protocol method is one JavaScript calls by name without reading a declaration: `toString`, `toLocaleString`, `valueOf`, `toJSON`, `then`, and a method keyed by a well-known symbol or by a protocol symbol of the pinned `effect`, such as `Equal.symbol` and `Hash.symbol`, which the table pinned with Effect lists (D369). Every other unannotated method is an Effect method, cold to TypeScript as an unannotated function is. Reason: under D92 a method's TypeScript type followed its body, so adding a service call to `toString` silently gave `` `${c}` ``, `JSON.stringify`, `+`, and a thenable an Effect object, and adding one to `reset()` turned a TypeScript caller's `counter.reset()` into a call that does nothing; D320 removed body-driven decisions for calls, and D385 says every VibeLang function is cold to TypeScript. Rejected: keeping D92 with only the protocol-name check, which would still let a body change a method's TypeScript signature. Language change: an unannotated method that is not a protocol method and does not implement a plain member returns a cold `Effect` to TypeScript callers, where it was plain when its body ran no Effect; a protocol method whose body can pause is EK4301. Annotate a method whose TypeScript callers need a plain value.

**D398. A contract-less function type carries channels only as a parameter's type** (2026-10-27; amends D12, D68 and D331; amended by D434 and D454; superseded by D500). D12's rule covers a parameter of any function, method, or function expression, and E and R are type parameters of the function that has the parameter. Anywhere else, as the type of a variable, a property, an interface's method or field, an element, or a return, a contract-less function type means what TypeScript means, a plain function type, and a function that can pause or fail there is EK4301; a contract written there says what it may do. Reason: D331 extended the channels to interfaces, aliases, and declarations, where no function carries the type parameters: a method `visit<E, R>(n): Effect<void, E, R>` would let the caller choose E and accept no implementation that fails, `Effect<void, any, any>` loses the channels, and an implicitly generic interface changes its arity for TypeScript, so two implementations could not agree and one reading was unsound. Language change: a `.vibe` interface member or variable typed with a contract-less function type is plain, and storing a function there that can pause or fail is EK4301.

**D399. A trusted call's listed failures are expected failures in every function** (2026-10-27; amends D101, D282 and D329). The exceptions a trusted contract lists, `JSON.parse`'s `SyntaxError` among them, are typed failures wherever the call is: in a function with a plain contract, one that can escape is EK2003 under a plain annotation and EK4301 in a plain callback, exactly as an escaping `throw` is, and the function's own `try` may catch it. No annotation turns an expected failure into a defect.
