Reason: D329 kept D101's clause that listed failures are defects in plain code, so `function annotated(text: string): unknown { return JSON.parse(text) }` failed with a defect that no `catch` around its caller could recover, while the unannotated function failed with a recoverable `SyntaxError`: an annotation changed which recovery runs, which no rule of Chapter 13's list allows, and D282 says a plain function never fails. Rejected: bridging typed failures across JavaScript frames (G15), which a compile error makes unnecessary. Language change: a plain function or callback whose listed failure can escape is a compile error; `lines.map((l) => JSON.parse(l))` becomes `forEach(lines, (l) => JSON.parse(l))`, a loop in an Effect function, or a `try` inside the callback.

**D400. A `trusted(impl, [Errors])` value is a plain trusted call in plain code** (2026-10-27; amends D249, D329 and D389; amended by D431 and D445; amended by D514, which makes the value one plain function and withdraws `f[$direct]` and the plain marker). The wrapper `trusted` returns carries its implementation as its plain form, `f[$direct]`, a function that calls `impl` and classifies its exceptions as the wrapper does, and its type carries the plain marker (D411). In a plain function or callback, a call of it is a plain trusted call of that form: a listed exception is an expected failure (D399), and any other is a defect; elsewhere the call runs the `Effect`, as before. Reason: the specification recommended `trusted(impl, [Errors])` as the repair for EK4401 in plain functions, but a call of the wrapper runs an `Effect`, so it was EK2003 in a plain function and EK4301 in a plain callback, and the repair did not compile. Language change: a `trusted` value whose listed failures cannot escape is callable in plain code; the runtime's `trusted` attaches the plain form.

**D401. `using` adopts a release only from an acquisition's record, never from `Scope` in a contract** (2026-10-27; amends D25 and D342; amended by D425, D451 and D481). `using x = e` runs `e` with the block's Scope. When `e` gives `v`: if an acquisition recorded that it registered the release of `v` itself in that Scope, the acquisition owns `v` and `using` adds nothing; otherwise, if `v` has a disposal method, `using` owns its one disposal; otherwise nothing more happens for `null`, `undefined`, or an initializer that requires `Scope`, and any other value is a `TypeError` defect. Only acquisitions record ownership: the runtime's `acquireRelease` and `acquireDisposable`, which TypeScript code calls and which every VibeLang reference to Effect's `Effect.acquireRelease` or `Effect.acquireDisposable` denotes, by symbol, each registering and recording in one uninterruptible step. `Scope` in `e`'s requirements only lets `using` bind a value it cannot dispose. `using` holds interruption off around a pausing initializer whose type can have a disposal method, until the owner is settled, and an acquisition with `{ interruptible: true }` keeps that policy for its acquire step inside the mask. Reason: D342 classified an initializer as a scoped acquisition by `Scope` in its requirements, and a contract may be wider than its body, so `function lease(): Lease!!Scope { return new Lease() }` made `using held = lease()` dispose nothing; a function that acquired an unrelated resource and returned a fresh `Disposable` did the same; and `Effect.scoped(Effect.succeed(disposable))` registers nothing on `effect@4.0.6`. Prior art: ECMAScript's `using` disposes the value it is given; Rust's ownership moves with the value, not with a capability. Rejected: deciding from whether the initializer registered any finalizer, since one may release another value; and rejecting every `Disposable` from a `Scope`-requiring initializer unless the acquisition is written at the `using`, which would reject wrappers that are correct. Language change: a `Disposable` that nothing recorded is disposed once where it was not disposed; a TypeScript acquisition of a `Disposable` must use the runtime's `acquireRelease` or `acquireDisposable`, or `using` disposes the value a second time (Chapter 26's trusted base).

**D402. A producer is one value, a `Stream`; its consumer decides the fiber** (2026-10-27; amends D26, D98 and D371). Calling a `function*` returns a `Stream` that carries the producer. Nothing converts it: a declared type, a parameter, or a return passes the same value on. `for...of` over that value runs the producer on the loop's fiber, whatever the static type; every other consumer, a Stream operator or runner or TypeScript code, reads it through the `Stream` interface, whose pull runs the producer on the adapter's fiber, afresh for each consumption. A `Stream` whose R holds a control operation is EK4201 where it reaches a consumer other than `for...of`. Reason: D26 converted a producer "wherever a `Stream` is expected", so `const b: Stream<number> = source()` inserted a separate fiber that the unannotated `const a = source()` did not, changing fiber identity, handler reach, and lifetime by an annotation, while Chapter 8 said a loop depends only on its value; and a declaration consumer saw only `Stream`, so source and declaration consumers could disagree. Rejected: an explicit `toStream(producer)`, which would cost tokens at every Stream operator call and leave TypeScript consumers, who see a `Stream`, needing the same adapter anyway. Language change: a producer reached through a `Stream`-typed binding and iterated with `for...of` runs on the loop's fiber, so a control operation it performs reaches the loop's handlers.

**D403. A module declaration compiles to a JavaScript module of its own** (2026-10-27; supersedes D303's inline lowering; amends D364). The compiler emits each module declaration the file imports as a module beside the file's output, `main.vibe.text.js`, and the file imports it with that specifier where the source wrote the identifier, so ECMAScript's own module evaluation orders it, its imports, and the file's other dependencies, with live bindings, temporal dead zones, cycles, and top-level `await` as ECMAScript defines them. Reason: D303 kept the body inline and hoisted its imports to the file's top level, which runs a later module declaration's dependencies before an earlier declaration's body: with `module first` pushing `"first"` and `module second` importing a module that pushes `"dependency"`, ECMAScript's order is `first,dependency` and the inline lowering gave `dependency,first`. Declaration output already wrote each body to a file of its own. Language change: a module declaration's dependencies now evaluate in import order, where they ran before every inline body. Cases `module-declaration-order`, `module-declaration-live-bindings`, `module-declaration-cycle`, `module-declaration-reexport`.

**D404. JavaScript's own `for...of` is emitted only where its iterator close keeps both failures** (2026-10-27; amends D356 and D371; amended by D424, which withdraws the run-time check). The compiler may emit the native loop only with the runtime's close helper around every exit, the head's destructuring and defaults included, which combines a failing `return()` with the failure in flight, or where the emitted code checks at run time that the iterator has no `return` method, as the built-in iterators of arrays, strings, `Map`s, and `Set`s have none. A loop whose body can pause runs through the runtime loop. The static type no longer decides the emit. Reason: Chapter 8 said a static type with no `Stream` and a body that cannot pause made JavaScript's loop "already" call `return()` on every exit, but when a synchronous body fails and `return()` throws, ECMAScript's `IteratorClose` keeps the body's failure and drops the second, which Chapter 17's iterator rule keeps; a Node check during the review recorded the drop. Language change: none for the language's meaning; implementations lose an invalid shortcut.

**D405. Every arm's test runs** (2026-10-27; supersedes D294; amends D368). An exhaustive match's last arm is tested like every other arm, with its reads, matcher expressions, and comparisons, and a last arm whose test fails is a defect. Reason: D368 omitted a test when its reads were a data value's tag or literal-typed fields, which it called reads that run no code, but a literal-typed property may be a getter or a proxy trap: a structurally valid value of `data Only { One }` whose `get _tag(): "One"` counts its reads is read once by Effect's own variant test and not at all by the omitted test, so the shortcut changed an observation. Language change: a subject with getters observes the last arm's reads.

**D406. `throw` has one meaning, and `Effect.failCause` rethrows a cause** (2026-10-27; supersedes D177 and D348). `throw e` fails with `Fail(e)` whatever `e` is. A `throw` whose operand's type is a Cause type, or a union with one, is EK2140, because it would fail with the `Cause` object itself; `Effect.failCause(cause)` fails with every reason, as in Effect code. Reason: D348 decided by the operand's declared type whether `throw x` rethrew the reasons or failed with the object, so widening a `Cause` to `unknown` turned a propagating defect or interruption into a recoverable expected failure without a diagnostic, and Chapters 20 and 26 gave `throw` the single meaning `Fail(e)` while Chapter 17 gave it two. `throw` meaning different things by context is what D282 exists to prevent, and Effect's own idiom is `Effect.failCause`. Rejected: a typed elaboration to a separate `failCause` transition, which keeps two meanings for one keyword. Language change: `throw cause` with a Cause-typed operand is a compile error; write `Effect.failCause(cause)`.

**D407. `error` declarations are nominal, and a class arm counts toward exhaustiveness only for a closed class** (2026-10-27; amends D278 and D346; amended by D427, D428 and D465; amended by D501; superseded for coverage by D515). An `error` declaration's class has a private member that no code names, as TypeScript makes every class with a private member nominal, and its declaration file writes it; another package's class with the same tag and fields, or the same declaration in a second installed copy of its package, is not assignable to it without a cast. A class arm removes its class from a match's subject only when the class is closed: an `error` declaration, or an error class of the pinned `effect`, where neither it nor a class it extends declares its own `[Symbol.hasInstance]` or `[customMatcher]`. Any other class arm removes nothing, as a guarded arm does. Reason: D346 counted any concrete class with a literal tag as covered by its arm, but structural assignability let another package's same-shaped class reach a match proven exhaustive and fail its `instanceof` test, and full class semantics (D278) let a class define `static [Symbol.hasInstance]` to reject its own instances. Error classes keep every class semantics; only the credit their arms get toward exhaustiveness depends on this. Rejected: counting only literal-tag unions and `data` types as closed, which would require `default` in every match over error classes. Language change: an error from another package or a second copy of one is a type error where it was accepted; a match over classes a `.ts` file declares needs `default`.

**D408. A fiber belongs to a region the source names, never to braces** (2026-10-27; amends D19, D47, D166 and D353; amended by D464). A fiber's owner is the innermost of: a function body, an arrow's expression body included; one iteration of a loop body; a `try`, `catch`, or `finally` block; a block that declares `using`; and a `provide` or mask block. A block that is only braces, such as an `if` branch or a bare block statement, owns nothing. Reason: under "the innermost block", `if (flag) fork work()` lived until the function returned and `if (flag) { fork work() }` was interrupted at its `}`, while the formatter may add braces around a body of one statement and D372 says it changes layout only; D353 had removed this dependence for arrows alone. Prior art: Kotlin's `coroutineScope` and Trio's nurseries are named scopes, not braces. Language change: a fork in an `if` branch, a `switch` case, or a bare block now lives until its enclosing named region exits, where it was interrupted at the block's end; EK1005's repair becomes `detach(() => …)`.

**D409. A fiber stays with its owner by a flow rule the compiler checks** (2026-10-27; amends D353 and D386; amended by D455, D464 and D469). The value a `fork` gives may go only to a `const` or `let` of its owning region and reads of it, `join`, the prelude's `interrupt`, a function of the pinned `Fiber` module whose result type holds no `Fiber` and the methods those functions give, and a `fork` operand, await element, or `for concurrent` body in the same region. Anything else is EK4838: a return, an assignment outside the region, a collection, any other call, or a capture by a function literal or block param. Reason: "captured by a function that outlives the block" had no static criterion, and `registry.add(fiber)` or `queue.offer(fiber)` escaped with no diagnostic. Language change: those escapes are compile errors.

**D410. A handler's left-out returns and its continuation's uses are read from syntax** (2026-10-27; amends D351; EK2128; amended by D440). For a handle statement's answer type, the returns left out and checked afterwards are every `return` of a clause that some path through the clause reaches after a non-tail `resume` of that clause. In a clause of a `handle(...)` call, `k` may appear only as the first argument of `resume`, `resumeWith`, or `abandon` in the clause's own body, outside any nested function; any other use is EK2128. Reason: "computed from a non-tail `resume`'s result" defined no dataflow, so two checkers would leave out different returns, and "`k` stays inside the clause" was stated with no check or restriction. Language change: storing, returning, or capturing `k` is a compile error.

**D411. Every plain function carries a type-only plain marker, and a declaration consumer trusts it** (2026-10-27; amends D268, D366 and D384; amended by D421, D431, D445 and D483; superseded by D500). A function that compiles to plain JavaScript, one with a plain return annotation and an eligible unannotated one alike, declares `$plain` as a type alias in a namespace merged with it, never as a value. A declaration consumer calls a function that carries the marker as a trusted plain call whose exceptions are defects. Reason: D384's marker was a `const` in the namespace, so stock TypeScript accepted `double.$plain(2)` while the runtime supplied only `double[$direct]`, a value the declaration promised and the program lacked; and a plain-annotated export had no marker, so every consumer compiled against declarations reported EK4401 on it while the same function's unannotated form was trusted. Checked with TypeScript 5.9.3: the type alias merges with the function, `type P = double.$plain` resolves, and `double.$plain(2)` is TS2339. Language change: declaration consumers accept calls of plain-annotated VibeLang exports; TypeScript code that called `double.$plain` gets TS2339.

**D412. A plain function's recursion continues on heap frames whether or not it is annotated** (2026-10-27; amends D10 and D333). A function with a plain return annotation is an ordinary JavaScript function to its callers, and its own plain call cycles recurse as an unannotated plain cycle does: on the JavaScript stack, then past the depth budget on heap frames that run to completion before the outermost call returns, since nothing in a plain function can pause. Recursion through a synchronous foreign callback keeps the host's limit, annotated or not. Reason: an unannotated `sumTo(100000)` completed while `function sumTo(n: number): number` died with `RangeError`, so an annotation changed a program's exit, a place Chapter 13's list did not name. Language change: deep recursion of plain-annotated functions completes where it overflowed.

**D413. Chapter 13 lists every place a type decides** (2026-10-27; amends the list D320 introduced; amended by D447). The list now has nine entries: whether a call runs, stated as the language's one deliberate type-directed execution; a function value's form; a contract-less callback type's provenance; a method's form where it implements a plain member; a plain return annotation's TypeScript signature; data-type methods; an await operation's non-literal operand; whether `using` holds interruption off; and `wire`'s build order. The `Cause` throw (D406) and the `for...of` emit (D404) leave it, and optimizations are outside it because rule E11 makes them unobservable. Reason: the list called itself closed while D101's failure classification, a plain annotation's stack limit, D92's methods, D331's provenance, and D363's build order decided behavior outside it; this revision removes the first three and lists the rest.

**D414. A repeated `infer` name is EK2129** (2026-10-27; amends D57; amended by D504). A name other than `_` written after `infer` in two channels of one contract is EK2129, since the channels are not connected and TypeScript's repeated `infer` would connect them. Reason: `Effect<infer Value, infer Value, never>` read as a promise the checker does not keep. Language change: such contracts are compile errors.

**D415. A service key called with arguments is EK3007** (2026-10-27; amends D281). For a service whose shape is a function type, `Fn(3)` is EK3007, whose quick fix writes `Fn()(3)`: the key's call takes no arguments and returns the implementation. Reason: the call drew TypeScript's TS2348 with a suggestion of `new`, which is EK3003. Language change: a better error for a program that never compiled.

**D416. EK9100 is an error in every renderer** (2026-10-27; amends D248 and D366). A renderer other than the compiler that cannot give a program the compiler's meaning reports EK9100, an error that no setting lowers; the emit switch and the await-element clause leave its description. Reason: D366 says such a renderer reports an error, while the registry made EK9100 an adjustable warning and still described await elements that D377 made programs by syntax. Language change: none for the compiler.

**D417. An array pattern over an iterable that may not be an array is EK4704** (2026-10-27; amends D214). Where a subject's type admits an iterable that is not an array, an array pattern is rejected with EK4704; `match ([...xs])` matches the iterable's elements. Reason: Chapter 30 listed the run-time no-match as a restriction, but a restriction never gives a program another meaning (D374), and the proposal iterates such a subject. Language change: those patterns are compile errors.

**D418. A curried pipe stage is a call** (2026-10-27; amends D272). `x |> f(args)(%)` evaluates `x`, then `f(args)`, then calls the result with the topic, under rule R like any call. Only typing borrows from `x.pipe(f(args))`: `f(args)` is checked with the stage's parameter as its contextual type, so data-last functions infer their type arguments from `x`; the stage never reads a `pipe` member of `x`. Reason: "types and lowers as `x.pipe(f(args))`" could read a custom `pipe` member, or none, and could classify the call differently from rule R. Language change: none for Effect's data types, whose `pipe` is the same call.

**D419. The grammar is normative, and collections convert by one ordered rule** (2026-10-27; amends D49 and D111; its collection rule is superseded by D423; amended by D443 and D452). Appendix "Grammar" states VibeLang's changes to TypeScript's pinned grammar: its contextual words and where each is a keyword, the productions for contracts, declarations, statements, expressions, and patterns, the line-break restrictions, and the precedence. Where a destination converts a collection of functions to programs, the argument converts as part of its own evaluation, before the call: an array literal element by element, any other array or iterable read once into a new array with its iterator closed if reading fails, and a record's own enumerable string-keyed properties read once into a new record, a later key replacing an earlier one. An await operation's object operand with equal keys keeps the later element and starts only its program. Reason: the rules were reconstructible only from chapters and decisions, and the conversion's reads, enumeration time, duplicate keys, and failure cleanup were unstated. Language change: none intended; an implementation that enumerated a converted iterable lazily now reads it at the call.

**D420. EK3205 is an error by default** (2026-10-27; amends D387). An entry file whose `main` needs a service that nothing provides is EK3205, an error by default, which a project may still lower. Reason: as a warning, the program compiled and died with `Service not found` when the host started it. Language change: such entry files fail to compile unless the setting lowers the code.
## The third review: forms, collections, masks, and declarations (2026-10-27)

A third review on 2026-10-27, by four reviewers working apart, found that an Effect contract and a plain variant gave one function two forms, that a function literal's own form was read before its parameters had types, that a parameter type still made a call traverse a collection, that the native loop's entry check and `using`'s mask read less than a run-time value can hold, and that declaration files lost a call's provenance and installed copies of a package shared a type. These decisions repair them. Each names what it amends, and its last sentence says what changes for an implementation.

**D421. An Effect contract keeps a function's Effect own form, and the plain marker records own form** (2026-10-27; amends D67, D268, D384, D392 and D411; amended by D445 and D448; amended by D500). A function's own form is plain only for a plain return annotation, or, without an annotation, when it is eligible; a function with an Effect contract has Effect own form whatever its body does. The plain variant the compiler may give an Effect-contract function is an optimization of its calls, held to rule E11, never a form a destination receives: where only a plain function fits, it is EK4301 to source and declaration consumers alike. The plain marker is emitted exactly for functions whose own form is plain, and it goes with the declaration under another name, through `export *`, as a default export, and through a `const` bound to it. For eligibility, a call of an Effect-contract function is an Effect operation even where the callee has a plain variant, because a caller sees the contract, not the body; inside a strongly connected component, a call counts as plain for eligibility only when its callee has no Effect contract. Chapter 13 tabulates each kind of function's own form, call, TypeScript representation, and marker, and a function stored where no type is declared keeps that representation, so an exported `const` arrow is cold to TypeScript and its own form is still its body's. Reason: rule F gave `twice(n: number): number!!` Effect own form and made `[1, 2].map(twice)` EK4301, while Chapters 15 and 16 and Chapter 27's rule 3 passed its plain variant to `map` and gave `[2, 4]`; the marker did not record which, so source and declaration consumers could not both follow F. Prior art: an `async` TypeScript function that awaits nothing still returns a `Promise`; its signature, not its body, is its interface. Rejected: letting a verified plain variant reach a plain destination, one reviewer's alternative, since a caller's acceptance would then change when an annotated function's body changes, which a written contract exists to prevent (Chapter 14), and declarations would need a second marker for the body's eligibility. Language change: an Effect-annotated function passed where a plain function is expected is EK4301 (write a plain annotation, `(n: number): number`); an unannotated function that calls an Effect-annotated one is not eligible, so its own form is Effect and passing it as a plain callback is EK4301.

**D422. A function literal's own form is read with its parameter types** (2026-10-27; amends D392; amended by D450, which corrects its reason, and by D482; superseded by D500). Eligibility reads a literal's body with its parameter types: those it writes, and for a parameter it leaves unannotated, the type its destination's contextual signature gives, as TypeScript types a callback's parameters. The destination's return type never decides the own form. In overload resolution, each candidate gives such a literal its own contextual parameter types, so the literal has an own form per candidate, and where the candidates a step accepts give it different own forms the call is EK1021, whose repair writes the literal's parameter types or return type. Reason: rule F said the own form was "fixed before any destination is read", but `(x) => x.find(id)` has none until `x` has a type: read with `x` implicitly `any`, its call is plain; read under a candidate that types `x` as a service, it runs an Effect. TypeScript itself types context-sensitive arguments per candidate in `chooseOverload`, and the specification's own evidence classified each literal per candidate. Language change: none against D392's intent; a call whose candidates make a literal's body differ in form is EK1021 instead of taking the first candidate.

**D423. Only a literal converts element by element; nothing traverses a collection for a destination** (2026-10-27; supersedes D111 and D419's collection rule; amends D49 and D69; amended by D453). Where a destination's element or field type converts functions to programs, an array literal written there converts each element after evaluating it and an object literal each field, as rule F converts any function at its destination, and the elements a spread in such a literal supplies convert as the spread reads them, once, at its place, as ECMAScript's spread reads, by the spread's static element type: a function type with no required parameters converts, and an element type that mixes one with other members does not, so no run-time test decides. Any other value, a variable, a call's result, a `Set`, or a record built elsewhere, is passed as the value it is: where its elements or fields are functions and the destination expects Effects, the call is TypeScript's own type error, whose note and quick fix write the spread, `all([...jobs])` or `all({ ...jobs })`. Reason: D419 read a nonliteral iterable into a new array before the call when, and only when, the parameter type converted it, so the same `discard` function, reached through `(xs: unknown) => string` and through `(xs: Iterable<Effect<number>>) => string`, drained an iterable in one call and not the other, ran its iterator code, and received a new collection; Chapter 13 says no parameter type changes what runs, and a TypeScript reader never expects passing a variable to iterate it. It also copied an array that already held Effects, which `Effect.all` would otherwise read when it runs. A literal is built where it stands, so converting its elements traverses nothing that existed before; a spread is the traversal, written where it happens. Rejected: listing the traversal among the places a type decides, one reviewer's alternative, which keeps a drain a reader cannot see; and a lazy view converting as the callee iterates, which cannot stand in for an array or a record that a callee reads by index or key. Language change: `all(jobs)` over an array, iterable, or record of functions is a type error; `all([...jobs])`, the quick fix, reads `jobs` at the call as D419 did; an array of Effects is passed uncopied.

**D424. JavaScript's own `for...of` is emitted only with the combining close helper** (2026-10-27; amends D404). The native loop needs the runtime's close helper around every exit that can close the iterator, and the helper looks `return` up only when it closes, once per close, as `IteratorClose` does. The run-time check that the iterator has no `return` method is withdrawn, and the trusted base no longer assumes that built-in iterators keep their methods. Reason: a custom iterator can gain a `return` method while the loop runs, which an entry check does not see, and `IteratorClose` then calls it and drops its exception when the body is failing, so the shortcut lost a cleanup failure the iterator rule keeps; and the check read `return` once more than the reference loop, which a counting getter sees (rule E11). A reviewer's Node check recorded both. Language change: none for the language's meaning; implementations lose a second invalid shortcut.

**D425. `using` holds interruption off around a pausing initializer unless its value can only be a primitive** (2026-10-27; amends D401; amended by D451; superseded by D481). Where evaluating `e` can pause, `using` holds interruption off from the start of `e` until the value's owner is settled unless `e`'s value type admits only `null`, `undefined`, `void`, `never`, and primitive types, since only an object can carry a disposal method `using` may own. `object`, `unknown`, `any`, a type parameter, an object type that declares no disposal method, and a union with any of them hold it off. An acquisition with `{ interruptible: true }` inside `e` keeps that policy for its acquire step. Reason: Chapter 6 chose the owner from the run-time value but the mask from whether the static type declared a disposal method, so `lease(): object!!Scope`, returning a `Lease` after a pause, was unmasked: cancellation during the pause left the lease acquired and never released, while the same function typed `Lease` was masked and released it; a `Disposable` is assignable to `object` without a cast. A reviewer's run against `effect@4.0.6` gave `["acquire"]` unmasked and `["acquire","release"]` masked. Language change: a `using` whose initializer can pause and whose value type does not name a disposal method holds interruption off around the initializer; cancellation reaches only its acquisitions' interruptible steps.

**D426. Declaration emit keeps a call's data provenance with `AsData<T>`** (2026-10-27; amends D366 and D393; narrowed by D449; superseded by D458). Where declaration emit writes a call signature whose return type is definitely an Effect while the declared return type that rule R reads in the source is not, it writes the return type as `AsData<T>`, the runtime's alias `type AsData<T> = T`. Rule R reads an alias through its body, here a type parameter, so a call of the emitted signature returns data, as a consumer of the source's call does, and stock TypeScript sees `T`. This covers every signature emit writes from an inferred type, an exported `const` bound to a generic call's result, an extracted or bound method, a returned callback, a property of an inferred object type, and their aliases and re-exports, in the `.ts` files the compiler compiles as well as `.vibe` files. A declaration stock TypeScript emitted carries no alias, and its written signature decides, as every foreign declaration's does. Reason: rule R reads `compose`'s enclosing `C` uninstantiated, so for a `.ts` file's `export const composed = compose(f, g)` whose `g` returns an Effect, `composed("x")` returned the Effect as data to a consumer of the source, while the declaration TypeScript writes, `(a: string) => Effect<number>`, has no type parameter left, and a declaration consumer ran it, against Chapter 27's first rule; a reviewer's probe emitted exactly that. Rejected: reading every export as its emitted signature, which would make extracting `compose(f, g)("x")` into a `const` change whether the call runs; and a call-kind marker per export, which cannot reach signatures nested in properties and returned functions, where an alias at the return type can. Language change: none for source consumers; declaration consumers of such exports return data where they ran.

**D427. Two installed copies of one package version are one type and two classes; a class arm needs one copy** (2026-10-27; amends D407; superseded by D515: a class arm tests the tag, which both copies carry). TypeScript identifies two installed copies of a package by name and version and gives their declarations one type, while the run time loads two constructors, so the private brand separates packages, never copies. A class arm removes its class only when, besides being closed, its package resolves to one installed copy in the program's module resolution; where the compiler finds a second copy that TypeScript merged, the arm removes nothing and EK4701 names the copy. The trusted base gains the assumption that the run time loads each package from the copy the compiler resolved. Every arm's test still runs (D405), so a value from a copy the compiler never saw fails the match as a defect and never takes another arm. Reason: a reviewer's probe compiled `const value: Left = new Right()` for two installed copies of one `same-error@1.0.0`, each declaring the private member, and `value instanceof Left` was `false`, while Chapters 3 and 14 said the private member made the copies unassignable. Rejected: requiring `default` for every class from another package, which would make a shared package's error families unmatchable without one. Language change: a match whose coverage counts a class of a package installed twice is EK4701.

**D428. A class arm covers only what its test is guaranteed to accept** (2026-10-27; amends D346 and D407; amended by D515: an open family's base arm removes nothing). An arm that names a closed class, an open family's `error` base included, matches every value of that class's type and every class below it; an arm that names a class that is not closed, `Error` and a class a `.ts` file declares included, removes nothing, since a value of its type need not pass its `instanceof` test. Reason: Chapter 3 said "an arm that names an open class itself, or a class above it, matches every value of that type", which gave coverage to classes whose structural type or own membership test admits values their test rejects, against the closed-class rule beside it. Language change: a match that relied on `when Error:` or a `.ts` class arm to cover its subject needs `default`.

**D429. Four stale statements follow their rules** (2026-10-27; no rule changes). Chapter 24 said a plain-annotated function keeps the host stack limit, which D412 removed: a plain cycle continues on heap frames, annotated or not, and only recursion through a synchronous foreign callback keeps the host's limit. Chapter 4's `Sessions.make`, an unannotated static method that neither implements a plain member nor is a protocol method, was an Effect method under D397, so `Layer.sync`'s callback calling it was EK4301; it now has the plain annotation `Sessions.Shape`. Chapter 1 called `welcome`, which calls services, "a plain function", the glossary's term for a function that performs no Effect operation; it is an ordinary function declaration. Chapter 2 said the order of overloads never changes whether a call runs; where two overloads accept the same arguments, the first decides, as in TypeScript, and what the order never changes is the form a function argument takes (D392). Reason: each sentence contradicted a rule in force. Language change: none.

**D430. An Effect never satisfies a plain function type** (2026-10-27; amends D397 and rule F; amended by D448, D453 and D485; amended by D500; amended by D512 and D513). In a `.vibe` file, wherever TypeScript checks one type against another, in an assignment, an argument, a return, an initializer, or an `implements` clause, a call signature or method of the target whose return type is neither definitely an Effect nor a union with such a member is not matched by a source signature or method whose return type is definitely an Effect: that check is EK4301. Rule F's conversions apply first, so a function value whose own form is plain still reaches a plain destination as its plain variant; the rule checks objects, class instances, and function values known only by their types. A class meets a plain member only through a plain method: one with a plain annotation, a protocol method, or one that implements that member of a type its class declares (D397). An object literal's method is a function at its field's destination, so rule F gives it its form, and a protocol method in an object literal is checked as a plain function wherever the literal goes. Reason: TypeScript makes `() => Effect<void>` assignable to `() => void`, so `const target: Resettable = new Counter()`, with `Counter`'s unannotated `reset()` an Effect method under D397, type-checked, and every `target.reset()` returned a program nobody ran; a reviewer's probe left the counter unchanged through a `.vibe` interface and through a TypeScript caller. Prior art: Kotlin rejects a `suspend fun` implementing a non-suspend member, and typescript-eslint's `no-misused-promises` (`checksVoidReturn`) rejects the hot-Promise form of this mistake. Language change: assigning an object, a class instance, or a function value whose method or signature returns an Effect to a type that expects a plain function is EK4301; declare the interface with `implements`, or annotate the method.

**D431. A class names its plain methods in a marker, and a `trusted` value's marker lists its error classes** (2026-10-27; amends D400 and D411; amended by D460 and D483; amended by D500). A class, or an exported object, whose methods include plain ones declares a type-only `$plain` member in the namespace merged with it, whose type is the union of those methods' names, and `$plainStatic` for its plain static methods; a declaration consumer calls a named method as a trusted plain call. A `trusted(impl, [Errors])` value's marker is its type, the runtime's `Trusted<F, Errors>`, which records its plain signature and its listed classes. A declaration consumer's plain trusted call treats an exception of a class the marker lists as an expected failure (D399) and any other as a defect; only a `trusted` value's marker lists any. Reason: a namespace merges with a function, a class, or a `const`, never with a method, so a plain method of an exported class, `toString` and `format` included, was foreign code, EK4401, to every declaration consumer, the state D411 set out to end; and Chapter 27's rule 2 and the trusted base said every function a marker names throws only defects, while D400 made a `trusted` value's listed exceptions expected failures in plain code and D411 gave it the marker. Language change: declaration consumers accept calls of a class's plain methods, and a `trusted` value's listed failure in a declaration consumer's plain code is typed, so one that escapes is EK2003 or EK4301 there as in source.

**D432. The prelude's `fork`, `join`, `sync`, `try`, and `callback` are used only under their own names** (2026-10-27; amends D330, D391 and D396; amended by D452). An import that renames one of them, and any reference to one other than as the callee of a call spelled with its own name, such as `const spawn = fork`, is EK4410. A call spelled `fork(x)` or `join(x)` is the keyword when the name resolves to the prelude's binding, and an ordinary call when it resolves to a binding the program declares or imports; a hatch is recognized the same way. `join` joins `fork` in the prelude's table. Reason: rule H5 made every import alias of the prelude's `fork` the keyword, so `spawn(work())` deferred `work()`, a by-name argument chosen by a callee's resolved symbol, which a reader of the call cannot see; a reviewer's probe printed `after-spawn;work;`, and another reviewer found that a `const` alias and an import alias behaved differently. The hatches' first argument, which rule D lists as deferred, had the same exposure through a renamed import. Rejected: making a renamed `fork` an ordinary call of the runtime's function, one reviewer's alternative, since the name would then mean two things by spelling; and dropping the hatches' single-expression form, which D396 keeps for its tokens and which a fixed spelling makes visible. Language change: a renamed import of a keyword's or hatch's binding, and a stored reference to `fork` or `join`, are compile errors.

**D433. A `fork` of a program value is EK4613** (2026-10-27; amends D394). A `fork` whose operand's type is a program, definitely an Effect or a function with no required parameters, is EK4613, as an await element of that type is, since the new fiber's result would be that program unrun; the repair writes `fork job()`. Reason: with `const job = () => heartbeat()`, `fork job` forked `() => job`, whose fiber succeeded with the function and never ran `heartbeat`, a silent no-op that D394 had closed for await elements only. Language change: such forks are compile errors.

**D434. A written contract names a contract-less callback's channels `E` and `R`** (2026-10-27; amends D68 and D398; amended by D455; superseded by D500). In a function that has a contract-less callback parameter, a written contract names the channels D68 adds as `E` and `R`, then `E2` and `R2` for the second such parameter, in parameter order, in its return annotation and its other parameter types, so `run<A>(work: () => A): A!TxError | E!R` passes the callback's channels through. A contract that does not name them keeps its meaning. Where the signature names one while a type parameter of that name is already in scope there, from the signature or an enclosing declaration, the name is EK2130, whose repair writes the callback's channels as type parameters of the author's choosing. Reason: a service member must write its contract, and `service Tx { run<A>(work: () => A): A!TxError }` gave `work` channels no text could name, so every implementation that called `work` was EK2001 and EK2002; `withTransaction`, `withLock`, and `bracket` members all have this shape. Rejected: requiring explicit type parameters for every such member, which costs tokens at each declaration, against the language's terse forms, for a meaning D68 already gives the names. Language change: such contracts compile; a colliding name is EK2130.

**D435. A type parameter at a destination is read by one ordered rule, and `flow` does not compose Effect functions** (2026-10-27; amends D324 and D393; amended by D453, D488 and D494; amended by D513). A destination's type parameter that another argument, the receiver, or an explicit type argument fixes is read as its instantiation; one that only the function itself fixes is read as not an Effect, so as a destination function type's return type it accepts the plain form, and as the whole destination it accepts the function's own form, as any type that is not a function type, an Effect, or a constrained type parameter does (`any`, `unknown`, `object`, `Function`). A constraint includes an Effect when it is definitely an Effect or a union with a member that is. So `flow(parse, fetchUser)` is EK4301, since `flow` calls its second function as a plain function returning its type parameter, and rule R's example and Chapter 30's case use `new Box(program).get()` alone. Reason: "counts as no type" read either as the "None" row or as the plain row, the table had no row for `any`, `unknown`, `object`, or a receiver-fixed parameter, and rule R's example `flow(parse, fetchUser)(raw)`, with a conformance case requiring it to return data, was EK4301 under rule F, as a reviewer's probe reported. Language change: none against rule F; the `flow` example and its case are withdrawn.

**D436. EK4802 spares a type parameter the receiving function returns** (2026-10-27; amends D321; amended by D463). A callback's contextual success type names no value only when it is `void`, `unknown`, `any`, or an unconstrained type parameter that the declared return type of the function receiving the callback does not mention. So `ids.map((id) => () => fetchUser(id))` keeps its programs, since `map` returns `U[]`, and a release returning its program to `acquireRelease`, whose `unknown` result is dropped, stays EK4802. Reason: the rule rejected callbacks that build programs for a caller that keeps them, against EK4301's own repair, which writes `() => () => call()`. Language change: those callbacks compile.

**D437. `p()` on a value typed by a type parameter constrained to an Effect runs it** (2026-10-27; amends D321). `p()` runs `p` when `p`'s type has no call signature and is definitely an Effect or extends `Effect`, or is a type parameter whose constraint is. Reason: rule R said both that `p()` on an Effect value runs it and that a type parameter is never a trigger, which left `function run<T extends Effect<number>>(p: T) { return p() }` with two readings; rule F already reads such a constraint as an Effect destination, and TypeScript gives the call no other meaning. Language change: none intended; the case is stated.

**D438. A declaration that writes no return type is read through the one TypeScript infers** (2026-10-27; amends D321 and D393; amended by D449). Where the declaration a call's signature comes from writes no return type, as an unannotated TypeScript function or arrow does, rule R reads the return type TypeScript infers for that declaration, with every type parameter in scope there uninstantiated. Reason: "the return type written in the declaration" left `export const findName = (id: string) => Effect.gen(…)` with nothing written, and a literal reading made every call of it data. Language change: none intended.

**D439. A synchronous hatch's result is its value** (2026-10-27; amends D330; amended by D503). A hatch function not written `async` gives its result as it is, a `Promise` or another thenable included, as `Effect.sync` and `Effect.try` return one; only an `async` function's promise is awaited. Reason: Chapter 10 awaited any result with a callable `then`, a run-time adoption like the one D394 removed from await operations, so `sync(() => queryBuilder)` ran a lazy thenable such as a query builder that `Effect.sync` would return. Language change: a synchronous hatch function returning a promise gives the promise; write the function `async` to wait.

**D440. EK2128 covers every clause** (2026-10-27; amends D410; amended by D455). The clauses argument of `handle(...)` is an object literal, and any other clauses argument is EK2128; a `resume`, `resumeWith`, or `abandon` written in a function nested in a handle statement's clause is EK2128 too. Reason: `const clauses = { approve(r, k) { saved = k } }; handle(p, Approval, clauses)` was not syntactically a clause, so the check never saw `k` escape, and a statement clause's nested `resume` had no diagnostic. Language change: those programs are compile errors.

**D441. An object pattern matches only an object** (2026-10-27; amends D214). An object pattern matches a value whose `typeof` is `"object"` or `"function"` and that is not `null`, and reads no property of a primitive; Chapter 30 lists it as a rule, no longer as a restriction. Reason: the restriction's "run-time no-match" was either another meaning than the pattern's, which a restriction never gives (D374), or the meaning, which belongs in the rules. Language change: none; the behavior is stated as a rule.

**D442. A plain call in a module cycle before its variant exists runs the Effect form** (2026-10-27; amends D268; superseded by D446). A cross-module plain call through `f[$direct]` that runs in a module cycle before the exporting module's body has attached the variant calls the hoisted function's Effect form and runs that program at once, rethrowing a defect as its own exception. Reason: the exporter's function declaration is hoisted, so the source call succeeds there, while `f[$direct]` was still `undefined` and the call threw a `TypeError`. Language change: none for the language's meaning; the emitted call keeps it.

**D443. `fork` and `join` are keywords only before an operand, and `match`'s brace shares its line** (2026-10-27; amends D391 and D419; amended by D452). `fork` and `join` followed by `as`, `satisfies`, `in`, `instanceof`, or `is` are identifiers, so a local binding named `fork` keeps `fork as T`; a match's `{` is on the line of its parenthesized subject, and its first arm may begin on the next. Reason: the follow sets admitted any identifier, and the `match` row read as if `when` had to share the line, which no example did. Language change: none for programs that compiled.

**D444. Wording that contradicted rules in force** (2026-10-27; no rule changes). Chapter 17 defined a Scope region as "a block that declares `using` or calls `fork`", against D408's named regions; Chapter 7 said the keyword forms take every operand as a program by syntax, and that an `Exit` never runs in an await operation, both false for non-literal operands; Chapter 13 said every call expression is one of three kinds, though `fork(x)`, `join(x)`, and the await operations are syntax, gave EK1001 in a parameter default a reason E9 contradicts, and called rule R the one type-directed execution beside the await operand row; Chapter 5 named `Schema.decodeUnknown`, which `effect@4.0.6` does not export; Chapter 4 did not say that two installed copies of a package are one service; Chapter 15 did not say that `using` makes a plain function EK2003; EK4838's repairs did not show how to bound a `join`; Chapter 7 did not allow a literal of fibers as `join`'s operand; the glossary ran a producer "on its own fiber"; Chapter 3's EK2004 message showed a different source line; Part III's snippets did not state Part I's convention; Chapter 28 described its unlisted codes by implementation status; a placeholder's source was unexplained; and D392 cited Kotlin for a property its lambdas lack. Reason: each sentence contradicted a rule or a fact. Language change: none.

## The fourth review: plain calls, bound type parameters, and the rest (2026-10-27)

A fourth review on 2026-10-27, by four reviewers working apart, found that rule R forbade in plain code the calls of plain functions that other chapters allowed, that it read a function a generic call returned as returning data, that a literal's own form needed a typing TypeScript never performs, that `using`'s mask kept interruptible acquisitions from being cancelled, that the parenthesized keyword forms had two parse trees, that several rules left cases unstated, and that some statements contradicted the rules or the facts. These decisions repair them. Each names what it amends, and its last sentence says what changes for an implementation.

**D445. A call of a plain function is a plain call of its plain form, wherever it stands** (2026-10-27; amends D65, D103, D321, D400, D411 and D421; amended by D460 and D480; amended by D500). A call whose callee resolves to the declaration of a function whose own form is plain, an eligible unannotated function read from its source, a method whose own form is plain, or a function or method that a declaration's plain marker names, or to a `trusted(impl, [Errors])` value, resolves to the callee's plain call signature, the one its marker records, and is a plain call of its plain form: it binds the parameters, runs the body once, synchronously, and its value is the body's. Its E and R are `never`, or, for a `trusted` value, its listed failures, which stay expected failures (D399). It is legal in plain code, a module's top level, a class field initializer, a constructor, an accessor, a static block, a parameter default, a function with a plain contract, and a plain callback, and it starts no fiber, calls no runner, and builds no program; in Effect code the compiler may run the Effect form's program instead, with the same observations. The kind follows own form, never an optimization: a call of an Effect-contract function is a call that runs, EK1001, EK2003, or EK4301 in plain code, even where the compiler gives that function a plain variant, and every compilation emits an eligible function's plain form, one that lowers every other function to frames included. Reason: rule R classified an eligible function's call as one that runs, by its Effect signature, and so made it EK1001 at a module's top level, EK2003 under a plain annotation, and EK4301 in a plain callback, while Chapter 15 let a plain function's body call functions whose own form is plain, D65 ran plain calls inline at a module's top level, D442 required a cyclic importer's top-level call of an eligible export to give its value, and D411 had a declaration consumer call such a function as a trusted plain call, so a declaration consumer rejected less than a source consumer. Two reviewers' witness, an unannotated `double` called from a plain-annotated function, a `map` callback, a field initializer, a constructor, an accessor, a parameter default, and a top-level `const`, had two meanings, and the compiler as built accepted every call. A TypeScript reader expects `const initial = double(3)` to give 6, as it would for the TypeScript function that `double` reads as. The reviewers offered two repairs, a permission for plain code on a call that still runs, and a plain call; they agree on every program, and the second needs no exception to rule R's checks. Rejected: rejecting those calls and requiring a plain annotation on every helper that plain code calls, which costs an annotation where the compiler already knows the form and withdraws D65 and Chapter 15's plain call graph; and letting an Effect-contract function's plain variant stand in, since a caller's acceptance would then change with the body of a function whose contract says it is an Effect (D421). Language change: calls of eligible unannotated functions, of marked declarations, of plain methods, and of `trusted` values are plain calls of the plain form, legal in plain code, where rule R's checks rejected them; an implementation emits an eligible function's plain form in every compilation.

**D446. A plain function's plain form is reached through its hoisted declaration before its module's body runs** (2026-10-27; supersedes D442; amends D268; superseded by D500). The program a plain function's Effect form returns records that function's plain form, bound to the call's receiver and arguments, where the runtime reads it. A call from another module that reaches the function before the exporting module's body has attached `f[$direct]`, as one in a module cycle can, calls the hoisted function, which builds that cold program and runs none of it, then calls the recorded plain form once, synchronously, in plain code and Effect code alike; a plain function passed as a value before the attachment is passed as a function that does the same at each call. The call starts no fiber, calls no runner, and evaluates no module's body early, and a binding still in its temporal dead zone throws `ReferenceError`, as in the source's call. An Effect-contract function's plain variant is called only from code a fiber runs (D421), so a call of it before the attachment runs the hoisted function's Effect form on the current fiber, as a call compiled without the variant does. The runtime's `$plainOf` reads the plain form either way. Reason: D442 ran the Effect form's program "at once", which at a module's top level, where no fiber runs, needs a synchronous runner and a new root, against the rule that a call never starts a fiber, and it named no mechanism; and D268's run-time fallback, `f[$direct] ?? f`, passed a plain function's Effect form to `map` before the attachment, so `[1, 2].map(double)` in a module cycle gave unrun programs. Rejected: a calling convention through `this` or through a flag the runtime sets around the call, which a method call's receiver or a reentrant call could disturb. Language change: none for the language's meaning; the emitted cross-module call and the plain-form lookup change.

**D447. The list of places a type decides says what a function value's form decides** (2026-10-27; amends D413). The function-value row of Chapter 13's list says that the form a destination receives decides what the receiver's call of the function does: a call of the plain form runs the body, and a call of the Effect form builds a program whose body runs only when the receiver runs it; what the form never decides is that passing or converting the function runs anything. Rule F's section shows a trusted TypeScript API that accepts `() => number | Effect<number>`, calls its callback once, and drops the result: an unannotated callback's body runs, and the same callback with an Effect contract builds a program that never runs. Reason: the row said a function value's form never decides "when its body runs once something calls or runs it", while rule F defines the Effect form as a function whose call runs none of the body, and a reviewer's run of the two representations under one union-typed consumer counted one run of the body and none. Language change: none; rule F is unchanged.

**D448. A `const` that names a function keeps its own form; any other function value takes its type's** (2026-10-27; amends D397, D421 and D430; amended by D460 and D483; superseded by D500). A function's own form is its declaration's for a function declaration, a `const` whose initializer is a function literal, and a `const` or import alias that names such a function, through any number of them, as the plain marker follows it (D421). A class's method follows its declaration (D397); an object literal's method is a function at its field's destination, so its own form is read as a function literal's is (D430). Any other function value, a `let`, a property or field read, a parameter, or a call's result, has the form its type gives, read as rule R reads a call of it, so one whose calls return data, such as an extracted `Box<Effect<number>>`'s `get`, is plain, and one whose calls run is Effect; D430's check reads a source signature the same way. Reason: rule F gave `const g = double` the form its type gives, Effect, while Chapter 27 carried the plain marker through such a `const`, so `[1, 2].map(g)` was EK4301 from source and `[2, 4]` from declarations, and the compiler as built passed two unrun Effects; a property read's form was stated twice, as its declaration's in Chapter 13's paragraph on stored functions and as its type's in rule F; an object literal's method was an Effect method by D397 and a function by D430; and "the form its type gives" did not say whether it read the instantiated type or rule R's reading. Language change: `[1, 2].map(g)` with `const g = double` passes `double`'s plain form; `xs.map(api.double)` through an object's field is EK4301 from source and declarations alike, repaired by naming the function or annotating the field; an object literal's method that only computes is plain.

**D449. Rule R reads a type parameter by who declares it and what bound it** (2026-10-27; amends D321, D393, D426 and D438; amended by D458). A type parameter that a class, an interface, or a type alias declares is left uninstantiated in the declared return type, whatever the receiver's or an annotation's type arguments (D393). One that a function, a method, or a call signature declares is left uninstantiated in a call of that declaration itself, which binds it, and inside that declaration's body (D378); in a signature that an earlier, completed call returned, held in a `const`, a property, or a call's result, it is read as that call bound it, as TypeScript shows it. A `const` bound to a call's result declares no signature of its own; an annotation on a binding that holds a function is that binding's declaration. `AsData<T>` (D426) is needed only where declaration emit drops a class's, an interface's, or an alias's type parameter, as for an extracted or bound method of a generic class. Reason: `effect@4.0.6`'s `Effect.fn(name)(body, …pipeline)` declares its result `(...args: Args) => A`, with `A` the call signature's own type parameter, which the pipeline binds to an Effect (`Effect.d.ts` lines 17928 and 18146), so rule R read every such function, Chapter 6's `readReport` and the Effect columns of Chapters 3 and 7 among them, as returning data in a `.vibe` file, while Chapter 10 says a TypeScript function declared to return an Effect runs, a hover shows the Effect, and a stock-built declaration of the same code ran it; D438's wording also fit both readings. Reading a completed call's binding keeps D378, since `identity(program)` is a call of `identity`'s own declaration, and D393, since no call binds a class's parameter, and it makes extracting `compose(f, g)("x")` into a `const` change nothing, the property D426 had used against this reading. Language change: a call of a function that a generic call returned, such as an `Effect.fn` function with a pipeline or `compose(f, g)`, runs where its bound return type is an Effect, where it returned data; declaration emit writes no `AsData<T>` for such signatures.

**D450. A function literal's own form is read once, with the parameter types TypeScript assigns** (2026-10-27; amends D392 and D422; amended by D457 and D482; amended by D500). TypeScript types a context-sensitive literal's unannotated parameters once, from the first candidate that accepts the call's other arguments, and keeps them for later candidates; the literal's own form is read with those types. Where two candidates that accept the call's other arguments, as TypeScript's first pass finds them with the literal set aside, would give an unannotated parameter different types, and their destinations accept different forms of the function, the call is EK1021, so the order of overloads still never picks a function's form; the check reads only the candidates' parameter types. Reason: D422 gave a literal an own form per candidate and said TypeScript types context-sensitive arguments per candidate in `chooseOverload`; it does not: `g((x) => x * 2)` against `(cb: (x: string) => string)` then `(cb: (x: number) => number)` keeps `x: string` and fails the second overload with "Argument of type '(x: string) => number' is not assignable", on stock TypeScript 5.9.3 and on the pinned fork, so a per-candidate form needed a typing no checker performs, and its conformance case could not be computed. Rejected: reading the form under the first candidate's types alone, which lets declaration order pick the form, against D392. Language change: a call whose candidates would type a literal's parameter differently and take different forms is EK1021 in either order; a call whose candidates differ in parameter types and take the same form keeps TypeScript's choice.

**D451. `using`'s mask records the interruptibility it replaced, and the runtime's interruptible acquisitions run with it** (2026-10-27; amends D401 and D425; amended by D459; superseded by D481). Where `using` holds interruption off around an initializer (D425), it records the interruptibility in force before; the runtime's `acquireRelease` and `acquireDisposable` with `{ interruptible: true }` run their acquire step with that recorded interruptibility wherever `using`'s mask is the innermost one, so cancellation from outside the `using` reaches the wait. An `uninterruptible` block written inside the initializer keeps the step masked. Effect's own `Effect.acquireRelease`, and a mask that TypeScript code applies, cannot read the record, which the trusted base states. Reason: Chapter 6 said an interruptible acquisition keeps its policy inside `using`'s mask and that the runtime's acquisitions do what Effect's do, but Effect's `acquireRelease` runs its acquire under `uninterruptibleMask`'s `restore`, which is the identity inside an enclosing mask (`internal/effect.ts:4670-4682`), so `sessionWithin`'s `timeout` could not cancel a hanging connect; a reviewer's run on `effect@4.0.6` gave 104 ms bare and 2,001 ms inside a mask. Letting the step out is safe, since the acquisition registers and records the release in the uninterruptible step that ends it (D401). Rejected: stating the limitation and dropping `using` from the example, which leaves interruptible acquisitions uncancellable under the language's own resource form. Language change: the runtime gains the record and reads it; a `using` whose initializer makes an interruptible acquisition can be cancelled during the acquire step.

**D452. The parenthesized keyword forms are calls in shape** (2026-10-27; amends D391, D419, D432 and D443; amended by D462). `fork(x)` and `join(x)` always parse as TypeScript parses a call of an identifier, and `await.all(x)` and its siblings as a call, so a member access, an element access, or a call after the `)` applies to the form's value: `fork(work()).join()` forks and joins, and `await.all([a(), b()]).length` counts the results. Rule H5 still reads `fork(x)` and `join(x)` by the binding they resolve to, as the keyword for the prelude's own and as an ordinary call for a binding the program declares or imports, but the parse no longer depends on it. A member of a namespace import of the runtime, `R.fork(x)` or `R.sync(…)`, is EK4410, as a renamed import is. Reason: the grammar parsed `fork(x)` as a ForkExpression whose operand is a UnaryExpression, so `fork(x).y` forked `(x).y` as the keyword and read `.y` of the call's result as an ordinary call, two trees no parser could choose between without the binding; and the text left a namespace member's meaning open. Rejected: making the parenthesized forms keywords only and rejecting every program binding named `fork` or `join`, one reviewer's repair, which rejects programs that declare `join`, a common name, and keeps a precedence that a TypeScript reader of `fork(x).y` would not expect. Language change: `fork(x).y`, `join(x)[0]`, and `await.all(xs).length` apply what follows the `)` to the form's value, where they applied it inside the operand; `R.fork(x)` and `R.sync(…)` are EK4410.

**D453. Rule F's destinations are completed** (2026-10-27; amends D423, D430 and D435; amended by D461 and D463; amended by D500). A destination whose function type returns a definitely-Effect type also accepts, unlifted, a function whose own form is plain and whose plain return type is itself definitely an Effect, as `(acc, x) => acc` in a `reduce` over Effects or `(v) => cached` at `flatMap`, since a call of it already returns the program the destination expects. A literal argument's element and field types are the contextual types TypeScript derives, through the constraint of a type parameter the call has not fixed, as `Effect.all`'s `Arg` gives them; D435's ordered rule reads a type parameter only as a function value's whole destination or a destination function type's return type. A destination reaches through parentheses, a conditional's arms, the right operands of `??`, `||`, and `&&`, and the operand of `satisfies`, as TypeScript's contextual typing does, never through `as`. A spread converts by the static type of each element it supplies: a tuple's per position and an object's per property. D430's check is part of whether a candidate accepts an argument in rule F's first step. Reason: lifting a function that already returns an Effect gave `Effect<Effect<number>>` where TypeScript accepts the call; `all([() => a(), () => b()])` converted only by an unstated derivation; a literal in a conditional's arm had no destination by the letter, though EK4802 names those positions; a mixed tuple's union element type stopped a conversion whose types are known per position; and with `f(x: { reset(): void })` declared before `f(x: { reset(): Effect<void> })`, D430 read as an error after TypeScript's choice made `f(new Counter())` EK4301, while read as acceptance it resolves to the second. Language change: those calls compile; an overload that D430 rejects no longer stops the search.

**D454. Rule R covers union callees and contract-less callback parameters** (2026-10-27; amends D321 and D398; amended by D500). A callee whose type is a union of function types resolves to TypeScript's union signature, and the call runs when every member's declared return type is definitely an Effect, and is plain otherwise. A parameter of a `.vibe` function whose type is a contract-less function type is read with the channels D68 adds, `(a: A) => B!E!R`, so a call of it runs, and the call table has the row. Reason: a union signature has no single declaration for rule R to read, and Chapter 16 runs `f(item)` in `mapAll` though rule R, read alone, read its written return type `B`. Language change: none intended; both readings are stated.

**D455. Four diagnostics cover what their rules meant** (2026-10-27; amends D282, D409, D434 and D440; amended by D500). EK2130 applies where `E`, `R`, `E2`, or `R2` names any declaration with a type meaning in scope, a type alias, interface, class, enum, or imported type as well as a type parameter, so a contract-less callback's channel never silently shadows a type the file means. EK2128 covers a `resume`, `resumeWith`, or `abandon` written in a form of rule D that runs its body on another fiber or later, `fork`, an await operation's element, a block param, or a `for concurrent` body, as well as in a nested function. EK4838's flow rule follows a fiber through parentheses and a conditional's arms. A failure that can escape a constructor, an accessor, or a static block is EK2003 only, and EK1001 reports a call that runs a program there. Reason: a file that declares `type E` and writes `A!E` meaning it got the callback's channels instead; `fork { resume(v) }` moved a continuation to another fiber with only the run time's `ContinuationWrongFiber` to catch it; `const f = ready ? fork a() : fork b()` was EK4838 by the letter; and two codes claimed one failure. Language change: the first two reject programs that compiled; the third accepts programs that were rejected.

**D456. Statements that contradicted rules or facts** (2026-10-27; no rule changes). Appendix C's row for `async` and `await` allowed `async` only in a hatch's function, against Chapter 10, which keeps TypeScript's `async` and `await` in a `module` declaration's body and in `.ts` files too. Chapters 10 and 12 and Appendix C wrapped `Effect3.runPromise` in a synchronous `try` hatch function, whose result D439 makes the promise itself, unwaited, so its rejection never reached the mapper; they now write the function `async` and pass the hatch's `AbortSignal` on to `runPromise`, and Chapter 30's hatch case checks that a rejection reaches the mapper; EK4812's repair `sync(promise)` had the same fault. Chapter 24 said Kotlin never runs a woken coroutine on the waker's stack, while `kotlinx.coroutines`' unconfined dispatcher resumes inline through an event loop that bounds nesting (`Unconfined.kt` and `DispatchedContinuation.kt`, 1.10.2). Chapter 10 said TypeScript code calls the hatches by the same convention, though TypeScript evaluates a single expression before the call. Chapter 13's principle and its three kinds left out a hatch's deferred first argument, and its principle said no annotation on a receiving binding changes whether a call runs, which D449 now states precisely. Chapter 3 did not say that a union of `.ts`-declared error classes is open, or that matching its `_tag` covers it, a form EK4701's quick fix now offers. Chapter 10 said a declaration consumer behaves as a source consumer, where Chapter 27's rule 2 lets it reject more. Chapter 15's first condition counted calls inside a function literal its body only builds. Chapter 13's list of places a type decides did not name the spread's static element types (D423). Chapter 28 did not say that a `.ts` source's declarations import `AsData`. Chapter 30 claimed a case for every rule change, but D437, D438, D441, and D443 had none; they have one now. Reason: each sentence contradicted a rule, a primary source, or the conformance claim. Language change: none.

## The fifth review: overload typing, type parameters, nested masks, and the rest (2026-10-27)

A fifth review on 2026-10-27, by four reviewers working apart, found that overload order could still pick a callback's form through destinations that accept both forms, that rule R read a type parameter by the construct that declared it, so a type alias, an interface, or a class ran differently from the same type written inline, that a `using` nested in another's initializer defeated the record D451 added, that some edges of the plain-call rule were unstated, and that several chapters kept sentences the fourth review's rules had replaced. These decisions repair them, and record six rulings made with the implementation. Each names what it amends, and its last sentence says what changes for an implementation.

**D457. The candidates that accept a call's other arguments must type each function literal they reach alike** (2026-10-27; amends D392 and D450; superseded by D482). Where two or more overload candidates accept a call's other arguments, as TypeScript's first pass finds them by assignability with every context-sensitive argument set aside, every function literal their destinations reach, an argument, a method or function literal in an argument's object or array literal, or a function literal at a return position of one of these, at any depth, must be typed alike by them: one type for each unannotated parameter, compared as TypeScript would give it under each candidate, with earlier literals contributing their one typing and an uninferred type parameter fixed as TypeScript fixes it, and, where a function, array, or object literal stands at one of the literal's return positions, one return type. Otherwise the call is EK1021, whatever forms the destinations accept; the quick fix writes the parameter types TypeScript gives now. Reason: D450 rejected only candidates whose destinations accept different forms, but two destinations whose callback returns `number | Effect<number>` both accept both forms, so with overloads that type a callback's `run` parameter as `() => number` and as `() => Effect<number>`, or an object parameter's `run` as returning an `Exit` and as returning an `Effect`, the literal's own form, and so whether the API's call of it ran its body, followed the order of the overloads: two reviewers' witnesses, whose typing premise stock TypeScript 5.9.3 confirms, ran the body once in one order and never in the other. The parameter types also decide, through rule R, what each call in the body does, which no destination's form protects. Rejected: rejecting only where the forms the destinations accept include both the plain and the Effect form, one reviewer's repair, which closes both witnesses but lets the order still decide calls inside a body passed to plain-only destinations, such as a nested callback's form or a data-type method; and exempting a literal with a return annotation, which fixes its own form but not the types its body's calls are read with. Cost: `Effect.catchIf(p, isNotFound, (e) => …)`, whose refinement and predicate overloads in `effect@4.0.6` type `e` as `NotFound` and as the whole error union (stock TypeScript gives `NotFound` in the declared order and the union in the reversed one), is EK1021 until `e` is annotated; `Effect.filterOrFail(p, (n) => n <= 20, (n) => …)`, whose candidates give `n` the type `number` in both orders, compiles. Language change: calls whose candidates would type a literal differently are EK1021, where D450 accepted those whose destinations accept the same forms; an implementation compares, before typing any body, the types each surviving candidate gives every literal it reaches.

**D458. Rule R reads a type parameter as TypeScript shows it for the call, except one the callee's own signature declares** (2026-10-27; amends D378, D393 and D449; supersedes D426; amended by D486). A type parameter in a call's declared return type is left uninstantiated only where the resolved signature itself declares it, so that this call binds it (D378), and is a type parameter where the call stands inside the generic declaration that declares it; every other type parameter, whatever construct declares it, a class, an interface, a type alias, a function, or a call signature, is read as TypeScript instantiates it for the call: by the receiver's, an annotation's, or an alias's type arguments, or by the completed call that returned the signature (D449). Declaration emit writes the types TypeScript infers, and the runtime's `AsData<T>` is withdrawn. Reason: D393 left a class's, an interface's, and a type alias's parameters uninstantiated while D449 read a function's as the completed call bound it, so calls that stock TypeScript 5.9.3 types alike, as `Effect<number>`, split by which construct declared the returned `A`: `always(program)()` and `makeBox(program).get()` ran, while `Function.constant(program)()` through Effect's `LazyArg<A>` alias, the same function through a `Thunk<A>` alias, `makeGetter(program).get()` through an interface, `new Box(program).get()`, and a call through a `Thunk<Effect<number>>`-annotated `const` returned data (a reviewer's probe). Extracting an alias, which D331 says changes nothing, or an interface, or turning a factory into a class, changed whether a call ran; and D449's reason that no call binds a class's parameter was false, since `new Box(program)` binds it as `makeBox(program)` does. Keeping `Effect.fn(name)(body, pipeline)`'s functions running, as D449 decided and another reviewer confirmed against `effect@4.0.6`, leaves this rule as the only one without spelling dependence, since `always`'s `() => A` and `Effect.fn`'s `(...args: Args) => A` are the same case; the compiler as built already runs all six source calls of the probe. Rejected: keeping D393 and stating the spelling dependence with a diagnostic wherever the readings differ, which keeps a surprise D331 promises away; and reading every type parameter uninstantiated, as D393 first did, which makes `Effect.fn` functions return data. Cost: a generic container's method that returns its element type runs when the element is an Effect, `new Box(program).get()` and `[a, b].reduce((acc, x) => acc, initial)` over Effects among them, where D393 returned data; a property read, or a function generic in the element, `unbox(box)` or `reduce<Effect<number>>(…)`, returns it as data. Language change: those calls run; declaration emit writes no `AsData<T>`, and the runtime does not export it.

**D459. The interruptibility `using` records is the fiber's without any `using` mask, and VibeLang's masks keep it** (2026-10-27; amends D451; superseded by D481). While a `using` mask is in force on a fiber, the runtime keeps the interruptibility the fiber would have with no `using` mask. A `using` mask leaves it unchanged, so a `using` nested in another's initializer, directly or in a function the initializer calls, inherits the outer one's. A mask written in VibeLang, a mask block or a reference that resolves to Effect's `Effect.uninterruptible`, `Effect.uninterruptibleMask`, `Effect.interruptible`, or `Effect.interruptibleMask` (rule H4), which denotes the runtime's recording form as a reference to `Effect.acquireRelease` does, sets it for its extent, and a `restore` sets it back to its value before its mask. The runtime's interruptible acquire step runs with it wherever a `using` mask is the innermost mask around the step. A mask that TypeScript code applies with Effect's own functions cannot set it, so inside a `using` initializer the runtime's step escapes such a mask too, a deviation from Effect that Chapter 6 states rather than the trusted base.
