Reason: D451 recorded "the interruptibility in force before" each `using`, which inside another `using`'s initializer is that `using`'s own mask, so a helper that binds a lock with `using` and returns a resource, itself bound with `using`, waited out a 2-second lock past a 100 ms timeout that either `using` alone lets cancel the wait (a reviewer's case; `effect@4.0.6`'s `uninterruptible` returns its operand unchanged on a fiber already uninterruptible, `internal/effect.ts:4604-4612`). The text also said an `uninterruptible` block inside the initializer keeps the step masked without a mechanism the step could read, and left unstated whether the step escapes a `.vibe` call of Effect's own `Effect.uninterruptible`, which is neither a block nor TypeScript code (two reviewers; on 4.0.6 an `Effect.interruptible` acquisition inside an outer `uninterruptible` was cancelled after 101 ms, so the record is implementable). Rejected: listing `.vibe` calls of Effect's own masks in the trusted base, which would leave VibeLang code with a mask the runtime's step ignores. Language change: a nested `using` no longer keeps an interruptible acquisition masked; `.vibe` references to Effect's four mask functions denote the runtime's recording forms, which the runtime adds; Chapter 30's mask case calls the masked acquisition from a helper function, since a block cannot stand inside an initializer expression.

**D460. An object literal's methods take a function literal's own form and have a stated plain form; every holder of a named function is stated** (2026-10-27; amends D397, D431, D445 and D448; amended by D483; superseded by D500). An object literal's method, and a function literal written as an object literal's field, takes its own form as a function literal does (D448), and Chapters 10 and 14 and the function-kinds table now confine D397's "an Effect method otherwise" to a class's methods. One whose own form is plain, an eligible unannotated one, is declared as TypeScript code receives it, a method returning a cold `Effect`; it carries its plain form at run time as `m[$direct]` on the method, as a function does, and its object's marker names it, its plain call signature being its declared signature with the outer `Effect<A, never, never>` read as `A`. D445's plain call covers it. A member of a namespace import names the declaration it resolves to, as an import alias does (rule H4), and an anonymous `export default` of a function literal is a `const` bound to it, which declaration emit writes under a fresh name with its merged namespace; both keep the function's own form. A `let` does not: `let g = double; g(2)` is a call that runs, EK1001 at a module's top level. Reason: rule F and D448 made an object literal's eligible method plain, while Chapter 10, Chapter 14, and the function-kinds table called every unannotated method other than a protocol method or a plain member's implementation an Effect method; D445 made a call of such a method plain without saying where its plain form lives at run time, since a method has no named variant, or what plain signature a declaration consumer calls, since an object's marker is a name and not a signature; and rule F's holders left out a namespace import's member and an anonymous default export, while the `let` case could be read two ways (three reviewers). Language change: none intended for the programs the rules accept; an implementation attaches `m[$direct]` to an object literal's plain methods, names them in the object's marker, and gives an anonymous default export a marker.

**D461. A destination reaches both operands of `??` and `||`** (2026-10-27; amends D453; amended by D488). A destination reaches both operands of `??` and `||`, and only the right operand of `&&`, as TypeScript's contextual typing reaches them. Reason: D453 named only the right operands of all three, while TypeScript contextually types both operands of `||` and `??` and only the right of `&&` (a reviewer's probe on TypeScript 5.9.3: no TS7006 for an unannotated parameter on the left of `||` or `??`, TS7006 on the left of `&&`), so `retry(job ?? (() => a()), policy)` converted the right operand and left the left one a function, a type error TypeScript's own typing would not raise. Language change: the left operand of `??` and `||` converts where the destination asks for a program or another form; programs that compiled keep their meaning.

**D462. A parenthesized await operation's literal operand is deferred, and `join`'s operand is evaluated** (2026-10-27; amends D391 and D452). The elements of an array or object literal written as an await operation's operand are deferred by rule D whether or not the operand is parenthesized, and the grammar's note on CallExpression arguments says so for an AwaitCall. `join`'s operand is evaluated once and joined (E7); rule H's text no longer calls it deferred. Reason: the grammar note said a CallExpression's arguments follow rule E1 except `fork(x)`'s, and an AwaitCall is a CallExpression, so by the letter `await.all([a(), b()])` evaluated `a()` and `b()` before the operation and then rejected them as programs, against D391's rule that parentheses never turn a deferred operand into a running call; and H4 and H5 called `join`'s operand deferred, which rule D and E7 contradict (a reviewer). Language change: none intended; the text states the meaning D391 and D452 gave.

**D463. EK4802 covers an Effect-form callback that returns a program where an Effect-returning callback is expected** (2026-10-27; amends D436 and D453; amended by D480; amended by D500). A function whose own form is Effect, at a destination whose function type returns a definitely-Effect type, that returns an Effect value or a program is EK4802, unless its return annotation writes the nested Effect; the quick fix runs it, `return cached()`. Reason: D453 passes `(v) => cached` unlifted, so `flatMap` runs `cached`, but `(v) => { log("seen"); return cached }` has Effect own form and returns `cached` as its success value, so adding a log line silently stopped `cached` from running, and types caught it only where the result was used as the success type (a reviewer). Effect's language service reports the same shape inside `Effect.gen` as `returnEffectInGen`, which Chapter 28 already maps to EK4802. Rejected: a new warning code, which would split one rule of Effect's language service between two codes. Language change: such a return is an error by default, adjustable as EK4802 is.

**D464. `do` blocks and `match` arms own no fibers, and three escape edges are stated** (2026-10-27; amends D408 and D409; ruled with the implementation). A `do` expression's block and a `match` expression's arms are only braces: a fiber forked inside one belongs to the region around the `do` or the `match`, as for an `if` branch or a bare block. A plain `await f` of a fiber is EK4609 alone, the more specific diagnostic, never EK4838 as well. A function literal or block param inside a `fork` operand that captures an owned fiber is EK4838, as a capture anywhere is, though the operand itself may read the fiber. Reason: D408 listed the regions and the blocks that own nothing without naming `do` and `match`, D409 left open whether its allowed `fork` operand covered a function written inside it, and one `await f` drew two errors; the implementation follows these rulings. Language change: none for programs the rules decided; the three edges are stated.

**D465. A compiled `error` class carries `#private` and a merged-namespace `$error` marker; `effect`'s error classes are those extending `YieldableError`** (2026-10-27; amends D407; ruled with the implementation; superseded by D501). An `error` declaration's class declares `#private;` in its declaration file, which makes it nominal to stock TypeScript, and a namespace merged with it declares `type $error = ErrorBrand<"pkg/path#Name">`, with `ErrorBrand` a type import of `vibelang-runtime`. Read from a declaration file, a class is a closed `error` declaration only when its merged namespace has `$error` of the runtime's `ErrorBrand`; any other class is open, and a match over it needs `default`. "An error class of the pinned `effect`" means a class the pinned `effect` package declares that extends its error base `YieldableError`, such as `NoSuchElementError` or `TimeoutError`, not every class the package declares. Reason: D407 named a private member as the brand without saying how a declaration consumer tells a closed `error` declaration from any other class, and "an error class that the pinned `effect` exports" fit every class the package declares. A brand on the class itself would be inherited, so a TypeScript subclass, which may add its own membership, would count as closed, and inherited brands of two families conflict (TS2320); an instance member would mark TypeScript subclasses the same way. A namespace is not inherited. Language change: declaration files gain the marker; a TypeScript subclass of an `error` class is open to a declaration consumer.

**D466. A service's emitted declaration has one stated shape, and "only identifiers in R" is assignability** (2026-10-27; amends D361; ruled with the implementation; superseded by D501). A `service` declaration's file imports `ServiceBrand` as a type from `vibelang-runtime`; declares `interface Users extends Users.Shape, ServiceBrand<"…">`, a key of type `ServiceKey<Users, Users.Shape>`, or `ReferenceKey<Users.Shape>` for a service with a default, and `declare namespace Users { interface Shape { … } }`; a class that implements the service writes `implements Users.Shape`, and a `.vibe` class's `implements Users` is emitted so. Chapter 4's "only identifiers appear in R" is a statement about assignability: a structural type in R is assignable to no requirement a provider removes, and TypeScript's assignability error reports it where R must be discharged, with no new EK code. Reason: Chapter 27 imported only `Effect` in its example and named `ReferenceKey` without its argument, and Chapter 4's sentence read as a check no diagnostic made. Language change: none; the emitted shape is the one the implementation writes.

**D467. A handler's answer type adds `undefined` only for a completion other than by returning** (2026-10-27; amends D351; ruled with the implementation; amended by D487). H is the type of what the try block and each clause return, plus `undefined` only when the block, or a clause that has not resumed, completes other than by returning; with a written success type, H is that type plus `undefined` under the same condition. Reason: Chapter 9 said "ends another way", which left open which endings count; the implementation reads a completion other than a `return`, and the annotated case already said "under the same condition". Language change: none intended; the wording is the implementation's.

**D468. Statements that contradicted rules or facts** (2026-10-27; no rule changes). Chapter 15's plain lowering and Chapter 27's helper table kept D177's `throw cause` lowering through `$expectedCause`, which D406 replaced: every accepted throw now wraps its operand whatever it is, and a `Cause`-typed operand is EK2140. Chapter 10's introduction to the hatches said a hatch waits for any value whose `then` is a function and that a runtime hatch tests each value, against its own Sync-or-async rule and D439; it now says a hatch waits only for a function written `async`. Chapter 27 said conversion changes a function's representation "never when its body runs", and Chapter 5 said a declared Effect type changes "only how the arrow is passed", both against D447's account that the receiver's call of a plain form runs the body while a call of the Effect form builds a program; Chapter 13's conversion sentence said the same of programs. Chapter 13's call table classified a `trusted` value's call as Run outside plain code, and Chapters 2 and 10 said its call runs an `Effect`, against D445, which makes it plain everywhere, with running the wrapper's `Effect` in Effect code an optimization; rule R's *Not a trigger* now names `trusted` values. Chapter 13 gave a `trusted` value's escaping failure EK1001 in a constructor, an accessor, and a static block, where D455 made it EK2003. EK4812's repair read a `.vibe` binding inside a hatch, which is EK4405; it now passes the promise as an input. Chapter 30's claim omitted D419, which now has a case, as D406's plain path does. Chapter 15 did not say that a `.vibe` function with a contract-less callback parameter is never eligible, a consequence of D12 and D454 that a plain higher-order helper must type its parameter in TypeScript. Chapter 13's principle said overload resolution is TypeScript's without D453's exception, and its three kinds read Run off "the callee's declared return type", which for an eligible function is an Effect though its call is plain. Chapter 6 said a release returning a program never releases, true for a function and false for an Effect value, which D453 passes unlifted. Appendix C7 read as if calling a runner at the top level were EK1001. The introduction and Appendix A cited D1-D444. Reason: each sentence contradicted a rule, a primary source, or the conformance claim (four reviewers). Language change: none; the compiler on `main` already emits no `$expectedCause` for a source `throw`.

**D469. A fiber may sit in a literal its region holds only to join it** (2026-10-27; amends D409; ruled with the implementation). An array or object literal held in a `const` or `let` declared in the owning region may hold owned fibers when every read of that binding, and there is at least one, is the operand of `join` or an argument of a `Fiber` function D409 allows: `const fibers = [fork a(), fork b()]` then `Fiber.joinAll(fibers)` or `join fibers`. Any other read of the binding, `fibers.length` or `box.fiber`, is EK4838, as is storing a fiber in any other collection. A getter or method written in the literal is a function literal, so one that captures the fiber is EK4838 as before. Reason: holding forks in a local array and joining them all is the shape Effect code writes, every such fiber finishes inside its region, and D409's "a collection" rejected it. Language change: programs that hold fibers this way compile; nothing that compiled is rejected.

**D470. `vibelang --fix` writes `Effect.gen` in direct style** (2026-10-28; amends D291). EK1002's quick fix rewrites `Effect.gen(function* () { … })` and `Effect.gen({ self: this }, function* () { … })` where the call is the whole body of an arrow, a function, or a method, as that function's body, and where the declared type at its destination is an Effect and no function, as `() => { … }`, which converts to a program (Chapter 13, rule F). Each `yield* e` becomes what runs `e` where it stands: the call `e` when it runs (rule R), `e()` for an Effect value such as a service key or a `Config`, and `throw e` for an error instance in a statement or a return. `vibelang --fix` applies it, and EK1002's note says that it has a fix, or why not. It refuses where the direct style would mean something else (Chapter 28): a call in the generator's own code that builds a program no `yield*` runs, which would run; a callback whose program-building call is not the value it returns; a `yield*` of a value that is not definitely an Effect, of an optional call, or whose success value is itself a program; `throw` and `try`, which mean JavaScript exceptions in a generator; `using`; `this` without `{ self: this }`, `arguments`, `new.target`, `super`, and a nested `function*`; a parameter with a default or a pattern, which TypeScript binds at the call and VibeLang when the program runs (rule E9); a name the generator declares that a parameter has; and, for a function without a contract or an expected type that asks for an Effect function, a body that runs no Effect, which would compile to plain JavaScript and change the function's type. A refused fix is remembered by its whole call, so one refusal does not keep another `Effect.gen` from being fixed in a later round. Reason: a migration from Effect TypeScript renames files to `.vibe`, and `Effect.gen` is its most common construct, 44,235 sites in four migration targets, which agents rewrote by hand. Rejected: rewriting `Effect.gen(…).pipe(…)` and programs held in a `const`, whose direct style depends on each stage and use; and `Effect.fn`, whose span and stack frames a body does not keep. Evidence: `TestEffectGenQuickFixes`, `TestEffectGenRefusals`, `TestEffectGenFixRounds`, and `migrate-effect-gen` with its `-fixed` twin, whose declarations after the rule are the TypeScript's (`TestMigrationCasesFix`). Language change: none.

**D471. An arm that only binds handles every case** (2026-10-28; amends D209). An arm whose pattern is a binding alone, `when let other:`, removes every case from a finite union's remainder, as `default` does, so a match that ends in one is exhaustive. Reason: the binding matches every value (D206's pattern table), and the parser writes its arm as a block with no test, which makes the `$noMatch` after it unreachable; TypeScript gives the subject its declared type in unreachable code, so EK4701 named every case left. A guarded binding, `when let n and if (n > 0)`, still removes nothing. Evidence: `match-binding-exhaustive` and `match-binding-guard.fail`. Language change: programs that EK4701 rejected compile; nothing that compiled changes.

**D472. A choice written with Effect's `Match` functions is EK4839** (2026-10-28; extends D284-D291). `Match.value(x).pipe(…)`, `pipe(Match.value(x), …)`, and `Match.type<T>().pipe(…)` that end in `Match.exhaustive`, `Match.orElse`, `Match.orElseAbsurd`, or `Match.tagsExhaustive` draw EK4839, a warning like EK4830-EK4837, whose quick fix writes a `match` expression (D206), and `(u: T) => match (u) { … }` for `Match.type`, keeping the parameter name its function type gives. Each stage becomes an arm: `when`, `whenOr` (an `or` pattern), `whenAnd` (`and`), `not`, `tag` and the fields of `tags` and `tagsExhaustive` (`{ _tag: "A" }`), and `orElse` (`default`, or `when let v:` when its handler reads the value). A pattern has a form when it tests what Match's predicate tests: a literal by `===`; an object of patterns by `typeof` `"object"`, not `null`, each key `in` the value and each value's pattern, as an object pattern does (D441); `Match.string` and the other primitive refinements by `typeof`; `Match.null`, `Match.undefined`, `Match.defined`; `Match.instanceOf(C)` by `instanceof`; and a constant of a literal type by equality. A predicate function, and an array, which Match does not check by length and an array pattern does, have none. A handler has a form when it is an arrow function whose parameter binds with `let`, or with `{ let field }` for a destructured one, and whose block, if any, returns once at its end, which becomes a `do` block (D187); a handler that builds a program has none, since an arm's call runs (Chapter 13). The match's type is its arms', so where they do not share the chain's type and nothing written where the match stands gives one, the fix writes the type: `withReturnType`'s, the handlers' common return type, or a primitive type by name, on a `const` or as `as T`. A chain whose value runs, or that ends in `Match.option` or `Match.result`, draws nothing. Reason: migrations rewrote these chains into `match` by hand (foldkit has 333). Rejected: `Match.discriminator` and predicates rewritten as guards, whose arguments would be evaluated per arm rather than once. Evidence: `TestMatchChainQuickFixes`, `TestMatchChainRefusals`, `TestMatchChainNotReported`, and `tsforms-match` with its `-fixed` twin, which return the same result and emit the same declarations. Language change: none; a new warning.

**D473. A pipeline of the `Option` module's functions is EK4840** (2026-10-28; extends D284-D291). `pipe(x, stages…)` and `x.pipe(stages…)` whose every stage is a function of effect's `Option` module, `Option.f` or `Option.f(…)`, draw EK4840, a warning like EK4830-EK4837, whose quick fix writes the pipeline with method syntax (D152): a stage applied to an `Option`, a `None`, a `Some`, or their union, which the `Option` module declares, becomes the method call `x.f(args)`, which reaches the function's data-first overload, its first parameter named `self`; a stage applied to any other value becomes the data-first call `Option.f(x, args)`, or `Option.f(x)` for a function stage. Effect's duals give the curried and the data-first call one implementation, so each stage computes what it did. The pipe evaluated every stage's arguments before it ran the first stage, and the chain evaluates each stage's arguments after the stage before it runs, so the fix requires arguments that are functions, names, literals, properties of names, or object literals of those, whose evaluation no program can tell apart at either time; and each stage must have the overload its call needs. Comments must lie inside the stages. Reason: migrations rewrote these pipelines by hand (foldkit has 214), and the skill teaches the method form. Rejected: rewriting a pipeline with a stage of another module, whose receiver has no method syntax. Evidence: `TestOptionPipeQuickFixes`, `TestOptionPipeRefusals`, `TestOptionPipeNotReported`, and `tsforms-option` with its `-fixed` twin, which return the same result and emit the same declarations. Language change: none; a new warning.

**D474. A schema constant just after its type alias is EK4841** (2026-10-28; extends D280 and D284-D291; amends D286; amended by D501). `const X = <schema>`, one binding without a type annotation, exported or not, at any scope, draws EK4841, a warning like EK4830-EK4837, when the statement just after it is its type alias `type X = typeof X.Type` with the same export (before D501, just before it), neither has JSDoc, and the schema maps back through D133's mapping as a struct field's does (D286's list: `Schema.Literals`, `Schema.Literal`, the keyword schemas, `Schema.Union`, `Schema.Array`, `Schema.Tuple`, `Schema.Record`, a struct inside them, and names of other schemas), other than a `Schema.Struct`, which is EK4832's. The quick fix writes the schema alias `schema X = <type>` (D280), which the parser writes back into the same constant and type alias, so the program, its types and its exports stay the same, and it leaves every import in place. The declaration file stays the same too: under D501 a schema alias's declaration file lists its constant, then its type, which is what the source says only when its constant comes first and neither has JSDoc, the order of 818 of the 820 such pairs in the migration targets (foldkit, t3code, alchemy, opencode). A type written before its constant keeps its form and draws nothing: the rewrite would reorder the module's declarations, which a migration's declaration gate rejects. Before D501 the parser listed an alias's type first, so the rule took the other order. The same order makes EK4832's fix reorder a struct's declarations. Reason: migrations rewrote these pairs by hand (t3code's `BrowserProfileKind`), and D286 named this extension for when the parser accepted the alias form. Rejected: warning at a constant written before its type, which a migration could neither fix nor keep, since its style gate rejects EK48xx warnings and its declaration gate the reorder; and listing an alias's type after its constant, which changes every schema declaration's declaration file and is a compiler decision, not a rewrite's. Evidence: `TestSchemaAliasQuickFixes`, `TestSchemaAliasNotReported`, and `migrate-schema-alias` with its `-fixed` twin, which return the same result and emit the declarations TypeScript emits for the original (`expected/kinds.d.ts`). Language change: none; a new warning.

**D475. An import of one of Effect's modules under a JavaScript global's name is EK4842** (2026-10-28; extends D284-D291 and D310). An import that binds one of Effect's modules under the name of a JavaScript global VibeLang keeps for the global (D310: `Array`, `String`, `Number`, `Boolean`, `Function`, `Symbol`, `BigInt`, `RegExp`), as `import { Array } from "effect"` or `import * as Array from "effect/Array"`, and is not type-only, draws EK4842, a warning like EK4830-EK4837. The quick fix imports the module under another name, `Arr`, `Str`, `Num`, `Bool`, `Fn`, `Sym`, `Big` or `Regex`, or `EffectArray` and its siblings when the file already uses the short name, and renames each reference to the import, writing a shorthand property `{ Array }` as `{ Array: Arr }`. The import shadowed the global in the whole file, so every reference to it meant the module and still does; a name the import does not resolve, such as the type `Array<T>`, which the module does not declare, meant the global and is left. The warning stands only where the rename keeps the module's declaration file: no `export { Array }` or `export default Array` names the import, no type is written with it, as `Array.NonEmptyReadonlyArray<T>`, which a declaration file prints as written, and the type of nothing the file exports mentions a declaration of the module, which the declaration file would print through the import's name; a type too large to search counts as one that does. Reason: VibeLang's documents import these modules under other names (`import { Array as Arr } from "effect"`), the migrate flow's rules ask for it, and 414 files of the four first targets import one under its own name (399 in foldkit), seven of which the foldkit run renamed by hand. `Arr` and `Str` are the names the documents and examples use; the others follow them. Rejected: removing the import, which gives every reference the global; and warning where the fix would change the declaration file, which a migration could neither fix nor keep. Evidence: `TestGlobalImportQuickFixes`, `TestGlobalImportNotReported`, and `migrate-global-import` with its `-fixed` twin, which return the same result and emit the declarations TypeScript emits for the original (`expected/util.d.ts`). Language change: none; a new warning.

## The sixth review and three rulings (2026-10-28)

A sixth review on 2026-10-27, by four reviewers working apart, found that a plain function returning a program ran by one rule and returned data by four others, that a release running inside a `using` initializer lost its mask while an interruptible acquisition could leave earlier resources of the initializer unregistered, that the overload checks were too strict for `Effect.tap` and too narrow for a named function or a generic call at a return position, that an object literal's protocol method was both plain and cold, that declarations had no plain form for a method of an anonymous returned object, and that D430 depended on how a container type is spelled. Three rulings settle the first three: calls whose type is an Effect run, `using` is the Effect program a TypeScript developer would write, and overloads resolve as TypeScript resolves them. Each decision names what it amends, and its last sentence says what changes for an implementation.

**D480. A call whose type is an Effect runs, whatever its callee compiles to** (2026-10-28; a ruling; amends D445 and D463; amended by D500). A call runs where the declared return type of the signature it resolves to is definitely an Effect, and for a plain function that is its plain call signature's return type: with `function pick(p: number!!) { return p }`, which compiles to plain JavaScript, `const n = pick(load)` calls the plain form and runs the program it returns, so `n` is a `number`, and at a module's top level the call is EK1001, as every call that runs is there. The plain JavaScript a function compiles to never changes the value its callers get, so a function whose own form is Effect and that returns a value whose type is definitely an Effect is EK4802 wherever it stands, not only where an Effect-returning callback is expected, unless its return annotation writes the nested Effect, as Effect's language service reports `returnEffectInGen` inside `Effect.gen`: `function pickLogged(p: number!!) { log("pick"); return p }` would give its callers `load` unrun where `pick` runs it, and repaired with `return p()` it gives the number, as `pick` does; a returned function, `() => v`, is a value and not EK4802 there. To hold a program unrun, write a function where an Effect type is declared: `const held: number!! = () => pick(load)` converts the arrow to that Effect (rule D), and each run of it calls `pick(load)`. An annotation on the receiving binding holds nothing: `const n: number!! = pick(load)` runs the call, so it is EK1018, whose quick fix writes the `() =>`. Reason: rule R's trigger and the call table read a plain function's plain signature and so ran `pick(load)`, while rule R's *Not a trigger* list, the plain-call section, rule P, and the glossary returned `load` as data, and the build followed the second (a reviewer's probe gave `const=data let=data`); the ruling is that a call whose type is an Effect always runs, as `await` always waits, whether or not the callee compiles to plain JavaScript. Rejected: returning the program as data, which made a call's result depend on whether a log line had made the callee an Effect function (the reviewer's repair); and holding a program by an annotation on the receiving binding alone, which would make what a call does depend on the annotation rather than on syntax. Language change: a call of a plain function whose plain return type is definitely an Effect runs that program, where today's build returns it unrun; such a call is EK1001 in plain code and makes its caller ineligible for the plain form; an Effect-form function that returns an Effect value is EK4802 wherever it stands; a returned function is never EK4802 under D463's clause, which had caught returned thunks.

**D481. `using` is the Effect program a TypeScript developer would write** (2026-10-28; a ruling; amends D25, D342 and D401; supersedes D425, D451 and D459; amended by D490, under the ruling's own condition). `using x = e` adds no mask and no interruptibility of its own. Where `e`'s requirements include `Scope`, `e` is a scoped acquisition, which Effect code runs with `yield*` inside `Effect.scoped`, and `using` is `Effect.acquireRelease(e, release, { interruptible: true })` with the block's Scope: `e` runs with the interruptibility in force where the `using` stands, and each acquisition inside it keeps its own policy. Otherwise `e` gives a disposable, which Effect code binds with `Effect.acquireDisposable(e)`, and `using` is `Effect.acquireRelease(e, release)`, whose acquire step is masked, an interruptible acquisition inside it included, since `uninterruptibleMask`'s `restore` is the identity under an enclosing mask. `release` is D401's one disposal: nothing when an acquisition recorded the value's release, nothing for `null` or `undefined`, and otherwise the disposal method read once; Effect registers it in the uninterruptible step that ends the acquire, so no interruption separates `e`'s value from its release. A resource that `e` makes and does not return is `e`'s to own, against interruption as against an expected failure. The runtime's `acquireRelease` and `acquireDisposable` keep only their ownership record, and every `.vibe` reference to one of Effect's masks denotes Effect's own. D401's record stays: it decides whether `using` disposes the value, which neither Effect program decides, since Effect has no disposing `using` and a contract may name `Scope` while registering nothing, and without it `using` over an acquisition that returns a `Disposable` would dispose that value twice. Reason: D425 masked a whole pausing initializer, D451 let interruptible acquisitions escape that mask through a recorded interruptibility, and D459 made a nested `using` inherit the record, which only VibeLang's masks set, so a release that a nested block ran inside an outer initializer, under Effect's finalizer mask, let an interruptible acquisition escape and was cut short (a reviewer's probe on `effect@4.0.6`: 101 ms with the release unfinished, against 505 ms and finished without the outer `using`), and an escaping acquisition could cancel an initializer that had made another resource and not yet registered it (a second reviewer). Effect's own behavior, checked on `effect@4.0.6` under `timeout(…, "100 millis")`: `acquireRelease`'s default acquire runs to its end and its release runs once (506 ms); with `{ interruptible: true }` the acquire is cut short and registers nothing (101 ms); an interruptible acquisition inside `acquireDisposable`'s acquire stays masked (502 ms); the same acquisition in a scoped program run with `yield*` is cut short (101 ms); and a release runs to its end under Effect's finalizer mask, inside `acquireDisposable`'s acquire or not (301 ms, 302 ms). Rejected: more recording forms, for Effect's finalizer functions, a reviewer's repair, which extends a mechanism Effect does not have; and one interruptible translation for every initializer, which differs from `Effect.acquireDisposable` where Effect code binds a disposable with it. Language change: a pause in a `using` initializer that requires `Scope` can be interrupted, as `yield*` lets it be, where D425 masked it; an interruptible acquisition in an initializer that does not require `Scope`, a nested `using` in a helper that returns a disposable included, stays masked, as Effect masks it, where D451 and D459 let it be cut short; the runtime drops the interruptibility record and its recording masks, and keeps the ownership record.

**D482. Overloads resolve as TypeScript resolves them** (2026-10-28; a ruling; amends D392, D422 and D450; supersedes D457; amended by D494; amended by D500; amended by D510). An overloaded call resolves to the first candidate that accepts it, in declaration order after TypeScript's `reorderCandidates` step, by TypeScript's subtype pass and then its assignability pass, with each function argument tried in its own form first (D392). TypeScript types a function literal argument once, with the contextual type the first candidate it checks the literal against gives it, and the literal's parameter types, its return positions, its own form, and every call in its body follow that typing; VibeLang compares no other candidate's typing, so D450's and D457's EK1021 for candidates that would type a literal differently is withdrawn. EK1021 remains only where TypeScript has no answer: no candidate accepts the arguments in their own forms, and the candidates that accept them with lifting and conversion give a function argument different function forms or two function arguments prefer different candidates; a candidate that converts an argument to a program ranks below one that takes it as a function, and is no tie (D380). Rough edge: reordering a library's overloads can change whether a callback runs as plain or Effect code, as reordering changes a callback's parameter types in TypeScript. With `consume(f: (run: () => number) => number | Effect<number>)` before `consume(f: (run: () => Effect<number>) => number | Effect<number>)`, `consume((run) => { run(); events.push(1); return 0 })` types `run` as `() => number`, the callback is plain, and `consume`'s call of it pushes once; reversed, `run()` runs, the callback is an Effect function, and `consume` builds a program it never runs. The fix is to write the form: `(run): number!! => …` gives the callback one own form in either order, and `(run: () => number) => …` gives its body one typing. Prior art: TypeScript's overload resolution, which picks the first matching overload and types a callback once from it, with the handbook's advice to declare the most specific overload first. Reason: D457 compared the candidates TypeScript's first pass accepts, but as worded it counted candidates that pass only because a context-sensitive argument is set aside, which made `Effect.tap(self, (a) => …)`, `Effect.andThen(self, (a) => …)`, and `"abc".replace(/b/g, (m) => …)` EK1021 (a reviewer's probe on TypeScript 5.9.3 shows TypeScript's own first pass rejects those candidates), while two other reviewers found order-dependent typings it did not reach, a named function at a callback's return position and a generic call there whose type arguments TypeScript infers from the candidate's return type; each repair added a carve-out, and the ruling chose TypeScript's own resolution over a VibeLang check across candidates. Rejected: a complete comparison of every candidate's contextual typing, which rejects `Effect.catchIf` and the `reduce` flatten idiom that TypeScript resolves; and ranking candidates that accept the Effect form first, which departs from the overload TypeScript shows. Language change: calls whose candidates would type a literal differently compile, typed as TypeScript types them, where D457 made them EK1021; their meaning follows the declaration order of the overloads.

**D483. A function's plain form is part of its type** (2026-10-28; amends D268, D384, D411, D431, D445, D448 and D460; amended by D493; superseded by D500). Every function whose own form is plain, one with a plain return annotation, an eligible unannotated one, and an object literal's method or function-literal field whose own form is plain alike, has the runtime's type `Plain<F, C>` wherever it is held with no declared type: `F` is its plain call signature, `C` the signature TypeScript code calls, `F` itself for a plain annotation or a protocol member and the cold `(…) => Effect<A>` for an eligible function, and `Plain<F, C>` is `C & { readonly [$direct]: F }`, whose member the run time supplies, an eligible function's plain variant or a plain-annotated function itself. A function value's form is its type's: a value whose type is `Plain<F, C>` or `Trusted<F, Errors>` is plain, and a call of it is a plain call of `F`, whoever holds it, a `const`, a `let`, a field, an element, a parameter, a call's result, or an import; a declared type replaces it as it replaces any value's. Declaration emit writes `export declare const double: Plain<…>` for an exported function whose own form is plain, and the same type, as a property, wherever it writes such a function inside a type: an exported object's field or method, a returned object's, or an element's, at any depth. A class keeps D431's marker for its plain methods, since a method declaration cannot carry the type; the namespace `$plain` signature of D384 and D411 and the object marker of D460 are withdrawn. Reason: the plain form followed names, a function declaration and the `const`s, import aliases, namespace members, and default exports that name it, while a `let`, a shorthand field, and a property read took the type's form, so one function was plain or Effect by its holder, silently so at a destination that accepts both forms (`once(g)` ran the body with `const g = tick` and built an unrun program with `let g = tick`, a reviewer's witness), and a declaration had no place for the plain form of a method of an anonymous returned object or of a nested field, so `makeApi().double(2)` gave 4 from source and was EK1001 from declarations (two reviewers). Carrying the plain form in the type, as the run time already carries it on the value, needs one rule where those needed a list of holders, and it is TypeScript's own pattern: a type goes where its value goes, and `trusted` already worked this way (D400). Rejected: normalizing calls through returned objects to the cold form, which would have made `makeApi().double(2)` EK1001 in source too and left the holder list; and path markers on the object's namespace, which cover nested fields of an exported `const` but not a function's return type. Language change: `let g = double; g(2)` gives 4 where it was EK1001; `[1, 2].map(api.double)` with `const api = { double }` gives `[2, 4]` where it was EK4301; a `let`-held or field-held plain function passed where both forms are accepted passes its plain form; a method of a returned object is a plain call from declarations; declaration emit writes `Plain<F, C>` and a `const` for each function whose own form is plain, and the runtime attaches `[$direct]` to plain-annotated functions and object-literal plain members too.

**D484. A field of an object literal at a protocol key holds the plain function** (2026-10-28; amends D397, D430 and D460; amended by D497; amended by D500). JavaScript calls a protocol method by its key, so a field of an object literal at a protocol key, `toString`, `toLocaleString`, `valueOf`, `toJSON`, `then`, a well-known symbol, or a pinned `effect` protocol symbol, written as an identifier, a string literal, or a computed key whose type is that symbol's own `unique symbol` type, is a destination that accepts only the plain form, whatever the literal's contextual type. A method or function literal there is checked as a plain function (EK4301), another function value there is passed its plain form, and an Effect own form there, an Effect-annotated method included, is EK4301. The object holds the plain JavaScript function at the key, with no cold wrapper and no other plain form to find, TypeScript code receives it with its plain signature, and its type is `Plain<F>` (D483). A class's protocol method keeps D397's rule, since a class declares its methods' types. Reason: Chapter 10 checked an object literal's protocol method as a plain function, while Chapter 13's object-method row, Chapter 10's table, and Chapter 27's rule 3 emitted every eligible object-literal method as a method returning a cold `Effect`, so `String({ toString() { return "ok" } })` called the cold wrapper, got an object back, and threw a `TypeError`, and a literal's `[Symbol.dispose]` built a program instead of disposing (a reviewer's run of the two representations on Node); no rule chose between the rows. Rejected: leaving the representation to the object's marker, which a protocol call never reads. Language change: an object literal's protocol members are plain JavaScript at run time and to TypeScript; a function with an Effect own form at a protocol key is EK4301.

**D485. D430's check and a function value's form read a member by its declaration** (2026-10-28; amends D430 and D448; amended by D492). D430 rejects a source member whose form is Effect where the target's member is plain, and a member's form is now read from its declaration: a VibeLang function's or method's own form, and otherwise its declared return type read as rule R reads it, except that a type parameter of the class, interface, or type alias that declares the member stays uninstantiated. A function value's form, at a destination that takes only a plain function, reads a member's signature the same way, through any holder, since TypeScript's instantiated signature keeps its declaration. So a member whose declared return type is its container's type parameter, `get(): T` in `Box<T>`, is plain whatever the container holds: it returns what the container holds, and a caller of a plain type receives that value, as a TypeScript caller does. Rule R is unchanged, so `new Box(program).get()` runs where it is written (D458): the check asks what the member is, which its declaration says, and rule R asks what a call of it gives. Reason: D430 read a source member "as rule R reads a call of it", which after D458 instantiates a container's element type, so `Box<Effect<number>>` failed against `Getter<unknown>` and `{ get(): unknown }`, which TypeScript compares member by member, but passed against `Box<unknown>`, which it relates by variance, and `Effect<number>[]` failed against `ReadonlyArray<unknown>` through `reduce` by the letter while an implementation built on TypeScript's relation passes it (a reviewer); the rejection protected nothing, since `get` does no work of its own. Rejected: instantiating the container's parameters, which makes the check depend on how the target is spelled; and comparing members wherever TypeScript relates by variance, which would reject every array of Effects passed as `ReadonlyArray<unknown>`. Language change: those assignments compile; a getter that returns its container's element is passed where a plain function is expected; Effect methods and members declared to return an Effect still fail.

**D486. Rule R reads every type parameter but the callee's own as TypeScript shows it, and an instantiation expression binds the callee's own** (2026-10-28; amends D378 and D458; amended by D491). A type parameter that the resolved signature itself declares stays uninstantiated however it is bound, by the arguments, an explicit type argument, the call's contextual type, or an instantiation expression `f<X>`, directly or through a `const` that holds it; every other type parameter is read as TypeScript instantiates it for the call, so one that TypeScript shows uninstantiated, as `T` in `this.get()` inside `class Box<T>`, is a type parameter and is read as one. Reason: D458's clause "one in scope where the call stands" made `other.get()`, with `other: Box<Effect<number>>`, return data inside `Box`'s methods and run outside them, though TypeScript shows `Effect<number>` in both places, and `const idE = identity<Effect<number>>; idE(program)` matched neither of D458's cases (a reviewer). Language change: `other.get()` on another instance inside a generic class's methods runs where it returned data; a call through an instantiation expression of a generic callee returns its own type parameter as data.

**D487. A handler's answer type gains `undefined` only where the block can complete without returning; a `throw` adds nothing** (2026-10-28; amends D467). H is the type of what the try block and each clause return, plus `undefined` only when the block, or a clause that has not resumed, can complete normally or by a `break` or `continue` that leaves the handle statement; a completion by `throw` adds nothing, since a failure gives `resume` no value. Reason: D467's "completes other than by returning" counted a `throw`, so a try block that only throws widened H with `undefined` that no resume could receive (a reviewer). Language change: such an H is narrower, which can only accept programs that compiled before and reject none.

**D488. A type parameter that only the call's contextual type fixes is read as not an Effect at a destination, and a destination reaches a comma's right operand** (2026-10-28; amends D435 and D461; amended by D494). D435's ordered rule reads a destination's type parameter as its instantiation where another argument, the receiver, or an explicit type argument fixes it, and as not an Effect where only the function itself, or only the contextual type of the call's result, fixes it, so an annotation on the binding that receives a call never decides a function argument's form, as the principle says of every receiving annotation. A destination reaches the right operand of a comma expression, as TypeScript's contextual typing does. Reason: with `declare function g<T>(cb: (y: T) => T): T`, stock TypeScript types `y` in `const held: Effect<number> = g((y) => y)` as `Effect<number>`, from the binding's annotation, and the rule named neither that source nor its complement (a reviewer's probe); TypeScript's `getContextualTypeForBinaryOperand` reaches a comma's right operand, which the list of propagation forms left out. Language change: none intended for programs the rules decided; the two cases are stated.

**D489. Statements that contradicted rules or facts** (2026-10-28; no rule changes). Chapter 13's table of where a type decides, and a sentence of rule R, said that an alias, an interface, a class, and the same type written inline always mean the same, while an interface that extends `Effect` is data and an alias of `Effect` runs; they now say that which construct declares a type parameter is what never matters. Chapter 13's plain-call section called every call of a plain function Plain after D480 made one that returns a program Run; it now gives both kinds. Chapter 26 said declarations keep their kind without saying that a declaration file writes a plain function as a `const` of `Plain<F, C>` (D483). The introduction, Chapter 29, and Appendix A cited D1-D468 while D469 existed; Chapter 30's conformance claim named D392-D468 and had no case for D469, which changed what compiles. Chapter 13 and Chapter 30 said `retry(job ?? (() => a()), policy)` converts "whichever operand it evaluates", though with a nullish `job` both are evaluated; it converts the operand whose value it takes. Chapter 30's `reduce` case did not say that its result follows `lib.es5.d.ts`'s order of `reduce`'s overloads.

## The seventh review (2026-10-28)

A seventh review on 2026-10-28, by four reviewers working apart and graded with five added criteria (no contradictory requirements, a common-sense design review, simplicity, TypeScript's patterns, and detail), found that `using` chose its interruptibility by whether an initializer's requirements include `Scope`, which an unused `Scope` or a generic requirement changes; that an instantiation expression and an extracted method carried a fact into rule R and rule F that declaration emit drops; that an object or array that already holds a plain function reaches TypeScript holding its cold form while D430 read it as plain; that D485 read a member by its declaration on the source side only; that an annotation on the receiving binding reaches a callback's parameter types through TypeScript's inference, against the principle's wording; that an inferred plain form in a published declaration had no defined failure when a later release loses it; and a set of statements that lagged D480 to D482. Each decision names what it amends, and its last sentence says what changes for an implementation.

**D490. `using` is one Effect program for every initializer** (2026-10-28; amends D481 under that ruling's condition, and D25, D342 and D401). `using x = e` is `Effect.acquireRelease(e, release, { interruptible: true })` in the block's Scope, whatever `e`'s type: its `restore` runs `e` with the interruptibility in force where the declaration stands, as `yield* e` runs it, and `Effect.acquireRelease` registers `release` in the uninterruptible step that ends the acquire, so no interruption separates `e`'s value from its release, as ECMAScript's `using` registers its value when the declaration completes. Each acquisition inside `e` keeps its own policy: Effect's default `acquireRelease` and `acquireDisposable` mask their acquire, and `{ interruptible: true }` does not. Rule D builds no program here: `e` is evaluated where the declaration stands, under rule E, and the declaration adds only the registration. A masked acquire runs to its end except where its own code enters `interruptible { }`, `Effect.interruptible`, or `Effect.interruptibleMask`, which set the fiber interruptible under any mask, as Effect's do; a release, which runs under Effect's finalizer mask, has the same exception. "`e` requires `Scope`", which rule 4's static check and rule 2's last case still read, means that `Scope` is a member of the R TypeScript shows for `e`; a type parameter there is read by its constraint, and `any` and `unknown` are not `Scope`. A `using` supplies `Scope` to its initializer, so the function's R omits it, written `Exclude<R, Scope>` where the initializer's R holds a type parameter, a contract-less callback's channel included (D68). Reason: D481 chose between `{ interruptible: true }` and a masked acquire by whether `e`'s requirements include `Scope`, so an unused `Scope` in a contract, which Chapter 14 allows, turned a deadline that cancels a wait into one that waits for it (a reviewer's `waitLease(): Lease!!` and `waitLeaseWide(): Lease!!Scope` with one body), a generic `withResource(open)` could match only one of its inlined instances, a helper that gained an acquisition changed every `using` of it with no edit at the `using`, and the predicate had no reading for a type parameter (three reviewers); and Chapter 6's "interruption reaches no pause in `e`" was false on `effect@4.0.6`, where `Effect.interruptible` and `Effect.interruptibleMask` inside a masked acquire are cut at about 100 ms under `timeout(…, "100 millis")` (two reviewers' probes). D481's ruling names its own condition: where the translation depends on a type in a way Effect TypeScript written from the same source would not, the translation follows the source. Effect code binds a value with `yield*`, which masks nothing, and ECMAScript's `using` registers at once, so the one program is the interruptible one; the acquisition that wants a mask writes it, as Effect code writes `acquireDisposable`. Rejected: keeping the two programs and defining the predicate for type parameters, which leaves the unused-`Scope` and inlining differences; and one masked translation, which would hold a deadline at an acquisition that asked for `{ interruptible: true }`, as D425 did. Language change: a pause in an initializer that does not require `Scope` can be interrupted wherever the `using` stands in interruptible code, where D481 masked it, so an `openDb()` that waits and returns a `Disposable` is cut by a deadline and registers nothing; to wait it out, write `using db = Effect.acquireDisposable(() => openDb())`, whose acquire Effect masks and whose record makes `using` add no release; a function whose `using` initializer has a generic R omits `Scope` from its own R; the runtime's `$using` takes the region's Scope and the initializer's program and always passes `{ interruptible: true }`.

**D491. Rule R reads the callee's type as TypeScript writes it, so an instantiation expression is a value of its instantiated type** (2026-10-28; amends D378, D458 and D486). Rule R leaves uninstantiated a type parameter that the signature of the callee's type, as TypeScript writes that type in a hover and in a declaration file, itself declares, however the call binds it, and reads every other as TypeScript instantiates it for the call. An instantiation expression, `identity<X>`, is a value whose type TypeScript writes `(x: X) => X`, with no type parameter, so a call of it, `(identity<X>)(p)` or a call through any binding that holds it, a `const`, a `let`, a field, or an import, reads that signature; `identity<X>(p)`, a call with an explicit type argument, is a call of `identity`, whose signature declares `T`, and returns data as before. Rules R and F read nothing that the checker keeps and declaration emit drops. Reason: D486 kept the callee's own type parameter through "a `const` that holds" an instantiation expression, a fact the checker keeps in the instantiated signature's target and that declaration emit drops: stock TypeScript 5.9.3 writes `export const idE = identity<Effect.Effect<number>>` as `export declare const idE: (x: Effect.Effect<number>) => Effect.Effect<number>`, so `idE(program)` returned the program compiled with the `.ts` file and ran it compiled against its declaration, against Chapter 27's rule that a declaration "never runs or types a call differently" (two reviewers' emit probes), and a `let` or a field holding the same value ran while the `const` returned data (a third reviewer). Rejected: writing a provenance marker into declarations, which a declaration stock TypeScript emits does not have and which adds an ABI channel; and keeping D486's clause for the `const` only, which leaves the source/declaration split. Language change: a call through a binding that holds an instantiation expression of a generic callee runs where its instantiated return type is definitely an Effect, where D486 returned data; a call with an explicit type argument is unchanged.

**D492. A function value's form reads its type as TypeScript writes it, and D430 reads a member by its declaration on both sides** (2026-10-28; amends D397, D430 and D485). A function value's form, at any destination, is the form its type gives as TypeScript writes that type, read as rule R reads a call of it: `new Box(program).get`, written `() => Effect<number>`, is an Effect-form value wherever it is held, and D485's reading of a member's signature by its declaration "through any holder" is withdrawn. Chapter 14's check, which compares two object types, reads a member by its declaration on the source side and on the target side alike, with its declaring type's own type parameters left uninstantiated: a class's, an interface's, or a type alias's member by that declaration, and any other member, such as an object literal's property, by its type as TypeScript writes it. So a target member declared to return its container's type parameter is a plain member, as a destination function type returning a type parameter is (D435). The same reading applies at an object literal's field whose contextual member a declaration declares (rule F), and in D397's test of a method that implements a plain member. Reason: a declaration file keeps an object type's member declarations with the type, `Box<Effect<number>>` names `Box`, but writes an extracted method as `() => Effect<number>`, so D485's "through any holder" made `extracted = new Box(Effect.succeed(1)).get` plain from source and an Effect-form value from its stock declaration, accepted at a plain destination by one and EK4301 by the other (a reviewer's emit probe); and D485 read the source side by declaration and the target side instantiated, so an Effect method entered `Source<number!!>`, which accepts an Effect method once instantiated, and then passed as `{ get(): unknown }`, whose TypeScript caller dropped its work (a reviewer's probe on today's build: one `read` where two were due), as did a `.vibe` subclass of a `.ts` `Box<number!!>` overriding `get` with an Effect method. Rejected: instantiating the target only, which makes the check depend on how the target is spelled, as D485 found. Language change: an extracted or held member whose written type returns an Effect is an Effect-form value, EK4301 where only a plain function fits, from source and from declarations; an Effect-form method assigned to a generic container type whose member returns the container's type parameter, or implementing or overriding such a member, is EK4301, and a plain method returning the program replaces it.

**D493. D430 reads what an existing object or array holds: a `Plain<F, C>` there is `C`** (2026-10-28; amends D430 and D483; superseded by D500). Rule F converts a function value written at a destination, and a literal's fields and elements, but nothing converts what an existing object or array holds (D423), so a function of type `Plain<F, C>` held there is, at run time, the cold `C` that TypeScript code receives, with `F` only on its `[$direct]` property. D430 therefore reads a member, an element, or a type argument TypeScript relates by variance whose type is `Plain<F, C>` by `C`: plain only where `C` is `F`, for a function with a plain return annotation or a protocol member, and Effect otherwise, so passing such an object or array where a plain function type stands at that position is EK4301. The quick fix writes a literal at the destination, `invoke({ ...api })` or `runAll([...fs])`, whose fields and elements rule F converts, or a plain return annotation, which makes `C` be `F`. Reason: D483 carried the plain form into arrays and objects as a type, and D430 read that type as plain, so `runAll(fs)` with `const fs = [tick, tick]` compiled and ticked 0 times while `runAll([tick, tick])` ticked twice (a reviewer's probe on today's build), and `register(handlers)` with `const handlers = { onSave(doc) { … } }` handed TypeScript the cold method, whose call it dropped (two reviewers). Rejected: converting an existing object or array at the call, which would copy or proxy it and change its identity, as D423 found; and storing the plain function at an object literal's ordinary key, which changes D460's TypeScript-facing form and leaves `{ double }` and arrays of top-level functions unsolved. Language change: passing an existing object or array that holds a cold plain-form function where TypeScript would call it as plain is EK4301, where it compiled and dropped the work; a literal written there, or a plain-annotated function, compiles as before.

**D494. A function argument is typed as TypeScript types it, and the rough edge covers the call's contextual type** (2026-10-28; amends D435, D482 and D488; amended by D500). TypeScript types a function literal's unannotated parameters from the contextual signature it gives the argument, whatever fixes that signature: the overload it picks, the other arguments, the receiver, an explicit type argument, or, through its inference, the call's own contextual type, such as an annotation on the binding that receives the call. The literal's own form and every call in its body follow that typing. The principle's invariant covers the call and its operands: nothing the call instantiates, no annotation on the receiving binding, no type of a parameter the call fills, and no spelling changes when, whether, or how often the call runs or its operands are evaluated. D482's rough edge is stated for both sources, with its exact reach: a different typing can turn a call in the literal's body into one that runs and the literal's own form into Effect; at a destination that accepts only the plain form the Effect form is then EK4301, at one returning an Effect the plain form lifts, and only at a destination that accepts both forms (D112) does the receiver's call run the body under one typing and build a program under the other. A destination's type parameter that TypeScript infers from the call's contextual type is read as that instantiation, as one fixed by another argument is; one whose only source is the function itself is still read as not an Effect (D435), and D488's reading of a result-context type parameter as not an Effect is withdrawn. The overload VibeLang picks is TypeScript's with each function argument typed by its own form, `F` for a `Plain<F, C>` value, less D430's exclusions, so `register(double)` picks the plain overload where stock TypeScript, which types `double` by `C`, picks the Effect one. Reason: with `keep<T>(cb: (x: T) => number | Effect<number>): (x: T) => number | Effect<number>`, an annotation on the binding that receives `keep(…)` fixes `T`, and with it `x`'s type and whether `x.run()` runs, so the literal's form, and a TypeScript caller's run of its body, followed the annotation, against the principle's "no annotation on the binding that receives the call's value" and D488's stated purpose (a reviewer's TypeScript 5.9.3 probe: `x` is `Data` under one annotation and `Job` under the other); D488's carve-out reached only a type parameter at a destination's whole type or return type, so it bought no invariance while adding a case; Chapter 27 still said the order of overloads never decides when an expression is evaluated; and "the candidate picked is the one TypeScript itself picks" was false for a `Plain<F, C>` argument (two reviewers). Removing result-context inference from a literal's typing, a reviewer's alternative, would reject or retype `pipe(load, Effect.map((a) => a + 1))`, whose `a` TypeScript infers that way, so the rule follows TypeScript and states the cost. Rejected: an EK1021 for an unannotated literal at a both-forms destination whose form depends on a contextually typed parameter, a check D482's ruling declined; it is listed for the owner as the smallest change that would remove the rough edge. Language change: a type parameter at a destination that only the call's contextual type fixes is read as TypeScript instantiates it, where D488 read it as not an Effect; no program the conformance cases name changes its result.

**D495. An inferred plain form is part of a package's public type, and a plain call that finds none fails with a defect** (2026-10-28; amends D268, D446 and D483; superseded by D500). An eligible unannotated export's declaration writes `Plain<F, C>`, inferred from its body, so its plain form is part of its package's public types, as an inferred return type is part of a TypeScript package's: a later version whose body loses eligibility changes its declared type, a breaking change. The runtime's `$plainOf` reads `v[$direct]` where the value has it, and otherwise calls the value with the same receiver and arguments and calls the plain form that the returned program records (D446); a value that gives neither fails at the call with a defect naming the function and the plain signature its type recorded, and never runs the Effect form in its place. A bound function and a wrapper TypeScript types as returning its argument's type, `double.bind(null)` and `once(tick)`, reach the plain form that way, and the trusted base names them. A union callee whose members are each a `Plain<F, C>`, a `Trusted<F, Errors>`, or a trusted plain function type is a plain call, which takes what the call of a member of the last kind returns. A package that wants only written forms in its declarations sets TypeScript's `isolatedDeclarations`, which requires every exported function's return type (TS9007), and a written return type decides the form. Reason: a dependent compiled against a declaration that wrote `slug` as `Plain<…>`, installed beside a semver-compatible release whose `slug` logs, reached `$plainOf(slug)` with no defined behavior (a reviewer); Chapter 26's trusted base said a `Plain`-typed value lacks `[$direct]` only through a cast, while `bind`'s and `once`'s declared types keep `Plain<F, C>` (a reviewer's TypeScript 5.9.3 probe); and a union of `double` and `Math.abs`, which TypeScript types `number | Effect<number>` at the call, was neither a call that runs nor a plain-function call. Rejected: falling back to the Effect form, which at a module's top level would need a runner, against "a call never starts a fiber"; and writing only annotated forms into declarations, a change to the cost model of pure helpers that is listed for the owner. Language change: the skew and the union call have a defined behavior where they had none; the runtime's `$plainOf` raises the defect.

**D496. Statements that lagged D480 to D482** (2026-10-28; no rule changes). Chapter 13's function-kinds table, its call-generation table's plain-annotation row, the registry's EK1001 and EK2003 rows, Chapter 15, Chapter 10, Chapter 27's rules 2 and 3, and Chapter 29's P20 called every call of a plain function plain and legal in plain code, where D480 runs one whose plain return type is definitely an Effect; they now say so, the kinds table in one note over its call columns. Rule R and the plain-call section said a plain function that "returns a program" runs it, which a function returning a thunk does not; they say "whose plain return type is definitely an Effect". Chapter 27 said Effect code may run a plain function's Effect form instead with the same observations, which needs a second run where `F` returns an Effect, since the cold form's program returns that Effect unrun (a reviewer's `pick`); it now says so. Rule R's "never this EK4802" for a returned function was read against both of its sentences, while Chapter 6's release that returns a function is EK4802; it now names the sentence. `pick`'s declared type, eta-expansion of `pick`, a class field holding a function literal, `Plain<F, C>` written as a declared type, EK1018's `let` and field initializers, a `Pause` glossary entry, `x catch y` in the principle's list of deferred operands, Chapter 12's list of functions TypeScript receives plain, the introduction's date, and Chapter 29's unassigned numbers are stated or corrected.

**D497. Three smaller rules the seventh review found unstated** (2026-10-28; amends D374, D483 and D484; amended by D500). A computed key whose type is a protocol name's string literal type, `{ ["toString"]() { … } }`, is a protocol key, as JavaScript calls it by that key. A holder whose inferred type is `Plain<F, C>`, `let g = double`, keeps that type, and assigning it a function whose own form is Effect is EK4301, reported in place of TypeScript's TS2741 for the missing `[$direct]` member, whose message named the runtime's symbol; declaring the holder's type lets it hold either form. The combination count of nested data patterns, a restriction, is stated: at most 64 combinations, of fields at most 8 levels deep in the subject, the bounds the compiler uses; past either, the match needs `default` (EK4701). Reason: D484 named identifiers, string literals, and `unique symbol` keys, and left `["toString"]` out (a reviewer); TS2741 named `[$direct]` to a person who wrote no such member (a reviewer); and Chapter 11's "a fixed bound" let two implementations reject different matches (a reviewer). Language change: a computed string-literal protocol key holds the plain function; the TS2741 at such an assignment becomes EK4301; no program changes meaning from the stated bound.

**D498. An exported `const` or `let` named `main` that holds a function is `EK4843`** (2026-10-28; lint; extends D135). D135 starts only a function declaration named `main`, so `export const main = () => { … }` ran nothing and the program printed nothing; `EK4843`, a warning like EK4830-EK4840, says so, and its quick fix writes `export function main() { … }` where that declaration means what the function means. Language change: none; a new warning.

## Written forms (2026-10-28)

A ruling on 2026-10-28 settled which functions are plain. The seventh review left standing three mechanisms that inferred what the source did not write: a function's form came from its body, one value carried two forms, and a contract-less callback type in a `.vibe` file was effect-polymorphic. Two design studies compared four designs against Effect's own API and three migrated packages; the ruling adopts the one that writes every form, keeps the rules it already had for calls, and records the decision as controversial, with the alternatives it did not choose.

**D500. Written forms: a function without a return annotation is an Effect function, and a return annotation that is not an Effect makes it plain** (2026-10-28; a ruling; **Status: controversial**; supersedes D12, D68, D331, D398, D411, D422, D434, D446, D448, D460, D483, D493 and D495; amends D9, D10, D65, D67, D112, D268, D380, D384, D385, D392, D421, D430, D431, D445, D450, D453, D454, D455, D463, D480, D482, D484, D494 and D497; amended by D510, D511, D512, D513, D514 and D516). A function's form, plain or Effect, is read from syntax and declared types only. Its body never decides it, and no value carries two forms. The rule:

- **Unannotated means Effect.** A function declaration, function expression, arrow function, or method written in a `.vibe` file without a return annotation is an Effect function, whatever its body does: TypeScript sees `(…) => Effect<A, E, R>`, a VibeLang call of it runs (rule R), and plain code may not call it (EK1001, EK2003, EK4301). Its contract is inferred from its body as before (Chapter 14). Not functions in this sense, as before: a producer, `function*` (D370); a constructor, an accessor, and a static block, which run as plain JavaScript (D282); a class's unannotated method that is a protocol method or implements a plain member its class declares, which is plain (D397); and a function literal at a declared destination (below).
- **A return annotation that is not an Effect means plain.** A function or method whose return annotation is a type that is not definitely an Effect, in rule R's sense, is a plain function, for VibeLang and TypeScript alike: one JavaScript function, declared with its TypeScript signature. Its body may not run an Effect, let a `throw` or a listed failure escape, or need a service (EK2003, D282). A definitely-Effect annotation, `A!E!R` or `Effect<A, E, R>`, is an Effect contract, and the function is an Effect function whatever its body (D421).
- **`: infer _` is the inferred plain return.** A return annotation written `infer _` makes a function plain and leaves its return type to TypeScript's inference from the body, as an unannotated TypeScript function's is, so a helper whose type is long, a `Layer<…>`, a `Stream<…>`, or an object type, need not spell it. It reuses D57's spelling, in which `infer _` already means "what the body gives". A plain function whose return type, written or inferred, is definitely an Effect returns that program as its value: a TypeScript caller receives it unrun, and a VibeLang call runs it, since the call's declared return type is an Effect (rule R, D480).
- **A function literal takes the form its destination's declared type gives it, never its body's.** At a destination, as rule F lists them, a literal is an Effect function where the type's function returns a definitely-Effect type, a program where the type is an `Effect` and the literal has no required parameters (rule D's conversion), and a plain function everywhere else, a function type returning a union of an Effect and another type (D112) or a type parameter that only the function fixes (D435) included. The literal's own return annotation overrides its destination: a plain annotation makes it plain, and an Effect annotation an Effect function, which is EK4301 where only a plain function fits. A literal with no declared destination, `const half = (n: number) => n / 2`, is an Effect function by the first rule.
- **A callback type `(a: A) => B` means what TypeScript means.** It is a plain function type wherever it is written, a parameter of a `.vibe` function included, written in place or through an alias. A callback that may run Effects is typed `(a: A) => B!E!R`, with `E` and `R` declared as type parameters like any other, as Effect TypeScript declares them. No channel is added that the source does not write.
- **Calls still run where they stand, and `() =>` holds a program.** Rule R is unchanged: a call runs where the declared return type of the signature it resolves to is definitely an Effect (D480), and a function written where an `Effect` is declared is a program (rule D). A function value has the form its type gives, as TypeScript writes that type (D492): it is held and passed as it is, and nothing converts one form into the other, except that a function with no required parameters converts to a program where an `Effect` is declared (D381). In a `.vibe` file, a signature that returns a definitely-Effect type never satisfies one that returns another type (D430, EK4301).

What follows:

- **Withdrawn.** Eligibility as a form, the body analysis that made an unannotated function plain (D10, D392, D422, D450); the runtime type `Plain<F, C>`, the plain marker, and every rule that read it (D411, D448, D460, D483, D493, D495, and D497's holder clause); the runtime's `$plainOf`; a class's `$plain` marker (D431); the channels a contract-less callback parameter carried, their names, and EK2130 (D12, D68, D331, D398, D434); and the lifting of a plain function to an Effect function. D446's recorded plain form goes with them: a plain function is the hoisted JavaScript function itself.
- **Eligibility stays as an optimization.** Where an Effect function's body performs no Effect operation and its E is `never`, the compiler may give it a plain variant for its calls in Effect code, which rule E11 makes unobservable (Chapter 15, D421). It changes no form, no declaration, and no diagnostic, so adding a log call to an unannotated function changes nothing outside it, and adding one to a plain-annotated function fails that function's own build with EK2003.
- **Declarations write the form the source wrote.** A plain function's declaration is its TypeScript signature, and an Effect function's its Effect signature (Chapter 27). What a declaration cannot say, that a plain function was compiled from VibeLang and so never fails with an expected failure and needs no service, the compiler records outside it: beside the declaration files it emits, it writes `vibelang.manifest.json`, which lists each declaration file it emitted from a `.vibe` file, relative to that directory. A consumer that resolves a declaration file listed in the nearest such manifest above it trusts that module as D261 trusts a package in `trustedPackages`: its plain functions and methods are trusted plain calls, whose exceptions are defects. A package publishes the manifest with its declarations. The manifest is not part of any `.d.ts`, so stock TypeScript reads declarations identical to the TypeScript original's.
- **Function values.** A plain function whose return type is definitely an Effect fits a destination whose function type returns one, as in TypeScript, which replaces D453's unlifted clause. A plain function is never lifted: where only an Effect function fits, a plain function returning another type is TypeScript's assignability error, and a literal there, `(x) => slugify(x)`, takes the Effect form. An unannotated literal at a destination that returns an Effect is an Effect function, so `flatMap(x, (v) => cached)` returns the program `cached` as its success value and is EK4802 (D463), repaired by `(v) => cached()`.
- **Union callees.** A call of a union of function types whose members differ in form, some declared to return a definitely-Effect type and some not, is EK4301, since its plain call would build the Effect members' programs unrun (amends D454, whose clause for a contract-less callback parameter is withdrawn).
- **Overloads.** TypeScript types a literal argument once, by the first candidate it checks the literal against (D450, D482); the literal takes that destination's form, and the candidate accepts it or not by TypeScript's assignability and D430, never by what its body does. So a library's overload order, or the call's contextual type (D494), decides whether a literal compiles and, for one whose body compiles in either form, which form it takes (D510): a literal whose body runs an Effect at a plain destination is EK4301, whose quick fix writes an Effect return annotation. At a destination that accepts both forms, a literal is plain unless an Effect annotation says otherwise, so the `consume` and `keep` cases of D482 and D494 are EK4301 under the typing that makes their bodies run an Effect, where they silently built programs before. EK1021 keeps one case, two function arguments whose conversions to programs prefer different candidates (D380); its lifting pass is withdrawn with lifting.
- **Migration.** `vibelang --fix` writes the return type on each function an earlier rule made plain by its body and that code calls or passes where only a plain function fits, `: T` where TypeScript prints `T` in at most 12 tokens and `: infer _` otherwise; rewrites a contract-less callback parameter of a `.vibe` function that receives an Effect function to `(a: A) => B!E!R`, adding `E` and `R` to its type parameters under fresh names where those are taken; writes an Effect return annotation on a literal at a destination that accepts both forms whose body runs an Effect, which the earlier rule passed as an Effect function; and turns `return cached` into `return cached()` in an unannotated literal at a destination that returns an Effect. Each rewrite keeps the meaning the site had (D372). An exported function whose TypeScript API an annotation would change, from a cold `Effect` to a plain function, is EK1023 and is not rewritten (D382).

Reason: the review findings that remained after the seventh review traced to three inferred mechanisms. A function's form came from its body; one value carried two forms, `Plain<F, C>`, plain to VibeLang and cold to TypeScript; and a contract-less callback type in a `.vibe` file was effect-polymorphic.
