Through them, the order of a library's overloads, an annotation on a receiving binding, a stored array of helpers, a `let` reassigned to another function, and a log line added to a dependency could each drop work, or change it between a source consumer and a declaration consumer, and an exported arrow and an exported function declaration emitted different forms. A dogfood migration found the third: t3code's `(value: unknown) => string | null | undefined` parameter was read as an Effect callback, so `SchemaGetter.transform`'s synchronous callback was EK4301 and unchanged TypeScript consumers got TS2322. Written forms remove the inference and the second representation, and keep calls running where they stand (settled decision 1) and "every function is an Effect" (D385) with exactly one exception, a plain return annotation. Effect 4 marks the form in its own types the same way: `SchemaGetter.transform` takes a plain callback and `transformEffect` an Effect one, `Stream.filter` and `Stream.filterEffect` likewise, and of the 628 exported functions of `effect@4.0.6` whose own parameters include a function type, 437 take only plain callbacks, 187 only Effect callbacks, and 4 both, in different parameters; none overloads one name on its callback's form. Cost, measured with the o200k_base tokenizer the token counts use: the 47 cheat-sheet pairs take 2,112 VibeLang tokens against 2,110 (+0.09%), still 13.1% fewer than Effect TypeScript's 2,431, and one entry changes; across t3code's server, contracts and shared packages, foldkit, and alchemy's domain package, 6,757 named functions, of which 5,533 (82%) return a non-Effect type and 4,109 of those already carry a return annotation, need 576 more annotations in 557 files with Effect code, median 2 tokens (`: void`, `: string`), 121 of them long inferred types that `: infer _` spells in 3 tokens.

**Status: controversial.** The rule makes the most common TypeScript move, calling an unannotated local helper inside `.map` or at a module's top level, a compile error, EK4301 or EK1001, until the helper writes its return type. A TypeScript developer or an agent meets it on first contact, and the repair can read as ceremony. The quick fix writes the annotation, `--fix` writes it on migrated code, and the measured cost on migrated code is about one annotation per file with Effect code. The rule is reopened if new code written in VibeLang needs more than 3 such annotations per file with Effect code, counted as the migration was (Chapter 30, G23).

**Alternatives considered.**

- **(A) Every `.vibe` function is an Effect function; plain code lives only in TypeScript or a hatch.** Rule: every function written in VibeLang is an Effect function, a plain return annotation on one is an error, and a plain function exists only in a `.ts` file, a `module` declaration, or a hatch's body.

  ```ts es
  module text {
    export function slugify(title: string): string { return title.toLowerCase() }
  }
  import { slugify } from text

  const slugs = titles.map((t) => slugify(t))         // EK4301 under (A): the arrow is an Effect function
  const name = Option.map(maybeUser, (u) => u.name)   // EK4301 under (A)
  const trimmed = SchemaGetter.transform((s: string) => s.trim())   // EK4301 under (A): move it to TypeScript
  ```

  Not chosen: Effect's own API needs plain callbacks. 437 of the 628 callback-taking functions in `effect@4.0.6` take only plain callbacks (`Option.map`, `Array.map`, `SchemaGetter.transform`, `Order`, `Predicate`, `Match`), and JavaScript calls a `sort` comparator, a `replace` callback, `toString`, `toJSON`, and `[Symbol.dispose]` synchronously. In the measured packages, the 3,798 pure helpers used from plain code, and every exported one, would move to TypeScript, and a `module` body cannot read the file's bindings (D229), so a helper that uses an `error` class or another `.vibe` helper cannot move alone. A variant that compiles only function literals at plain destinations as plain still rejects every exported pure helper.

- **(B) Call-site unwrap in the `async`/`await` style: a call runs only as `await f()` or `yield* f()`.** Rule: every call is JavaScript's, `f(x)` evaluates to what `f` returns, a cold Effect included; `await e` runs `e` and gives its success value; a function whose body awaits is an Effect function, typed as an `async` function's `Promise` is, and every other function is plain; combinators take Effect values.

  ```ts es
  function slugify(title: string) { return title.toLowerCase() }   // (title: string) => string
  function greet(id: string) {
    const user = await Users.find(id)
    await log(`hello ${user.name}`)
    return user.name
  }
  const fresh = await retry(greet(id), Schedule.recurs(3))
  if (canPublish(user)) await publish(post)   // canPublish returns an Effect, which is truthy: this always publishes
  ```

  (B) is the only design that removes type-directed running entirely: no type decides whether a call runs, so rule R, D378, D486 and D491 would go. Not chosen: it reverses settled decision 1, that calls expose results with no marker at each call; "every function is an Effect" (D385), since a pure function would be plain; and D226, that a plain `await` is an error. It costs about 27 `await`s per file with Effect code on t3code's server, which writes 13,788 `yield*` in 454 Effect files, about 12,200 of them `await`s once the service lookups VibeLang already folds are removed, 994 of them parenthesized as `(await x).y`; and it costs 2,151 tokens on the cheat sheet (+1.9%). It brings back the forgotten-`await` bug class that TypeScript's `no-floating-promises` and `no-misused-promises` lints and Effect's `floatingEffect` rule exist for: `if (canPublish(u))` is always true. And a plain export that gains an `await` changes its calling convention silently for a dependent compiled against its earlier declaration, unless every Effect function also carries a declared marker.

- **(C-keyword) A `sync function` keyword marks a plain function, in place of a return annotation.** Rule: `sync function f(…) { … }`, `sync (x) => …`, and `sync m() { … }` define plain functions; every other function is an Effect function.

  ```ts es
  sync function slugify(title: string) { return title.toLowerCase() }
  const slugs = titles.map(sync (t) => slugify(t))
  ```

  It has written forms' semantics. Not chosen: the keyword has no TypeScript or TC39 precedent, and new syntax must read as TypeScript or come from a TC39 proposal; it collides with the prelude's `sync` hatch; it writes a marker on every plain callback, where written forms take a literal's form from its destination and write none; and its one advantage, a plain function whose long return type is inferred, `: infer _` gives with D57's existing spelling.

Two more were weighed and set aside: an unannotated function plain and an Effect function writing its contract, which contradicts "every function is an Effect" and costs a contract on most functions that run Effects; and body eligibility kept with one representation, which costs no tokens but keeps the body deciding the form, makes 82% of the measured functions plain to TypeScript, and turns a log line added to an exported helper into a silent change of its calling convention for dependents compiled earlier. Language changes: a function the body made plain is an Effect function unless it writes its return type; a contract-less callback parameter is plain; an exported pure helper's declaration is its TypeScript signature where annotated and its Effect signature otherwise, never `Plain<F, C>`; `flatMap(x, (v) => cached)` is EK4802; a mixed union callee is EK4301; a literal whose body runs an Effect at a plain or both-forms destination is EK4301 where the earlier rules passed it as an Effect function. Evidence: the conformance rows of Chapter 30 for D500, among them the design studies' probes, each of which compiles with the meaning its text gives or fails with a named error under this rule.

## Declarations as Effect TypeScript writes them (2026-10-28)

**D501. A `service`, `schema`, or `error` declaration declares what idiomatic Effect TypeScript declares** (2026-10-28; a ruling; supersedes D361, D465 and D466; amends D93, D128, D133, D281, D285 and D407; amended by D515 and D519). Each declaration emits, and its declaration file writes, exactly what the Effect TypeScript it replaces emits, so a migrated library's public `.d.ts` stays the same:

- `service X { … }` declares `class X extends Context.Service<X, Shape>()("<key>") {}`, with `Shape` the object type of its members, written in place, as Effect TypeScript writes it; `service X: T` declares the same with `T` as `Shape`. The key is Effect's own string key: the derived key (D93) unless the body writes `static readonly key = "<key>"` with a string literal, which is then the key and is not emitted as a static property of its own, so a migrated service keeps the key its TypeScript original wrote. A service with `static readonly default` declares `const X = Context.Reference<Shape>("<key>", { defaultValue: () => … })`, Effect's own reference.
- `schema P { … }` declares `const P = Schema.Struct({ … })` and `type P = typeof P.Type`; `schema P = <type>` declares the schema constant and the same type alias.
- `error E { … }` declares what TypeScript declares for `class E extends Data.TaggedError("E")<{ readonly … }> {}`, and `schema error E { … }` what it declares for D279's `class E extends Schema.TaggedError<E>()("E", { … })`, with no member, marker, or namespace of VibeLang's.
- Nothing else: no `ServiceBrand`, `ServiceKey`, or `ReferenceKey` type, no merged `namespace X { interface Shape }`, no `#private` member, and no `$error` marker.

Identity rests on Effect's own:

- **Services.** A service's identifier, the type `X` that R holds, is the instance type Effect's class gives it, `{ readonly [ServiceTypeId]: …; readonly key: "<key>"; readonly Service: Shape }`. The literal type of its `key` and Effect's unique `ServiceTypeId` make it nominal by key: no structural type, `{ get(): number!! }` included, is assignable to it, and two services with different keys are different types, so the relabeling D361 closed stays closed with no brand of VibeLang's, and EK3001 keeps a key to one declaration. What changes is that the identifier no longer carries the shape's members, as Effect's does not. `X()` returns the implementation typed as the shape, as `yield* X` does in Effect; a parameter that takes an implementation is typed `X.Shape`, which a `.vibe` file reads, for every service key `X`, as Effect's `Context.Service.Shape<typeof X>`, the spelling declaration emit writes; and `implements X` in a `.vibe` class checks the class against the shape and is emitted `implements Context.Service.Shape<typeof X>`.
- **Errors.** An `error` class's identity is its class at run time, as in Effect: `instanceof` and the constructor tell two declarations apart, and TypeScript's types, which are structural, do not, as they do not in Effect TypeScript. A class is **closed** (D407) when its instance type extends the pinned `effect`'s `YieldableError`, as every `error` declaration's, every `Data.TaggedError`, `Data.Error`, and Schema error class's, and `effect`'s own error classes' do, and neither it nor a class it extends declares its own `static [Symbol.hasInstance]` or `[customMatcher]`, which its declaration file shows; one installed copy is still required (D427). The checker relies on that and on the run-time test that D405 keeps in every arm: a value of a same-shaped class of another declaration, which TypeScript accepts where the class is expected, fails each arm's `instanceof` and reaches the end of an exhaustive match as a defect, never another arm.
- **Schemas.** A schema's type is an alias of its decoded type, structural, as Effect's is.

Reason: a migrated library's declarations must stay what its TypeScript users compiled against. Dogfood migrations of foldkit, alchemy, and t3code each changed their public declarations where a service or a schema was rewritten in VibeLang: a service's declaration wrote VibeLang's brand, key types, and shape namespace in place of Effect's class and dropped its written key, and a schema's type became an interface. D361 wanted a requirement to be nominal, and Effect's class already makes it nominal by its key; D407 wanted a match over error classes to be sound, and the run-time test it kept already makes a same-shaped impostor a defect rather than another arm. Language changes: a parameter typed with a service's name, `db: Users`, takes the identifier and no longer accepts an implementation, so it is typed `Users.Shape`; another package's same-shaped error class is accepted where an `error` is expected, as in Effect TypeScript, and a value of it that reaches an exhaustive match is a defect at its end; a match over `Data.TaggedError` classes that a `.ts` file declares now counts toward exhaustiveness without `default`; a migrated service keeps its key through `static readonly key`, and EK4831's fix writes it there.

## Smaller rulings (2026-10-28)

**D502. Statements that contradicted the implementation: the guard and an exported `const main`** (2026-10-28; no rule changes; amended by D517 and D520). The appendix grammar wrote a match arm's guard after its pattern, `MatchArm : "when" Pattern Guard? ":" …`, and Chapter 3 wrote `when X if (…)`, while D207's subset, D212's repair `and if (n < 10)`, the user guide, and the parser write a guard as a pattern term joined by `and`: `when P and if (e):`. The grammar now has the guard as a `PatternTerm`, `"if" "(" Expression ")"`, and Chapter 3 and Chapter 26 write it so. D135 starts an exported function declaration named `main`; Chapter 13 now says that an exported `const` or `let` named `main`, even one holding a function, is not an entry point, so the module starts nothing, and the compiler warns, and that a `main` with a plain return annotation, not one whose body compiles to plain JavaScript (D500), runs through `Effect.sync`. Reason: a review probe of `when Shape.Circle { let r } if (r > 0):` reported TS1005 against the grammar the specification calls normative (D419); the entry rule named only function declarations without saying what a `const` does.

**D503. A hatch's first argument whose type is a function is the hatch's function** (2026-10-28; amends D396 and D439; narrows EK4411). A first argument that is not a function literal is evaluated when the hatch runs, inside its boundary, as D396 says. Where its type is a function type, the hatch calls that function with the inputs, and the call's result is the hatch's: `sync(fn, input)` is `sync((x) => fn(x), input)` with `fn` read when the hatch runs, and `sync(Math.random)` calls `Math.random`, as `Effect.sync(Math.random)` does. A function value has no syntax that says it is `async`, so its declared return type says it: one returning TypeScript's `Promise<T>` is asynchronous, so the hatch waits for its promise and passes the `AbortSignal` after the inputs where the function declares a parameter there, other than a rest parameter, whose type accepts an `AbortSignal`; any other function value is synchronous, as D439 makes a function literal not written `async`. EK4411 remains for inputs after a first argument whose type is not a function, which nothing would receive, and for a first argument whose type mixes a function with other members, which could be called or returned. Reason: inputs exist so that values reach a hatch's TypeScript without a capture (D229, D230), and a function imported from TypeScript, written in first place, is TypeScript the hatch may read; D396 rejected `sync(fn, input)` only because "a function" was undefined, and calling the function is what a TypeScript reader of `Effect.sync(fn)` and the runtime's `sync` do. Three shipped acceptance cases write the form. Language change: `sync(fn, …inputs)`, `try(fn, mapper, …inputs)`, and `sync(Math.random)` compile and call the function, where D396 made them EK4411.

**D504. A repeated `infer` name is a warning** (2026-10-28; amends D414). EK2129, an `infer` name other than `_` written in two channels of one contract, is a warning by default, adjustable in the `diagnostics` setting, not an error. Reason: the name after `infer` is documentation only (D57), so each channel is inferred on its own whatever the names say and the contract means what it means with distinct names; the code reports a misleading spelling, as the EK48xx warnings report style, and an error rejected programs that compile and run. Language change: such contracts compile, with the warning, where D414 rejected them.

## The eighth review (2026-10-28)

An eighth review on 2026-10-28, by four reviewers working apart and graded with the same five added criteria, found that the text claimed overload order and a call's contextual type decide only whether a literal compiles, which is false for a literal whose body performs no Effect operation; that it never said what type a literal has while a candidate checks it; that `xs.map(pick)` was accepted by one rule and EK4301 by another, and that declarations can give only the second answer; that a binding typed to return a value or an Effect laundered an Effect function to a plain one; that a structural error type was credited with exhaustive `instanceof` coverage it cannot have; that rule F's bullet and its table disagreed at destinations that give no form, so `once(() => n++)` ran its body on every call; that `trusted(impl, [Errors])` still carried two forms; that the manifest trusted a re-exported TypeScript function; and a set of statements that lagged D500 and D501. Each decision names what it amends, and its last sentence says what changes for an implementation.

**D510. The overload rough edge, stated exactly, and a literal's type during overload resolution** (2026-10-28; amends D482, D494 and D500 under D482's ruling). D482's rough edge stays, as the owner ruled: overloads resolve by TypeScript's procedure and no candidate is re-checked. What the order of a library's overloads and a call's contextual type decide is now stated exactly: a literal's parameter types, whether it compiles, and, for a literal that compiles in either form, its form. A literal whose body runs an Effect at a destination of the plain form is EK4301, as before. A literal whose body performs no Effect operation compiles in both forms, so the first candidate checked decides whether its body runs at each call the receiver makes or at each run of the program the receiver gets, never if it runs none: with `consume(f: () => number)` before `consume(f: () => Effect<number>)`, `consume(() => { events.push(1); return 0 })` pushes once per call, and with the declarations reversed it pushes only when `consume` runs the program it receives. No diagnostic reports it; the fix is a written form, `(): number =>` or `(): number!! =>`, which takes the same overload and form under either order. The type each candidate checks a literal against is its type in the form the first candidate gave it: its parameters as that candidate typed them, and its return type the body's, wrapped as `Effect<A, E, R>` where the form is Effect, so a literal whose body returns a number fits an Effect-returning candidate that stock TypeScript would pass over. The principle, rule F, Chapter 27, G22, D500, and two conformance rows that said "never what a literal that compiles does" or "in both orders its body runs once per call" now say this. Reason: reviewers showed, each with a trusted implementation that obeys both signatures, two accepted elaborations that push `[1]` and `[]`; the text denied what its own "Where a type decides" row and conformance row stated. Rule change for implementations: the acceptance type is now normative, so an implementation that wraps stock `chooseOverload` must type an unannotated literal in the first candidate's form before later candidates check it; conformance expects both counts for the reversed `consume` and `register` cases. No new diagnostic.

**D511. A function value's form is its type's, a plain declaration that returns an Effect included** (2026-10-28; amends D480, D492 and D500). Rule F's declaration bullet decides how a function compiles, one JavaScript function whose body is checked as plain, and what TypeScript callers receive; as a value, every function has the form its type gives, as TypeScript writes it (D492). So `pick(p: number!!): infer _ { return p }`, whose type `(p: Effect<number>) => Effect<number>` is an Effect function's, is an Effect-form value, and `xs.map(pick)` is EK4301, from source and from declarations alike, as `xs.map(run)` is for an Effect function `run` with the same declaration. `xs.map((p): infer _ => p)` collects the programs as data. A call of `pick` still runs the program it returns (D480). Rule R's "written `: infer _` or with its type" now reads "written `: infer _`", the one spelling of a plain function whose return type is an Effect, since a written Effect type is a contract (D421). Reason: Chapter 13 and a conformance row accepted `xs.map(pick)` while rule F's value bullet, D430, Chapter 27's ABI, and the glossary rejected it, and the declarations of `pick` and `run` are identical, so only the rejection is implementable without a marker D500 withdrew. Language change: `xs.map(pick)` for a plain function whose return type is definitely an Effect is EK4301 where the earlier text passed it.

**D512. A function that may return an Effect does not satisfy a plain function type** (2026-10-28; amends D430). D430's target is unchanged: a call signature or method whose return type is neither definitely an Effect nor a union with such a member. Its source side now also covers a signature whose return type is a union with a definitely-Effect member: with `tick` an Effect function, `const both: () => number | Effect<void> = tick` is accepted, since its target declares that it takes either, and `const erased: () => unknown = both` is EK4301, as `const erased: () => unknown = tick` is, and so is `xs.map(both)`. Reason: a reviewer showed that an honestly typed intermediate binding turned an Effect function into a value rule F reads as plain, which D430 then passed to a caller that drops its program. Language change: such an assignment or argument is EK4301 where it compiled.

**D513. A destination typed by a constrained type parameter, or `Function`, gives its constraint's form, and D430 checks constraints** (2026-10-28; amends D430, D435 and D500). Rule F's bullet made a literal plain "everywhere else" while its table made it an Effect function at `Function` and at a type parameter only the function fixes. Now: a destination whose type is a function type returning a type that is not definitely an Effect, `Function`, or a type parameter that only the function fixes whose constraint is one of these, gives the plain form, as TypeScript takes the literal's contextual signature from that constraint; a type parameter whose constraint is a function type returning a definitely-Effect type gives the Effect form; and `any`, `unknown`, `object`, an unconstrained type parameter, and any other type give none, so a literal there is an Effect function, as one with no destination is. D430 also applies where TypeScript checks an inferred or explicit type argument against its constraint. So with lodash's `once<T extends (...args: any) => any>(func: T): T`, `const init = once(() => { n++ })` is plain and `init(); init()` adds one, as in TypeScript; `useCallback((e) => log(e), [])` is EK4301; and `once(fetchUser)`, with `fetchUser` an Effect function, is EK4301. Reason: under the table's reading, `once`, `memoize`, `debounce`, and `useCallback` made their literal an Effect function, so a cached program ran on every call with no diagnostic, and two implementers would disagree; the constraint is part of the destination's declared type, so the change keeps D500's "a literal follows its destination". Language change: a literal at `Function` or at a function-constrained type parameter is plain where it was an Effect function, and a function value that violates such a constraint is EK4301.

**D514. `trusted(impl, [Errors])` returns one plain function** (2026-10-28; amends D249 and D400 under D500's "no value carries two forms"). The value `trusted` returns calls `impl` with its arguments and returns what `impl` returns, and carries its list as `f[$errors]`; its type, `Trusted<F, Errors>`, is `F & { readonly [$errors]: Errors }`. A TypeScript caller receives a function of `impl`'s type that throws what `impl` throws. A VibeLang call of it is a plain trusted call, as a built-in's is (D399): an exception of a listed class is an expected failure where the call stands, in every function, and any other is a defect. As a value it is a plain function: `lines.map(slugify)` passes it when its list is empty and is EK4301 when the list is not, since a listed failure would escape the receiver as a defect. `f[$direct]`, the plain marker, and the Effect-returning wrapper are withdrawn. Reason: D400 kept the two-form value D500 withdrew everywhere else, on D411's marker, which D500 superseded; its form as a value was undefined, and the built-ins already show the one-function design. Language change: TypeScript callers of a `trusted` value receive a plain function where they received one returning an `Effect`; `lines.map(slugify)` is defined, accepted with an empty list and EK4301 with a non-empty one; the runtime's `trusted` returns a plain function carrying `$errors`.

**D515. A class arm over a tagged class tests its tag, and exhaustiveness rests on the tag** (2026-10-28; amends D213, D346, D428 and D501; supersedes D215 for class arms, D407's closed-class coverage, and D427). D501 made error types structural, as Effect TypeScript's are, so another declaration's class with the same tag and fields is assignable to an `error` class and fails its `instanceof`; no `instanceof` arm can then cover the type. A **tagged class** is one whose instance type declares `_tag` as a string literal or a union of string literals: an `error` declaration with a literal tag, `Data.TaggedError`, `Data.TaggedClass`, `Schema.TaggedError`, and `effect`'s tagged errors. A class arm, `when C:` or `x is C`, over a tagged class that declares no `[customMatcher]` of its own matches an object whose `_tag`, read once, is in `C`'s tag set, as Effect's `catchTag` and `Match.tag` do, and removes from the subject every case whose `_tag` type that set covers; over a subject whose declared type has no such case, `unknown`, `any`, or `object`, it tests `instanceof` and removes nothing, since no type vouches for the tag there. A base whose subclasses carry their own tags lists them, since EK2117 requires it, so the arm still matches the class and its subclasses. A class arm over any other class, an open family's base whose `_tag` is `string`, an `abstract error`, `Error`, a class with no `_tag`, or one with its own `[customMatcher]`, tests that matcher or `instanceof` and removes nothing. The closed-class test (`YieldableError`, no own `[Symbol.hasInstance]`), the one-installed-copy check, and trusted-base items 8 and 9 and item 4's closedness clause are withdrawn. Reason: two reviewers showed, with two `.vibe` modules each declaring `error Missing { id: string }`, a well-typed argument that reached the end of a match the text proved exhaustive; the tag is what the type guarantees, and it is the test Effect's own `catchTag`, which a migrated program uses, makes. Language change: `when C:` over a tagged class tests `_tag` and no longer `instanceof`, so a same-shaped class of another declaration, or a second installed copy, takes the arm where it reached the match's end as a defect; an arm over an open family's base no longer covers the family, so such a match needs `default`.

**D516. The manifest lists the exports it trusts, and is found by real path** (2026-10-28; amends D500). `vibelang.manifest.json`'s `declarations` maps each declaration file the compiler emitted from a `.vibe` file to the exports the trust covers: those the file binds by a function or class declaration, or by a `const` or `let` initialized with a function literal or an object literal written there. A re-export, `export { parse } from "./legacy.ts"`, and a binding initialized from any other expression are not listed, so a declaration consumer reads them as foreign code (EK4401). A consumer finds the manifest after TypeScript's module resolution, `exports` conditions, `typesVersions`, and symlinked installs included, from the resolved `.d.ts`, `.d.mts`, or `.d.cts` file's real path, up to its package's root; a declaration file no manifest lists, such as a rolled-up one, is foreign. The manifest is the producing package's assertion, trusted as its declaration files are (Chapter 26). Reason: the per-module manifest trusted a re-exported TypeScript function, so a declaration consumer compiled a call that a source consumer rejects, against Chapter 27's "more conservative, never different"; the resolution of a manifest was unstated. Language change: the manifest's format changes from an array of files to a map of files to export names, and a re-export of foreign code is EK4401 to a declaration consumer where it was trusted. No consumer opt-out is added: a consumer that does not trust a package's assertions does not trust its declarations either.

**D517. The exported `const main` warning is `EK4843`, and the hatch summary names its type case** (2026-10-28; amends D502). `EK4843` now covers an exported `const` or `let` named `main`, which is no entry, with the fix `export function main()`; EK1016 remains the warning for an unexported, unreferenced zero-argument `main`. Chapter 10's "A hatch has one calling convention, which syntax decides" now says that the spelling and the first argument decide it, a literal by its syntax and any other first argument by its type (D503). Reason: D502's warning had no code, and the summary contradicted D503. Language change: the warning has a code and the `diagnostics` setting adjusts it.

**D518. Statements that lagged D500 and D501** (2026-10-28; no rule changes). Chapter 1's snippet convention declares `toUser`, `isAdmin`, `isActive`, `toRow`, and `isError` plain helpers with return types, so the examples that pass them to `find`, `filter`, and `map`, or call them from TypeScript, compile as shown. Chapter 13's rule D row for `x catch y` says `x` runs at once as a catch region's body and `y` only after an expected failure; rule D states how a literal converted at an `Effect<A, E, R>` destination is typed, which Chapter 27 already said; rule F says that extracting a literal into an unannotated `const` makes it an Effect function, with the repair; Chapter 14 gives the spelling of a plain method that implements a generic interface at an Effect type argument and says that an unannotated recursive function is an Effect function, so EK2102 applies to it; Chapter 6's `using` trigger is every declaration; E5 cites D480 and D500 for D68; the plain-variant wording in Chapters 26 and 27 names an Effect function's variant; the grammar note no longer puts accessors under the Effect default; Chapter 2 names EK1001 and EK4301 at first use; the glossary's declared return type covers plain functions; the introduction and Appendix A say D1-D520; and a conformance row drops the withdrawn name `eligibleM`. Reason: reviewers quoted each statement against D500 or the rule it restates.

**D519. A service's `Shape` and a defaulted service as types** (2026-10-28; amends D501). A `.vibe` file accepts `typeof S.Service`, Effect's own spelling, as the same type as `S.Shape`; where `S`'s declaration, or a namespace merged with it, declares a type member named `Shape`, `S.Shape` is that member, as TypeScript reads it. A service with `static readonly default` declares a value only, `const S = Context.Reference<Shape>(…)` with its shape written in place: `S.Shape` names the shape it holds, and `S` written as a type, in R or an `implements` clause, is TypeScript's TS2749. Reason: `X.Shape` collided with a merged namespace's `Shape` with no stated precedence, and the defaulted service's listing named a type that its emitted TypeScript does not declare. Language change: `typeof S.Service` is accepted, and a merged `Shape` member wins over the shape.

**D520. A guard's bindings and its exhaustiveness credit, wherever it stands** (2026-10-28; amends D502). The grammar admits a guard, `if (e)`, as any pattern term. It reads only the bindings of the terms tested before it, left to right and depth first (Chapter 26's pattern order), and a reference to a binding that a later term makes is EK4704; an arm whose pattern contains a guard anywhere, after `and`, in an `or`, or under `not`, removes nothing for exhaustiveness. Reason: the meaning of "the terms before it" inside `or`, `not`, and nested patterns, and the credit such an arm gets, were unstated. Language change: a guard that reads a later binding is EK4704 where its meaning was undefined.
